# Architecture

How The Administration is built. The player is the President of the United States among the 196 governments a President deals with (the 193 UN members plus Taiwan, Kosovo and Palestine). What the game is meant to be is in [NORTH_STAR.md](NORTH_STAR.md), the data shapes are in [SCHEMA.md](SCHEMA.md), the standing rules are in [DECISIONS.md](DECISIONS.md), and what the player sees and does is in [DESIGN.md](DESIGN.md).

## 1. Workspace layout

**Leadership setup extension (2026-10-10).** Both web setup paths use the existing
founding draft, candidate pool, preview and seal. Short presents identity, ticket,
Cabinet and oath, with all eight stages available through customization. Long
uses those eight stages with twenty questions in politics. Player-core owns the
versioned question text; engine `leadership-profile.ts` owns deterministic scoring
and trait conflict resolution. `leader.ts` validates and composes the same `Person`
shape used by existing disposition, affinity and education-network consumers.
The web seal checks for unapplied questionnaire changes as well as a missing
name. Answers and final overrides persist in the existing founding snapshot.

The campaign is reproduced from seed **and founding choices**. Profile changes
alter the starting person and the consumers of their ideology/traits; they do
not reroll the world or silently replace appointed candidates. Historical leader
matching, additional NPC calibration and maritime disruption state are still
open work, tracked in BACKLOG. None is implied by the new questionnaire.

A pnpm workspace (`pnpm-workspace.yaml`: `packages/*`, `apps/*`, `services/*`, `studio-cli`). Root `pnpm install && pnpm run build/typecheck/lint/test` builds every package from a clean checkout, the path `.github/workflows/rebuild-ci.yml` runs (every workflow is disabled on GitHub as of 2026-10-08).

```
packages/
  engine/       Pure deterministic simulation. No I/O, no Date.now, no Math.random.
  world/        The dataset: zod schemas, the dataset validator, and sources/ (provenance files).
  content/      Authored content schemas, pool loaders, the audit rule family, the validator.
  player-core/  Presentation-independent player logic: campaign store, saves, selectors, glossary, navigation.
  design/       Platform-neutral tokens, including the classification banners, the situation-room set and the product mark.
  ui/           The Tamagui design system for the universal player: theme, primitives, shell, icons. Source-only.
  ui-web/       Legacy React DOM primitives, flags, icons and the drawn-face renderer (portrait subpath); frozen until cut-over.
  contracts/    Shared API/DTO types between app and services. No logic.
apps/
  player/       The universal player: Expo (Expo Router) for iOS, Android and the web, on packages/ui (see §7, "The universal player").
  web/          Legacy Vite + React DOM + TanStack Router player. The map-first shell (see §7); frozen until apps/player reaches parity.
  desktop/      Electron Forge host for the player's web export (the legacy web build until cut-over).
  studio/       Internal console (plain Node HTTP): run dashboard, scenario reader and simulator, dataset inspection.
services/
  api/          Accounts, the entitlement verifier port, the starter-pack manifest audit.
studio-cli/     The generation harness CLI: jobs, drivers, validators, publish.
content/        The writers' room: ledger, reference, cover-locks (tracked); corpus/, jobs/, dist/ (gitignored build output).
design/         The app logo and icon set (brand-kit/logos, brand-kit/icons) and the pattern tiles the token stylesheet prints (patterns/).
assets/         Player imagery.
scripts/        Proof gates and data tools.
docs/           The canonical documents listed in CLAUDE.md.
```

## 2. The dependency-direction rule, enforced by tooling

**`packages/engine` depends on nothing but `packages/world`'s data; nothing in `packages/engine` imports a UI, network, or platform module.** This is not a convention — it's enforced by `eslint.config.mjs`'s `no-restricted-imports`/`no-restricted-syntax` override on `packages/engine/**/*.ts`, which bans:

- UI/platform imports (banned): `react`, `react-dom`, `react-native`, `react-native-web`, `expo`, `electron`, `steamworks.js`, `next`, …
- Network imports: `express`, `@supabase/supabase-js`, `axios`, `ws`, …
- Node I/O built-ins: `fs`, `net`, `http`, `child_process`, …
- Non-deterministic calls: `Date.now()`, argless `new Date()`, `Math.random()`

`scripts/verify-boundary-lint.mjs` proves the rule actually fires: it copies a deliberate-violation fixture (`packages/engine/__fixtures__/boundary-violation.ts.fixture`) into `packages/engine/src`, runs eslint, and asserts a non-zero exit. The verify-gate loop runs it.

## 3. The three verticals

Select, Resolve and Simulate are a conceptual split rather than directories. The engine is a flat module tree (`packages/engine/src/*.ts`), one file per concern, which keeps the dependency-direction rule easy to hold.

| Vertical | Location | What it does |
|---|---|---|
| **Select** | `@admin/content`, `scenario-cast.ts` | Condition gate (metric, alignment, relationship, counterpart trait, cabinet attribute, flag, turn, removal pressure, charter institution, war state, active condition, revealed preference), then counterpart binding, urgency weight boost, the **pacing director** and a seeded draw, reached from the player through `pendingConversation`. Selection lives above the engine because it reads authored content, which the dependency-direction rule forbids the engine from importing. |
| **Resolve** | `instruments.ts` + `resolution.ts` | See below. |
| **Simulate** | `turn-loop.ts`, `turn-phases.ts`, `relationships.ts`, `relationship-graph.ts`, `removal.ts`, `npc-agent.ts`, `npc-turn.ts`, `operators.ts`, `divergence.ts`, `cabinet.ts` and the phase modules | The turn loop and its phase list (below), relationship memory (favors and grievances aging by the calendar toward each pair's standing reading) and removal pressure each tick; the NPC utility agent decides what a nation wants and `npcActionPhase` commits it; `operatorPhase` runs the non-state actors; the divergence probe holds the "no two campaigns feel the same" claim; `cabinetConsequencesPhase` gives the President's own ministers' loyalty and ambition their own pressure. |
| **Apply** | `effects.ts` | `apply(state, EffectPacket)` is the only way state changes, regardless of source (player, NPC, operator, godmode). Every packet must carry narration or it is rejected, and every packet must name its `actor` (`"player" | "world" | "developer"`), which `apply()` copies onto the `ActionRecord` it appends — the ledger's only way to tell a decision from a thing that happened. `apply()` also stamps every entry with a `causal` provenance (`causality.ts`) — a deterministic id, an origin, the date — whether or not the packet supplied one, so no consequential event is unnameable. The stamp is computed **before** the effects are folded and the fold runs inside it, so a handler creating a record that will outlive the turn can write that id onto the record as `causedBy` and a phase months later can name it. `apply()` also decides who may move the President's alignment: an `alignment` effect is folded only when the packet's actor is `"player"` or `"developer"` (`ALIGNMENT_ACTORS`), and is dropped from any packet the world wrote, so an NPC's statement, a bill that lapses into law unsigned and a veto override move no axis. See `SCHEMA.md` §7b. |

### Endogenous fragility

`fragility.ts`. The balance harness said for a long time that a reckless presidency could not be lost, and the reason was not that the punishments were too small. It was that **nothing compounded**: a bad administration was only ever charged the flat per-act price of each act, so twenty bad decisions cost twenty times one and never more. Real governments fail because the second crisis arrives at a government the first one already hollowed out.

Ten terms, each read as *health* on the same 0..1 scale so a sign error cannot become a difficulty setting nobody can find: agency capacity, congressional support, political capital, institutional trust, cabinet cohesion, fiscal space, crisis load, market credibility, bureaucratic morale, alliance confidence. Every one is a reading of live state the player can see and move, and several of them only exist because the institution graph does.

**Fragility multiplies rather than adds.** `computeRemovalPressure` applies it to the five weighted conditions, so a healthy government's amplification is 1 and the line changes nothing for it, while a hollowed-out one absorbs the same conditions far worse. The ceiling is deliberately modest — a fully deteriorated government pays half again for a mistake, not double — because fragility is meant to be why a failing administration cannot recover easily, not a second health bar that ends the game on its own.

**Nothing is amplified invisibly.** `selectFragility` returns the terms carrying the fragility, worst first, each with a remedy the player could act on, and `selectPressureComposition` reports the amplification as its own accounted line so the components plus fragility plus any standing beat still add up to the reported pressure. `pressureCompositionTotal` is that sum, read off the same live campaign as the rows; a surface prints that sum as this week's reading rather than quoting `RemovalArc.pressure`, which is the last removal phase's figure and holds until the next tick, so a mid-week act can move the rows before it moves the arc. An amplification a screen could not account for would be exactly the hidden difficulty multiplier the design forbids. Costs are amplified and gains are not: a failing government gets less out of everything and more out of nothing.

This closed three of the four standing balance failures. Removal now fires on the reckless proxy at 0.42 against a ceiling of 0.6, while the prudent and passive proxies stay at zero, so what moved is the cost of governing badly and not the difficulty of governing at all. The fourth is still open.

### Removal is two votes, not a reading

`removal.ts` and `removal-votes.ts`, with the arithmetic in `congress.ts`. The ladder — `BEAT_THRESHOLDS` at 0.45 / 0.52 / 0.6 — used to be the whole mechanism: cross the third rung and the presidency ended, whatever the Congress thought. That taught the one falsehood the American removal clause cannot survive, that a president is removed by being unpopular enough.

**Pressure decides when the question is put; the chamber decides the answer.** The first beat is still the reading alone — talk starts because things are bad. The second additionally requires a **House majority counted against the administration**, and the third a **Senate two-thirds**, both counted by caucus from the seated chambers (`countVoteAgainstByCaucus`). The opposition votes on the room's disposition and the case, independents vote with the caucus they sit with, and the President's own members break only when the charge is grave (the last rung), and then by how many of the President's own identifiers have turned: `caucusBreakShare` reads their approval, a logistic centered at 75% under a ceiling of 0.65 of the caucus (`electorate.ts`), so the headline alone no longer opens it. A reading past a rung with no majority behind it leaves the arc exactly where it was, and the week narrates the count rather than shrugging ("The Senate counts 50 of the 67 votes needed to convict; 17 of your own 48 would have to break.") — which is what makes the gap something a president can see and work on.

**The counts are not persisted.** They are projected from `campaign.congress` and the President's own identifiers' approval whenever they are read, and they add nothing to `RemovalArc` or `Campaign`. The one stored part of removal is the inability watch below. That is what makes them whippable: seating a chamber leadership, spending disposition, an inquiry cooling the room or a midterm taking the caucus away all move the count the same week, through `chamberDisposition`, with no second arithmetic anywhere. Only `impeachment` charters are gated — a coup and a clerical council hold no floor vote.

**What counting by caucus moved, and why.** Counting by caucus replaced the conviction defection band (0.5, against the override's 0.35). That band spread the room's mood across every seat, so a Senate soured enough convicted a President whose party held more than a third of it, and the reckless proxy was removed in 12 of 12 campaigns for every party. No Senate has convicted a President: 1868 acquitted by one vote, and 1999, 2020 and 2021 acquitted with the President's party holding. The break constants are anchored on those trials rather than fitted to the harness. The ceiling, 0.65, is August 1974, when the party's Senate leaders counted about two thirds of the President's senators ready to convict. The slope (18.4) and midpoint (0.75) are solved exactly through the 2020 and 2021 roll calls on a reading of the President's own identifiers (own-party approval of about 94% and 82% in January 2020 and 2021, against 1 of 53 and 7 of 50 voting to convict), so they have no degrees of freedom and are labeled judgment; Clinton in 1999 is the one out-of-sample point, and the model puts 1.9 of 45 against 0, which is too weak a test to reject it (`docs/audits/2026-10-12-approval-model/DESIGN-VERIFY.md`). The break reads own-identifier approval, not the headline and not the projected vote it first read: the projection charges the record, and one charge on it is the arc's own beats (0.035 each), so a beat lowered the share that let the next beat carry. The earlier rule keyed the break to the headline (`BREAK_APPROVAL` 0.36 to `CEILING_APPROVAL` 0.24); both constants are deleted. The removal rates the earlier text quoted for the reckless proxy (none of twelve campaigns) were measured under that rule, and the balance harness has not been re-run on the new reading.

**Surfaces read the counts, never a copy.** A surface draws the count by caucus for each chamber, how many of the President's own must break, and the two facts that would break the caucus: the grave charge against 60%, and the approval of the President's own identifiers against the break reading (`CaucusBreak.approval`). The ladder keeps one line per counted rung, beside the five weighted terms. The removal summons clock imports `BEAT_THRESHOLDS` instead of transcribing the rungs and carries the next step's `yeas / required`.

**A President can also be removed for inability, with no charge (`inability.ts`, v66).** The ladder ends a presidency over conduct; a President who has committed nothing grave but has lost their own party had no way out short of the next election. `advanceInability` runs in `removalPhase` on the arc the ladder leaves, so a presidency the ladder ended that week is not also declared unable, and only on an impeachment charter. It watches the approval of the President's own identifiers: below `INABILITY_OWN_LINE` (0.70) for `INABILITY_SUSTAIN_DAYS` (91), the Vice President and a majority of the cabinet declare the President unable (Twenty-fifth Amendment, §4) and `countInability` takes two thirds of each chamber through the same `voteOn` and the same caucus break, with the President's own identifiers as the reading. A passing count resolves the arc with `INABILITY_ENDING`; a failing one records `failedTurn`, and no new declaration comes for `INABILITY_RETRY_DAYS` (182). The state is `RemovalArc.inability` (`{ belowSince?, failedTurn? }`), absent until it has something to record. No roll call has ever been taken under that section, so the line and both clocks are design judgment, labeled in source (DECISIONS 13.9).

### Trade as a network with an incidence

**A tariff rests on a statute.** `tariff-orders.ts` holds the statute half of an order: `tariffStatuteStanding` (whether a statute's finding holds, its room under the cap), `imposeTariff(campaign, statute, …)`, `openTariffInvestigation`, `fittingTariffStatute` (the ladder rung and scenario answers take the first statute that fits, answering a partner's own tariff under §338 first, and refuse when none does) and `tariffFindingPhase`. `trade.ts`'s `tariffPacket` is the pricing half. The act workspace reads `tariffLaws` (player-core `act/tariff-law.ts`): the President picks a law, and when its finding is missing the act orders the investigation instead of the tariff. A scenario reply reaches the same two functions: `tariffAction` lays a reply's tariff on the counterpart or answers a pinned retaliation, and `tariffLiftAction` lifts the answered tariff through `liftMeasure`, and with `theirs` the retaliation too. The decision screen cites the statute each will use (`apps/player/src/decision/authority.ts`), and the content audit's trade-act rules keep the reply's words to what it does (SCHEMA.md, "A scenario reply's trade act is a typed command"). The studio's scenario pages (library and run items) render `previewScenario` as "What each reply does": the engine's commit path on a live reachability board, never a second formula, with a board and counterpart picker (`apps/studio/reply-effects-view.mjs`).

**Authority runs on an episode clock** (DECISIONS §6.7). `Campaign.authorityClocks` holds one `AuthorityClock` per Section 122 payments episode or emergency episode. `emergency.ts#authorityDeadline` reads the merged enacted ceilings from `legislation.ts#lawConstraints` (`authorityDays`); the same effective deadline governs action checks, renewal and daily expiry (`emergencyExpiryPhase` in `day-step.ts`). Section 122 issuance in `tariff-orders.ts` reuses one national episode and writes no new clock because its partner, sector or measure changes; `tariffFindingPhase` records the national predicate's recovery, after which a later deficit can open a new episode, and `tariffAuthorityPhase` corrects an overlong legacy deadline each day before `expireTradeMeasures`. Emergency termination and renewal are catalog resolutions whose target (`Bill.target`) is frozen at introduction; `enactmentEffects` applies them on signature, unsigned enactment and successful override, and a termination reaches every declaration of its named episode but no unrelated later one. Episode matching checks every preserved predicate (`AuthorityClock.predicates`), so narrowing a national declaration to a state it already covered cannot open a fresh clock. The emergency board, the calendar, renewal cases and action comparisons are projections of `authorityDeadline`; comparisons apply the prospective packet before reading it, and renewal case identity follows the effective calendar deadline. Construction issuance (`order-subjects.ts`) and later contracting (`military-construction.ts`) select current declarations through `selectDeclaredEmergencies`; obligations already made stay payable after authority ends. The IEEPA register (`Campaign.externalEmergencies`) is a separate model and not on this clock.

**Same-turn tariff signatures remain distinct.** `imposeTariff` allocates deterministic measure and retaliation identifiers from the target's retained orders for that turn, preserving the original unsuffixed identifiers for its first order and adding an ordinal for later signatures. It checks both registers for collisions. `applyTrade` therefore retains each rate and causal origin instead of overwriting a prior signature; `currentRate` sums the live orders, and lifting one leaves the others in force. Expected retaliation points at the exact signature it answers. Existing saves use the same string-keyed records, with no schema migration. Nonfinite rates are refused before incidence is computed.

`trade-sectors.ts`, `trade.ts`, `trade-effects.ts`. The tariff rung was a treasury credit and a relationship knock, and a player could not answer the only question a tariff actually raises, which is who pays for it. It was also free at home, which is why it was always the right move.

**Edges are derived, never stored.** `tradeEdge()` computes a bilateral sector edge from the Census-recorded opening (`US_GOODS_TRADE`), moved by the two economies' growth and cut by the measures in force (`trade-volume.ts`); the dollar figure on a country file is a simulated continuation of real trade, not a shape. Before this the edge was a few percent of the smaller economy scaled by the relationship's rounded dependence, which read Canada at $20 billion a year and most small partners at nothing a tariff could touch. Nothing walks all pairs on a turn tick, the save gains no seventy-thousand-entry table, and `campaign.trade` carries only what a decision made: the measures, the expected answers, and the price each sector is carrying.

**The action shows a distribution, not a delta.** `tariffIncidence()` returns revenue, border and shelf price rises, the import fall, the diversion share, the domestic gain, the input-cost rise, the employment effect and the retaliation outlook, and the player sees exactly that: who gains, who pays, who may answer.

**Receipts are booked where they actually go.** A tariff used to credit `player.metrics.treasury` directly, which made customs duties a presidential revenue stream — the miscellaneous receipts statute inverted. `weeklyReceipts` retires the nation's own `economy.debt` by the week's collection, floored at zero, through the ordinary `nationStat` effect: the money is the general fund's, borrowing not done, never the President's own account. `tradePhase` books that week every week a United States tariff is still in force (`selectCustomsReceipts`), and the act of imposing the tariff does not. Duty is charged on the live edge (`tradeEdge`), whose imports already carry the fall every tariff in force causes, times the rate and `customsCollectionShare` (0.6 plus 0.4 of Customs' delivery). `tariffIncidence(...).revenue`, the figure the order shows, is the same product taken on the edge as it stands before the order, so a signed tariff books next week what its order showed, within rounding. A lifted tariff collects nothing. Crude, wheat and corn keep founding anchors in `commodities.ts` (Brent $69 a barrel for 2025, wheat $5.52 and corn $4.24 a bushel for 2024/25). The indexes shown at the oath are forecasts, 74/69, 6.40/5.52 and 4.80/4.24, and they are not written into inflation.

**It composes with the institution graph.** A sector names the institution whose authority a measure is taken under, and collection reads the delivery of the body the law gives customs collection to (`CUSTOMS_COLLECTION` in `executive-work.ts`, owned by CBP), so hollowing out Customs and Border Protection costs revenue on tariffs the administration has already levied, and a healthy USDA or Commerce does not collect it in CBP's place.

### The executive branch as a graph

`institutions.ts`, `institution-catalog.ts`, `institution-effects.ts`, `directives.ts` and `nomination.ts`. The executive branch used to be seven charter seats, which could not answer any of the questions the game asks of it: which agency executes a policy, what a vacancy costs, who holds an authority the President wants used, what a budget cut does to a backlog. None of those are properties of a chair.

**The distinction the module exists to make is between deciding and executing.** An institution has a mandate it is asked to deliver, a staff whose size does not move on presidential whim, a leader who may be absent or acting, and an appropriation that may not cover the mandate. What comes out is `implementationQuality`. It is a derivation, never a stored dial, and every term in it is a fact the player can see and move — which is what makes degraded delivery endogenous fragility rather than a hidden difficulty multiplier.

**Work is led by the body the law gives it to** (`executive-work.ts`). A `WorkDef` names its owner and the authority the owner holds for it, plus typed dependency edges: `approval` (a serial stage another body must clear) and `support` (a body working alongside). `selectWorkDelivery` returns the owner's quality, the delivered capacity and a status: `delivering` at the lead-dominant blend (owner 0.7, mean support 0.3); `delayed` when an approval is slower than that blend, which caps capacity at the approval's own quality because work cannot move faster than its slowest stage; `blocked` (capacity 0) when an approving body sits below `APPROVAL_FLOOR` (0.25). `workIsLawful` checks every owner and edge against the catalog's authorities. This replaced `selectExecutorsFor`/`selectDeliveryCapacity`, which sorted every body tagged with a domain by health and let the healthiest lead: customs collection was USDA's whenever USDA ran better than CBP, and a hollowed CBP collected more duty while the other trade bodies were well.

**A department's case work is a service, not a backlog number** (`services.ts`). `institutionTick` keeps one backlog per body, which drags its delivery figure and is the right reading of a body's general load. A named public service needs different facts: what arrived, what was decided and decided wrongly, what came back as rework, what was abandoned, and how long the rest has waited. `ServiceRuntime` stores those as source quantities (weekly cohorts by weeks waited, the rework pipeline, last week's flows) and every total and rate is derived on read, so a week that clears a backlog by refusing cases reads as more refusals and more errors rather than as a shorter queue. Capacity comes from `selectWorkDelivery` on the service's `WorkDef`, so the owner leads and an approval caps it: arms transfers (owner DoD) cannot close faster than State approves them. `servicePhase` runs right after `institutionPhase`, emitting one `InstitutionEffect` with `services`. `SERVICES` (`service-catalog.ts`) holds thirteen, one per department: visas (State), tax returns (the IRS, rolled up to Treasury), arms transfers (Defense, approval by State), disability claims (Veterans Affairs), federal criminal cases (Justice, approval by the Federal Judiciary, which no Secretary or directive moves), farm program applications (Agriculture), export licenses (BIS, rolled up to Commerce), wage and hour complaints (Labor), drug applications (the FDA, rolled up to HHS), fair housing complaints (HUD), student aid applications (Education), disaster assistance claims (FEMA, rolled up to Homeland Security) and drilling permits (Interior). A Secretary is read by a service only through its `shortcut`, the corner a late backlog tempts the building to cut, and only by integrity (`selectChairRunner`, so an acting Secretary counts): refusals for disability claims and farm applications, forced pleas for federal cases. Justice is therefore never scored on how many cases it brings: a pliant Attorney General closes more cases and shortens the backlog, and the reading turns bad because more of them are wrong and come back. The other services read no Secretary: a department's delivery excludes its Secretary and a component is led by its own head; those Secretaries' levers are the existing mechanisms (stewardship, program delivery, war command, appropriation quality, the pace a directive is carried out at). `portfolio-perturbation.test.ts` swaps each of the fifteen Secretaries between a strong and a weak profile and proves each moves at least its named mechanisms and nothing owned by another seat or by any institution, independent ones included. player-core's `government/services.ts#departmentServices` reads every service for the Washington executive screen: a status (keeping up, falling behind, delayed, cutting corners, labeled "clearing by refusal" or "clearing by forced pleas", failing, stopped), why, who owns the work, and what the President can do, with the summary counting services by status rather than pooling rates across services on different intake scales.

**Grant programs are project delivery, not a queue** (`projects.ts`). A department picks projects; each clears a review another body owns, then is built. `projectTick` moves review at the slowest approving body's pace (stopped below `APPROVAL_FLOOR`) and construction at the owner's, and an award arrives already `READY_AT_AWARD_MAX × competence` through review, the one place the Secretary is read: which projects to fund is the department's choice, the review's pace is not. `PROJECTS` holds highway and transit grants (Transportation, reviewed by EPA) and advanced reactor demonstrations (Energy, licensed by the independent NRC). `projectPhase` runs right after `servicePhase` and emits `InstitutionEffect.projects`. player-core's `government/projects.ts#departmentProjects` (carried on `ServicesReading.projects`) gives each program a status (on schedule, slipping, failing, review stopped), counts by stage, the stage holding it back, and an action; where the holding body is independent it says the President cannot direct it and points at the Secretary's pick. Neither services nor programs feed an outcome yet (opinion, standing, the economy): they are the authoritative record the screen reads.

**Delivery reaches the public, and that wire was missing.** `selectDeliveryShortfall` is the staff-scale-weighted vacancy drag across the executive branch, normalized so a branch whose every office has been empty long enough to bite in full reads 1, and `opinionTargets` reads it into two of the six drivers: `services`, which is whether the state is coping with what it carries, and `competence`, which is whether the government works at all. Measured on the vacancy alone rather than on capacity, and that distinction is what makes it fair rather than a permanent tax: capacity also carries morale and backlog, which stay depressed long after the offices are filled, so charging a president for those forever would mean no administration could recover from a bad start. It is written as a drag from zero, so a fully led branch reads exactly what it read before this existed.

Before it, an administration could leave every office empty for a year — delivery halved, fifty-two agencies unled — and the country's opinion of that president did not move at all. Staffing the government was, in the score, optional. The balance harness models it too: the `prudent` proxy now runs a nomination pipeline, and the width it needs to actually seat a government (twenty in flight, three sent up a week) is itself a finding — at five in flight, eight years of careful government seated eighteen of fifty-two offices.

**The branch below the cabinet opens staffed** (`career-incumbents.ts`). A transition empties the cabinet and nothing else: the outgoing administration's appointees leave with it, and the Commissioner of Internal Revenue, the Administrator of FEMA and the Chair of the Federal Reserve are all in their offices on the morning of the twenty-first. So `withCareerIncumbents` seats an incumbent in every non-cabinet leadership office at founding — forty-five of them, in catalog order — and the whole branch used to open headless, which charged every president a delivery shortfall for a transition no transition performs and left the player unable to tell a real vacancy from the default state. The incumbents are the campaign's own cast, drawn from the seeded person pool under one role (`role-agency-head`, US-only, appended last in `ALL_ROLES` so no cast seeded before it moves), so the same seed seats the same people and each one carries the attributes and the bio every dossier already reads. Nothing about how a vacancy is priced changed: `vacancy: false` is the same fact a Senate confirmation writes, and the seating re-derives `implementationQuality` exactly as the confirmation writer does.

**Removing one is a real act with the vacancy as its price** (`removeOfficer`). The counterpart to `dismissMinister` for a body with no cabinet chair behind it: the office is emptied through the `vacate` field on `InstitutionEffect` — its own field rather than `changes: { leader: undefined }`, because an explicit undefined does not survive the JSON round trip a save is — the person's `currentRole` is cleared in the same packet, and the drag accumulates from zero the way any other vacancy's does. What the office may be emptied *for* is read off the catalog rather than assumed: at-will on the President's word, for-cause only with a stated cause (and a for-cause removal raises that body's `politicization`, which is what bending a statutory body toward an administration means), term-protected not at all. The same dismissal act serves an officer and a minister.

**Telling a body to do something, and being told no** (`directives.ts`). `InstitutionRuntime.directives` was a list of free sentences nothing wrote and nothing read, and `InstitutionRuntime.investigations` was a second list of the same shape describing what an agency was being looked into for — which `campaign.oversight` has held as a staged, clocked, directional `OversightMatter` since the oversight spine landed. So the single most consequential fact about the American executive branch, that some bodies take direction and some do not, existed only as a word in a catalog. `investigations` is retired and its one reader routed to the oversight store; `directives` is now a list of ids into `campaign.directives` (shape and version step in SCHEMA §`campaign.directives`).

`issueDirective` generalizes `intelligence.ts`'s three rules off the intelligence community and onto the rest of the government. A barred instruction is not refused at the call site — it *succeeds as a refusal*: a memorandum that prints its statutory reason unaltered, politicization and oversight pressure at the body that had to write it, and, on the second refusal at that building, a matter opened by that body's inspecting department pointed at the administration rather than at the agency. Refusing at the call site would let a president try every agency for free until one of them said yes.

`directableKinds(institution)` is what a surface offers from: the kinds that body holds a function for, and nothing else, so no control there can come back saying there was nobody to address. Whether an instruction the body *could* carry is one it *may lawfully* be given is deliberately not previewed — that answer is the memorandum, and it costs politicization to get.

**`directiveRefusal` is deliberately one function, not one per family**, and it serves the Bureau, the Service, the Board and eight independent commissions. Three families sit in a `STATUTORY_BARS` table — law enforcement (Justice, the Bureau, DEA, ATF, the Marshals) on one shared authority, the IRS on the taxpayer-specific bar, the Federal Reserve on the Federal Reserve Act for every kind — and anything else whose `independence` is `independent` gets a memorandum built from its own catalog entry. Five refusal paths would have been five places for one fact to drift out of agreement and five registers for the one thing the player is meant to learn.

**The contrast is the lesson.** The same call that refuses the Federal Reserve a cut carries the EPA an enforcement posture, because the EPA is an executive agency and the Board is not. Every pressure option at the Fed has a real cost and none of them moves the rate: `policy-rate` remains an authority held by exactly one institution, no new machinery was added for it, and what the slice built there is the refusal being legible.

**No new metric channel.** A refusal is charged through dials that already exist: `politicization`, which already drags `implementationQuality` and already opens inspector-general audits, and `oversightPressure`. The matter the second refusal opens reaches the Hill through `selectOversightPressure` exactly as an audit does. A lawful instruction that lands on a `semi-autonomous` body, or on one whose delivery is below `SLOW_WALK_QUALITY`, is `slow-walked` rather than carried — read off the branch's own delivery figure rather than a second one.

**Independence is modeled as insulation in both directions.** An independent body is less degraded by presidential neglect and less improved by presidential attention, and it is excluded from `selectExecutiveCapacity` altogether. `policy-rate` is an authority held by exactly one institution in the catalog, and there is no path by which the President acquires it.

**Confirmation is a process, not a vote.** `nominationPhase` advances a nomination only when its current stage's clock has actually run out, measured in calendar days. Senate stages stretch with disposition and controversy; the White House's own vetting does not, because a Senate that dislikes the president cannot slow down the FBI paperwork. Floor congestion is real and is the binding constraint on staffing a government quickly. `openNomination` deliberately does **not** refuse a name the Senate would reject: choosing to have the fight in public is a presidential act, and refusing it would make the Senate a validator rather than an obstacle. `verify-engine-reachability` holds both actions to a player call site by their due wave.

The catalog is real institutions and fictional officeholders. No current real-world officeholder appears in it or in anything seeded from it. The structural facts it encodes — the fifteen departments, the eighteen-element intelligence community's boundaries, the Joint Chiefs as advisory rather than in the operational chain, the Vacancies Act's per-office variation — are recorded against primary sources in `docs/research/DEFENSE-IC-STRUCTURE.md`.

### The security half: theaters, the community, and the paper

`command-catalog.ts`, `combatant-commands.ts`, `ic-elements.ts`, `intelligence.ts`, `assessments.ts`, `findings.ts`, `war-powers-report.ts`, `security.ts` and `security-effects.ts`. The engine already had force: a ladder, a readiness rate, an order of battle, standing commitments, running wars and a war powers clock. What it had no model of at all was the institutions that stand between the President and any of it. So an order went from the desk to the front with nothing in between, a president could not relieve anybody, intelligence was a number the covert ladder spent rather than a body that could say no, and a covert operation needed no authority behind it.

**The chain is President → Secretary of Defense → combatant commander, and the Chairman is not in it.** `commandAuthority(campaign, nation)` resolves the theater a country sits in and reads that command's chain through `commandChain(campaign, command)`, which returns the breaks with an `execution` reading, never zero, so every surface that shows a theater's or a country's chain prints one chain from one function: an empty Pentagon is the most expensive break on the list because the statute puts the Secretary between the President and every commander in the world, and an acting secretary signs what a confirmed one does while carrying none of the standing. The Joint Chiefs advise and may carry an order; they never issue one.

**A commander commands what is assigned, and assignment is the Secretary's act.** `foundingAssignment` gives each of the eleven commands a share of each service, every service summing to one across the eleven and asserted by test. `assignedFormations` derives what a theater may actually employ from the national bench rather than keeping a second order of battle beside it. `reassignForces` is refused outright with no Secretary of Defense — a president who can order a war and cannot move a division to it.

**The map is a document.** A country's theater is its region's default plus the named exceptions that were decisions rather than geography, and `amendUnifiedCommandPlan` moves one, storing only the deviations for the reason trade stores only its measures.

**A commander is relieved, not fired, and the difference is modeled.** The deputy takes acting command the same afternoon — a theater is never unled — the officer reverts to a permanent grade and stays in the service, and the Senate confirms a successor only after `SUCCESSOR_CONFIRMATION_DAYS`. The residue lands on the institution and on the chain's execution reading rather than on a vacancy counter, which is the civilian side's mechanism and would have been the wrong one here.

**A boundary is a missing executor, not a difficulty check.** `IC_ELEMENTS` carries the eighteen elements with what each may collect and where. `taskCollection` does not refuse an unlawful request — it *succeeds as a refusal*: a memorandum, raised oversight pressure at the agency that had to write it, and, for a request aimed at American politics or a second refusal at the same element, a matter that leaves the executive branch on its own fourteen-day clock. Refusing at the call site instead would let a player try everything for free until something worked.

**A standing requirement files, on a clock.** `intelligencePhase` ticks each open tasking into a dated `CollectionProduct` every `PRODUCT_CYCLE_DAYS` (28), kept as an immutable archive plus the current product per requirement. Only fresh useful findings from an open tasking or surveillance report support analysis; closing a tasking removes current support but retains its archive. The analysis side reads it: `collectionSupport` raises `evidenceFor`'s confidence for the matching question and target by up to `COLLECTION_CEILING` (0.2), and `draftAssessment`'s own `confidenceBasis` names the collection on the paper. A government running a standing pursuit against the United States gets an intent judgment written unasked, not oftener than every 28 days — the pursuit ledger is the only record that names which government acted.

**The collection question is scoped before sources are ranked.** A new tasking selects a typed focus and a discipline; the engine intersects their eligible topics before drawing a bounded, deterministic cycle's findings. General reporting covers only the chosen discipline's topics, not every fact in the world. External intentions, force posture, economic pressure and domestic public conditions have narrower intersections; the last is reserved for DHS I&A open sources. An impossible focus/discipline pair is unavailable on the review surface and refused by `taskCollection` before authorization. Optional prose is a recorded note, not an instruction the engine interprets. Legacy taskings keep their old free-text question and broad discipline coverage, and each later report explicitly says its wording did not choose findings. This differs from `AssessmentQuestion`'s intent/capability/stability judgments: collection reports a sourced observation, while an assessment weighs what it means.

**Person-level collection needs a case, and a domestic person is a referral, never a tasking.** `intel-authority.ts` derives the only people the desk may name from state the engine already holds: the seated leader of a government with an active pursuit of the United States, and the person a public inspector-general graft finding names. `taskCollection` hard-rejects (`ok: false`, nothing logged) a missing subject, an unknown or dead person, a closed or changed case, a snapshot older than `SUBJECT_FRESH_DAYS` (28), a case of the wrong jurisdiction for the chosen kind, and any foreign-person focus other than `external-intent`. A well-formed request that no sourced pathway covers takes the existing `BoundaryRefusal` path (logged, oversight pressure, possible inspector-general referral). The only sourced foreign pathway is CIA human-source collection abroad (50 U.S.C. §3036(d)(1), (3)); any other element or discipline is refused rather than approximated. A person in the United States is refused for every element: `domesticPersonRefusal` keeps the FBI's "not an instruction" wording and extends it to the rest. The one lawful act is `referToAttorneyGeneral`, one per case and person, recorded on `agReferrals` with `U.S. Const. art. II, §3` as its only cited basis. A person-level product reports only the pursuit finding behind the case and says in `gaps` that it does not establish the person's private conduct; it does not firm up the government-level judgment (`collectionAgainst` reads `foreign-nation` products only), and it cannot be shared with the person's own government. `subjectRevalidationPackets` closes a stale person-level tasking in the weekly phase before it can file, and `withSubjectTaskingsRevalidated` does the same on load. Player-core's `personLeadRows` and `referChoices` list only these leads; the screen control is not yet rebuilt.

**An assessment is never a truth value.** `draftAssessment` returns a judgment, a likelihood word, a *separate* confidence with its basis, characterized sources, named gaps, an alternative hypothesis and attributed dissent. It reads the same world the player is in — a nation's own stats, the bilateral relationship, the live wars — rather than a private oracle, so a good community is worth having because it reads accurately and says how sure it is. `pressAssessment` moves the likelihood one rung, leaves the confidence exactly where it was, adds a dissent line, records the change on the paper forever, and costs the drafting agency morale, integrity and politicization — which lowers the confidence of everything it writes afterward. Past a politicization threshold the redrafting leaks. A president who wants the assessment to say something else has one honest option: change the world it is reading.

**Covert action has an authority behind it or it does not.** A `CovertFinding` has three honest states — written first, recorded now and reduced to writing inside forty-eight hours, or unwritten, which cannot be signed because that is what a finding stops being — and three notification tiers, where withholding prior notice requires a statement of reasons. An objective aimed at an American audience cannot be papered, and the attempt is recorded rather than discarded. Covert rungs 3 through 5 call `recordCovertAction`, so every act either counts against a standing finding or lands on the permanent list of acts with nothing standing over them.

**The forty-eight hour report is a separate obligation from the sixty-day clock.** Military rungs 2 through 5 open a `ReportObligation` whether or not Congress authorized the action. `terminationDaysRemaining` counts from `terminationStartDay`: the day the report was filed or the day it was *owed*, whichever is earlier (50 U.S.C. 1544(b)). That is the fact the mechanism exists to carry: not reporting buys nothing past the two days the statute already gives. The report states an authority, a scope and an estimate, and `warPowersReportPhase` marks a missed deadline once and prices it once, and bills every force still committed past the sixty days a compounding weekly cost for continuing — because § 1544(b) leaves three answers at day sixty and no fourth. The continuing bill reads the clock on the week's closing day (`weekCloseDate`), so the office-day cut bills the same weeks at the same compounding scale as the one-shot week; the missed report is read on the phase's own day, because its forty-eight hours can still be met later in the week.

**All of it reaches the player.** Every act in this section is an engine action held to a player call site by `verify-engine-reachability`. DESIGN.md places them under `/world/security`.

### Game time, attention and the one advance

Four pure modules sit beside the turn loop and are exported from the engine's index. They exist because the presidency was experienced in week ordinals over a turn counter, and because the app and the balance harness had no shared answer to "how far should the world run before it needs the player".

- **`clock.ts`** — the constitutional calendar over the existing pure `GameDate`: inauguration, term, ordinal day, and the next milestone with its distance in days. It is what a screen shows instead of a week number. It is arithmetic only; it reads no wall clock, because nothing in the engine may. It is also the one derivation of "what day of this presidency is it": `campaignDay(campaign)` counts days from the stored founding date and is the same number `CampaignClock.ordinal` reports, so a nomination's opening day, a war powers window and the date a screen prints are all on one counter. The oath is `CAMPAIGN_OPENING_DATE` = `FIRST_INAUGURATION`, January 20, 2029. `milestonesBetween` is the only source of election dates, and it also yields `gubernatorial-election` (Virginia and New Jersey, November of years ≡ 1 mod 4); the never-produced `term-end` kind is gone. The fiscal helpers take a fiscal year: `fiscalYearStart` and `appropriationsDeadline(2030)` (September 30, 2029), `fiscalYearOf`, `fiscalYearEnd`, `turnOnDate`/`selectTurnOfDate` (the turn a day's business is filed under: `max(1, ceil(days / 7))`, the day loop's own convention), `selectFiscalYearEndTurn` and `selectBudgetYear`. `campaignFoundingDate`, `selectCampaignClock`, `selectDateOfTurn` and `selectNextMilestones` live here too. `TERM_TURNS` (208) remains the weekly integration count of a four-year term, now derived as the whole weeks from January 20, 2029 to January 20, 2033, not a quota of decisions.
- **`pace.ts`** — the only converters between days and weeks: `weeksFor(days, "floor" | "ceil" | "round")`, `elapsedDays(turns)` (the pace defaults) and `turnsFor`. Every private conversion goes through them. `scripts/verify-one-day-counter.mjs` and its proof hold that with four rules (a second day counter, weeks multiplied into days, days divided into weeks, and an appropriation written as `turn + TURNS_PER_YEAR`); it has no debt list.
- **`causality.ts`** — the provenance vocabulary, the graph read over the one ledger, and the fold-scoped event id (`enterFold` / `currentEventId`) the record writers stamp `causedBy` from. Stamped inside `apply()`, never by a caller. **`CausalProvenance.marks` carries the flags an event leaves** (minus the `flagset:` turn-stamps, recognized by rebuilding the marker from `marked-flags.ts`'s own `flagMarker` rather than by a copied prefix), which is what finally made a presidential decision a cause of anything: a player act's effects were only `relationship`, `flag` and `metric`, none of which creates a durable record with a `causedBy` on it, so over a 52-week run a decision produced zero conditions, zero commitments and zero pursuits — the `enterFold`/`currentEventId` mechanism that makes tariffs and conditions traceable had nothing to attach to, and "why did this happen" was empty forever. **One edge** is drawn from it: the situation's own gate — the flags an earlier decision set, which this week's scenario required. A second was built and withdrawn on the owner's call the same day: the thread between two capitals, meaning the last act that passed between them. It carried 87–90% of the graph's whole reach, and that is exactly why it went: reach is not what is worth optimizing in a causal record, and an edge that asserts a link from adjacency between two governments rather than from anything either act stated makes a "why this happened" a player cannot trust. `selectExchangeBetween` remains as a reading a surface may ask for by name; what it may not do is enter the graph as though the record had claimed it. The cost is stated rather than hidden: an instrument act declares no gate, so the coercion ladder is outside the causal graph until content-driven measurement lands. It lives here rather than in `effects.ts` because both the mutator and the domain writers it calls already depend on this module, and the alternative would make every writer import the mutator that calls it. See `SCHEMA.md` §7b.
- **`institutions.ts` — `seatCabinetOffice`, the one writer of a chair.** `InstitutionDef.cabinetRole` joins a cabinet seat to the body it runs, and every path that seats or empties one (founding, `applyCabinet`, a confirmed nomination) goes through this function, which writes `player.cabinet` and the institution's `leader`/`vacancy` together, and the person's `currentRole` and `loyaltyAtAppointment` with the seat. `seatOfficer` is the same writer for any body (a department goes through `seatCabinetOffice`, any other body is led directly), and both end acting authority. The race guards run both ways: `openNomination`, the appointment pool, `selectAppointableFor` and a no-cost withdrawal in `nominationPhase` all refuse or step around a chair or a person the other path has already taken. Before it, the two were unrelated records keyed differently — `player.cabinet` by charter role, `campaign.institutions` by institution id — so a seated Secretary of State sat above a Department of State reading "No confirmed officer" on the same screen. One office was two records and they disagreed in front of the player.
- **`fillVacantOffices` — sending names up for every empty office at once.** Fifty Senate-confirmed offices open on inauguration day, and the appointment that matters is who goes where rather than the fifty presses it took to say so. It takes the President's picks (`NominationPicks`, office to person) and sends the pool's first name only where there is no pick. Each packet is built against the state the previous ones produced, so the returned list must be committed in order and the same person is never sent up twice: a pick already before the Senate for an earlier seat is refused by `openNomination` for a later one. Nothing is seated by it: every name still runs vetting, committee and the floor, so a president who sends fifty up at once buys the queue and the floor congestion a real one gets.
- **The appointment pool: one ranking and one path for every Senate-confirmed seat** (`appointment-pool.ts`, `appointment-eligibility.ts`, `appointment-lens.ts`, `appointment-bench.ts`; 2026-10-13, `docs/audits/2026-10-13-appointments/`). Cabinet chairs once ranked party first, agencies by years in a coarse career table, and the courts by competence alone, so the Chief Justice's seat was offered an economist, one engineer topped four health and science agencies, a civilian topped the Joint Chiefs, and the same cabinet chair could be filled by a same-day vote from its office page or by the pipeline from Oversight. Now:
  - **One path.** `openNomination` fills every seat the Senate confirms, a cabinet chair included (its packet carries the crossing-the-aisle loyalty cost for a cabinet seat the day it is announced); the deputy acts meanwhile. `appointMinister` and `projectAppointment` are gone. `openNomination` refuses an office the Senate does not confirm (`seatKindOf` undefined), a foreign national, a person already before the Senate, and anyone a seat's rules forbid (`seatRuleRefusal`). `selectNominableFor` is the pool's people without its reasons; the balance proxy nominates its first name.
  - **Hard rules** (`appointment-eligibility.ts`), sourced in the audit's `SOURCES.md`: the Secretary of Defense and the service secretaries from civilian life past the statutory wait (10 U.S.C. §113(a), §§7013, 8013, 9013), read off the career (`isServingOfficer`, `yearsOutOfUniform`; twenty years in command stands in for flag rank, which is not modeled); the Chairman of the Joint Chiefs a serving officer (10 U.S.C. §152(a)); a legal career for the federal bench, the administration's standard and not law (`hasLegalCareer`: read law, practiced it or sat as a judge). A cabinet chair's bench stays its `eligibleRoles` gate (DECISIONS §4.9). Below the cabinet a candidate must have done or studied what the seat's primary domain hires.
  - **Relevance** (`appointment-lens.ts`): per domain, weighted careers and fields; academia counts only for someone who studied the seat's own field; the Attorney General weighs reading law as much as a decade of practice (28 U.S.C. §503 sets no qualification); the bench weighs a judge first; the Joint Chiefs weigh years in command. A circuit nominee from the seat's circuit ranks up, read from birthplace because residence (28 U.S.C. §44(c)) is not modeled.
  - **Rank**: relevance, then party as a weight rather than the first key, politics beside the President's, and the record the Senate reads; bodies kept at arm's length weigh the record over politics, the Joint Chiefs weigh no politics, and life seats weigh a tenure band from age. Each `PoolCandidate` carries its `PoolReason`s (career years, study, the agency bench, serving or veteran, home circuit, tenure band, party side), every one a fact the dossier already shows. No credit is given for a role another charter has.
  - **The bench a vacancy brings** (`vacancyBenchEffects`): when a Supreme Court, circuit or Joint Chiefs seat opens (`judicialVacancyPhase`, `removeOfficer`), the engine draws judges or serving officers with `generateRolePerson` under the vacancy-bench roles, up to the seat's `POOL_MINIMUM` of credible names, keyed on seat, day and slot, and writes them as `person` effects. Never from a selector, so reading a pool creates nobody and waiting never yields a better bench; a second call for the same vacancy names the same people and writes none twice. A circuit's judges are drawn from its own states.
- **`attention.ts`** — `AttentionMatter`, classified hard, soft or ambient, deduplicated by `subject`, and capped at three (its `AttentionSource` union includes `market-shock` and `coup`; see "a world that moves on its own"). This is the thing that stops the world, and it is deliberately not a scenario: a vacancy, a war, a bill and an order review all raise a matter, and an authored scenario is one source among them rather than the only one. Dedupe by subject is what replaced `SummonsSpec.badgedBy`: a clock naming the same subject as a standing condition is that condition on a deadline, not a second claim on the President, so badge ownership holds by construction instead of by a hand-kept field. A matter can also be put off rather than opened — putting a matter off from its own row and closing the stop brief both commit the same `deferUntil` packet through `apply()`, so a deferral lands on the ledger like any other decision rather than a screen quietly forgetting a row. `deferUntil` never defers past a matter's own deadline, with one exception: a deadline that has already arrived is put off to the presidency's next closing day, the weekday it was sworn in on (`closingDayOnOrAfter(from, foundingDate)`), so a lapsed matter comes back at most once a week on the rhythm Continue already keeps; a caller that passes no founding date keeps the older one-day put-off. Held to its own date, a due-today matter was deferred to a day that had already come, so a lapsed appropriation or a removal summons due that day stopped every Continue before its first day and the presidency could not move. The player has read it and let it lapse; the day it lapses on runs, and if it still stands afterward it is raised again. `AttentionSource` gained `standing-threshold`: a standing figure (approval, the press's standing) crossed a line the country noticed and nothing asked the President for anything. `market-threshold` is the fiscal readings' alone, so a per-kind setting on one never mutes the other. It also carries `scandal` (a story the country can already read; it pauses on arrival), `incident` (a home incident — one bad afternoon in one place; it pauses on arrival) and `resignation-offer` (a minister offering to go; it waits for a last call). The three are declared on the union, listed in `ATTENTION_SOURCES` and defaulted in `attention-policy.ts` in the same change, because that list carries a compile-time exhaustiveness check and the build fails without them. A minister's own proposal uses the declared `agency-request` rather than a tenth kind.
- **`advance.ts`** — `advanceUntilAttention`. It walks the calendar **one day at a time** until a matter is raised, the caller's horizon is reached, or the presidency ends. Each day, `holdElectionsDueToday` holds an election or the second-term oath on its own date before the week's tick, so a midterm is filed on Tuesday, November 5, 2030 whichever weekday the oath fell on. `ended()` is `campaignIsOver`, so `StopReason` `"ended"` is produced by removal, defeat and the term limit alike, with no tick after election night. A federal Election Day in a presidential or midterm year returns `StopReason` `"election"` when the President is still in office; an odd-year governors' night does not. a second Continue on an ended campaign returns `"ended"` with no days elapsed. Weekly simulate/select phases still run on seven-day boundaries via `tickWeek`, unless the phases carry `days`, in which case each office day runs its own group and the close still happens on the seventh day. The player store and the balance harness both pass that cut; `advanceTurn` still runs the flat list; a 1–6 day horizon can now return a real elapsed span instead of zero. Due issued acts (Hill lobbying, press, treaty consent, force authorization) resolve on their due date, in id order within each kind, before that day's look for matters. The day an answer the President was waiting on comes back, the run stops once on it (`answersReturned`): a trade investigation's finding (`finding:<statute>:<subject>:<turn>`, source `investigation`), the Senate's vote on a treaty (`issued-treaty:<act>`) or Congress's on a use of force (`issued-aumf:<act>`), each `soft`, read off the change between the run's start and the day rather than raised as a standing matter, the same way an operation's first report and partners' replies stop it. When the caller passes `stagingSources` or `lapseSources`, that same day admits held documents and, on a document's `closes` date, applies the lapse packets and removes the entry (`settleHeldDocket`). Omitting both leaves `campaign.docket` absent. `advanceTurn` is still one tick. `AdvanceResult.ambientEvents` is every feed item filed during the call (`state.feed.slice(campaign.feed.length)`), not every item filed under a later turn: a one-day step onto Election Day files its press outcomes under the turn it started in, which a read of the feed by later turns would drop. **The app drives this function; the balance harness drives `advanceTurn`.** The harness and the fixture generator run whole weeks, whose phases see the week's opening date, so under them an election is held at the tick that closes its week and the ledger dates it to that week's opening day, one turn later than the day loop files it; `term-balance.test.ts` asserts that offset. The player supplies `matterSources` to the campaign store and Continue calls `store.continueUntilAttention()`, so there is one way to spend time and the engine decides how much of it a press buys. The supply is the desk module's standing conditions, the fourteen statutory clocks and the constitutional calendar — one collapsed list behind `packages/player-core/src/desk/attention.ts`'s `deskMatterSource` — plus the scenario director's own `scenarioMatterSource`. `AttentionSource` gains `pledge`: `packages/player-core/src/desk/attention-pledges.ts` turns `selectDuePledges` (open pledges due within 14 days, nearest first, overdue ones included) into soft attention rows, so Continue stops a fortnight before a promise falls due rather than on the day it is broken. `governor-request` (`desk/attention-requests.ts`: one row per emergency rather than per state, soft inside a week of the claim's expiry), `consequence-return` (`desk/attention-outstanding.ts`), `market-threshold` (`desk/attention-fiscal.ts`: a rating cut below the notch the books opened on, the rating outlook turning negative, the coupon market rate up a quarter point or more in 13 weeks (`selectBooksChange`), interest passing a quarter of revenue having opened at or under it (`selectBooksOpening`'s opening share against `selectFiscalStatement`), the debt limit close and then binding; ambient notices rather than asks, since no economic act answers a rating agency or the Treasury's headroom, each limit row dated, the approach on the week the room runs out and the crossing on its X-date) and `standing-threshold` (the press turning) are wired; `intelligence-warning` is the one value still unwired.

**The world stops on change, not on a level** (DECISIONS §2.2). `AttentionMatter` carries an optional `signature`: what the matter looks like to the President now. `lookForMatters` (`advance.ts`) filters the gathered matters through `withoutDeferred` and then `withoutNoticed` (`attention.ts`) before `composeAttentionStop`, so a matter whose signature equals its entry in `Campaign.noticed` is held back like a deferral and cannot crowd a matter that is news out of the three places. `reviseNotices` then records what was presented and drops every entry whose matter has gone or changed form, which is what re-arms a matter that left and came back. `withNotices` writes the change as a presentation packet (`presentation: true`, an `attention` effect with `notice`/`forget`), which `apply` folds with no ledger entry and no causal id, and writes nothing when the look changed nothing. The player-core desk gives each row its signature (`desk/attention.ts#signatureOf`: hard or one level for soft and ambient, stance, deadline stage open/far/week/due); the removal summons's is `rung:<next rung>` (`summons/due-executive.ts#removalEpisode`). Scenario matters carry none and stop on every look. `presentedItems`, which holds Continue and sets `holds` on a Brief row, composes over the same `withoutNoticed(withoutDeferred(...))`, so Continue holds on exactly what the engine would stop on; `owedItems` is unchanged, so a noticed matter stays on the work list. The `AttentionStop` shape and `advanceUntilAttention`'s signature did not change; `AdvanceResult.campaign` now carries `noticed`. The store's byte-identical one-day-step test compares the campaign without `noticed`, the one field that legitimately differs with the settings.

**The player chooses which kinds of matter stop the world** (`packages/player-core/src/desk/attention-policy.ts`, read through `PreferencesProvider`). `Preferences.attentionByKind` sets each `AttentionSource` to `pause` (stop on arrival), `wait` (held back until its own last call, `LAST_CALL_DAYS` 2 before its deadline, and if the deadline is missed anyway presented on the founding weekday's cadence) or `log` (never stops Continue; offered only for the kinds in `LOG_ELIGIBLE`, which carry no deadline and price no silence). `applyAttentionPolicy(matters, settings, date, foundingDate)` filters the candidates before `composeAttentionStop` runs, never after, so a waiting matter cannot hide behind a paused one it collided with and the cap of three runs on the list the settings produced. A hard matter is never touched, a staged scenario always pauses (`POLICY_EXEMPT`), and the defaults (`defaultAttentionSetting`) pause the constitutional and appropriations deadlines, a bill on the desk, an escalation, an intelligence warning, a disaster and a scenario, and let the rest wait. `ATTENTION_SOURCES` fails to compile the day the union grows a member it does not list. Silence is charged at most once per document per week (`campaign/store.ts`).

**Continue is held while work is open.** The player holds Continue and the one-day step while a staged decision is waiting or a blocking matter is open, and names the work (the map-first Brief has to enforce this; it is not built yet). Continue does not defer that work as part of the same press, and reading a decision is not an answer. A matter is put off from its own row, which still commits `deferUntil` through `apply()`. `continueUntilAttention` still prices every scenario a caller leaves unanswered, and still appends the `deferUntil` put-off for that scenario's matter (`silencePutOff` in `campaign/store.ts`), so a loop that reached it would not re-raise the same document on the same day. Continue is not that caller while the decision or the blocking matter is open. Stepping the clock one day can still spend a day while a matter is open; that is the living-clock contract, and a store test holds it. Beside a held Continue sits one more press, **"Hand the rest to the Chief of Staff and continue"** (`store.handOffAndContinue`, planned by `campaign/hand-off.ts`). What must come from the President stays: a constitutional deadline, a bill's signing window, anything a statute runs a clock on, and any matter at its last call (`mustComeFromPresident`, reading `LAST_CALL_DAYS`); while one stays, the press is disabled and the note names it. Everything else is taken by the path the player already has, one player act apiece, so each is on the Record and none is cheaper than doing it by hand: every owed attention row is put off with its own row's `deferMatterPacket`, and every waiting decision is charged the `inactionPacket` Continue charges a walked-past decision (skipped where Continue skips it), its case closed `allow-default`, and the desk closed for the week as an answer closes it. Then the world runs as Continue runs it. **Both ways of spending time share one set of day sources.** `continueUntilAttention` and `stepClockDay` build their options from one `advanceOptions` in `campaign/store.ts` (the phase cut plus `scenarioStagingSource` and `scenarioLapseSource`), so a press and a running clock admit and lapse the same documents on the same day. **The week's decision is a case.** Both paths call `seatScenarioCases` before they run: the director's pick is frozen into an `authored-scenario` case (`conversation/scenario-cases.ts`) and `pendingConversations` returns the frozen cast while any such case is open, so a later draw does not replace it. A case carries the desk's own response window as an `opportunity` clock (`RESPONSE_WINDOW_DAYS`) and `allow-default` among its dispositions, so the engine's `withDueCaseDefaults` lapses an unanswered decision when its window closes and the next draw can come; the lapse source still prices a case that lapsed before its held document closed. Putting a decision off defers its case as well as its matter (`scenarioPutOffEffect`, never past the day before its window closes), a case put off is off today's desk, and a case whose bound record is over is withdrawn at the next seat rather than holding the desk. When `selectEnding` reports the President is no longer in office, the player shows that ending in place of the open-work list. Removal and a completed second term are their own sentences and do not quote a vote. A defeat names the stored presidential share and the Electoral College count that share wins on the current map. `projectVote`'s drivers are listed only while that live share still matches the stored night.

**A presidential case is durable campaign state.** `Campaign.cases` is optional and keyed by id. `{ kind: "case" }` either raises one case or appends one transition. History is not rewritten. A closed case, a forbidden disposition, a second raise of the same id, a deferral on or past a deadline, and a delegation to a blank institution throw from `apply()`. `normalizeCampaign` drops a damaged entry and does not record that drop as a decline. `dueDefaultEffects` emits an `allow-default` transition for a due case that allows it. That transition changes status only. Selectors `openCases`, `blockingCases`, `dueCases` and `deferredCases` live in `packages/engine/src/cases.ts` and do not import content or a screen. `advanceUntilAttention` applies `withDueCaseDefaults` before each day, which sets a due case to `defaulted` and leaves it on the record. An open authored-scenario case is what `pendingConversations` returns, ahead of a new draw from the pool, including when `desk:closed:<turn>` is set. A case settled today is not replaced today. A desk with no case still uses that flag to space the next draw. Answering a scenario closes the case of the same id when one is open. Five families raise their own cases from the records that already hold them (`packages/player-core/src/desk/family-sources.ts`): a bill on the desk (`case:<billId>`, statutory, dated on the last of its ten days after presentment, Sundays excepted, and raised only while the President can still sign or return it), a nominee with findings (`case:nomination:<id>`, no clock), the appropriations deadline once it is closing and a lapse (`case:budget:<throughTurn>` and `case:budget:lapse:<turn>`), a relief request and a declaration in its continuation window (`case:emergency:…`, operational; a relief request carries the condition it is about, its headline, place and link, and that condition has no attention row of its own: the week the emergency arrives the case row takes the condition's kind, so the player's disaster setting still stops the run on arrival), and an allied government's compact call (`case:foreign-request:<org>:<conflict>`, opportunity, dated on the grace's end). A sixth source is the markets (`desk/family-commodity.ts`): four factories raise a case while a commodity's band stays past its line — a strategic-reserve release for crude (`emergency`, elevated or worse), allied supply for grains (`foreign-request`, spiking), critical-mineral supply (`emergency`, spiking) and farm inputs (`emergency`, elevated or worse), as `case:commodity:<factory>:<commodity>:<turn>` with a `market-threshold` attention row. One open case per factory and commodity; a case whose market came back before anything was decided settles as `allow-default` and says so. The index is read through the content package's own commodity reading (`commodityIndex`, `scenario-pulse.ts`), which is itself raised from the market model's stored prices via the engine's `selectCommodities`, so nothing is raised until the market model stores one. `seatFamilyCases` raises a new source and closes a case whose source is gone with what happened (signed, vetoed, withdrawn, funded, lapsed) at the store's run, step, commit and hand-off seams. Each source's attention row carries its case id, so the attention composer, Continue and the Chief of Staff read the same matter the case queue draws, and a put-off writes the attention deferral and the case deferral together. A family matter is drawn only in the case queue.

**An act and its save are separate answers.** `commit`, `commitAll` and `continueUntilAttention` resolve with an `ActReceipt` (`eventIds`, `persisted`, and `saveError` when the write failed) rather than rejecting when only the write fails, because by then the act has applied and been published and a rejection would tell the screen the act did not happen. They still reject when nothing applied: no campaign, an ended presidency, or a stale basis. A packet carries the campaign it was priced against (`CommitOptions.basis`), and a mismatch refuses with `STALE_ACT_MESSAGE`, which is what turns a double press, or a preview the week moved under, into a refusal instead of a second charge. `retrySave()` rewrites the campaign in play and replays nothing. The player reads the save state and offers the retry.

**The wall clock never crosses the boundary at all.** The engine is handed no timestamp and no elapsed duration: the only thing that buys world time is a player present and pressing Continue, and what that buys is decided by `advanceUntilAttention`'s horizon in days, not by how long anybody was here. This is what closes the defect where `store.restore()` converted hours of absence into drift steps and could run a term out through a closed laptop — restore advances nothing, because absence accrues no currency to catch up with.

**Modeling a choice runs the same advance on a copy.** `packages/engine/src/modeling-projection.ts` is the workbench's engine half. `projectRun(campaign, alternative, options)` starts from `believedCampaign(campaign, member, options.basis)` (`projection-belief.ts`: the world as the government believes it, a staff forecast in normal play and the truth only in God Mode; DECISIONS 7.11), spread into a detached copy whose seed is `projectionSeed(seed, member)` (`<seed>::projection::<member>`), builds the alternative's packets on that copy with that copy's streams, applies them through `apply()`, then runs `advanceUntilAttention` seven days at a time for the horizon, carrying past every stop (no matter sources; a stop is something the President would answer, and a projection takes no further decision). It returns `projectionReading` at day 0 and at each week's close: approval, budget authority, stability, standing, readiness, removal pressure, the six judgments and every cohort's support. Nothing is stored and no field is added to `Campaign`. The live seed is never used, so a projection cannot show the realized outcome of a roll the commit will make; every alternative in one ensemble member shares that member's seed, so a paired difference is the choice and not the draw. `projectAlternatives` runs `PROJECTION_ENSEMBLE_SIZE` (5) members per alternative; horizons are `PROJECTION_HORIZON_WEEKS` (4, 13, 26, 52). `packages/player-core/src/modeling/` supplies the alternatives under the option ids of the screen that commits them (a decision's replies against leaving it unanswered, an executive order against not signing, the draft budget against the book in force), each built with the same engine call that screen commits with; the store's own phases, staging and lapse sources and case seating (`modelingAdvance`); and the view model (`modelingView`: median and range per metric per alternative per week, paired deltas against not acting on the members both sides have finished, the groups helped and hurt, the biggest movers, a sentence on whether the runs agree). `forecastSource` (player-core) picks the seat that forecasts each choice (State, Defense, the intelligence director, Treasury, the Attorney General, Homeland Security, the Chief of Staff, or the department the charter gives an order), takes its occupant's competence as the basis and labels the forecast with source and confidence in the slot; the forecast for the option taken is filed on the Record behind the act (`forecastPacket`, `Campaign.forecasts`, `adviserTrackRecords`). There is no modeling screen: the projection is laid into the choice list where the choice is made (`ChoiceList`/`CompareAuthorize`'s optional `projection` slot in `packages/ui-web`, filled by `apps/web/src/modeling/ChoiceProjection.tsx` on the decision, executive order and budget screens), off until a horizon is pressed, run in a staggered pool of module workers, rows filling as their runs finish.

### Apply: delta versus absolute semantics

An effect field named for movement moves the value; an effect field named `set` replaces it. The two are never confused, because a caller that writes a delta and gets an overwrite silently destroys state it never meant to touch.

| Effect | Field | Semantics |
|---|---|---|
| `metric` | `deltas` | **Additive.** Added to the live metric and clamped: treasury is floored at zero (an administration cannot be overdrawn on its own budget authority), every other metric to 0..1. Two keys are *not* additive. `approval` is not a key the effect admits: `MetricEffect.deltas` is `MetricDeltas`, which types `approval` as `never`, so a site that tries to write it, even through a widened `Partial<MetricSet>`, fails to compile; the headline is settled from the public at the end of the packet by `recomputeApproval` and never nudged (a score an effect can set is a score nothing explains), and `applyMetric` still skips the key for a packet that arrives untyped. `militaryReadiness` which is the force itself rather than a panel number and is routed onto the stat by `spendReadiness` and mirrored back. Several `metric` effects in one packet sum. |
| `relationship` | `deltas` | **Additive**, clamped to the scalar's `Relationship` schema range (`trust`/`alignment` −1..1, `tension`/`tradeDependence`/`militaryThreat` 0..1), so no caller can write a campaign that would fail dataset validation. |
