| `relationship` | `set` | **Absolute** — for the rare genuinely absolute change, such as a full embargo taking trade dependence to zero. Applied before any delta on the same field. |
| `nationStat` | `setStats` / `setTraits` | **Absolute**, deep-merged. Every caller computes the new figure against live nation stats. |
| `city` | `deltas` / `set` | **Additive** employment, `taxBase`, US `adminLean` (clamped ≥ 0 / ≥ 0 / −1..1) and US `demShare` (0..1); **absolute** `taxHolidayTurns` (≥ 0). Unknown city throws. Foreign `adminLean` deltas are ignored. Employment rolls into that nation's unemployment in the same handler. |
| `alignment` | `pull` | **Additive** (`alignment.ts`), so alignment reflects decision history, and folded only from a packet the President or god mode wrote (`ALIGNMENT_ACTORS` in `effects.ts`); a world-written packet's alignment effect is dropped. |
| `opinion` | `deltas` / `polarize` / `heal` / `salience` | **Relative, none absolute; `salience` is a record.** `deltas` moves the six judgments undamped: polarization acts once, inside the five blocs (`electorate.ts`), not on the deltas. `polarize` (positive-only) and `heal` (a distance toward the era's anchor, from either side) move `polarization`, which is a stock (the era's anchor 1 plus a drift held in [−0.15, +0.25]) and not one of the six. The stock has several writers in a turn (the impact model raises it on every divisive decision, `demosPhase` adds a dispersion term, `opinionPhase` reverts it by `weeklyReversion`, a 104-week half-life), so no field may write the settled figure: a whole-value write computed by one writer would erase whatever another had already added, whichever of them the sequence runs first. Because both moves are relative, healing and dividing compose to the same figure in either order, up to the step's saturation. Each push is a share of a fully divisive week (`polarize` over `MAX_TURN_POLARIZE`, `polarization.ts`, provisionally 0.012, a balance figure the harness owns rather than a contract constant); the **joint** ceiling on what one turn may add is enforced once in `advanceTurn`, as a rise of no more than one fully divisive week from the stock the turn opened on (`maxWeeklyRise`), against the turn-start figure it already holds beside `closing` and `closingPressure`, because that is the only scope that can see the sum. Only the rise is capped. `salience` replaces `Campaign.salienceMemory` whole and is written by `opinionPhase` alone. Recurring background writers are budgeted apart from all this: `WEEKLY_DIRECT_MOOD_BUDGET` (0.05 per judgment per week, `capTurnMood`). |
| `issuedAct` | `set` | **Absolute** replacement of one issued-act record by id. Issuance writes the pending act; resolution writes the same id as `resolved` or `expired`. |
| `conflict` | `set` | Full replacement of one war's record, recomputed by `conflicts.ts` from the week it just resolved — the same contract as `removal`, so state never holds a half-written war. |
| `condition` | `set` | Full replacement of one `NationCondition`, same reasoning. |
| `pledge` | `set` | **Absolute** replacement of one `Pledge` by id (appended when the id is new). The ledger's only writer, emitted by `pledgePhase` when a pledge settles. |
| `appropriation` | `fundTurns` / `throughTurn` | A **patch** on `congress.appropriation`, emitted by a continuing resolution: `throughTurn` becomes the larger of the book's own and the stopgap's (it never shortens), `onPriorYear` goes true and `priorYear` empties. |
| `acting` | `seat` / `person?` | Sets or clears `Campaign.actings[seat]`. Its own kind rather than a variant of `cabinet`, because an acting is not a confirmed holder and writing one into `player.cabinet` would claim a Senate vote that never happened. A packet with no `person` **removes** the key rather than setting it undefined, so the save cannot carry a seat that is neither filled nor empty. Produced by `actingEffects` (`appointments.ts`): `appointActing`, `vacates` (every departure), and the seal's contested chairs; the week's agenda clears a key whose body's authority has lapsed. |
| `cabinetAgenda` | `set` | Full replacement of `Campaign.cabinetAgenda`, the same reasoning `removal` and `conflict` replace whole: the agenda is recomputed from the week that just ran, and a field-by-field merge would leave a withdrawn proposal standing because nothing explicitly removed it. Produced by `cabinetAgendaPackets` (`cabinet-week.ts`, appended to `cabinetLoyaltyPhase`) and by `answerResignationOffer` / `takeMinisterProposal`. |
| `removal` | `arc` | Full replacement, recomputed each turn from live state. A surface coloring the reading asks `removalSeverity(arc)`, which derives calm/caution/adverse from the beats the arc has genuinely opened rather than a hand-picked figure that matches no threshold in `removal.ts`. |

### The registries: what each figure is, and who reads and writes it

Three registries describe the figures the game keeps, and they are extended rather than duplicated. `packages/engine/src/metric-registry.ts` (`METRIC_REGISTRY`) is the campaign's own metrics as a graph: approval, polarization, the appropriation and obligations, budget authority, treasury health, stability, readiness, standing, the six judgments, removal pressure and its terms, congressional hostility and the instruments, each with its parents and children, unit, cadence and weight table. Each node also carries `kind` (a `QuantityKind`, the vocabulary `packages/world/src/metrics/definitions.ts` shares with the reference-series comparison; a container node such as `instruments` has none), `writePolicy` (`founding-only`, `derived-only`, `phase-written` or `effect-written`, the same `WritePolicy` the world registry uses) and, for a node the campaign stores rather than recomputes, `field` (`MetricSet.stability`, `PublicMood.security`). `metric-registry.test.ts` holds that every leaf node has a kind and every named field is one the campaign keeps. The studio's metrics page draws it beside the world's `METRICS`. The authored fields' readers are in `packages/world/src/dataset/stat-consumers.ts` (`SCHEMA.md` §3), named as `path#declaration` sites the paired test resolves.

`scripts/report-metric-registry.mjs` is the check on both, and `scripts/verify-metric-registry.mjs` turns it into a failing gate. The report builds one TypeScript program over the world, engine, content, player-core and web sources, with every workspace import mapped to source (through the built `d.ts` each zod shape is inlined again into every schema that embeds it, so one field would have many declarations), and walks it with the type checker: a property access, a computed key over a literal union, a destructured binding, `Object.values`/`entries` over a typed record, a typed local initialized from a registered record, and a call one level deep (a registered record handed to a helper that reads it through its own parameter type) all resolve to the field's declaration. A typed patch handed over whole is followed down to its leaves, across a union of literal patches; a narrower patch spread into one counts as its keys; a patch built with computed keys (`{ [group]: { [field]: next } }`, as `policy-program.ts#statEffect` and `order-lineage.ts#ownerWeight` build theirs) is resolved from the dotted path in scope typed as a literal union (`PolicyStatPath`) and credited with every field that union names. It lists, per registered field and per stored engine node, the top-level declarations that read and write it, keyed `path#declaration`, and writes `scripts/metric-registry-report.json`; the committed report's diff is the audit. It names what it cannot resolve rather than guessing: `claimedNotSeen` (a registry site the checker did not see read the field, usually through a helper's return value), `leaky` effect payloads (an effect literal handed a patch typed as the whole patch type: 20 at R2, every one a whole `MetricDeltas`), `computedKey` patches with no dotted path in scope (none at R2), and `fieldsWithNoDeclaration` (the cohort shares, whose schema is built from a computed key list). One handler reroute is modeled explicitly (`HANDLER_REROUTES` in `scripts/lib/metric-sites.mjs`): a readiness delta on the panel is a write of `military.readiness`. A `metric` effect's write is credited to the site that builds its deltas, by the keys it sends, and the handler (`applyMetric`, listed under `handlers`) is not credited: `MetricDeltas` is a `Record` over `MetricSet`'s keys with no declarations of its own, so its keys are mapped onto `MetricSet` by name (`PAYLOAD_ALIASES`), in the engine and content packages only, since in player-core and the web app a `MetricDeltas` is a price restated for the player. `scripts/lib/content-gates.mjs` reads the corpus the player is dealt (the authored packs and the baked shards, from `dist/`) and counts per field the scenarios whose conditions or `band` cast gate on it (`contentGates`), through a table whose every row names the evaluator that reads the field (`scenario-cast.ts#conditionMet`, `#operatorPressure`, `scenario-pulse.ts#pulseMet`, `scenario-counterpart.ts#bandMatches`); kinds that read no registered field are listed with what they read instead. The gate fails on an unregistered stored field, a registered field with no declaration outside the named blind spots, a field with no reader and not owed, a field whose write policy says a campaign moves it with no writer outside the dataset package and the engine's `founding*` modules, a `founding-only` field written anywhere else, a scenario gate credited through an evaluator the walk does not see reading the field, a condition kind the gate table does not classify, an unconfirmed claim beyond the ratchet baseline `scripts/baselines/metric-registry.json`, a blind site (leaky payload or unresolved computed key) beyond the ratchet baseline `scripts/baselines/metric-registry-blind-sites.json`, and a stale committed report; it prints its blind spots (the cohort shares, claims outside the typed packages, gates on relationship edges and the President's alignment) on every run. Run both after `pnpm run build`.

### Simulate: one phase list, owned by the engine

`turn-phases.ts` exports `campaignPhaseSequence(charter)`, a named ordered list, and `campaignTurnPhases(charter, layout?)`, the turn's whole `simulate` sequence built from it. The list in source is the canonical order; the table restates it with the reason each phase sits where it does.

| # | Phase | Why it sits here |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | `npcActionPhase` | The world moves first: nations act. |
| 2 | `pursuitPhase` | Right after the acting, so a campaign nobody pressed this week is already stale by the time anything else reads it. |
| 3 | `operatorPhase` | Actors outside any government's writ. |
| 4 | `conflictPhase` | The wars take their week. A join and an NPC settlement decide inside it. |
| 5 | `munitionsPhase` | The week's draw on stocks reads the fighting it paid for. |
| 6 | `worldEventPhase` | After the week's actors, so an event between two governments answers their moves rather than racing them. |
| 7 | `commodityPhase` | Prices move before the economy drifts, so a shock reaches inflation and growth through the pressure the drift reads rather than by a level write. |
| 8 | `macroDriftPhase` | Before the conditions, which read this week's economy. The only writer of growth, inflation and unemployment: a condition states its macro pressure and this phase applies it. |
| 9 | `defaultPhase` | A default abroad reads the economy the drift has just settled. |
| 10 | `conditionPhase` | The conditions the country is living through take their week. |
| 11 | `cityPhase` | After conditions, before capacity: local lean mean-reverts and tax holidays count down through `apply()`. |
| 12 | `obligationPhase` | A claim opened this week must be on the books before `statePhase` counts what is owed, and cannot expire in the week it opened. |
| 13 | `emergencyPhase` | Beside the obligations: one bills the silence, the other bills the emergency powers still being held. Bills each declaration's compounding weekly cost and lapses any that went `EMERGENCY_RENEWAL_TURNS` (52) unrenewed. |
| 14 | `statePhase` | Lean reverts toward `base.lean + grievanceLean(marks, congress)`; marks decay; pending claims expire into omission marks. At most one packet per moved state. |
| 15 | `capacityPhase` | Refit is what the week after an action is. |
| 16 | `commandPhase` | A theater's posture draws on the same force the refit is restoring. |
| 17 | `warPowersReportPhase` | A report owed within forty-eight hours of this week's introduction of forces is marked before the chamber's mood settles. |
| 18 | `blocPhase` | What a compact is worth is read off the board the wars and campaigns have just moved. |
| 19 | `compactPhase` | A call is read off the same two facts the bloc phase priced, so the fracture rule and the call rule never disagree about a week. |
| 20 | `tradePhase` | Retaliation lands before the budget counts the week. A landed answer costs the President standing; the exporters' lost sales reach the economy through `external-pressure.ts#retaliationCut` under the coercion cap, and nothing is charged to the Treasury. |
| 21 | `tariffFindingPhase` | After retaliation lands, so a partner's answer this week counts toward a trade finding due the same week. Rules on every due investigation (`tariff-orders.ts`). |
| 22 | `nominationPhase` | A nomination confirmed this week is in the chair when this week's policy is executed. |
| 23 | `judicialVacancyPhase` | After nominations: one saved annual assessment per occupied Supreme and circuit seat (a death on the life table with the judges' own adjustments, or a retirement), so a seat that opens this week is filled through a later nomination. |
| 24 | `officeSuccessionPhase` | Beside the bench's own vacancies, before the institutions and the foreign week: deaths in every other occupied modeled office and the fillings that come due (`succession.ts`, `officeholder-mortality.ts`, `vacancy-law.ts`). A Secretary's acting deputy is seated before the department's week, and `foreignPoliticsPhase` seats a dead leader's successor the same week. |
| 25 | `institutionPhase` | What an agency can deliver is settled before anything asks it to deliver. |
| 26 | `servicePhase` | Right after the branch's own week, because a service is processed at the delivery its owner and its approvers have just settled at. |
| 27 | `projectPhase` | Beside the services and for the same reason: a project's review and construction move at the delivery the branch has just settled at. |
| 28 | `militaryConstructionPhase` | With the projects, at the delivery the branch has just settled at. |
| 29 | `offshoreLeaseSalePhase` | With the projects, for the same reason. |
| 30 | `cleanPowerPurchasePhase` | With the projects, for the same reason. |
| 31 | `blockedAssetPhase` | With the projects, for the same reason. |
| 32 | `intelligencePhase` | After the institutions: the agency has had its week before the record reads its standing. |
| 33 | `cabinetLoyaltyPhase` | Before the consequences draw, so a week that cost a minister their regard is priced at the loyalty it left. |
| 34 | `cabinetConsequencesPhase` | |
| 35 | `policyPhase` | The standing program does another week's work. |
| 36 | `apportionmentPhase` | Before the accounts draw: the week's release of the enacted discretionary appropriation (`discretionary / TURNS_PER_YEAR`), and the only phase that credits the administration's authority. Nothing is released during a lapse. |
| 37 | `budgetPhase` | After the program, because both draw on the same treasury. Pending impoundment messages step first (`impoundment.ts`: the count on day 25 of continuous session, default release on day 45, the Comptroller General's report and suit, a pocket rescission's lapse), then the week's spend reads `selectSpendableBudget`. |
| 38 | `commitmentPhase` | A promise abroad is drawn on what the budget left, not on what the vote said. |
| 39 | `vitalsPhase` | Before `fiscalPhase`, so the books read this week's population. The one writer of `demography.population`. |
| 40 | `fiscalPhase` | Writes `economy.debt += -balance / TURNS_PER_YEAR`, compounds `economy.gdp` and `gdpPerCapita` by `growth / TURNS_PER_YEAR`, and derives `sovereignRating` from debt-to-output and the balance. |
| 41 | `inspectorGeneralPhase` | Before the chamber's mood settles, because being investigated is one of the things that mood reads. |
| 42 | `committeeInquiryPhase` | An audit the President has just moved against becomes a committee file in the same week. |
| 43 | `specialCounselPhase` | After the two files that could be its predicate, before the ripening that would advance them. |
| 44 | `oversightRipeningPhase` | Last of the three, so a file opened this week is not also advanced by it. |
| 45 | `congressPhase` | The chamber's mood is a reading of the week that has just happened. |
| 46 | `delegationPhase` | A delegation's target is a displacement on this week's disposition, and the votes are counted after it. |
| 47 | `governorPhase` | Shares the delegation slot until the two are composed into one packet. |
| 48 | `legislationPhase` | What Congress sends the President is read off the disposition this week produced. |
| 49 | `shutdownPhase` | A stopgap carried and signed this week has already moved the date this phase reads, so the government reopens in the week it was funded. |
| 50 | `judicialReviewPhase(charter)` | The courts, whenever a challenge filed weeks ago comes back at its drawn stage wait (`court-procedure.ts` `COURT_TIMING_DAYS`). In the late-June week it also records the October term's aggregate background docket and any consequential rulings (`background-docket.ts`). |
| 51 | `rulemakingPhase` | After the courts, so a rule stayed this week does not take effect in it; after the chamber, so a resolution of disapproval enacted this week undoes one. |
| 52 | `orderExpiryPhase` | After the courts and the rulemaking, so an order a ruling ended this week is not also expired by the calendar. |
| 53 | `docketPhase` | The regulatory bodies' own statutory clock, after the courts for the same reason. |
| 54 | `pressPhase` | Coverage hardens around the week's record, rulings included. |
| 55 | `scandalPhase` | A story is closed against the coverage it died in. |
| 56 | `electionPhase(charter)` | Opinion on election night is the one the coverage has just shaped, and a recomposed chamber is the one removal reads. Also where `recompose` re-apportions delegations. The day loop holds elections on their own date (`holdElectionsDueToday`); this phase holds them for whole-week drivers (`advanceTurn`, the balance harness, the fixture generator). |
| 57 | `governancePhase` | Every other government's week. |
| 58 | `foreignPoliticsPhase` | A seat changes hands after its government has set the week's program, so the new leader's offset reaches the next week's decisions. |
| 59 | `regimePhase` | After the week's changes of government, every running regime operation is closed against a government that fell, exposed by its weekly hazard, or lapsed (`regime-acts.ts`). |
| 60 | `grudgeDecayPhase` | One week of relationship memory: trust, tension and military threat settle toward each pair's standing reading (founding derivation plus durable causes), a favor faster than an injury; grudges fade toward their cause's floor. One background packet for the graph. |
| 61 | `pledgePhase` | Due pledges settle before the public's week is read, so a promise broken this week weighs on this week's opinion. |
| 62 | `opinionPhase` | The public revises its reading of the administration. |
| 63 | `demosPhase` | After the public's revision: cohorts cancel matching causes, decay at valence-split rates, and emit a dispersion polarize under the joint turn ceiling. |
| 64 | `removalPhase(charter)` | Removal reads last of the world's week, so its pressure is pressure after everything that happened. |
| 65 | `familyLapsePhase` | An arc lapses on the week's closing date, and nothing earlier in the week reads whether one did. Writes only through the `scenarioFamily` effect. |

**Office days.** `weekGroups` cuts the same sequence into the seven office days without reordering it: day 1 is `npcActionPhase`, day 2 is empty, day 3 runs `pursuitPhase` up to `commodityPhase`, day 4 runs `commodityPhase` up to `nominationPhase`, day 5 runs `nominationPhase` up to `inspectorGeneralPhase`, day 6 runs `inspectorGeneralPhase` up to `pressPhase`, and day 7 runs the rest. `simulateRuns` throws if the cut drops or reorders a phase. The cut is defined but not switched on: `campaignTurnPhases` is not called with `layout` set, so a week still settles in one pass (DECISIONS.md §2.4).

**The Court keeps a dated procedural record.** `judicialReviewPhase` in `judicial-review.ts` advances threshold review, interim relief, lower merits, appeal and Supreme merits through stored proceedings, then writes immutable opinions to `campaign.judiciary.cases`. `decideCase` in `court-votes.ts` derives individual votes from eligible justices' reasoning under shared case uncertainty; it does not allocate votes to match a predetermined holding. No quorum delays a merits disposition, and equal division leaves the lower judgment standing. Adverse lower executive-order judgments suspend future operation while the President chooses acceptance or further review; a stay requires its own later court decision. Final remedies use recorded contribution lineage and the applicable implementation reader, preserving spent funds, debt, contractual liabilities and unrelated later acts. Lower-court service remedies return to their specific applicants, employers or agency records. The Court file exposes parties, questions, evidence, dates, choices and outcomes without turning internal draws into public facts.

**Legal exposure runs through one stream** (`legal-exposure.ts`). Filing reads salience, injury, plaintiffs and venue (`order-exposure.ts`, `state-suits.ts`): the strongest state plaintiff, suing through its attorney general (a governor's appointee follows the governor; otherwise the state's lean), and each private class are independent chances to file, scaled by the act's loudness; overreach and the statutory ground are not inputs. Merits read the grounded reach (a statutory share an authorizing act discounts, a constitutional share it does not), the bench and precedent (`strike-odds.ts`, `background-docket.ts#doctrineShift`). Stage waits are drawn once per proceeding inside sourced bands (`court-procedure.ts` `COURT_TIMING_DAYS`: interim relief 2 to 21 days, lower merits 60 to 270, circuit merits 90 to 365, cert disposition 28 to 240). A filed ordinary challenge opens a `CourtMatter` of kind `order-challenge` naming its parties. A strike-down's cost follows the act's salience and whether the remedy undoes something in effect (`strikeDownCost`); `accumulatedOverreach` counts only orders a court struck down. `selectLegalExposures` links every representation of a dispute (proceeding, case, committee inquiry) to one origin; public misconduct findings enter as referrals with a dated charging decision (`CHARGE_CHANCE` 0.3, a design value), and a removal from a contested commission seat enters with its own dated filing decision and a merits chance priced once in the administration's favor (`REMOVAL_SUIT_CHANCE` 0.85, `REMOVAL_REINSTATEMENT_CHANCE` 0.2, both design values). A filed removal suit runs as a court proceeding (`removal-court.ts`, called from `judicialReviewPhase`) on the shared clocks: D.C. venue, interim reinstatement denied while the priced chance sits below even, district and circuit merits at the *Slaughter*-bound chance, a cert decision through `appealGrantChance`, and at the Supreme Court a vote of the seated justices at that chance spread by each one's lean (`background-docket.ts#narrowingPropensity`); a final win reinstates the member where the seat can be returned and otherwise awards back pay (`removalRemedy`). The Courts docket labels it, the case file reads it, and the officers board lists every contested removal with its status (`player-core` `judiciary/courts.ts`, `judiciary/case-file.ts`, `oversight/appointments-pipeline.ts#removalSuits`). A tariff carries its statute and citation and no exposure. The aggregate background docket (`background-docket.ts`, from `judicialReviewPhase`) draws each October term's filings, argued cases and signed opinions ±10% around the 2024 Term (3,856, 73 and 56), gives each of six modeled doctrines a 0.18 chance a term of a consequential ruling, decided by every seated justice and recorded once in the late-June week as a `CourtCase` with subject `background-docket`; it never becomes a playable case, and `congress.ts#struckRecently` (the Hill's reading of orders struck this season) leaves it out.

**The runner is sequential, so the order is a guarantee and not an intention.** `turn-loop.ts`'s `runPhase` walks the sequence one step at a time: each step's packets go through `apply()` before the next step is planned, so a phase is handed the country the phases above it have already moved, and the ledger reads in list order. The read-after-write orderings are pinned by tests in `turn-phases.test.ts`, which fail if the runner is put back on a shared turn-start snapshot.

**Which orderings are load-bearing.** A load-bearing ordering is a real read-after-write: the later phase reads state the earlier one wrote this turn, and swapping them changes results. Examples:

- `statePhase` before `delegationPhase`. The delegation target reads `grievanceReading(state.marks)` after this week's decay and claim expiry.
- `congressPhase` before `delegationPhase`. The delegation target is a displacement on `congress.disposition`.
- `delegationPhase` before `legislationPhase`, and before any vote counted in the turn. `chamberDisposition`'s delegation term is read by `countVote` and by the override path.
- `opinionPhase` before `removalPhase`. The arc reads an approval that already includes this week's revision.

`demosPhase` reads and writes `campaign.cohorts` alone and never `campaign.player.opinion`, so its place after `opinionPhase` is convention. A conventional ordering may change with a stated reason. A load-bearing one may not change without moving the read that justifies it.

**`recompose` outranks the drift.** `apportionDelegations` is also called inside `recompose` during `electionPhase` (21), after `delegationPhase` (17). On a midterm turn the recomposed table overwrites that turn's drift, which is correct: `ChamberComposition` is authoritative and the delegation table is its apportionment. The House half starts from the seats as held and moves only the seats the election moved, closest first (`house-seats.ts`'s `electHouse`), so an unchanged chamber reseats every state exactly as it was.

**Nothing outside the engine assembles a phase list.** The player's campaign store and the balance harness both call `campaignTurnPhases`; neither keeps a copy, because two copies diverge and the thing measured stops being the thing played. The store takes the phase list as `(charter) => TurnPhases` so a test can substitute one, and defaults to the engine's.

The arc's final beat sets `resolved`, which is terminal. `store.continueUntilAttention()` refuses to run the world on a resolved campaign, and the player shows the removal ending in place of the open-work list: the beats in the order they landed, with the turn each fell on, and the arc's `endingSummary`.

### Supporting engine modules

**`backstop.ts`** (`backstopRelief`): a stabilization package in the last 52 weeks takes 2 points off the target's crisis premium in `sovereign.ts` and half the weekly build off its fiscal stress in `national-books.ts`; a second package renews it rather than adding to it. **`congress-votes.ts`** (`voteOn`): one way to ask a chamber a question, delegating to `countVote` so every count is identical; force authorization, appropriations, treaties, removal votes, bill stages and confirmation counts all ask through it. **`founding-confirmations.ts`** (`foundingConfirmations`): called inside the seal before the opening approval is recomputed. **`article-incidents.ts`** (`incidentArticle`): `conditions.ts`'s feed writer stamps a condition item's `facts.topicTags` with `INCIDENT_TAG` whenever `condition.lifetime === "incident"`, which routes it to `incidentArticle` instead of the standing-condition prose. **`founding-dyads.ts`** (`applyFoundingDyads`): the head of `buildInitialRelationships`'s return, laying the sourced dyads over the derived graph, so anything appended after it reads a sourced pair rather than a derived one.

### Simulate: political geography — state memory shape

`state-geography.ts` carries `GrievanceMark` (`{ turn, signature, weight, valence: 1 | -1, decayRate, reason }`), `PendingClaim` (`{ signature, openedTurn, expiresTurn, weight, reason }`), `SenateSeat` (`{ party, seatClass: 1 | 2 | 3, disposition }`), `StateDelegation` (`{ house: ChamberComposition, senate: readonly [SenateSeat, SenateSeat], disposition }`) and `GovernorStanding` (`{ person, party, disposition }`) — in their own module, which keeps `state-campaign.ts` under the file-size line. `StateRuntime` lives here with them and carries `delegation?`, `marks: readonly GrievanceMark[]`, `claims: readonly PendingClaim[]`, `governor?`. `MAX_STATE_MARKS` (8) and `MAX_STATE_CLAIMS` (3) are the deterministic eviction bounds `applyState` enforces: drop the lowest `weight`, ties broken by lowest `turn`, ties broken by `signature` ascending.

**Lean drift is proportional.** `STATE_LEAN_DRIFT = 0.06` closes that fraction of the lean→anchor gap each turn (`states.ts`), not a capped absolute step of 0.003. At the old cap, lean could close at most ~0.048 over a mark's ~16-turn life while `grievanceLean` (net weight × `GRIEVANCE_LEAN_SCALE` 0.12, capped ±0.25) may open a 0.25 gap — the drifting anchor was arithmetically unreachable. Mark decay stays at `BASE_MARK_DECAY = 0.06` / `OMISSION_DECAY = 0.03`: reachability comes from the drift formula, not from slowing every mark. A held pattern pulls lean into the anchor inside ~20 turns; a single decaying mark is not permanent.

**The narrow reading is in the type, not left to the writer.** Claim expiry and obligation-derived neglect could both write the same fact (an unanswered domestic disaster) into the same grievance store, which would count it twice. `PendingClaim` closes that off structurally: its doc comment states it is opened only by a named act or a named scenario reply, never by scanning live state for an unanswered condition, so the obligation ledger stays the one enumerator of *derived* neglect and never needs a `PendingClaim` of its own to state it. Both a routed act and a claim's expiry write the identical `GrievanceMark` shape into one array, `StateRuntime.marks`, so a single future selector can sum `weight * valence` over it once and be a complete count — there is no second place a state's grievance could be hiding.

**State approval is a reading of the headline, not a second public (`regional-opinion.ts`).** `stateApprovals` places each state where the electoral map already puts it relative to the country (`stateShareShifts`: the `mapAlignment` term `administrationShare` reads, plus the ticket's home-state tilt), adds how much harder the economy's change since the authored opening lands there through the state's `vitalAnchor` unemployment and cost of living, and slides the whole map by one log-odds offset, found by bisection, so the population-weighted mean is the headline, which now carries the honeymoon because the honeymoon is part of the valence every bloc reads. It clamps only after solving. A state's own bloc reading (national shares tilted by the state's lean) is designed and not built. Marks reach it only through lean. Nothing reads it to decide an outcome, so the opinion constants, the doctrine gate and the balance harness are untouched by it. `closeWeek` keeps a weekly `stateApprovalHistory`, sampled into `WeekOpening` by `openWeek` (`turn-loop.ts`, shared with `day-step.ts#rememberOpening`) so its rows line up with `metricHistory`. `SCHEMA.md` §5i, "State approval", has the arithmetic.

**State-by-group support is a reading of two margins, not a third public (`state-cohort-opinion.ts`).** For each cohort axis, `stateCohortOpinion` fills the state × group table between each state's approval (`stateApprovals`) and each group's national figure (`cohortSupport`) by raking in log-odds, MRP-style: `logit p = a_g + b_s + beta_g · z_s + tilt`. `z_s` is the state's partisan context from `stateShareShifts`; `beta_g` is how much more or less than the average voter a group's vote follows its state, fitted from the 2020 and 2024 Cooperative Election Study (`state-cohort-sensitivity.ts`) and jittered once at founding (`Campaign.cohortSensitivity`, v60, its own stream key); `tilt` moves a group further in the states a remembered act reached, read from `CohortStrain.where`, which `routeCohorts` now writes from the routed state effects (`routedReach`: each state's share of the act's reach on the group over its share of the group's people). Both margins hold to 1e-7 after a few sweeps; the group targets take one shared log-odds step per axis so their total meets the states'. Derived on every read and memoized per campaign object; nothing decides an outcome on it, so the opinion constants, the doctrine gate and the balance harness are untouched. Readers: the state file's People view (`player-core` `statePeople` ratings) and the electorate States view's groups-by-state heatmap (`stateGroupGrid`). Noted for Congress phase J, not built: a member's exposure at home is this table weighted by the member's state (or district) mix. `SCHEMA.md` §5i, "State-by-group support", has the arithmetic and the source.

**`routeDomestic`** (`domestic-signature.ts`) turns a `DomesticSignature` into per-state lean deltas and grievance marks. Scenario cast, `signOrder`, and legislation `enactmentEffects` all splice those effects into their own packets. **`statePhase`** (`states.ts`) is the weekly pass: mark decay via `replaceMarks`, claim expiry into `omitted:` marks weighted by `federalDependence`, lean drift toward `base.lean + grievanceLean(marks)` — at most one packet per moved state. `openingStates` seeds `marks: []` and `claims: []`. **`apportionDelegations`** (`delegations.ts`) fills `delegation` at founding (`sealFounding` → `withApportionedDelegations`) and inside midterm `recompose` (which also emits `setDelegation` effects so the live table matches the recomposed chambers). **`delegationTarget` / `delegationPhase`** (`delegations.ts`) drift each bloc toward room + lean + `grievanceReading(marks)` at `DELEGATION_DRIFT` after `congressPhase`; `chamberDisposition` takes an optional states table as its third displacement term (`DELEGATION_LEVERAGE`), never written back into `congress.disposition`. **`withSeatedGovernors` / `selectGovernor` / `governorPhase`** (`governors.ts`) seat a `GovernorStanding` on every non-DC state at founding from `PersonRuntime.state` + `Person.party`, opening at the delegation's disposition, and drift weekly toward that disposition at `DELEGATION_DRIFT` (sibling call in the same slot). Saves older than v51 are not readable (see *Save format* below), so the state, cohort, rulemaking, lapse, appropriation, bill-stage and committee fields they introduced are all present in every readable save.

### Resolve: the projection contract

`instruments.ts` owns *what an action is*: five instruments (diplomatic, economic, covert, military, assistance) × five rungs, each a capability gate plus an effect builder, one shared code path for player and NPC actors. Military rungs 2–5 additionally accept a `ForcePackage` so the player scales live formations rather than a hardcoded package; the rung *kind* (deploy / blockade / strike / invasion) is unchanged. The table is split across four files that `instruments.ts` re-exports: `instruments-military.ts` holds the five military rungs (the blockade's coastline and War Powers gates among them), `instruments-economic.ts` holds the economic and assistance ladders, and `instrument-gates.ts` holds the capability gates the rungs share (`sharesBorder` and the rest), so the catalog file stays one catalog.

**A blockade is an act of war with a standing record** (`instruments-military.ts#blockadeEffects`, `blockades.ts`). Military rung 3 is in `MAJOR_FORCE_RUNGS` and reports as `hostilities`, so the first blockade is gated by `warPowersStanding`, writes the unilateral-force record where nothing authorizes it, starts the sixty-day clock, and opens a war (or heats the running one) through `openOrEscalate` with `opening: "blockade"`; the second-act rule in `forceOnTheBoard` still governs strikes. A landed blockade writes a `Blockade` record and nothing onto the target's `stats.trade`: the record is in force while its war, its deployment commitment and its War Powers introduction all stand, so a recall, a war's end or `terminateForce` lifts it that week. Two readers price it while it stands and stop when it lifts: `external-pressure.ts#coercionPressure` takes the target's founding exports-to-output share times the heaviest cut on it off growth, under the same `COERCION_CAP` as sanctions, and `commodity-shocks.ts` keeps the target's supplier share times the cut (less what its wars already take) off each market as a `blockade` shock. A government may not blockade a coast its own blockade already holds, and two navies off one coast count as the heavier blockade, so repeats cannot compound. A failed act of force at rung 3 or 4 still spends readiness and reports an introduction into hostilities (`force-failure.ts`); its `escalation` branch, the projection's "hits back harder", is offered only against a government that can answer (`answersWithForce`: readiness at the rung-3 gate and a navy or air arm of at least `ANSWER_ARM_FLOOR` 0.3; elsewhere the failure mass is all `repulsed`), and is the target firing on the force: a `strike` on the target's own edge and a war opened with `opening: "retaliation"`, which reaches the market through the strait it contests where the target holds a shore. The rung-3 contest weighs the sea layer at the ground layer's weight and the ground at the sea's, since a blockade is decided at sea. The NPC agent's `restraint` term weighs a blockade at a strike's height (the same legal scruple and assembly drag), because both now open a use of force a government answers for. A failed exercise or forward deployment (rungs 1 and 2) puts no force on the target to fire on, so `force-failure.ts#hitsBackHarder` keeps the `escalation` branch off them and their failure mass is all `repulsed`.

**Other governments' coercion is rationed per term** (`npc-tempo.ts`, `coercion-tempo.ts`). `Campaign.npcTempo` is one dated ledger of rationed acts. A family is data, `TempoFamily = { id, perTerm, cooldownWeeks, noun }`, declared by the module that owns the act; the ledger holds no list of families. `tempoRefusal(campaign, { family, key, label? })` refuses where the world has made `perTerm` acts of the family in the last 208 weeks (`TERM_WEEKS`) or the key was acted on inside `cooldownWeeks`; `firstTempoRefusal` checks a decision's claims in order; `tempoEffect(campaign, actor, claims)` is the `npcTempo` effect that appends the entries; `pairKey(a, b)` keys a pair either way round. `coercion-tempo.ts` declares two families: `NPC_ACTS_OF_FORCE` (military rungs 3 to 5, 8 a term, a pair rests 26 weeks; planning values for the owner to set) and `NPC_STRAIT_CLOSURES` (2 a term, a strait rests 104 weeks; the owner's rate), and `coercionClaims(campaign, request)` names what an act would claim: the pair for any act of force, and each priced strait (`straitsANewWarWouldClose`: one of the pair on its shores, the pair not already at war, no war on the board already contesting it). `npc-agent.ts#weigh` refuses a candidate whose claims the ledger has no room for before projecting it (`missingCapability` "room in the world's tempo for this act"); `npc-turn.ts#npcTempoRecord` spends an act of force for every NPC act of force made, landed or not, and a closure of each such strait only where the act's packet opened a war. The President is never rationed. Another family (regime operations, for instance) declares its own `TempoFamily`, builds its claims, calls `firstTempoRefusal` where its agent decides and `tempoEffect` where its act lands, and needs no edit to `npc-tempo.ts`.

**Capture is a use of force that serves an indictment (2026-10-18).** The covert regime slot (`covert` rung 5) still carries the request field `regimeAct`, but only `coup-support`, `political-action` and `kill` are covert: they need a standing finding on the target for the President's own government (`finding-gate.ts`), tradecraft and covert reach, and write a covert record. `indict` and `capture` are not covert (`regime-rung.ts#isCovertRegimeAct`). `indict` is the Justice Department's act: it needs no finding or tradecraft, is refused against a treaty ally or a leader already under an open indictment, and writes an `Indictment` through the `indictment` effect (`world-effects.ts#applyIndictment`, kept for the whole campaign). `capture` is reserved to the President's government and needs an open indictment on the target (`regime-acts.ts#openIndictment`), no declared deliverable arsenal, no treaty ally unless a war stands between the two, air or land branch strength, projection reach at the limited-strike cap and the War Powers gate at rung 4 (`regime-rung.ts#captureRefusal`, `regimeActGate`). Its resolve opens the War Powers record and the 48-hour report (`warPowersRecord`, `forceIntroduction`) in place of a covert record, serves the indictment, and seats the charter's successor through the existing succession. The player meets them as the options `indict` (Pressure, Justice Department) and `capture` (Force); `player-core/src/act/catalogue.ts#configUnder` fixes the act whatever the address names. The assessment sheet gains a counsel's-opinion row on the lethal operation (`regime-rung.ts#lethalGround`) and a targeting-review row on strikes.

The economic ladder and the trade register are one account of the same acts. Rungs 3, 4 and 5 write a `TradeMeasure` (`sanctions` or `export-control` by what the partner actually buys, `asset-restriction`, `embargo`) through `coerciveMeasureEffect` and schedule the partner's answer through the same path `imposeTariff` uses, so a full embargo draws the dated, cause-linked reply a routine sectoral tariff already drew. Rung 3 files a denial package by comparing what each form would take of the target's trade (`trade.ts#denialMeasureKind`): an export control (BIS) where the target buys licensed goods from the United States and the control takes at least as much, and otherwise a designation (OFAC), which reaches the target's sales to everyone the dollar touches (`external-pressure.ts#measureCut`). A designation names the target's leading export to the world, and a further designation the next export not already designated (`trade.ts#measuredSector`). Two measures by one imposer on the same flow are charged once. Written only for the administration's own acts: every trade edge is derived against the founding nation. `TradeMeasureKind` is five members and `scripts/verify-trade-measure-kinds.mjs` fails a sixth nothing constructs.

**The fifth instrument is help.** Four ladders are ways of making another government do something it would rather not; `assistance` is the one a state spends most of its year on, and until it existed the only verb the board offered toward a friend was a gentler threat. Its rungs are a relief shipment, a development grant, a stabilization package, an arms transfer and a security guarantee, and each reaches the recipient's own numbers rather than only the record between the two: `softPower.aidReceived`/`aidGiven`, `economy.growth`, `economy.reserves`, `politics.regimeStability`, `military.readiness`, and the severity of any `NationCondition` the recipient is living through — the only route by which one country's decision shortens another's disaster. It gates on the giver's real capabilities the way every other ladder does (reserves, GDP, readiness, land strength, projection reach), and the top three rungs additionally read the *recipient*: a stabilization package needs a recipient actually in difficulty, an arms transfer and a guarantee need trust on the edge, because a promise nobody believes is not an instrument. `INSTRUMENT_COERCION` places it at 0.05 — below every coercive rung, so it opens no pursuit and draws no treaty scruple — and `RISK_IDS.assistance` names the three ways aid fails: `diverted`, `captured-by-the-wrong-hands`, `guarantee-called`. Its ripples run in the opposite direction from a coercive act's: a third party close to the recipient warms toward whoever helped it. A rung that gives away readiness charges the giver's own stats, and a metric effect is emitted only when the giver is the player. Emergency relief (rung 1) needs a need: the recipient must be living through an active condition that the giver's own relief is not already answering (`unmetNeed`); development grants need none. Each shipment delivers `min(0.06 × jitter, the condition's severity)`, and its money, goodwill and weekly operation cost scale with what it delivers. Aid goodwill is received at `aidReception = 1 / (1 + AID_SATURATION × remembered)`, where `remembered` sums the giver's earlier acts for the same purpose (civil: relief, grant, stabilization; security: arms transfer) on the record, faded at `FAVOR_HALF_LIFE_DAYS`, so switching rung names does not reset it (`AID_SATURATION` 1, a planning value).

**Not every instrument is a budget account, and not every account is an instrument.** `STATECRAFT_ACCOUNTS` is the four coercive instruments, declared `satisfies readonly InstrumentId[]` so renaming an instrument breaks the build here rather than orphaning an account quietly. Assistance is not among them: it is funded per act out of the treasury, and every capability its rungs gate on is already built by an existing account. `BUDGET_ACCOUNTS` is those four plus `domestic`, which is a budget account with no ladder — see §*The domestic line* below.

`resolution.ts` owns *what committing to it means*, and is the only path from a considered action to a changed world:

- `projectAction(campaign, request, streams)` → `ActionProjection`: `available` (always `true` on a projection that came back, so a bare instrument projection satisfies the same shape a scenario reply's `ReplyProjection` does and one shared reader can render both), the rung's `cost` (signed metric movement — **every** metric the act moves, which a surface must render in full: a military rung spends money, standing, calm at home and readiness, and printing the first mark alone misreports the other three), `successChance` and the `contest` it was read off (below), an exhaustive partition of named `risks`, the `ripples` it will send, whether those ripples are visible at all, `consultation` (who in the government has to have heard about it first, as role ids resolved through the charter's own titles at the point of rendering) `capacity` (what the act takes out of the establishment and how many weeks the establishment is short of it — present only on the rungs that spend one) and `coalition` (what the President's backers did to the odds and the standing cost, below — present only when it applied). `consultationFor` and `capacityCost` live in `instruments.ts` beside the rung table they describe. Pure and seed-independent.
- `resolveAction(campaign, request, streams, source, extras?)` → the drawn outcome plus an `EffectPacket` for `apply()`.

The load-bearing invariant is honesty: `resolveAction` calls `projectAction` and nothing else to obtain odds, cost and ripples, so the figures shown before commitment cannot drift from the ones that resolve the attempt. There is no second estimator in the engine. `successChance + Σ risks.chance === 1`, so an attempt either succeeds or fails in a way the player was shown; a test asserts the observed success rate matches the projected odds over 2,000 seeded runs.

**Every price is a share of the economy that pays it, and every capacity is billed once.** `INSTRUMENT_UNIT_COST` quotes money as `treasuryOfGdp`, a fraction of the *acting* nation's annual output, for the reason the policy catalogue does: a flat figure is a cost with no unit attached to anybody, so a sum that buys a mid-power's whole military option is a rounding error against a superpower's accounts and the ladder stops constraining exactly where the stakes are highest. **The military rungs are the exception: their money is what the units sent cost** (`force-costs.ts`, DECISIONS §6.8). `US_FORCE_PRICES` reads `FORCE_COST_ANCHORS` (world `dataset/force-cost-anchors.ts`, sourced and dated in `packages/world/sources/force-costs-sources.md`) in 2026 dollars by CPI-U: an exercise at $18.5M; a carrier strike group (carrier and air wing at CBO's direct cost, five destroyers) at $4.08M a day, a week's passage up front and $28.5M a week while it stays; a blockading destroyer at $0.22M a day on the same terms; a strike aircraft at two strike sorties of $2.19M, the Libya campaign's first ten days over its 370 US strike sorties, all-in, with nothing weekly; a brigade at $32M to ship and $213M a week in theater (CRS's FY2008 cost per Iraq brigade equivalent). `forceActBasis` multiplies by the units sent (the package's count, or the rung's preferred size when the request names none, which is also the count its commitment holds), by `campaignCostIndex` (the actor's authored military budget per active service member over the United States', a derived index; 1 for the United States and for a government with no standing force) and, up front only, by the distance factor. `ActionProjection.costBasis` carries the result, `cost.treasury` is its `upFront`, and the rung's standing commitment opens at its `weekly` (`standingCommitment`'s `amountPerTurn`), so the passage, the strike and the shipping are charged once and the days on station weekly. The limited strike is sized at 100 airframes (cap 150), the scale of the 1986 Libya raid and the 2025 strike on Iran's nuclear sites. Assistance is billed at the amount it delivers (`aid-amounts.ts`), and the diplomatic, economic and covert shares are planning values calibrated to a United States rung-3 act of about $20M, $60M and $150M. Force sent into one of the President's own states reads the same table: `domesticGuardWeek()` (a 3,000-member Guard call-up at CBO's per-1,000 monthly rate, about $13.5M a week) and `domesticBrigadeWeek()` (the 2018 border deployment's average week, about $14.5M, carried to 2026 dollars), each from `FORCE_COST_ANCHORS`; the federalize and troops acts' `commitment.amountPerTurn` call them instead of carrying a literal. The arms transfer's follow-on commitment is a tenth of the transfer over half a year, and the guarantee's basing is 48 fighters at about $9M a year each. A rung that raises money does not book a flat share of output in the same packet. The tariff rung places a United States measure on the trade register when the actor is the founding nation; customs collect that duty on the trade week. Another capital keeps the relationship movement only. Capacity is separate and single-channelled: the military rungs bill the force through `MILITARY_READINESS_COST` and covert rungs 4–5 bill the clandestine service through `COVERT_CAPABILITY_COST`, each quoted to the player by `capacityCost`. Neither instrument carries a second, smaller `militaryReadiness` line in `INSTRUMENT_UNIT_COST` — the covert one did, which charged clandestine work to the force it never used and could close the military ladder's gates through a channel nothing showed the player.

**The contest: odds decided one layer at a time.** `contest.ts` is where `successChance` comes from, and `contest-dials.ts` is the only place the numbers behind it live. An instrument names the few layers it is actually decided on — military is `ground / air / sea / cyber / space / reach / sustainment`, economic is `dependence / reserves / market / resolve`, diplomatic is `reach / standing / coalition / temperature`, covert is `collection / access / penetration`, assistance is `purse / delivery / absorption` — and each layer carries both sides' score *and the evidence for it* (a formation count, a share, a graded word), so a projection can be taken apart rather than only read. `edge` is the weighted sum of the layers' differences, less habituation (`repeatDrag`, taken off the edge because repetition is a fact about the pair, not about either force); `baseChance = 0.5 + contrast × edge`, clamped to the floor and ceiling. `projectAction` then applies the rung's difficulty, the reach penalty for anything that has to cross an ocean, the seated minister's sway, the defender's arsenal and the President's coalition — in that order, and nowhere else.

Every weight, floor, ceiling, rung-reliability figure, deterrence drag and default branch share is one exported `CONTEST_DIALS` object: balance is tuned by moving a number in one file, and a test pins that each instrument's weights sum to 1 and name exactly the layers the instrument declares. `assessContest` accepts a `dials` override, which is the seam a future campaign-level difficulty setting passes through.

`ActionProjection` therefore carries `contest` alongside `successChance`. It is read-only — the packet a replay depends on is unchanged — and it is what lets the country dossier show a matchup before any rung is chosen (`contestBetween`), quoting the same contest the resolver will use when the order is given.

**What a force is made of.** `forces.ts` turns the authored military aggregates into five domain readings — what the domain decides, the doctrine's weight in it, its personnel, how much of it could move this month, and the named formations and holdings where the dataset carries an `orderOfBattle`. Three nations carry an authored decomposition and forty-seven do not; the rest are split on `CONTEST_DIALS.defaultBranchShare` tilted by their own branch strengths, and every reading states whether it was counted (`authored`) or inferred, so a surface never presents a guess as a count. `nuclearReading` prices an arsenal as a saturating threat (the whole difference is between zero and a few hundred warheads) with the delivery triad as a separate fact. `liveForceDomains` and `liveNuclearReading` are the campaign-level readers.

**Stewardship.** `stewardship.ts` puts the player's own cabinet inside that one estimator: when the *player's* nation acts, the seated minister who owns the instrument's domain (foreign → diplomatic, finance/treasury → economic, interior/intelligence → covert, defense → military; role ids are charter strings, so domains match by hint) sways `successChance` by their `competence` — ±10% relative at the extremes, with a vacant or dead chair priced below a mediocre minister. Because the sway is applied inside `projectAction`, the odds the player reads already carry their government; NPC actors pass through unswayed, since their cabinets are not modeled.

**Coalition backing.** `coalition.ts` is the one reading of who would stand with a government on a move: partners that share a compact with the actor (`strongestShared`, defense before political before economic), trust it at `BACKING_TRUST` (0.5) or above, and are not tied to the target by the ripple rules' own test (those are counted as `torn` and priced only as reactions against, so nothing is counted on both sides). `coalitionFor` returns the list the act screen shows (player-core's `coalitionReading` is a shortlist of it). `coalitionBacking` prices only confirmed, requested participation still eligible in that list for the **player's own** acts; omitted/unilateral choices and unconfirmed drafts grant no bonus. `action-support.ts` persists pending requests and derives answers from current partner interests. Confirmed participation uses the existing weighting: weighted backers (`BACKER_WEIGHT` defense 1, political 0.6, economic 0.4) over `BACKING_SATURATION` (4) give a strength from 0 to 1, which multiplies `successChance` by `1 + BACKING_ODDS_LIFT[instrument] × strength` (economic 0.12, military 0.06, covert/diplomatic/assistance 0) and takes `BACKING_STANDING_RELIEF × strength` (0.35) off the cost's `standing` line. `ActionProjection.coalition` (`CoalitionBacking`, present only when it applied) states what was done so the screen can say it; the figures are already in `successChance` and `cost`.

`counsel.ts` derives each minister's *opinion* of a reply from who they are — hawks back the hard answer, doves oppose it at any odds, the cautious turn on thin chances — and emits it as structure only (`CabinetCounsel`: stance, named reason, conviction = the same competence that moves the odds). How much force a minister wants is the disposition reader's `forceAppetite` axis (`person-disposition.ts`, below), the same figure counsel computed inline before; `instinctFor(person, register, coercion)` exports the instinct the room starts from (−1..1, before the driver's 0.2 weight), so a rule asking whether someone believes in an act they are handed reads them as the room does. A minister reads a course's odds through their competence: the odds they argue from stray from the true chance by up to `0.3 × (1 − competence)` either way, drawn off a hash of the seed, the minister, the scenario and the reply, so a seasoned chair's advice tracks the real odds more closely than a weak one's and the same room gives the same advice however often it is read. The chair asked is the department that owns the decision's class: a charter with an ownership table (`department-ownership.ts`, below) routes to its owner, and only a charter nobody has written a table for falls back to matching by hint. The sentence a player reads is composed in player-core (`packages/player-core/src/conversation/counsel-lines.ts`'s `counselSentence`, one `CounselSentence` of speaker and line, plus the dissent) and rendered by the player, never by the engine; a raw stance or reason token is never printed as a word.

**Reading the record back.** `ledger-reading.ts` recovers what this administration committed to, and it is the base both the world's file on the player (`revealed-preference.ts`) and the cabinet's track record (`counsel-record.ts`) are derived from — on demand, never stored, so a government can change what is read off it by governing differently. Each entry states its own facts: an `ActionRecord` carries `commitment` (`{ instrument, rung, target, landed? }`) and, where a conversation held it, `counsel` (`{ role, person, stance, successChance? }`), both written onto the `EffectPacket` by the path that knows them (`resolveAction`, `attemptInstrument`, `commitReply`) and copied onto the entry by `apply()` — the same one write path `actor` takes. This replaced a parse of the narrating sentence, which had a defect that got worse the better the game was written: an authored consequence card replaces the narration wholesale, so a dressed entry carried no instrument, no rung and no outcome, and was dropped rather than guessed at. The parse survives as the fallback for pre-migration saves only (`SCHEMA.md` §7b), where it still refuses to read a dressed entry rather than invent one. `landed` is absent on the ladder's own path (`attemptInstrument`), which applies a rung outright rather than rolling for it; a counsel record deliberately carries no `landed` of its own, since the same entry's `commitment` holds it and a second copy could only disagree.

**The world's file on the player.** `revealed-preference.ts` derives a `RevealedPreferenceProfile` — `concedesUnderPressure`, `escalatesWhenTested`, `honorsFriends` — from those commitments plus the record's own dates for who was pressing at the time (`pursuits`, `conflicts`). Each scalar travels with the evidence count behind it, because 0.5 means "the record does not say" and not "balanced": a thin record has to be able to say so rather than assert a measure it has not earned. It has two consumers and both are live. `probePricingMultiplier(profile, counterpartTraits)` weights an already-scored move in `npc-agent.ts`'s `decideNpcAction` when the target is the player — an opportunist prices a probe cheaper against a government that pays, a vengeful state moves earlier on one that abandons its friends, and any of them charges the difference back against one that has shown it climbs. `describeRevealedPreference(profile)` renders the same profile as the lines a foreign service would file. The nation dossier is where the player reads them, beside that government's stance toward the player (DESIGN.md, `/world/nation/:id`; not built yet). Surfacing it is not decoration: a weighting the player cannot see is hidden difficulty, and breaking your own pattern is only a strategy if the pattern is readable. A scalar with no evidence behind it produces no line at all, so the reading's three states stay distinct — the file cannot be read, the file is empty, the file says something.

**Dissent.** A cabinet that always speaks with one voice is a wasted system, so `dissentingCounsel` scores every *other* living, seated minister (never the head of state — `leadRole` is excluded here too) against the same reply with the same scoring the primary uses, and surfaces the one furthest from the primary's stance, tie-broken by conviction. It stays silent — no manufactured second opinion — when the rest of the room genuinely agrees, or when the primary chair itself is vacant (there is no stance left to disagree with). `counsel-lines.ts`'s `counselSentence` composes it as a contrasting "But …" line (its `dissent`) to sit directly under the primary's.

**The room is seated by subject, not only by domain.** `CLASS_ROLE_HINTS` / `classRoles()` / `CLASSES_WITH_SEATED_STANDING` (`stewardship.ts`) name, for each of the eighteen scenario classes, which chairs have standing to be in the room — the counterpart of the domain-matching hint table Stewardship reads above, keyed on subject rather than instrument. `roomCounselOnReply` seats primary → strongest dissent → the class's remaining seats → conviction fillers, so a chair with real standing on the subject is never crowded out by the two structural seats alone, and `counselRole` routes a position reply to the class's first seated chair. `ActionCounsel.room: readonly ActionCounselVoice[]` (`{ role, person, stance }`) is that whole seated room stamped onto the record — every voice the player was actually shown, not only the primary and the dissent — so the Record can read back how the room as a whole split rather than a single contrasting line. See `SCHEMA.md`'s `ActionRecord` entry.

Cost is charged whether or not the action lands, climbs steeply with the rung, and is multiplied by distance for military and covert reach. Ripples reach every government with a stake in the target (a reading of it, trade with it, or a body in common), each reacting by the signed stance its own ties give (`resolution-ripples.ts#observerStance`): objection cools it toward the *actor*, charged at its magnitude times the share left of the room above a floor `OBJECTION_FLOOR` (0.25) below the observer's standing reading of the actor, so repeated coercion settles one observer's reading toward that floor and each new act still costs something (`objectionDelta`; owner ruling 2026-10-25); approval, only from a government already running the same policy, warms it at most to `APPROVAL_CEILING` (0.08) above its standing reading of the actor (`Ripple.approves`). Rivalry gives neutrality, never approval. The relation label (`bloc-partner`, `ally`, `bloc-peer`, `trade-partner`, or `observer` for a stakeholder that is none of these) is read off the ties for display only; observers are iterated in sorted id order. The actor's own institutions are reached too once a move is coercive enough — except for covert actions, which ripple only once a failure exposes them. Failure is never a no-op: the target records a grudge whose decay rate is set by its own vengefulness.

The resolver merges all signed metric movement (cost + outcome + ripple + any authored aftermath) into a single metric effect per packet, so a ledger entry reads as one net figure per metric rather than a stack of partials. Correctness no longer depends on that merge: `apply()` sums metric effects.

**A packet is charged to the actor, and only the player has a panel.** A metric effect is not addressed to a nation — `apply()` writes it to the administration — so every packet the resolver built used to bill the player whoever acted: an NPC recalling its own ambassador spent the player's treasury on the flight and took the standing hit out of the player's reading, three times a turn, invisibly. `assembleEffects` now drops metric effects when the actor is not the player's nation. The world's moves still move the world (relationships, nation stats, wars, grudges); only the player's own moves reach the player's accounts.

**Authored aftermath.** `consequences.ts` is the layer that lets a resolved outcome be *told* rather than reported. The twelve risk definitions above remain the floor, not the experience: a caller may hand `resolveAction` a pool of `ConsequenceCard`s in `extras`, and once the branch has been drawn the resolver picks one card written for that instrument, rung band and outcome — replacing the generic sentence with the card's one to three beats, and deepening (never redirecting) the effects of the branch that already fired. `ResolutionResult` gains `consequence: { id, valence, beats }` only when a card was drawn; a caller that passes no pool gets exactly the result the resolver has always returned, which a golden test pins.

Five properties make the layer safe to feed with generated content. **It cannot move the odds**, because it is only consulted after the branch is known — the projected risks stay exhaustive and the resolved branch is always one of them. **It cannot disturb replay**: card selection draws on a separate `"consequence"` stream, and streams are pure functions of their key. **It cannot expose what the odds did not price** — `visible` is the resolver's finding, the card type has no field for it, and a grudge, a remembered line or a public headline arising from a failure nobody caught is downgraded or dropped rather than leaked. **Its effect vocabulary is closed** to metric movement (optionally scaled by the act's severity), relationship deltas, a grudge, and a feed headline; nation stats, cabinet seats, people, operators and the removal arc are deliberately out of reach. **It costs nothing in save shape**: `once` and this-campaign memory ride the existing flag record as `consequence:<id>`, which is also how a card's `sets` flags open a later scenario.

`valence` splits a success in two without rolling for it: a draw in the top quarter of its own success interval reads as `partial`. Mechanically it is still a success — the split exists so an authored card can charge for a win that came expensive, and so old campaigns replay identically. `RISK_IDS` exports the branch ids per instrument, so a content audit can refuse a card claiming affinity with a failure that does not exist. The coverage matrix an authored pool must fill is therefore five instruments × three bands × three valences — forty-five cells, not thirty-six — and `packages/content/src/packs/consequences/assistance.ts` carries the bundled floor for the new nine.

**A position states an exposure where an instrument states a failure branch.** Everything above this line is an `ActionProjection`'s partition of named `risks`, each with a `chance` — an instrument is rolled for. A spoken `position` reply is never rolled for, so giving it a `risks`-shaped branch would print a probability nothing in the simulation draws against. `position-exposure.ts`'s `PositionExposure` is the honest counterpart: seven branches, ordered heaviest first (`front-*`, `briefing-unconfirmed`, `debt-borrowed`, `force-short`, `counterpart-hardens`/`-trust`/`-remembers`, `cohorts-strained`, `states-moved`, `alignment-staked`), each naming an effect the commit really applies rather than an outcome it might draw. No branch carries a chance, and no position may carry a `successChance` — the two vocabularies stay structurally distinct so a surface cannot quietly turn a certainty into an estimate. `conversations.ts` reads it into `ReplyProjection.exposure` (below). Every decision packet on both the instrument and the conversation paths now stamps `confidence` and `visibility` on its provenance, and a covert act stamps its recorded exposure as public or otherwise intelligence, so the Record can draw an assessed inference and an observed fact in different type.

**Staged resolution.** The aftermath layer tells the player what happened once it is over. The staged layer is the act itself, played as ordered steps between the order and `apply()`: `packages/content/src/resolution-beat.ts` defines `ResolutionSequenceDef`, keyed on the same instrument × rung band × valence × risk branch a consequence card is drawn on, with two to five `ResolutionBeat` steps each carrying its own settled outcome and its own named particulars. There are no runtime model calls anywhere near it — the whole family is pre-authored template data selected by a pure key, the article and consequence pattern extended to an ordered list.

Three properties keep it from being a second, unaccountable consequence path. **It carries no effects at all**: the schema has no effect field and is `.strict()`, so the outcome the player watches is the one the `EffectPacket` already holds, and there is no way for a sequence to double-bill an act the cost model has priced. **It cannot invent jeopardy**: the outcome is computed before the first step plays, no step outcome expresses "undecided", and the last step's outcome is fixed by the valence the key carries — a failure sequence that ends on a gain does not parse. **Its key space is enumerable**: `resolution-beat-reachability.ts` builds `SITUATION_KEYS` from the engine's `INSTRUMENT_IDS`, `rungBand()` and `RISK_IDS`, so `unreachableSequences()` answers exactly which authored documents no campaign can ever reach — the dead-content failure the scenario corpus paid for twice, given a decidable answer for once because a sequence is keyed rather than conditioned. `resolutionSequenceKey()` is the canonical `instrument:band:valence:branch` string, and the engine's `resolutionSituationKey()` writes the same string on the other side of the boundary as the seed the selection hash runs on, so the same read replays the same steps.

A fourth property was added after the first authored bank: **a sequence is filled, not just parsed.** `resolution-render.ts` is the seam between the bank and any surface — it draws one fictional `UnitDesignation` per sequence from `packages/world`'s `generateUnitDesignation`, narrowed by the step-level `unitAffinity` (branch, and a `unit`/`formation` echelon), and substitutes the four slots with the possessive and sentence-opening cases handled at fill time rather than written around in prose. The bank shipped before this existed, which is why nothing caught that "is wheels up" drew an undersea squadron five draws in six: the generator had no caller in the workspace, so no authored line had ever been read with a real designation in it.

A fifth property closes the loop from the engine's side: **the resolver chooses the sequence, and choosing costs nothing.** `packages/engine/src/resolution-sequence.ts` holds the engine's view of a banked sequence — `ResolutionSequenceEntry`, which is the key fields and nothing else, because the steps, their lines and their `unitAffinity` constraints stay on the content side and a copy of the fill rules here would be a second thing to keep in lockstep. A caller hands its bank to `resolveAction` as `extras.sequences`; once the branch is known, `selectResolutionSequence` draws a weighted pick keyed on `campaignSeed:resolution-sequence:actor:target:instrument:rung:turn` crossed with the situation key. The draw is a hash of that string rather than an `RngStream`, for the reason the article banks are: a resolution is re-read on the Record and re-rendered every time a screen mounts, and a stateful draw would give the same act different steps on the second reading. `ResolutionResult` gains `staged: { sequenceId, situationKey, seedParts }` — presentation only, no effects, and `seedParts` is handed back so `renderResolutionSequence` names the same formation the act named. Only military and covert are banked, so **most situations draw nothing and that is the ordinary answer**: an unbanked instrument yields no `staged` key at all and the caller plays the unstaged path, which a golden test pins by resolving a diplomatic act with and without a bank and comparing the whole result. `stagedResolutionFeedItem()` is the wire desk's hook: it builds a `FeedItem` naming the formation the rendered sequence named and stops there — the item lands through a packet like every other feed write, never by a second path out of the resolver.

**Not drawn yet.** `renderResolutionSequence` has no player caller, so an authored resolution sequence is validated and baked but not drawn. `commitReply` resolves the instrument through `resolveAction` and drops the `staged` key on the way back out, so the conversation path has to forward it before a decision can play the sequence it ordered. Assistance, diplomatic and economic sequences are unauthored.

### Resolve: one bundle over every projection

`packages/engine/src/projection.ts` is the normalized read across the eight per-instrument projections (`projectOrder`, `projectPolicyStance`, `projectRatification`, `projectPeace`, `projectForceAuthorization`, `projectConfirmation`, `projectAppropriation`, `projectLobbying`). A `ProjectionBundle` carries per-domain impact entries (direction, a magnitude *band* rather than a decimal, an engine-real horizon — `next-turn`, the instrument's own authored clock, or `term` — and a confidence class), the source projection's cost passed through unrescaled, odds only where a roll actually exists (a counted vote has none), a reversibility class, affected entity ids, the projection's own reasoning as assumption lines, and an explicit `notModeled` list for what that instrument's model does not simulate. The adapters (`bundleOrder`, `bundlePolicyStance`, …) invent no figures: every number in a bundle is a number the source projection already carried, and a property test pins that every impact domain traces to a source delta. `materialDecisionGate(bundle, treasury)` then answers, deterministically and from named thresholds, whether the decision is material — a material treasury share, a live war or removal exposure, irreversibility, cross-domain spread, or a constitutional clock — and names each tripped threshold in words a screen can print. The map-first player's compare-and-authorize pattern (DESIGN.md §1) is the planned reader of bundles and of the gate; it is not built yet. `verify-engine-reachability` holds each `bundle*` adapter to a player call site by its due wave.

### Simulate: policy teeth

`policyPhase` used to emit one `metric` effect and nothing else, which meant a standing program moved the player's five readings and left the country untouched — every stance in a domain the same kind of thing at a different magnitude, and nothing for the rest of the simulation to notice. Each of the 24 stances now also carries `nationEffects`: signed per-turn movement over a closed union of sixteen `NationStats` paths, applied to the nation running it through the ordinary absolute `nationStat` effect and clamped to each path's schema range. A surveillance state genuinely collects more (`security.intelCapability`, read by `hasIntelOnRelationship` and `feedVisibility`) and genuinely censors more; rearmament genuinely raises `military.readiness`, which `resolution.ts` prices as military leverage the next time the country reaches for the ladder.

**A program does not pay on the tick after signature.** A stance adopted on Monday moved the country on Tuesday, which is what made six standing programs read as settings rather than as governing. `policy-teeth.ts` withholds a newly adopted program's `perTurn` **and** its `nationEffects` for `PROGRAM_STANDUP_TURNS` (4) and says so on the surface, naming what the program rests on (`domainAuthority` — what the office may actually do about that domain) and who is organizing against it (`stanceOpposition` — the cohort whose own beliefs sit furthest from the direction the stance pushes, read off `demos.ts`'s `COHORT_MOTIVATION`, so a stance cannot be authored without an opponent). The clock is read off the ledger's own causal `marks` rather than a new field, so **no save migration is owed**. Inherited programs are exempt: they were already running the morning of the oath.

`policyNationEffects(runtime, program)` is deliberately written against a `NationRuntime` rather than the player: it is the same function an NPC's program will run through, and a nation the player does not govern has no `MetricSet` for a stance to move at all. It sums every domain's movement per path *before* building any effect, because `nationStat` writes absolutes and two domains can push the same stat in one week.

The contract is enforced the way the authored-field registry is (`policy-teeth.test.ts`): a non-inherited stance with no `nationEffects` fails, a stance whose every path is a registry *debt* rather than a live consumer fails, the four stances in a domain must differ in shape, and the written-but-not-yet-read pair (`politics.pressFreedom`, `energy.importDependence`) is pinned exactly — so writing a stat can never be quietly laundered as something reading it. See `SCHEMA.md` §7b.

### Simulate: the world governs itself

`governance.ts` closes the half of the policy system that only the player was on. Every other nation held exactly one stance per domain — the inherited one — for the whole campaign, so fifty countries were acted upon and never acted, and their stats moved only when somebody did something to them. Each now holds a program out of the same 24-stance catalogue, carried week by week through the same `policyNationEffects` C2b built for exactly this, clamped by the same ranges, scaled by the same competence band. A government's economic position is 0.6 of its governing party's platform and 0.4 of its cohesion-weighted slate (`governance.ts#economicPosition`). The governing party is the seat's (`foreign-leader.ts#governingPartyOf`), or the leader table's before any seat is written, so the opening programs and the reviews read the same party. The catalogue scale a program's need is measured against is computed on first use rather than at load, because `governance.ts` and the policy table sit on one import cycle.

What is added is only the part the player supplies for themselves: a decision. `scoreDomain` prices every stance in a domain on four terms — what the country needs (`needFit`), what kind of state it is (`characterFit`), what it can afford (`purseFit`) and how far ahead it looks (`horizonFit`) — plus an ambition term from its own agendas and an inertia bonus for the status quo, since a government is not indifferent between acting and not. It is pure in the runtime: no rng, so a replay cannot drift.

Three details carry the model:
