Jobs are the first Friday, consumer prices the 12th, the output estimate the 28th of January, April, July, or October, and the rate decision the same Thursday as the rule. A foreign selection is due in the month the calendar names, or on the exact day when the row has one. An attorney general of the other major party can raise one suit as a matter for the President. None of these writes a new approval term.

**The pump price and the grocery bill are derived.** `pumpPrice` holds the 2025 gallon at $3.10 and scales only crude's 51.4 percent share. `groceryIndex` moves the food-at-home farm share (18.5 cents) by a planning crop fraction of 37 percent. US munitions are five class indexes in `munitions.ts`. An opening number is a planning ratio. A class whose stock the table does not state opens with no index.

**Deterrence refuses force against a declared, deliverable arsenal, and is information below that.** `instrument-gates.ts#outsideNuclearDeterrence` refuses military rungs 3 to 5 against a government whose arsenal is declared (`declared` not false) with `deliveryTriad`, for every actor (the owner's answer of 2026-10-13: refused by data, blockades included); on the founding board that is the United States, Russia, China, the United Kingdom, France, India and Pakistan, so two of them never take an act of force against each other. Below that line `assessDeterrence` reads the defender's arsenal (and whether it is mutual), and `projectAction` both drags `successChance` down for military rungs 3–5 against a nuclear defender *and* states the assessment on the projection as `deterrence`. Because it is applied inside the one estimator, the number the player reads before committing already carries the arsenal pointed back at them, and they may still take the bet — a locked door would teach them nothing. A test pins that the projection the resolver used is identical to the one the player read. Asked for the rung that opens a war, it also names the governments likely to come in beside the victim and their share of the strength the attacker would face (`alliedSeverity`), and the resolver's drag multiplies in `1 − CONTEST_DIALS.alliedDeterrenceDrag` (0.3, a planning value) × that share, whether or not the victim is armed.

### Simulate: standing campaigns between capitals

`pursuits.ts` closes the other half of the dynamism audit's finding about NPCs. The agent chose the best single move available each turn, which produced a world where a nation sanctioned a neighbor on Monday, forgot about it by Friday, and picked whoever happened to score highest next week. Nothing accumulated, so nothing between two capitals ever became a story: there was no such thing as a state being *worked on*.

A `Pursuit` is that missing fact — one actor, one target, one aim, carried across turns. It is never declared: `stepPursuit` (`npc-turn.ts`) opens one out of a move that was **already made**, and only when the instrument clears `INSTRUMENT_COERCION >= 0.5`, so a démarche stays an exchange and does not make every state permanently be running a campaign against everyone it has an embassy in. A nation with no classifiable agenda opens none, because inventing an aim would put a motive on the board the dataset never authored.

**The agent reads it as preference, not as a script.** `focusFor` adds a seventh term to `NpcScoreTerms`, weighted 1 alongside the six that were already there, and it is exactly zero where no campaign is running — a world with no pursuits in it behaves precisely as it did before they existed, which is what keeps every balance figure comparable. Where one is running, the pull scales with `commitment` and is aimed at `wantedRung(pursuit)` rather than at the target as such: a state running an embargo does not suddenly find a démarche compelling because it is pointed at the right country.

**Escalation is emergent.** `wantedRung` holds where the campaign is while `progress > 0`, and steps up one rung once `commitment` has passed the point where the actor has something to lose and it still is not getting it. Nothing decides in advance that a pair goes to war; a campaign that starts working never climbs, and one that keeps failing walks the ladder up on its own. A success moves `progress` further than a failure moves it back — pressure that fails still tells the target it is being pressed — and both are weighted by the rung the move was made at.

**A campaign ends the two ways campaigns actually end.** `achieved` at `progress >= 1`; `abandoned` once `commitment` is spent and the target has moved backwards. `pursuitPhase` handles the third case, the one that is not an ending so much as an attrition: an untouched campaign loses commitment every week and lapses altogether after six, because a state that has stopped doing something has to stop counting or the board accumulates commitments nobody is honoring. Closings reach the feed as `kind: "pursuit"` — but only if `overt`, so a campaign run entirely in the dark is not reported as though third parties could see it. `INSTRUMENT_COERCION` moved from `npc-agent.ts` to `instruments.ts` in the same change: how coercive an instrument reads is a property of the ladder rather than of any one reader of it, and it now has three (the agent matching it against intent, `counsel.ts` pricing a reply by it, and a pursuit deciding by it whether a move was a campaign or an incident).

### The domestic instrument: executive orders

Everything the administration could *do* pointed outward. The four ladders reach other capitals, the policy program is a standing position rather than an act, and a scenario reply answers something that happened rather than starting something. A player who wanted to close a border, freeze a hostile government's money or move disaster relief had no way to try it — and the charter's institutions, carefully authored per charter, had nothing to actually check.

The catalog is twelve orders in ten US families, each naming the statute or clause it runs on (`ExecutiveOrder.authority`). Four keep the ids statutes, pledges and saves name (`order-border-controls`, `order-price-controls`, `order-asset-seizure`, `order-relief-program`); the other eight live in `order-families.ts`. Three rungs were deleted as powers no US law grants a President: detention without charge (18 U.S.C. §4001(a)), a reporting blackout, and rule by decree. The seizure was re-framed as IEEPA blocking, and price controls now require an act of Congress (`act-economic-stabilization`). `dayOneOrders` is the desk on the morning of the oath minus every emergency power; the transition's oath stage offers it, and picked orders are signed on the sealed campaign as the term's first records. An energy pledge rests on the energy order of its direction: the President has to have pulled the lever, and imports falling under a program the platform put in force at the oath do not keep it. A standoff strike (`standoff-strike.ts`) uses the existing projection profile. It does not invent a kilometer range, and a munition class whose stock is unknown does not fire.

`orders.ts` is the fifth surface. An `ExecutiveOrder` carries a `requires` gate (role hints matched the way instrument stewards are matched, an emergency flag, a stability floor), an `overreach` figure, a `MetricSet` cost, `nationEffects` in the **same closed sixteen-path vocabulary** a policy stance moves the country in, an alignment pull, and an optional `domestic?: DomesticSignature`. `orderStatEffects` (policy.ts) lands the nation paths once through the same absolute-against-live-stats clamping a program uses; when `domestic` is set, `signOrder` also splices `routeDomestic` StateEffects (lean + grievance marks) and `beliefSplitEffects` into that packet, so a defense-production priority and a price freeze hit the same states a scenario reply with the same signature would. Institutional orders without a geographic footprint omit the field. Nothing scales by competence here, because an order is carried out by the state as it is and how well it was drafted has already been priced in whether the courts let it stand.

**Availability is separate from odds**, deliberately: "you cannot do this" and "this will probably go badly" are different sentences, and collapsing them gives the player a button that does nothing. `orderAvailability` answers the first, `projectOrder` the second.

**The check is the charter's own.** `institutionalCheck` reads the charter's authored `institutions` for a judiciary (0.55) and an assembly (0.35) over a 0.15 floor — so it is derived from what a charter seats rather than from a table of charter ids, and a new charter is checked by its own institution list. The floor is what stops a junta compact, which answers to an officers' council rather than a court, from being a completely free hand.

**Overreach compounds, visibly.** Every previously *contested* order adds 0.12 to the next one's challenge chance, and `projectOrder`'s `reasoning` says so rather than moving the number silently. The cabinet is the other half: `ministerSway` reads the seated minister's competence *and* loyalty (0.6/0.4) for ±25%, because a well-drafted instrument survives review and a minister who does not want to be holding this is one the challengers can talk to. A vacant chair prices at neither end — a ministry with nobody in it neither defends the order nor betrays it. Both `accumulatedOverreach` and `ministerSway` live in `strike-odds.ts` beside `strikeDownChance` (below), moved out of this file to hold it under the 800-line budget — the arithmetic is unchanged.

**Where it is signed.** An order waits for the President beside any enrolled bill also waiting on them: the two are the same question at different tempos, a stance being what this government stands for and an order what it does about Thursday. An unavailable order gets exactly one line saying so, matching how an unreachable rung is stated: "you cannot do this" and "this will probably go badly" must never look like the same sentence. `orderRecordReading` states the standing charge, and states it as *contested* orders rather than signed ones, so a player can see that using the instrument is not itself the offense. DESIGN.md places signing at `/nation/policy/order/:id`.

**Projection parity holds exactly as it does for `resolveAction`**: `signOrder` calls `projectOrder` and nothing else to decide the odds, and the draw is seeded on (campaign seed, order id, turn) so signing a different order first cannot change the roll on this one. A contested order is a **bill, not an undo** the week it is signed — the country still changes, and the charge lands through the charter's own removal path. What a court reverses is a separate and later question, and it is now a real one: see the judicial review paragraph below.

**A rule is not a directive, and the clock is the whole difference.** An `ExecutiveOrder` may declare `viaRulemaking: true`, which says its substance is a rule rather than a directive: tightening border checks, opening a relief program, conditioning grants and leasing federal energy land are all things an agency has to *write* before they bind anybody, and the writing takes notice, comment and a final rule. Suspending entry, blocking a hostile government's assets and setting defense-production priorities are not — those bind the moment the page is signed, and the courts are the only thing that can take them back. Modeling every order as instantaneous made the unilateral track not merely the faster instrument but the total one, which is the single largest fidelity distortion the simulation carried.

A flagged order's `nationEffects` do not land in the signing packet. `signOrder` appends a `PendingRule` (`campaign.pendingRules`, keyed by the signing record's own id) with an `effectiveTurn` of `RULEMAKING_TURNS` — the same six weeks a challenge runs on, so the bench and the agency come back at comparable times — and `rulemakingPhase` applies the effects the week the proceeding finishes, once, with a `governance` feed line. Everything else about the signature is unchanged: the metric cost, the alignment pull, the domestic signature, the deed flag and the challenge roll all land at the signature, because the money and the standing are spent to *start* a proceeding whether or not it ever finishes.

**The interim stay follows from that ordering.** `rulemakingPhase` sits after `judicialReviewPhase` in the sequence, so a rule the bench strikes down before it is final is marked `canceled` rather than negated: there is nothing in the country to put back, and negating effects that never landed would have handed the government a windfall for being overruled. A struck-down rule that had already taken effect is negated exactly as a struck-down decree is. What is never refunded, in either case, is the standing.

**The Congressional Review Act is a bill, and is therefore run as one.** `act-rule-disapproval` is a `LegislativeAct` whose `arises` fires while any `PendingRule` has been `in-effect` inside `DISAPPROVAL_WINDOW_TURNS` (nine weeks — sixty days of session) and is not already the subject of a live resolution. Two optional fields carry what the catalogue cannot: `targetsOrder(campaign)` resolves the rule Congress is moving against the week it takes the resolution up, which `originate` stamps onto `Bill.target`, and `disapproves: true` marks the act as one. Everything else is the legislative machinery that already existed — it passes on the floor, reaches the desk, is vetoable through `vetoBill`, and is overridable through `projectOverride` — because a joint resolution of disapproval *is* a law and giving it its own path would have been a second legislature.

Enacted, it does two things, and neither is stamped by the enactment. `rulemakingPhase` reads the statute book each week and negates the target order's `nationEffects` through the same clamped vocabulary that applied them, marking the proceeding `disapproved`; matching from the enacted law rather than from the signing packet is what makes it hold across all four ways a bill becomes one (signature, desk lapse, override, and a veto that failed). And `lawConstraints` merges `bill.target` into `blocksOrders` alongside the catalogue's own static closures, so the reissue bar is enforced by `orderAvailability` with no new rule anywhere — a rule Congress has disapproved cannot be issued again.

**The world inspector carries the war line** off the same `nationWarLines` reader, so a country selected on the map states its running war beside its trust and tension. Trust and tension describe a relationship; a war describes the situation that relationship is being read inside, and a board that showed the first without the second was describing an ally the player was being shot at by.

**The ledger already carried the beats.** `conflictPhase` and `conditionPhase` each emit one packet per war and per condition per week, and `apply()` writes every packet's narration to the ledger — so war and condition beats reach the causal timeline and the approval-driver breadcrumb without a second display. A four-turn campaign under a live war shows "The war between United States and China intensifies" on turns 2–5 with its approval cost attached.

**The dossier says what a country is going through.** A nation dossier reads a country's wars, conditions and the operators pressing it off live state, so a country three months into an invasion, living through an outbreak or hosting an insurgency doesn't read like a country having a quiet year. It reports wars between *third parties* too, because a dossier is about its subject rather than about the player. Operator labels belong to player-core rather than to the engine's `crisis` sentences (those are headlines about an event; this is a standing fact), and every kind in `OPERATOR_KINDS` needs a label so none reaches the player as a raw id like `cyber-crew`. This reading is rebuilt with the World mode (W7 of the map-first program) and is not built yet.

**What the country is carrying reaches the attention list.** `board-state.ts` reads the active wars *this government is in* (somebody else's war is the world view's business) and the conditions running at home, worst first, and `attentionItems` puts them in the attention list beside the week's decisions. Removal pressure joins that list only when it is loud (caution or adverse). Stance can't come from the figure alone: intensity and exhaustion get worse as they rise and war support gets better, so each carries its own direction. The list is empty when there is nothing to carry, rather than showing a standing "nothing is happening" panel.

### Founding: one seal, in a fixed order

`sealFounding(seed, dataset, choices)` is the one writer of a founded `Campaign`, and `previewFounding` (player-core) is `validateFounding` then `sealFounding`, so the founding screen's preview and the oath cannot disagree. `generateFoundingPool` keeps a one-entry memo keyed by the seed, which is what makes a repeated preview 60–90 ms rather than a whole cast draw. The seal runs in this order, because each step reads what the one before it seated:

1. **The cast** (`resolveFoundingCast`, `founding-cast.ts`): the running mate, the three White House staff posts and the cabinet, each chosen or, where the choice was left open, the best fit on one ranking (`cast-fit.ts` `rankByFit`: the President's own party first, then 0.35 ideology closeness and 0.25 competence; an Independent ranks on politics and competence alone; `crossesTheAisle` flags a nominee from the other party). The draw order keeps the party-weighted score it always had, so a seed seats the same cabinet. The `fit` a card prints leaves party out (politics and competence over their weights), and `bandedAgainstList` bands each name by how far it falls behind the best fit on the list as shown: strong within 0.02, good within 0.05, fair within 0.10, poor beyond. Party is `PartyMark`, its own chip; folding it into the fit had read every co-partisan "strong" and every cross-party name "poor". Staff and running-mate lists are banded after they are cut to five. The ticket is balanced by `vice-president.ts`. A cross-party running mate, a staff pick across the aisle or off its post's bench, and a person seated twice are refused.
2. **The opening position** (`openingPosition`), with the President's career hook lines folded in (`founding-leader-hooks.ts`: each background and route buys exactly one hook through a writer that already exists).
3. **The runtimes**, with the ticket and the staff seated: the Vice President holds `role-vice-president` and leads `INST-OVP`, the Chief of Staff leads `INST-PRESIDENCY` and the National Security Adviser `INST-NSC`.
4. **The opening Congress** from `openingSeats(seed, openingMandate(seed, choices.mandate), party, anchor)` (`founding-congress.ts`): the House counted from the states' leans at spread 0.12, the Senate from the 2025 chamber (53 R, 45 D, 2 I), each moved by the mandate's coattails (landslide 0.06, comfortable 0.035, narrow 0.015, popular-vote loss −0.01, scaled by reach) and a seeded swing (±0.03 House, ±0.02 Senate), with no House independents and two in the Senate. The states' House delegations open on the Clerk of the House's 119th Congress roster (`HOUSE_DELEGATION_TABLE`) and are moved to those counts by `electHouse`, closest seats first, with the roster's two vacancies and one independent seat filled by the founding election. Then the room hook, party-scoped leadership with the Hill hooks, and party-scoped committees: every leadership post, gavel and ranking seat is taken from its own caucus's bench, independents caucusing with the Democrats, and only if a bench runs dry from the nearest platform.
5. **The Hill seats and the statehouses**: `seatGovernorsByLean` seats each governor from the state's leaning party, then the other major party, then anyone; the justices.
6. **The White House offices, then the political offices under a career acting** (`withPoliticalActings`: OMB, USTR, EPA and CIA open vacant, their most competent free career official acting on the office's Vacancies Act clock, recorded in `Campaign.actings` by `foundingActings`; the President fills them by nomination, as the Senate confirmed the EPA Administrator on March 10, 2021 and the Trade Representative on March 17), **then the career incumbents** (`withCareerIncumbents`, which passes over a chair with an acting and never seats an acting person twice) for every other non-cabinet office.
7. **The delegations, the governors' standings and the home hooks.**
8. **The platform** (`sealPlatform`): one pledge per plank, the `hold-program` stances spread over the inherited program, the dated `plank:` flags; and the inherited book, `openingAppropriation(CAMPAIGN_OPENING_DATE, turn)`.

The oath is on `CAMPAIGN_OPENING_DATE`, January 20, 2029. `FoundingChoices`' legacy fields are optional and `resolveFoundingFields` fills what is left out from the dataset, so a founding that omits them seals the same campaign as one that names them; compacts and flag are not chosen, because the United States answers each of them itself.

**The American cast is drawn by seat and party.** `people.ts` draws an American's presentation roll first and reads it once the party is known, draws the face from the seat's heritage mix and names the person from that face's group (six sourced US name groups, `SCHEMA.md` §5b), applies role-scoped party weights in one draw, then appends each major party's floor members on keys past the bench's depth. The floor members differ by seed, so the cast size does too (about 6,866). Across 16 seeds the drawn share of women per bench sits within three standard errors of the vendored table for every office group and party.

**A foreign government is drawn against the leader table.** Every foreign chair draws its presentation from the table in the same one roll (`SCHEMA.md` §5b, `LeaderConstraint`): the lead chair takes the table's sex and, for candidate 1, its age band in the same single draw, and carries the first governing party or none; the other chairs draw at the table's women-in-cabinet share. The incumbent is candidate 1 of the lead chair (`personIdFor(nation, lead, 0)`), and `foreignLeaderFor` ranks whoever holds the seated role first, then that incumbent, then competence, so a stronger rival drawn for the same chair never outranks the head of government the table seats. What a foreign leader's career line may claim is gated by the table's seat and regime (`careerGateFor`/`CareerGate` in `bio.ts`): `REIGNING_CAREER_LINES` go only to a reigning head of state, and `CONTESTED_CAREER_LINES` are kept from authoritarian, one-party, junta, absolute-monarchy and theocratic governments. Both the card bio and the long-form dossier (`dossier.ts`) pass the person's nationality, so a crown prince never took the crown. `drawName` and the name ledger live in `naming.ts`. A name is drawn from one tradition and written in its form (`composeName`: family name first in China, Korea, Vietnam and Cambodia; "bin"/"binti" and the father's name in Malaysia and Brunei; "Ould"/"Mint" in Mauritania; the father's given name second in the patronymic traditions). A multi-tradition nation's lead chair draws from its lead tradition (`leadNameGroup`: South Africa Nguni, Malaysia Malay, Nigeria Yoruba), and a monarchy's lead chair is named for its house (`royalHouseFor`, `royalName`). Every drawn name is refused if an ordered word pair of it is on the cited blocklist or the officeholder roster (`carriesRealName`), or, outside a royal house and a father's-name tradition, if its family name is a rare one of the nation's own leaders' (`carriesLeaderSurname`); the draw tries 24 times before it relaxes a repeated first name, and never relaxes a refused name. The roster (`scripts/vendor-officeholders.mjs`, hashes only, `SCHEMA.md` §5b) covers every foreign cabinet in the CIA World Leaders directory, every member of Congress since 2007, every President and Vice President, the Supreme Court, the sitting governors and federal officers, and every head of state and government since 1945 and of the UN, NATO and the EU; before it, only the people the leader table's sources cite were refused, and 45 of 120 founded campaigns carried a sitting officeholder's full name. Two gates hold it: `scripts/verify-leader-fidelity.mjs` (the table and cited blocklist re-vendored in memory, five founded campaigns against the research table, each lead chair's name form against the table's surname rule, fifty seeds of names, the American bench included, against the cited names and the roster, every name read back into a tradition `name-traditions.tsv` lists for its nation, short rosters refused, and, given the vendor's plaintext roster, words compared and coverage checked) and its proof, which plants an uncited officeholder the engine generates, a current head whose rare family name the engine hands his ministers, an uncovered roster, an uncovered source, a stale table row and a nation recorded as drawing from a tradition its people do not carry.

**A foreign selection is a calendar row, not a guessed day.** `election-calendar.ts` holds 195 governments copied from the audit table. `scheduledSelection` returns a carried or projected date. An estimate, a conflict (`IRL`, `LUX`, `NPL`, `SRB`), and a row with no date are not scheduling facts. A month cell keeps the year and the month and does not invent a day.

### The other branch: Congress, the war powers, and law

Everything an administration could do ran out of money, readiness or tradecraft, and nothing ran out of permission. The force ceiling (`capacity.ts`) fixed the tempo of one ladder; it could not fix the shape of the problem, because the top rung of the military ladder is one act against one country, so a president who is refused nothing simply aims at the next capital. The balance harness measured exactly that: the reckless proxy took a rung-5 military action in 96.6% of its actions and was removed in 58.3% of campaigns against a 0.5 design ceiling.

The missing constraint is not physical, it is political. `congress.ts` is the branch above the presidency — one legislature, the player's own, since every other capital is modeled by its standing program and giving fifty countries their own chambers would be inventing a branch nobody reads. Its shape is in `SCHEMA.md` §7b; what matters architecturally is that three separate constitutional checks share one number underneath them (`disposition`, what the Hill currently thinks of this administration) and one arithmetic on top of them (`countVote`, which returns a whole whip count rather than a boolean, because a president reads the count before deciding whether to hold the vote at all).

**War powers is the check on force.** `warPowersStanding` reads a proposed military rung 4 or 5 and returns one of four things. The President's own authority genuinely covers the first introduction of forces against a country — that is real, and modeling it as a prohibition would be wrong — for the sixty days the War Powers Resolution actually sets. Sustaining it past that, or opening a second front while the first has never been voted on, is not latitude the office has, and `instruments.ts`'s `military-4` / `military-5` gates refuse it with the reason stated as a missing capability like any other. An order already issued and still waiting to execute counts as the front it opens: `warPowersStanding` reads the President's pending rung 3 to 5 military acts in `issuedActs` that have no authorization against their target, so a second unilateral act is refused at issue rather than refused at execution with its escrow returned. The resolver marks the act being executed as resolved in its own view, so an order never blocks itself. The read is derived and changes no save shape. Running the clock out is not the way back to the rung: `expiredObligations` is the live duty the sixty days end in, and `terminateForce` — bringing the force home — is what discharges it short of a vote. Acting inside the latitude is still priced: `recordUnilateralForce` charges standing and stability, drops disposition, and appends a record that `removal.ts` then reads through a new `congress` pressure term, so repeated unauthorized action compounds without a counter having to be invented for it. The route back to the rung is `issueForceAuthorization` (`force-authorization.ts`): a three-day issued act (`kind: "authorization"`) freezes likelihood and confidence; `resolveDueAuthorizations` on the due date re-reads the live Hill so a lobbying play between ask and vote can still move the count, then writes the AUMF or the refusal. Ordinary play never sees the yeas until after the vote: the count is shown only once Congress has acted. `requestForceAuthorization` remains the issue-then-resolve-at-due helper for tests. A threat the country can see will carry the vote over a president nobody likes — which is the one route a badly-regarded administration still has to a lawful war.

**Confirmation and appropriation are the other two.** Both compose the existing writer rather than duplicating it. A confirmation is the nomination pipeline's last stage, counted by `confirmationVote` (`confirmation-count.ts`) against the Senate as it then is; the confirmation writer (`seatConfirmed` over `seatOfficer`) is the one writer of a chair. The count puts the Senate's deference to a President's officers on the room and the nominee's own record on each senator (`VoteQuestion.merit`, half of `nomineeAppeal`, added after the caucus position): added to the room it was clipped at the ceiling early in a term, and every capable nominee counted 68 to 32. `requestAppropriation` (`appropriation.ts`) composes `enactBudget`'s own packet where the vote carries. A president who cannot get a budget is not a government that stops — `appropriation.onPriorYear` goes true and the state runs on last year's numbers at a small weekly stability cost, which is what actually happens. A book pays for a fiscal year rather than a year from the week it passed: an enacted book funds through `selectFiscalYearEndTurn(selectBudgetYear(c))`, and the seal writes `openingAppropriation(foundingDate, turn)`, the inherited book, which lapses on September 30 of the oath's fiscal year (turn 37 for the 2029 oath). No `throughTurn` is ever `turn + 52`; `verify-one-day-counter` refuses one.

**Congress votes the book line by line.** `projectAppropriation(campaign, candidate?)` counts each account the request *changes* separately, on the reconciliation track (a majority in both chambers, because a budget's substance is spending), at that account's own appeal: a fixed disposition per line, plus a resistance term proportional to the raise being asked for. An account nobody proposed to move is already law and is not put back on the floor, so the Hill cannot strike out a line the President never touched. Three outcomes follow, and `Appropriation` keeps their scopes apart: `enactedTurn`/`throughTurn`/`onPriorYear` are facts about the **book**, `priorYear` is a fact about the **lines inside a book that passed**. Every line carries — new book, clock reset. Some lines carry — a book still passed, so `throughTurn` moves and `onPriorYear` stays false, and the refused accounts sit in `priorYear` at the rate the last Congress set for them (`carriedOnly` re-projects through `projectBudgetDraft`, so `enactBudget` never writes a share the floor refused). Nothing carries — no book, `onPriorYear` true, `throughTurn` unmoved, `priorYear` empty because there is no book for a line to be missing from. **Only `throughTurn` reaches the shutdown**, which is the invariant that keeps a lapse about the whole funding running out rather than about one department's number. The vote lives in `appropriation.ts`, split off `congress.ts` at the size ceiling on the seam `legislative-acts.ts` was split along; the dependency runs one way and neither `congress.ts` nor `budget-draft.ts` reads it back.

**Legislation is the half of American government the app did not have.** `legislation.ts` carries `LEGISLATIVE_ACTS` (code, not dataset — same reasoning as the policy stances and the executive orders) and `legislationPhase` originates on a seeded gap of eight to fourteen weeks (drawn per session off the last bill's turn, so a replay legislates identically while a player cannot set a watch by it) from the acts whose `arises` predicate the country currently satisfies, so the War Powers Enforcement Act reaches the desk of a president who has been making war and never reaches the desk of one who has not. A bill only reaches the desk if `projectPassage` carries both chambers: a president does not get to veto what Congress could not pass. It does not reach it the week it is taken up, either — see **a bill is a passage** below. `signBill` and `vetoBill` are the president's half, a veto message is required rather than optional, and `projectOverride` exposes the two-thirds count *before* the veto is taken — a veto held without knowing whether it can be survived is a gesture rather than a decision. That count is taken **against** the administration: the opposition's own seats are the base and defection from the President's own caucus is what carries it past two thirds, both on bands mirroring `countVote`'s. It used to run `countVote` itself, whose yeas are members voting *with* the President, so the friendlier the chamber the likelier it was computed to overrule him — an override never carried in natural play, and the only way to reach the branch was to force a *friendly* disposition. It now carries where the chamber is genuinely hostile (a disposition around 0.35 and below) and not where it is not. Catalogue acts may carry optional `domestic?: DomesticSignature`; every enactment path (`signBill`, desk lapse, override) splices `routeDomestic` and `beliefSplitEffects` beside the nation-stat packet, so the infrastructure act marks industrial states the same way a scenario reply would. Three further facts make a term legislable rather than frozen: a bill left unsigned becomes law after Article I's ten days, Sundays excepted, counted from presentment (`signing-window.ts`; `Bill.presentedOn` is the day it reached the desk, not the day it was introduced, so a bill that spent a month in markup still gets its ten days), with a small standing cost — letting a bill become law unsigned is a position and the country reads it as one. From the day after the tenth day `signBill` and `vetoBill` refuse, and the next weekly sitting of `legislationPhase` records the law; it is recorded at the sitting rather than on the morning so the week runs the same as one tick or as seven office days. The pocket veto is not modeled because Congress never adjourns in the game. The window matters because the chambers hold an ordinary bill at the floor while another is on the desk (a deadline measure such as a stopgap is presented regardless), and a president who never opened the folio used to freeze Congress for the rest of the campaign. The other two facts: every eligible act whose passage carries is a candidate for the draw rather than one act drawn and abandoned if the floor could not carry it; and an act may declare `recurs`, the number of turns after enactment it is written again — the defense authorization annually, the fiscal cap every two years — because a one-shot catalogue emptied its agenda inside the first year. Laws are durable and constrain later choices through `LawConstraint`: `blocksOrders` puts named executive orders out of reach entirely (statute, not risk — `orderAvailability` refuses them, on the same "you cannot do this" / "this will probably go badly" split that surface has always held), and `barsUnilateralForce` withdraws the office's own war-making latitude. `bindsTheExecutive` marks a law that limits the executive's own officers with no order to name (the press shield); it only makes the act count as a restraint on the floor, so a warm chamber votes it down.

**Sixty votes, not fifty-one.** `projectPassage` counts the House against `majorityOf` and the Senate against `clotureOf` — three fifths, the votes it takes to end debate — for everything on the ordinary track. Passage is a majority and always has been; a measure the minority will not stop debating simply never reaches the vote, so the bar that decides whether ordinary legislation becomes law is the cloture one, and a model that counted fifty-one taught the most consequential falsehood available about the American legislature. `LegislativeAct.track` names the exception: `reconciliation` is the budget track, where debate is limited by statute and a majority carries, and only a measure whose substance is spending, revenue or the debt limit may travel on it (in the shipped catalogue, `act-fiscal-responsibility` alone). Nominations stay at a majority through `projectConfirmation`, which is not an omission but the Senate's current rule. A seated Vice President breaks a 50-50 Senate tie on that majority and on no other question (`breakSenateTie`); an empty chair leaves the tie failing. A measure that restrains the President is counted with `countVoteAgainst` in `projectPassage`, so the chamber that dislikes him is the one that passes it. `floorThreshold(congress, chamber, track)` is the single writer of the number. The bar moved; the arithmetic moves only when the act carries a domestic signature. Then each caucus is counted on the room, the appeal, and its own `partyReading` scaled by `BELIEF_WEIGHT` (`readingsFor`), so the administration's seats and the opposition's seats no longer share one support figure. An act with no signature still counts as one figure. Measured against the fixture chamber, a Congress merely warm toward the administration now carries the defense authorization and the budget track and nothing else, which is the intended lesson and not a bug in the origination cadence.

**A bill is a passage, not a roll (`bill-stages.ts`).** Passing a law used to be a single week: Congress took a measure up, the count carried it to the desk or buried it, and the whole legislative process a player ever saw was the result. Most legislation does not die at the vote — it dies in the rooms in front of it. A measure now enters at `referral` with `status: "in-progress"` and moves one week at a time through `referral → markup → floor`, each week resolving into one of four outcomes: it advanced, it gained ground (`momentum`), it became the argument (`momentum` swings either way, and a swing against costs standing), or it stalled. Three stalls bury it, `dead` with the record kept. The draw is seeded per bill and per week off the campaign seed, so a replay legislates identically. `momentum` reaches the floor through `projectPassage(campaign, act, momentum)`, added to the act's own appeal rather than to the count — so a bill that carried on the day it was written can lose the room over a month of markup, and the cloture/track threshold still decides the floor exactly as it did. The desk clock, the veto and the override downstream of it are untouched.

Two carve-outs, both deliberate. An `urgent` act — the continuing resolution — is discharged straight to the desk, because a stopgap that spent a month in markup could not end a shutdown before the country had lived a season of one. And the appropriation stays a single-shot per-account vote in `appropriation.ts`: it is the President's own book rather than a measure Congress originated, it writes `congress.appropriation` rather than a `Bill`, and putting the shutdown clock inside a five-week resolution of the thing that stops it would be a worse model, not a richer one. Committee membership is not modeled here at all — the markup stage reads the global `congress.disposition` the way a confirmation count does, and the lattice is a separate piece of work.

The agenda does not silt. The origination gap is counted from the last introduction rather than the last signature, so the committee weeks are spent inside the eight-to-fourteen-week window Congress was already waiting; a 120-week regression holds the spacing at or under the drawn maximum, keeps one bill in the chambers and one on the desk, and resolves every bill inside fifteen weeks of introduction. The player reads the passage in two places and no third: the docket row names the stage it is at and what happened to it last week, and the bill folio prints the same three facts — the odds it moves on, the stalls left before it dies, and the count waiting on the floor — off `projectStage`, the same projection the week is resolved on. It is narrated on the existing wire: the legislation phase's own summary carries the week's beats, and no `FeedItem` kind was added.

**The President transmits, and the docket is a term's worth of business.** Two gaps closed together. `transmit(campaign, actId)` (`legislation.ts`) is the office's own legislative initiative: the President sends an administration-sponsored act to the floor *now* rather than waiting for `originate` to draw it. `transmittableActs` is `billsOnTheAgenda` narrowed to `sponsor === "administration"` — a president may lobby for and sign the opposition's bill but cannot introduce it — and the verb refuses four ways: an act nobody in the administration wrote, an act Congress will not take up this week, a desk that already holds a bill (Congress sends one at a time, the same rule `originate` keeps), and a measure this chamber has already voted on this week. The count is `projectPassage` and nothing else, which is the contract every projection in the engine holds: what the screen showed is the count the floor stage will run. What transmission buys is a place on the calendar, not a result — the bill is referred and takes the staged passage below like Congress's own, which is what stops the President's route to the desk being five weeks shorter than the legislature's. The price is `TRANSMISSION_DISPOSITION_COST` (0.03 of `congress.disposition`), paid on the asking, and the standing cost of losing now falls where the loss actually happens: the third stall that buries the bill. No new `Campaign` state: eligibility, price and outcome are all derived from the record. player-core's `transmissionRows` (`congress-docket.ts`) lists the administration's bills with the whip count, the summary and the price before a confirm-gated transmit; DESIGN.md places this in `/washington/congress`.

**Full-year money is an omnibus (`omnibus.ts`).** A regular appropriation counts each line the President moves on its own, and in a Senate that needs sixty none clears. `projectOmnibus` / `requestOmnibus` count the negotiated book once per chamber with the minority voting at the share it gives a measure its own appropriators wrote (`KEEP_GOVERNMENT_OPEN_OPPOSITION`) and the President's side reading the room; half of every change the President asked for is given away in the negotiation and the concession costs 0.02 standing. It clears 64 to 76 Senate votes on the founding chambers (the FY2023 omnibus passed 68-29). A book it cannot carry leaves the government to the stopgap. The balance harness's careful proxy (`balance-budget.ts`) sends a regular book when every line would carry and an omnibus otherwise, and answers every offer to resign that reaches it: it accepts a Secretary whose department delivers under 0.6 and asks the rest to stay. The spendthrift proxy runs `fiscal-investment` rather than redistribution, which raises the take by more than it spends.

**`LEGISLATIVE_ACTS` contains 40 measures at the 2026-10-12 integration checkpoint.** The original, fiscal and domestic modules feed one registry; the later construction appropriation and three housing lifecycle measures use the same bill pipeline. Conditions and current-law state determine which known proposals can be introduced. Routine appropriations, the farm bill, downturn relief, immigration and other domestic measures provide recurring and condition-driven business. `originate` uses the existing bounded introduction cadence; catalog growth does not automatically raise the volume of presidential interruptions. Housing, health coverage and clean-grid markup selects substantive provisions, while other measures retain their documented procedural abstraction. A catalog count is not a coverage or passage-throughput claim.

**The continuing resolution is an act, and the lapse is what happens when nobody passes it.** `appropriation.onPriorYear` already modeled a government running on last year's numbers at −0.004 stability a week, forever. What was missing is the cliff at the end of it, so a president could go a full term without a budget and never meet the Antideficiency Act. `act-continuing-resolution` (`legislative-acts.ts`) is an ordinary catalogue entry: it arises when the book in force, whatever it is, is within two weeks of running out or already has (`fundingRunningOut`), rides the reconciliation track, appropriates nothing, and answers to the desk clock, veto and override as every other act. The floor count is the exception: most of the other side votes to keep the government open, and that share does not shrink when they dislike the President (`countFundingContinuityVote`). Two optional `LegislativeAct` fields serve it — `funds: { turns } | { throughFiscalYear: true }`, which `enactmentEffects` turns into an `appropriation` effect (`{ fundTurns }` or `{ throughTurn }`, a *patch* on `congress.appropriation` emitted before the packet's whole-`Congress` disposition write so a stale copy cannot land back over it; it never shortens `throughTurn`, and it sets `onPriorYear` true, because a stopgap continues last year's appropriation and a government living six weeks at a time has no budget of its own), and `urgent: true`, which lets `originate` take it up the week it becomes eligible rather than at the drawn eight-to-fourteen-week session gap. A stopgap the floor cannot carry never reaches the desk, which is exactly how the lapse begins: a failed vote, not a calendar.

**The stopgap is offered before the deadline, not after it.** `fundingRunningOut` used to require `onPriorYear` as well, which was harmless while every book was a year of turns from the week it passed. Once the opening book became a real fiscal year ending September 30, 2029, the flag only went true when that book had already lapsed, so a continuing resolution could arise only after the government had closed and every administration met at least a short shutdown. The predicate now reads the date alone: an enacted book for the coming year moves `throughTurn` past the two-week window, so a book that close to its end has nothing behind it. The stopgap's own effect sets `onPriorYear`, because a stopgap passed before the deadline would otherwise leave a chain of them reading as a government with a budget, and the full-year continuing resolution, the weekly stability cost and the election's record penalty all read that flag.

`funding.ts` holds the arithmetic and `shutdown.ts` the week. Whether the government is closed is **derived, never stored** — `unfundedTurns` past `SHUTDOWN_GRACE_TURNS` (2) — so the moment a stopgap or a full appropriation moves the date, every surface reads open at once. `campaign.lapses` is the history, because a passed CR would otherwise erase the episode from the state entirely. `shutdownPhase` is the record's one writer and charges `LAPSE_STABILITY_COST` (0.04 a week, ten times the prior-year friction it is charged *on top of*) with feed lines naming what closed and what reopened. Delivery collapses through a dial that already existed and nothing had ever moved: `lapsedBudgetAdequacy` walks each `InstitutionRuntime.budgetAdequacy` down 0.12 a week to a floor of 0.2 and back up at 0.06 to the settled 0.8, and `institutionTick` applies it, so the collapse reaches the player through `deriveImplementationQuality`'s existing `BUDGET_WEIGHT` rather than a bolted-on multiplier. A body outside `independence: "executive"` takes half the fall and stops half the distance down — the same halving independence already buys in both directions, and not an exemption.

**The domestic line: what the carrier is finally paid for out of.** Four coercive accounts is not a budget. A president who could only move money between the foreign service and the navy was never making the choice the office actually makes — between what the state projects abroad and what it delivers at home — and every carrier in the game was bought out of nothing. `domestic` is a fifth account on the same continuous allocation, with the same sustainment line, the same cap and the same "only the change reaches the treasury" rule. It is deliberately **not** an instrument: nothing on any escalation ladder gates on it, it declares no stat targets and `gain: 0`, and `STATECRAFT_ACCOUNTS` stays the four that are.

What it buys is not a nation stat, because the delivery side of the state does not live on the nation — it lives on `campaign.institutions`. So the domestic line joins the dial the shutdown slice had just made move. `accountLevel(runtime, account, branchAdequacy?)` returns `branchBudgetAdequacy(campaign)` for this account (the mean `InstitutionRuntime.budgetAdequacy` across the branch), which is what its sustainment is priced against and what its headline reads. `settledBudgetAdequacy(campaign)` turns the domestic net share into the level a body settles at — `FUNDED_BUDGET_ADEQUACY + netShare × DOMESTIC_ADEQUACY_GAIN`, floored at `LAPSE_BUDGET_FLOOR`, capped at 1 — `institutionPhase` reads it once for the whole branch (a fact about the appropriation, not about any one agency, exactly as it already reads `inShutdown`), and `lapsedBudgetAdequacy` takes it as a fourth argument. **`institutionTick` remains the one writer of `budgetAdequacy`**: no new effect kind, no second path, and the consequence reaches the player through `deriveImplementationQuality`'s existing `BUDGET_WEIGHT` the same way a lapse does.

**The money reaches an agency through OMB.** Congress voting an increase is not the same event as an agency having it: appropriated funds are apportioned, and the executive-office body whose whole mandate that is has a delivery reading like any other. `apportionmentShare(campaign)` is `1 − OMB_APPORTIONMENT_WEIGHT × (1 − INST-OMB.implementationQuality)`, and `settledFromNetShare(netShare, apportionment)` applies it to a **raise only**. The asymmetry is load-bearing in both directions: a well-run budget office cannot apportion money nobody voted, so a starved establishment starves at the same rate however good the Director is, and a president who wins a domestic increase while leaving that chair empty banks most of it rather than all of it. Reading the stored `implementationQuality` rather than re-deriving it is a constraint, not a shortcut — `funding.ts` imports no value from anywhere, because `institutions.ts` imports it and `opinion.ts` reads the branch every week, so a reach from there into `institutions.ts` closes the cycle the file was split out to avoid. `budget.ts`'s `domesticHeadlineDelta` quotes the same apportioned figure, so the budget shows the level the branch will actually settle at rather than the one a perfect OMB would buy. At the founding share the net is zero and the apportionment multiplies nothing: doing nothing to the domestic line still costs nothing, which is the property every other account has.

The feedback through the sustainment line — a cheaper establishment is cheaper to hold — is a convergent loop rather than a runaway, because `DOMESTIC_SUSTAIN_AT_FULL × DOMESTIC_ADEQUACY_GAIN` is well under one. Its fixed points are the legible part: appropriating nothing settles the branch at 0.5, holding at sustainment keeps the 0.8 it opens on, taking the line to the account cap settles at 1. The treasury side is honest in both directions for the same reason — a larger safety net costs money every week forever, and cutting it pays back every week forever. Money arriving on time still never lowers a body; only an appropriation that no longer pays for it does, which is the one case the settling rule walks downward.

`ACCOUNT_CAP` and `accountCap` moved into `funding.ts` and are re-exported unchanged from `budget.ts`. `institutions.ts` needs the domestic reading, `budget.ts` reaches `institutions.ts` transitively, and a graph that let `institutions.ts` import `budget.ts` would close that cycle — the same cycle the paragraph below is about. `funding.ts` still imports no value.

On the surfaces the line is a fifth slider on the Appropriations board and nothing else new: it comes free from `BUDGET_ACCOUNTS` in `BudgetRoom`, `compositionOf` and `AppropriationReading`'s ceiling. A surface reads it as what it is rather than as an instrument: its headline is delivery, and it offers no instrument dossier, because there is no ladder to open. Anything that lists instrument doors walks `STATECRAFT_ACCOUNTS`. `AppropriationAct` reads the per-account count before the signature (`accountFloorWarning`), and `StandingOrder` names the lines of the standing book the floor struck out, which is a different sentence from the whole book failing.

`funding.ts` exists as a separate leaf from `shutdown.ts` for one hard reason: `institutions.ts` has to know whether it was funded, and `opinion.ts` reads the branch's delivery every week, so anything `institutions.ts` imports is inside `opinion.ts`'s own initialization. A module reaching `turn-loop.js` from in there closes a cycle that leaves `OPINION_DRIVERS` undefined at import time. The readings therefore live in a file with no runtime imports at all, and the phase that needs the calendar lives next door.

`appropriationClock` and `appropriationReading` state the clock and whether the government is closed or governing on last year; a stopgap reaches the President as a bill through the paths every other act already uses, so the attention stop comes with it. The appropriation clock (`appropriationDue`, player-core `summons/due-legislative.ts`) ranks on the turn after `throughTurn` but is dated on the day the money runs out: the fiscal year's September 30 when the book was bought through one, and otherwise the first day after a stopgap's last week. The calendar row for the same deadline takes the clock's subject when both name that day (`milestoneSubject`), so `collapseBySubject` keeps one row.

**An authorization permits; an appropriation pays.** `act-defense-authorization` used to charge the treasury and hand over readiness on signature, which inverted the rule that no officer may obligate money Congress has not appropriated. It now costs nothing and delivers nothing directly. What it carries instead is `raisesCap: { account, share, turns }`, and `enactmentEffects` writes an `AccountCapRaise` onto `campaign.capRaises`; `accountCap(campaign, account)` in `budget.ts` is `ACCOUNT_CAP` plus every live raise for that account, and it is what `selectBudget`, `budgetLine` and `projectBudgetDraft` clamp against. So the act widens the room the appropriators may work in for a year and nothing else: readiness arrives only if the money is then voted through `requestAppropriation` and the defense account builds with it week by week. Clamping on read rather than only on the way in is what makes the expiry bite — an account funded above the standing cap under last year's authorization falls back to the standing cap the week that authorization lapses, with nobody re-voting anything.

**The room has people in it, and they are the ones a whip count reads.** `Congress` was two seat totals and a mood, so a projection could say the House was six votes short and there was nobody to call about it — the PRD's "propose an agenda, lobby" had a verb for the first half and none for the second. `hill.ts` seats seven posts (Speaker, both floor leaders in each chamber, two committee chairs) out of the *same* person pool the cabinet is hired from, adding two role archetypes rather than a parallel person model, and appending them last in `ALL_ROLES` so no existing seed's cast moves. Architecturally the important part is that this did not fork the vote arithmetic: `countVote` stays the single writer of a count and now reads `chamberDisposition(congress, chamber, states, people)`, which is the room's own disposition displaced by the influence-weighted rapport of the members seated in that chamber and, when a delegation table is present, by the seat-weighted state blocs (`DELEGATION_LEVERAGE`). With nobody seated and no (or empty) delegation table the displacement is zero and the count is byte-identical to what it was — which is what lets an older save load with an empty Hill instead of being refused, and what kept the balance distribution unmoved when the feature landed. Members drift toward a target derived from the room, so every existing signal (approval, a budget that never passed, force used without a vote) reaches the Speaker through one account of the administration rather than a second opinion of it. A midterm that flips a chamber reseats its majority posts and keeps every incumbent whose caucus still fits, rapport included.

**What a member holds toward the President lives on the person, as a tie.** `PersonRuntime.tie` (`state-ties.ts`) is directed — their view of the President — and carries `rapport`, `trust`, a two-way favor ledger and grudges. It is on the person rather than the post so a Speaker who loses the gavel still remembers what was done to them, and so a cabinet secretary, a governor or a foreign leader can hold one by the same rules; members of Congress are the first holders. `ties.ts` is the one place a tie is read (`tieOf`, `rapportOf`, `figureRapport`, `openFavorsOwedByPresident`) and written: the `tie` effect kind goes through `apply` like every other change, names its cause and is refused without one. Three writers emit it: `congressPhase` (the week's drift, from `leadershipDrift`), `seatingPacket` and founding (a newly seated leader's opening rapport, from `seatLeadership`), and lobbying (at resolution, the member's new rapport and trust, the favor a landed trade leaves owed and the grudge a missed public appeal leaves; and the rapport the play costs other people — the President's caucus leaders on a landed trade across the aisle and on shared credit, charged at resolution and at issuance respectively, and the other side's leaders when the President campaigns for one of their own). These small rapport costs land inside the week; the weekly drift settles a holder within `FIGURE_DRIFT` of their target back onto it, so until grudges are read they are a cost for the week they land in. `applyBill` settles open favors on a bill when it becomes law or dies. The tie replaced `HillFigure.disposition` and `congress.promises`; neither survives beside it.

**And the room has committees, which is where a nomination is actually decided.** The institution catalog referred every confirmable office to one of thirteen `CMTE-*` ids from the day it was written, and nothing read the field back out: the committee stage of a nomination was a timed gate against `congress.disposition`, `selectNominationsBefore` had no callers, and only two of the thirteen committees existed as people at all. `committees.ts` seats each panel with a chair and a ranking member out of the same pool `hill.ts` draws from (`seatCommittees`, called at founding, inside midterm `recompose`), and derives the panel's reading rather than storing it — `caucusTarget` per member, weighted by the panel's balance, pulled `PANEL_GRAVITY` of the way from the room. The architectural rule is the one `chamberDisposition` follows: **the room stays the single account of how the administration is regarded, and a panel is a position on it**, never written back. Three existing selectors changed and no new one was added: `stageDuration` stretches a committee stage by `committeeDisposition` instead of the room, `committeeWouldReport` decides against the panel that has the nomination, and `advanceChance` reads the panel at the `markup` stage for an act the catalogue routes (`LegislativeAct.committee`, optional — the money bills answer to Appropriations and Budget, which confirm nobody and so have no catalog id, and are deliberately left on the room). An unseated panel and an unrouted act both read `congress.disposition` exactly, which is what keeps an older save counting as it did. The player surface is the Congress room's committee lattice (`TheCommittees`), reading `selectCommitteeDocket`: jurisdiction, the panel, and what is before it — doors, never a flat roster.

**Party is a named ticket with a three-axis belief vector, sealed for the Hill and stamped on people.** `Charter.parties` authors the tickets a constitution actually runs (the US constitution: Democrat, Republican, Independent). Each `Party` carries `beliefs: PartyBeliefs` — the same three axes as `Person.ideology` (`economicLeftRight`, `authLibertarian`, `nationalistGlobalist`) — replacing the retired `economicLean` scalar. `Person.party` is the optional `PartyId` stamped when a person is generated or a president is composed. A generated person's ticket is drawn first (majors weighted double, and on the US roles under the role-scoped weights and per-party floors of `usPartyWeightsFor` and `usPartyQuotaFor`) and their ideology is placed near its `beliefs`, at a distance `cohesion` sets (`rollPolitics`, `packages/engine/src/people.ts`); a president carries the ticket the player chose, with `presidentPartyFromIdeology` (nearest major on `beliefs.economicLeftRight`) only as the fallback for a charter that authors no such ticket. `Congress.administrationParty` (`AdministrationParty` — Democrat, Republican **or Independent**) is what the president ran as, chosen at founding rather than derived, and `Congress.caucusAnchor` (`UsMajorParty`) is the caucus they are counted against — themselves, or for an independent the major party their economics is nearest (`sealCaucusAnchor`). The chambers stay two-party whoever is president: seat shares, Senate seat parties and leadership posts are all read off the anchor, so the Speaker and Majority Leader are still whoever holds the chamber and an independent president does not rename a caucus. Midterms and `seatLeadership` move seats and caucus, not the party label.

**Control is counted by caucus.** `majorityCaucus(composition, caucusAnchor)` (`hill.ts`) counts the independents with the Democrats and gives a tie to the administration, and `panelBalance` counts them the same way; `benchFor` excludes anyone already seated elsewhere. `openingCongress` takes optional counted seats (the founding's `openingSeats`) and falls back to its legitimacy derivation without them. The player-core readings that print control (`congress-reading.ts`'s `holdsMajority`, and `chamberControl` in `government/machine-readings.ts`) read `majorityCaucus` rather than comparing two columns, so the Speaker shown is always the majority's.

An independent has no caucus, so "is this bloc mine" is replaced everywhere by a reading: `administrationAlignment(lean, standing)` (−1..1), with `administrationReach` (1, or `INDEPENDENT_REACH` 0.75) and `towardAdministration` for signed displacement, and `oppositionParties(party)` returning **both** majors for an independent — two oppositions rather than one. Governing as an independent is harder purely through that arithmetic: no coattails in `openingCongress`, an own-party floor scaled by reach in `countVote`, a leadership tilt scaled by reach in `figureTarget`, a partisan swing they bank less of in the vote drivers, and a flatter map to 270 in `electoralVotesWon`: every lean reaches them at three quarters of its strength, and the map is centered on the country's own count (`states.ts#mapAlignment` adds the standing gap back), so the states add up to the national share rather than sitting 7.5 points under it. No difficulty multiplier exists.

Hill readings (`hill-reading.ts`, chamber shares) derive Democrat / Republican / Independent from `caucusAnchor` plus caucus or share so a majority leader cannot invert; person dossiers also print `Person.party` for cabinet and available records, and the president is stamped with the ticket they chose (including `party-usa-independent`). Disposition stays a separate colored stance word (`with you` / `against you`). A save from any other build is refused rather than read, so no campaign ever arrives without the party the player chose. `PARTY_CONSUMERS` registers every party leaf; the dataset validator's `party-beliefs-distinct` check refuses two majors that differ by less than 0.3 on every axis, and `party-cohesion` refuses an independent bloc authored as disciplined as a whipped caucus. `party-beliefs.ts` ships `partyReading`, `ticketReading` (the platform reading scaled by `Party.cohesion`), `beliefSplit` / `ticketSplit`, and `selectPartyReading`, whose shape is one administration reading plus a *list* of oppositions. Acts carrying a signature emit `BELIEF_SPLIT_POLARIZATION × widestTicketSplit` through `OpinionEffect.polarize` under the joint `MAX_TURN_POLARIZE` ceiling (no separate allowance) — the widest any two seated blocs read the act apart, which under an independent is usually the two majors reading each other, because a president standing outside both caucuses does not make the caucuses agree. `figureTarget` takes an optional caucus-beliefs + signature term weighted by `BELIEF_WEIGHT`. There is deliberately no `partyBelief` scenario condition.

**`lobbying.ts` is the thing a president spends to change a count**, with the words in `lobbying-copy.ts`, the issued-act queue in `lobbying-due.ts`, and what a play costs with people other than the member in `lobbying-person.ts`. Five plays: the two that spend what the President would otherwise keep are sharing a live bill's credit (filed on the bill as `Bill.sharedCredit`, so the enactment carries a smaller favorable movement for the President, `credit-share.ts`) and campaigning beside the member at home (a `state` lean toward the member's party there, standing, and the base's faith when the member is across the aisle). Each play's projection carries who resents it and by how much, and the issue and resolution packets charge that list rather than recomputing it. The original three are a call (standing, persuasion, small), a budget negotiation (a political commitment on a later appropriation, not a treasury debit at the handshake), and going over their head to the country — which reads approval and press standing instead of charm, moves the room as well as the member, and is the only one whose failure leaves the administration measurably worse off than it started. The public-appeal preview includes that room shift (`PUBLIC_ROOM_GAIN` / `PUBLIC_ROOM_LOSS`); omitting it was an accounting defect, not a presentation choice. Ordinary play sees likelihood and confidence from the intelligence ladder, frozen on the issued act. `issueLobbying` writes that act; `resolveDueLobbying` rolls against the frozen `chance` on the due date, stream `lobbying-outcome` keyed by calendar day and post, so opening the dossier cannot consume the outcome RNG. `lobbyFigure` is the issuer. Resolution writes the `congress` effect (the room's shift and the record), a `tie` effect on the member (their new rapport, with the record's account as its cause), and closes the act. A landed budget negotiation also opens a favor the President owes on that member's tie (`lobbying-favors.ts`) against the live fiscal bill — reserve-checked via `obligationAvailability`, never debited here — and `applyBill` marks that favor repaid or defaulted when the bill becomes law or dies. Without eligible fiscal floor business the trade card is blocked with honest copy rather than inventing a project. Press engagements use the same `issuedActs` map under `kind: "press"`: `issuePressAction` schedules the hour, `resolveDuePress` (from `advanceOneDay`) rolls stream `press-outcome` keyed by calendar day, action, and desk, and closes the act — the Podium authorizes; Continue brings how the room took it.

**Both halves reach the player.** A chamber lists its members, and a member's post shows each play's assessed prospects, authorized cost and extra cost on failure, issuing rather than resolving (DESIGN.md, `/washington/congress` and `/washington/congress/post/:id`). The odds the engine rolls against stay on the projection; a surface does not print them as “N in 100” or a promised future disposition.

**Judicial review makes an executive order reversible in fact rather than in a label.** A contested order used to be a bill the government paid the same week, after which the order stood forever — which made the unilateral track the cheapest durable instrument on the board. `judicialReviewPhase` rules on it six weeks later; a struck-down order has its `nationEffects` reapplied negated through the same clamped vocabulary that applied them, while the standing it cost is not refunded. That asymmetry is the whole reason signing one is a decision. The nine-seat bench (`packages/engine/src/judiciary.ts`) is stored, one seat per single-office institution (`INST-SCOTUS-SEAT-1`..`-9`, `institution-catalog.ts`), filled and emptied by the same confirmation pipeline (`nomination.ts`) that seats a cabinet secretary — a seat stays whoever was actually confirmed to it until a new nomination for that seat's own office is confirmed, and reads vacant (never re-derived) once its holder dies or is never filled. A save whose `institutions` record predates these nine seats still reads the pre-existing live derivation — highest integrity and competence among the founding nation's own nationals not eligible for a cabinet chair — unchanged. `strikeDownChance(charter, order, bench?, ground = "silent")` blends institutional overreach with the seated bench's median distance from the order's domain lean plus an integrity/competence uncertainty term, clamped 0.05–0.9, so review is influenced by who actually sits without becoming a partisan scoreboard; the seated bench is passed into this call wherever the odds are shown, so the odds a player sees before signing match what the pipeline rolls against.

**The bench weighs whether Congress spoke, not only who sits.** `strikeDownChance`'s fourth argument is the order's `ConstitutionalGround` (`"authorized" | "silent" | "forbidden"`, `legislative-acts.ts` — see `SCHEMA.md`'s `LawConstraint` entry). An order a statute affirmatively backs (`authorizesOrders`) discounts the institutional term by `AUTHORIZED_RELIEF` (0.45) at both the challenge and the bench — Youngstown's category-one reading, a court weighing an order Congress asked for far more gently than one it never spoke to; an order a statute forbids returns `STRIKE_MAX` outright; a silent order prices exactly as it always has. `orderAvailability` refuses an order with no `authority` at all, then gates on `needsEmergency` (naming the National Emergencies Act) and `needsCongress` (naming the act that once granted the power), so an order like `order-price-controls` never reaches the desk to price a strike-down that was never really in play.



### Simulate: oversight — who is looking at the government

The presidency had every instrument and no auditor. A president could hollow out an agency, have an order struck down by the courts, run covert action in a country nobody voted to touch, and the only account anybody kept of it was the polling. There was no way, anywhere in the simulation, for a president to be **caught**.

`campaign.oversight` (shape in SCHEMA §`campaign.oversight`) is one spine for both halves of that: the inspectors general inside the executive branch, and the committees of the Senate. Both produce **matters**, and a matter is the same object either way — a direction, a subject, a stage, a clock, an irreversible public flag, and append-only beats on the `RemovalArc` pattern. Keeping them one type is the point: an audit and a subpoena are different things to receive and the same thing to sit through, so ripening is one rule (`oversightRipeningPhase`) rather than two that agree today.

**Nothing here ends a presidency, and nothing here can be closed by the President.** There is deliberately no engine verb that shuts a matter and no control that offers to. The only lever the office has is `fileInspectorRemovalNotice` — thirty days, in writing, with the reasons stated — and using it while an audit is open does not stop the audit: it marks it obstructed and public, permanently, and hands the committee with jurisdiction its own file the same week. That chain (`inspectorGeneralPhase` → `committeeInquiryPhase` → `oversightRipeningPhase`, all three before `congressPhase`) is the whole design: being caught is a thing that happens on a date, to a body with a chair and a jurisdiction.

**Removal, direction and the acting officers, after *Slaughter*.** The catalog's commission rows carry `removalLaw` (DECISIONS 4.14): the FTC and CFPB chairs are at will, and removing one writes `INDEPENDENT_AT_WILL_POLITICIZATION` (0.1); the SEC, FCC, CFTC, FDIC and NRC chairs keep for-cause protection with `removalContested`, so `removeOfficer` lets the removal through with or without a cause, charges the for-cause politicization and appends a `ContestedRemoval` that `legal-exposure.ts` reads as an exposure; the NLRB and the Fed are protected. `isDirectionBarred(def)` is true only for an independent body whose head is protected, so policy supervision reaches the FTC and CFPB alone, and `docket.ts#docketConstraint` says which of the three a rulemaking body is. Casework at Justice goes through the Attorney General's judgment, not `STATUTORY_BARS`. `fileInspectorRemovalNotice` takes a structured account and judges it only on the campaign's records. `actingClassOf` admits a first assistant, a person confirmed to another Senate-confirmed office or a senior bench employee (5 U.S.C. 3345(a)); `actingLapseDay` counts 210 days from the vacancy and applies 3349a's transition rule, so an acting seated at founding runs to day 300. A pending first or second nomination tolls the clock and its rejection, withdrawal or return restarts 210 days (5 U.S.C. 3346(a)(2), (b); `appointments.ts#actingClockEffects`, called from `nomination.ts`); a third nomination or a name withdrawn before submission changes nothing. When a nominee stops acting (`nominationEndsActing`), the next eligible name on the bench is seated the same day, and only when none exists does the office wait, its functions falling to the agency head alone (3348(b)(2)).

**Impoundment** (`appropriation.ts`, `impoundment.ts`, `congressional-session.ts`). `proposeRescission` and `proposeDeferral` write an `ImpoundmentMessage` onto the appropriation through the `congress` effect; `budgetPhase` steps the pending messages first (`impoundmentPackets`) and the week's spend reads `selectSpendableBudget`. A rescission is counted once on day 25 of continuous session and released by default on day 45, then cannot be proposed again for that line in the fiscal year; a pocket rescission (45 days ending after the fiscal year) is flagged contested and lapses with the year; a deferral's Comptroller General report comes on day 14, a suit 25 session days after an adverse one, and a court order 56 calendar days later. `congressional-session.ts` counts days of continuous session under current practice: pro forma sittings keep every recess in session, so the count runs on the calendar and only a new Congress convening restarts it (owner ruling, 2026-10-25). The Comptroller General reviews every deferral on the day it ends, and a new deferral of the same account inherits an earlier finding; a repeated policy deferral of an account in a fiscal year after an unlawful finding costs the Hill 0.02 for each earlier finding, capped at the first finding's 0.08 (`REPEAT_UNLAWFUL_DEFERRAL_STEP`). The Antideficiency Act is not engaged and not modeled. No act wiring or surface invokes either message yet.

**The third arm: a prosecutor nobody supervises.** Both halves above stop short of the one place an inquiry could actually end in a charge, because the department that would bring it is run from a cabinet chair the President fills and empties. `specialCounselPhase` (`special-counsel.ts`) is what the department does about that: when a matter aimed at this administration is public, obstructed and has **reported**, the Attorney General appoints a special counsel out of it, and the Judiciary Committee is told the same day. From that week the President has no verb that reaches its matter. The Attorney General supervises it under 28 C.F.R. 600.7 through `superviseSpecialCounsel(campaign, { kind: "countermand" | "remove", ... })`, decided by the Attorney General's own judgment (the `certify-order` weighing at reach 0.5 or 0.7, plus 0.25 when the inquiry is aimed at the administration): a declined request is recorded on the matter and costs nothing; a carried removal closes the matter, marks it public and (aimed at the administration) obstructed, and costs the Hill 0.12 (+0.10 aimed at the administration); a countermand leaves the counsel sitting and costs 0.06. No surface invokes it yet. One counsel sits at a time, and none is appointed with the Attorney General's chair empty.

What is left to the President is that chair, and emptying it is priced rather than refused: dismissing the Attorney General with a counsel sitting marks the counsel's matter obstructed for good, costs the chamber 0.2 of its disposition, and notifies the committee — through `dismissMinister`'s own packet, so the record shows one act and not two. The counsel does not stop. Its matter weighs `ORIGIN_WEIGHT` 1.4 against an ordinary inquiry's 1 in `selectOversightPressure`, which is modest on purpose: what makes a counsel expensive is mostly the facts that got it appointed. **No save version moves** — `MatterOrigin` took a third arm inside a list the store already held, which is what the spine was shaped for.

**Where it bites.** `selectOversightPressure` enters `dispositionTarget`, so a president under a reported inquiry works a materially colder chamber every week the matter is open and stops paying the week it closes. That is the same route repeated unilateral force already takes, and deliberately so — both are bounded counts of specific, dated, nameable facts rather than a mood, which is what makes them things a player can see coming. The reading is exported for the removal arc's own `congress` term as well; the seam is named in SCHEMA.

**Where the player reads it.** DESIGN.md places oversight at `/washington/oversight`: what is under way with each matter's direction and clock, what is on the record, and the inspectors in post with what emptying each office would mean. A special counsel carries its own origin reading (what appointed it, and that nobody in the administration supervises it), and the price of emptying the Attorney General's chair is stated where the dismissal is offered. Not built yet.

### The term clock: elections as the fixed dates

The presidency had a beginning and no end. Weeks accumulated, the removal arc was the only exit from the office, and nothing ever asked the country what it made of the record — so an administration could spend two hundred weeks compiling one that nobody voted on. That is a tenure, not a term, and it made the one deadline every real president governs against a number on a screen with nothing underneath it.

`elections.ts` reads its dates off the federal calendar rather than counting turns: `presidencyElections(foundingDate)` takes them from `milestonesBetween` (November 5, 2030; November 2, 2032; and a second term's November 7, 2034 and November 4, 2036), and `nextElection` is the schedule entry after the ones already held, with its `date` and `daysAway`.
