A win records the result on election night and nothing more; `pendingInauguration` turns the term over on January 20, 2033 (`termStartTurn` 209). `MIDTERM_TURNS` is retired. Nothing in it rolls. `projectVote` is derived entirely from what the player compiled — the bloc ballot for the country, then the constitutional record against it — and it is pure and seed-independent, the same contract every projection in the engine holds, because an election settled by a die would make the whole term irrelevant to it. The count is no longer a pure referendum: `projectVote` adds the turnout, momentum, matchup and statehouse drivers (see SCHEMA). It stays seed-independent in its decision: the matchup's margin is fixed by the seed at the draw and read out over the term, so the projection a President sees in any week is the count the night would run with the matchup as known that week, the band (`marginHalfWidth`) is exactly the unknown remainder, and on Election Day the band is zero. `holdElection` still reads `projectVote` and nothing else; `presidentialVerdict` decides `won` inside it, sending an exact college tie to `contingentHouseVote`.

Three architectural facts matter. **It composes the popularity model that exists**: the five partisan blocs already are the electorate, weighted by how much of it each is and how many of each turn out, and already reading the six judgments, so the election reads them (`turnout.ts#selectBallot`) rather than inventing a second opinion of a country that has one. **A midterm recomposes Congress and re-apportions every state's delegation** — `seatSwing` transfers seats between the two caucuses (the House midterm is `HOUSE_MIDTERM` in `election-seating.ts`: a bounded share of the seats the President's party holds in close states, fitted to Gallup's 1946-2014 table, DECISIONS 5.19; presidential-year coattails are unchanged, `COATTAIL_SENSITIVITY`), `recompose` reseats leadership and calls `apportionDelegations` so the delegation table matches the new composition exactly, and every downstream vote (`countVote`, and through it authorization, confirmation, appropriation, passage, override and ratification) is harder or easier from the next week onward with no new arithmetic anywhere. **Losing and being removed are separate endings and both are reachable**: `selectEnding` reads the removal arc first, the election path never writes `RemovalArc`, and the removal path never writes the clock — so the two can never print each other's sentence. `selectEnding` is the **single** answer to whether a campaign is over: the player branches on it, and `continueUntilAttention` refuses on it with one sentence per ending. A win opens a full second term; the end of that resolves as `term-limited` with no contest, because the President is not on the ballot, which bounds a campaign at eight years.

**A November election no longer recomposes Congress on the night.** `Congress.seatingOn?: { house, senate, seatsTurn }` (`SCHEMA.md` §7b) parks the chambers a midterm or presidential result elected, and `termEventsDue` seats them on January 3 — the nine lame-duck weeks the Constitution actually leaves between an election and a new Congress, which before this wave did not exist: a midterm used to recompose the chambers the instant the result was filed. `election-seating.ts` (`packages/engine/src/`) is the new module the seat arithmetic moved to, behind a re-export from `elections.ts` (`export * from "./election-seating.js"`) so no existing import moved; it holds `MIDTERM_PENALTY`, `SWING_SENSITIVITY`, `COATTAIL_SENSITIVITY`, `SENATE_FLIP_MARGIN`, `senateClassUpIn`, `seatSwing`, `recompose`, `withPendingSeating`, `seatingPacket`, `coverage` and `simulated` — the extraction is a file-size split (`elections.ts` was over the 800-line gate), not a contract change. `ElectionResult.swing` (coattails) is now written on a **presidential** result as well as a midterm one, since a presidential win pulls the winner's own caucus with it exactly as a midterm's national mood does.

`electionPhase(charter)` runs only under `holdsElections(charter)` — an executive that faces the country on a fixed date. A junta or a clerical council holds none, and under those charters the campaign is bounded by the removal arc alone, exactly as it was.

**What it measured.** The standing gate's horizon is 52 turns, and the first election falls in week 94 (November 5, 2030), so no campaign in it reaches an election. The first fiscal deadline does fall inside it: the inherited book lapses on September 30, 2029, turn 37.

**How the harness founds.** Every harness campaign founds the United States under its own charter for a given party (`HARNESS_PRESIDENTS`: a Democrat and a Republican mirrored on the economic axis, and a centrist Independent), with the seated cabinet a party-matched founding would seat, rather than under whatever charter a seed hash drew. `BALANCE_PARTY` picks the party the full gate runs for; `balance.test.ts` measures the other two at six seeds each, and the weekly workflow runs all three. The report also counts what the cabinet phase returns (leaks, challenges and resignations per term, `cast-breaks.ts`) and scandals (`selectScandals`, `selectLiveScandals`). Measured at twelve seeds, after the caucus break moved from the projected vote to approval: no proxy is removed in any campaign for any party. The reckless proxy's own year still carries the reading past the last rung (its standing reaches the floor from its own heavy acts and Congress's disposition falls to about 0.04), and the House impeaches, but approval ends the year at 0.413–0.427 and never nears the 36% line where the President's senators start to leave, so the Senate falls about seventeen votes short. That is the approval model holding even the heaviest act every week near 42%, and the balance gate reports it as it is: "can be lost by governing badly" fails for all three parties, and "leaves no player in a campaign that cannot be lost" fails with it. Passive play finishes the year above careful play on approval for every party (0.565–0.58 against 0.514–0.532). Standing reads 0.63–0.69 for passive play against 1.0 for careful play, and careful play ends at stability 0 for every party (POL-25). Nine bounds fail and wait on the owner: the three removal bounds above in each party's cross-party check, "can actually be lost" and the population's; the reckless proxy's treasury ending at zero against the 0.1 floor in `balance.test.ts` and `population-balance.test.ts`; a passive Democrat re-elected in one of four term contests against a bound of none (`term-balance.test.ts`); and the passive Independent's drift, 0.010 against a bound under 0.01.

**What moved approval.** The report credits each judgment's movement to the road it came in on (`approval-attribution.ts`). A decision almost never writes the mood: it moves where a judgment is heading (`opinionTargets`), and the weekly revision carries the mood there, so a report that credited whatever packet wrote the mood read the heaviest act every week as moving approval not at all ("decisions 0" for every proxy). The harness observes the state each phase plans against (`countedPhases`' observer) and keeps, per judgment, how far each road has pushed its target and the mood has not yet followed: the President's acts and appointments, and the standing program's weekly work in the policy phase, are "decisions"; the pledge phase is "pledges"; every other phase is "world". `playCampaign` may pass `PlayOptions.wrapPhase` to wrap each weekly phase before the run walks it. World-feel bounds on that harness: no stance held alone may finish a term more than 0.03 of approval above idle (`FREE_LUNCH_MARGIN` in `balance.test.ts`); a hawk's public order at a year is the stopgap test (`countFundingContinuityVote`), because a lapse that never closes was pinning it on the floor; foreign austerity stays under half where the books do not demand it (`macro-balance.test.ts`). A recession is a label on the cycle, not a second writer of growth: it opens only inside a contraction or a trough. Each week's revision is paid out across the roads by their share of the open gap. Measured at twelve seeds for D, R and I: passive play moves approval entirely through the world (decisions 0); reckless about half through its own decisions; careful play about two fifths, with security carrying about 0.59 of its movement because its order collapses (POL-25). `balance.test.ts` bounds that the report can see a President's hand (reckless decisions above 0.25, passive under 0.05); the ceilings per judgment are the owner's to set.

**A bound that had to move stays in the file as a pin.** Where the US founding moved a measured figure off a bound the gate held, the bound was not lowered and forgotten: the looser assertion runs, and the original runs beside it as `it.fails`, so vitest reports the day the mechanism is fixed and the old bound can come back. Four pins came off when their mechanisms landed. The founded budget-authority stock now equals the carry share and its ceiling (`fiscal-authority.test.ts`), because `openingPosition` no longer lets a seat's treasury push open the stock above the share. Strained boards stage more weeks than the same boards quiet (`attention-pacing.test.ts`), 82 against 18 over three boards and 35 against 7 on one, because the public now reads the live disorder and insurgency the strain wrote rather than the figures the country was founded on. And the reckless ceiling passes (`balance.test.ts`), because removal is counted by caucus. Still pinned: staffing the government lifting one-year approval above passive play (`population-balance.test.ts`: the US seal fills all 76 institutions, so the institutionalist has nothing to staff), a West Wing or running-mate swap leaving half a year byte-identical (`founding-cast.test.ts`), and a promise to hold a program kept by a President who did nothing (`pledges.test.ts`, `pledge-balance.test.ts`). The term margin over passive play is asserted at 0.1 (`term-balance.test.ts`).

**Every harness run founds on a platform.** A run that names no platform founds each proxy on the planks its own program enacts (`balance-platform.ts`'s `platformForProgram`: the plank whose deliverable is each stance of the opening program, in program order, one per program domain), filled to `PLATFORM_MAX_PLANKS` from its party's usual ground. A party's ground is every plank whose program points the party's way, read as `administrationWouldWrite`'s party half reads it (the plank's alignment against the party ticket's beliefs), strongest first; an Independent takes the two sides' ground alternately. Filler is limited to planks that promise something still to do (adopt a program, sign an order): a plank that holds a program opens the term on it at the seal, which would change the proxy under test instead of founding it, and only the proxy's own program may bring one. The first-hundred-days priority is the first plank a hundred days can settle, because the seal refuses a held program there. The idle proxy runs on its party's ground too, which is what makes a broken promise measurable. Each `CampaignOutcome` carries its `platform`, and every summary line prints it. `foundedForBalance` keeps founding on no platform when called on its own. **The gate on a platform (`pledge-balance.test.ts`).** That run names its own platform. `runBalanceHarness` and `foundedForBalance` take an optional `HarnessPlatform` (`platform`, `firstHundredDays`), and every `CampaignOutcome` carries its final `pledges`. The platform run founds a Republican on five planks with a first-hundred-days border pledge and plays passive and prudent to the first midterm (`HORIZON` = the turn of November 5, 2030, plus one). Measured at four seeds: passive keeps 4 of 20 and breaks 16; prudent, which pursues its own program and never reads the platform, keeps 4 and breaks 16 (held programs settle on their outcome, and only a law the President signed counts toward one); neither is removed. The gate asserts every pledge settles by the midterm, an unacted first-hundred-days pledge breaks, and a platform removes nobody careful or passive; the free keeping of held programs is pinned.

**The term gate (`term-balance.test.ts`, `TERM_HORIZON_TURNS` = 417).** The election arithmetic is measured now, and measuring it was not a formality. Run over both terms it found two defects that no shorter horizon could have shown. Vote share was `coalitionApproval` plus the incumbency edge — approval rescaled — so a well-governed administration took 82 percent of the vote and a drifting one 29, margins no republic has produced, and the contest was decided inside the first year. The count was anchored first by an evenly split electorate and a leverage of 0.36 on approval, which put a landslide near 61 percent and a rout near 43. The bloc ballot replaced both: `APPROVAL_LEVERAGE` is deleted and `ELECTORATE_ANCHOR` survives only so the displayed drivers still sum to the share, because party loyalty (`PARTY_LOYALTY` 0.9) does the work the leverage did. A bloc votes `logistic(logit(approval) + 0.9 × partisanship)`, so a partisan who disapproves still mostly votes their party; the design probe puts the share near 40 percent at a 30 percent headline and near 57 at 65, before incumbency and the record drivers (`DESIGN.md` §4.7), and the term gate has not been re-run against it. Second, `seatSwing` read the national swing against the *whole* chamber at a sensitivity of 0.35, so every measured midterm moved 77 House seats in one direction or the other, larger than 1894. Most seats are not competitive, which is what the sensitivity now encodes (house 0.12, senate 0.05): a ten-point swing costs about ten House seats and a rout costs forty. The aggregate constitutional record is also held to `RECORD_DRAG_CAP` (0.15) — five separately capped counts summed to 45 points of a vote, enough to put a popular administration under 20 percent, which is an erasure rather than an election. Drivers are scaled proportionally rather than truncated, so the list still adds to the share it is shown beside. The record also cannot take the President's party under `PARTY_VOTE_FLOOR` (0.415): the Republicans' 40.5% of all House votes in November 1974 (21,165,583 of 52,313,457, Clerk of the House), 41.5% of the two-party vote, is the worst any President's party has taken in the modern era, and a failing approval alone already lands near 43.

Measured on the US founding over four disjoint four-seed blocks: passive governments finish the term at a median 44–47 percent and are defeated in all sixteen contests; prudent ones finish at 55–57, are re-elected in all sixteen and serve out both terms, and *still* lose House seats at each midterm, which is the structural penalty doing what a midterm does. The prudent-over-passive margin is 0.087–0.111 (it was 0.158–0.188 when seeds founded under other charters), so the gate holds it above 0.08. The gate is a separate file because it costs about a minute — six of the ten charters hold no elections at all, so `electoralSeeds` founds each candidate seed and asks its charter rather than assuming.

### The press corps: standing as a lever

Coverage was prose and nothing else. Articles were composed from feed items, the mastheads were four names picked by a hash, and no matter what a government did — went to war without a vote, had two orders struck down, spent forty weeks without taking a question — the papers wrote in the same register forever.

`press.ts` makes it a lever on three counts. **Each masthead holds its own disposition**, in `press.dispositions`, and each drifts toward its own `outletTarget` — the shared drivers plus that paper's `lean` at `LEAN_WEIGHT` — at `PRESS_DRIFT` a week. A founding also places each desk by how it reads the President's party (`openingPressCorps`): that reading sums to zero across the roster, so the corps's standing, and the approval that reads it, stays the institutional figure. The target is **derived** from the administration's record every week rather than accumulated, for the reason removal pressure is derived: a corps warm to a government that has since lost three orders in court is not a memory, it is a stale number. The mastheads are the *same* ones `article.ts` prints — one press vocabulary, moved into `press.ts` and imported by the formatter, rather than a list of names in one place and a list of dispositions in another. And there are three plays with genuinely different prices rather than one lever at three sizes; only the two on-the-record ones stop the silence clock, which is what makes a leak a distinct decision instead of a cheaper press conference. Ordinary play authorizes through `issuePressAction` (one day to due); the corps movement and engagement land on `resolveDuePress`, the same issued-act contract as Hill lobbying.

**An outlet reads an act through a worldview, not a friendliness slider.** Every masthead carries `beliefs`, an `IdeologyVector` on the same three axes as `Person.ideology`, `Party.beliefs`, the cohort centroids and the player's `AlignmentVector` — outlets were the last actor in the game whose worldview was one number. `press-beliefs.ts` holds the mechanism: `presidentialActs` reads the window's acts off **both** `campaign.orders` and the `campaign.ledger` entries stamped `causal.origin === "decision"`, and `recentActPull` sums their pull. Reading orders alone was the press corps's central defect: the game's main loop is answering scenarios, and its substantive read walked a seven-item order catalogue — so an administration that answered a hundred crises and signed nothing read as having done nothing. A decision's pull comes from its `commitment` (instrument × rung) and its declared `institutional` costs, which are catalogued and authored facts, never prose; its weight (`DECISION_ACT_WEIGHT`, 0.08) sits below the smallest order's authored alignment. The window is `PRESS_ACT_MEMORY_TURNS` (16), and `outletReading` folds that pull against a masthead's beliefs as the same dot product `partyReading` uses on a platform and `cohortAffinity` uses on a cohort — one geometry, not a second one for the press. `outletTarget` is now the shared drivers, plus `lean × LEAN_WEIGHT`, plus that reading at `PRESS_BELIEF_WEIGHT` (0.4). Two consequences are load-bearing. Statutory price ceilings *delight* the adversarial daily and *enrage* the sympathetic weekly — the same act, opposite signed reactions, landing on the per-outlet dispositions — while a struck-down order still stings all four desks, because the six shared drivers are untouched. And the authored vectors sum to exactly zero on every axis, so the four readings cancel and the mean the coalitions and `opinionTargets` read is still `pressStandingTarget`: the substance rearranges the corps without moving it. `lean` is no longer authored — it is `beliefs.authLibertarian`, assigned by the one constructor the outlet table is built through, because a paper's standing posture toward *any* president is what it thinks of executive authority. An administration that has signed nothing alignment-bearing reads exactly zero, and the room prints no line rather than a manufactured one; nothing is ever inferred from prose.

**`press.standing` is an aggregate, not a figure of its own.** It is the evenly-weighted mean of the eighteen dispositions, and `corpsFrom` is its only writer, so the number the judgments read (`coverageCause`) and the lobbying read can never disagree with the outlets' own rows. Each outlet's disposition is its own figure, so one paper can move without the others. Two of the three plays now name a desk (`PressPlay.targeting`): an interview is with one reporter and a leak is placed on one desk, both landing in full there and at the play's own `spillover` share everywhere else; a press conference is `targeting: "corps"` because there is no honest way to hold one with a single paper. The leak's asymmetry is the sharpest of these — a scoop is worth almost nothing to the papers that did not get it (0.15), and sourcing traced back to the administration is worth all of it to every paper alive (1.0). `takePressAction` **refuses** a desk-targeted play with no desk rather than choosing one, since picking it would be the engine answering the question it is asking.

**Desks file, and remember how they felt.** A feed item is filed at a desk by relevance (a home condition with a dateline goes to its region's paper; anything else to a national desk covering its kind, weighted by √reach; never by hostility), no desk carrying more than `WEEK_SHARE_CAP` (0.4) of a week's filings; the desk's stance and a generated reporter (three per desk, drawn through `drawName`, never stored) are stamped on the item, so a dispatch reread months later reads in the voice it was filed in. **The President's week reaches the wire:** `pressPhase` files up to `WEEK_FILINGS_CAP` (3) public acts a week (`press-week.ts`), and every press engagement files its own `player-action` story naming what the room asked about (`podiumSubject`). **The press secretary** delivers part of the plays (`podiumSkill`: 0 interview, 0.35 conference, 1 leak) and can take one bad cycle for the President (`secretaryTakesTheHit`: 60% back, then briefs at half); nothing moves week to week because of who holds the post. `pressReading` is what content gates on (standing, silence weeks, hostile share, one desk).

The load-bearing constraint is where standing goes, and it is a constraint on *how* rather than on *whether*. Coverage writes no metric anywhere: `player.metrics.approval` still has exactly one writer (`recomputeApproval`), and nothing in `press.ts` can set a figure on the panel. What the corps's standing reaches is what the country *believes*, by two routes that are both readings of the public rather than second copies of it.

**A framing is an argument about an act, and the record can catch it.** `press-framing.ts` lets a president stand at the podium and say what the week's act *was*: `DECISION_FRAMINGS` holds four — resolve, restraint, necessity, regret — each with a small authored `pull` on the same three axes the outlets believe on. `outletFramingReading` folds each framed act's pull against a masthead's beliefs through `outletReading`, exactly as `recentActPull` is folded, and `outletTarget` adds it at `FRAMING_WEIGHT` (0.15) — deliberately below `PRESS_BELIEF_WEIGHT` (0.4), because an administration that could talk its way to the same place signing nothing would make the record decorative. The framing writes **only** that term: `frameDecision` emits one `orderFraming` effect and one feed item, so no metric, cost, outcome or quoted figure moves, and what happened is the same record framed or unframed. It lands on the act's own ledger entry (`ExecutiveOrderRecord.framing`, schema v42, written once and never rewritten), which is what makes the framing itself part of the record rather than a mood: the order's document prints what the podium said it was beside what it did.

**The one contradiction class, priced worse than silence.** `framingContradicts` folds the framing's pull against the *order's own authored alignment* and is true when the two point opposite beyond `FRAMING_CONTRADICTION_THRESHOLD` — calling emergency construction money a matter of restraint is the case it exists for. Detection is mechanical and derived, so nothing is remembered and no flag can go stale. The framing works for `FRAMING_SCRUTINY_TURNS` (1) week; then the desks have read the text, `framingCaughtItems` files one `press` item (no new `FeedItem` kind), and the reading inverts: a caught framing contributes `min(read, 0) − max(read, 0) × FRAMING_BACKLASH (1.75) − FRAMING_CREDIBILITY_HIT (0.25)`, which is strictly below both the framed and the unframed reading at *every* desk, and worst at the desks that bought the line. `projectFraming` discloses the contradiction before the podium rather than after it: a president knows whether the sentence is true, and the decision worth having is saying it anyway.

**A scandal has a life, in `press-scandal.ts`.** `integrity`'s scandal term used to be a bare count: `player.flags` starting `scandal:`, charged a flat 0.25 apiece for as long as the flag stood — a mechanism no content ever authored, so in the game as played it never fired. The live channel is a leak that goes badly: `press.ts` already records every `PressEngagement`, and a failed `leak` is, in its own words, "the one that can come back with the administration's name on it." `selectScandals` turns each into a `Scandal` — subject, origin, a `stage` (`breaking → developing → fading → resolved`, or straight to `resolved` if the corps never picks it up), and an `intensity` 0..1 — computed fresh every week from how old it is and how the corps currently reads the administration, never walked forward from a stored figure: the same "derived, not accumulated" choice `pressDrivers` makes, and for the same structural reason — a `Scandal` that genuinely accumulated would need its own `Effect` kind wired through `effects.ts`, which this pass did not touch. Momentum is the corps's, not an invented hostility scalar: `outletDisposition` and `outletActReading`, the same readings `outletTarget` already folds, read as the press climate (`pressClimate`): half the most hostile national desk and half the room's mean hostility. A story has legs only while its charge's gravity times that climate clears `SCANDAL_LEGS_BAR` (0.25); otherwise it dies within its first week. One partisan desk is not a climate, so a thin charge before a mostly friendly corps fizzles and a grave one before a broadly hostile corps runs. The climate decides whether a story gets its legs, the week after it broke, not whether a running story keeps them: `scandalPhase` marks a story that reaches `developing` with `scandal-legs:<id>` (bookkeeping, off the Record), and a marked story runs its course whatever the room does after. Measured over 52 weeks, three seeds and the three bracket proxies, the share of scandals with legs fell from 1.00 for every party to 0.79 (Democrat), 0.93 (Republican) and 0.78 (Independent); cabinet leaks mostly fizzle and failed covert operations mostly run. A story with legs grows for up to `SCANDAL_DEVELOP_TURNS` (6) weeks toward intensity 1, then decays at `SCANDAL_FADE_STEP` a week until it resolves. `selectScandalIntegrityCharge` sums live intensity × `SCANDAL_INTEGRITY_WEIGHT` (0.25) — the same weight and the same ceiling per scandal the old flat count always charged, so a campaign with only legacy flagged scandals reads `opinionTargets().integrity` byte-identical to before; what changes is that a leak-born scandal can now cost less than the old flat charge, or nothing once it is over. **A scandal has three origins, and none of them is opt-in.** The leak that goes badly was the only live one, which meant the sole route to a scandal in the shipped game was the player choosing to leak and losing the roll — a president who never reached for the press could not be embarrassed by anything they did. The second is `own-act`, read off the ledger: `emergency-powers-invoked`, `intelligence-source-burned`, or a covert commitment that did not land. The break week is the entry's own turn and the act's sentence is carried as `Scandal.context`, so the story and the week that caused it are one fact. The third, `flagged`, now lifecycles from the `flagset:<flag>:<turn>` marker every authored flag already carries, instead of standing at full weight forever. The charge set is deliberately narrow: `legal-authority-spent` is on 189 of 914 replies — the ordinary price of a hard decision — and admitting it would keep roughly three stories permanently live and pin `integrity` at its floor for the whole term. The two rare charges plus a failed covert operation give about three scandals a term.

**What a story is, who it names, how grave it is, and the President's answer.** A story's identity comes from `scandal-origins.ts`, with five origins: the bad leak, the President's own act, a cabinet leak (a world ledger entry whose provenance carries `systems` "cabinet" and "leak" and the leaker among its `entities`; one in three becomes a story, `CABINET_LEAK_STORY_SHARE`, drawn deterministically off the seed and event id), an inspector general's report (a received IG matter the week it reports and goes public), and the authored flag.

The own-act charges are `emergency-powers-invoked`, `intelligence-source-burned`, `conduct-in-question`, and a covert commitment that did not land. Each story carries a `charge` and a `gravity` (`SCANDAL_GRAVITY`: conduct in question, a failed covert operation and an authored flag 1; a burned source 0.8; emergency powers and a leak traced to the building 0.7; an IG finding 0.6; a cabinet leak 0.5). `Scandal.intensity` is heat × gravity, so every existing reader is scaled with no second integrity term, and a legacy flagged story costs exactly what it did.

A story names an officer (`Scandal.implicated`): the chair whose counsel the act took, or else the chief of the department that carried the charge out (DOJ for emergency powers and conduct in question, ODNI for a burned source and a failed covert operation); the leaker for a cabinet leak; the department chief for an IG report. Nothing about a story is stored. Ids are unchanged, and `scandal:leak:<eventId>` and `scandal:ig:<matterId>` are new.

`scandal-response.ts` has three verbs: come clean, fight it, give them someone. A hidden evidence strength (a hash of the seed and the story id), shifted by the charge, decides which fits; for every charge each verb fits on some seed. The only stored fact is the answer, `press.scandalResponses[<id>]` (`{ verb, turn, acting? }`, written by the answer's `press` effect; `acting` is who was left running the department when an officer was handed over); `scandalResponseOf` reads it, and falls back to the dated flag `scandal-response:<id>:<verb>` a save kept before the field. An answer that fits crests the story the week it is given; a middling one changes nothing; the worst adds `SCANDAL_WRONG_ANSWER_LEGS` (4) weeks of legs. The answer also moves integrity at once (−0.02 / −0.05 / −0.09).

"Give them someone" dismisses the named officer through `dismissMinister`, which leaves through `vacates` and seats the chair's deputy acting; where the dismissal leaves nobody acting, the next eligible person who is not the officer handed over is named through `appointActing`. It is refused when the story names no seated officer. The page shows counsel's read of the file (`scandalCounselRead`, right three times in five), not which answer fits.

`scandalPhase` raises `scandal:live` (dated) the week the first story stands and lowers it the week none does, in a background packet. It also dates, once, every authored `scandal:` flag that arrived without a `flagset:` marker (`stampPacket`, background), reading the board as it will stand once the stamps land, so the live flag agrees the same week. `Scandal.brokeTurn` is never -1 and `ScandalReading.weeksRunning` is always present.

`desk/attention-scandal.ts` raises one attention row per live story. The row is soft only in the week a dated story broke and before it is answered, and ambient after that. An undated story is never soft. With `scandal` in `PAUSE_BY_DEFAULT`, that is one pause per story, on arrival.

The story that keeps a scandal's legs is read off the national desks only (`leadingDesk` over `NATIONAL_OUTLETS`): a regional paper files only what happens in its region.

`selectScandalForInquiry` is the exported handoff for `committee-inquiry.ts`'s `nextCause`, and **it is wired**. Until it was, the two halves ran past each other: the press could keep a story alive for six weeks while every committee behaved as though nothing had been printed, so a scandal cost approval and never once cost the President a hearing — which is backwards, since most real inquiries begin with a reporter rather than a subpoena. Only the loudest live story, and only one file at a time; which gavel takes it follows the charge, with a burned source or a failed covert operation going to intelligence and everything else to oversight.

**Press standing reaches approval by one route, `opinionTargets` in `opinion-causes.ts`.** A second route through the coalitions (`coverageDriver`, weighted on the coalition that read the papers most) is deleted with them. The surviving route is the one that reaches what decides an ending: a decomposition nobody writes back meant an administration covered for a full term as a scandal polled exactly like one covered as a success, and removal, which reads approval, could not tell them apart. `(press.standing − 0.5) × PRESS_COVERAGE_WEIGHT` (0.5) is clamped to ±`PRESS_COVERAGE_CAP` (0.15) and added to the `integrity` and `competence` targets — reading the aggregate rather than any single desk, which is the semantics per-outlet state was built to preserve: the country hears the corps, not one masthead — those two only, so coverage cannot touch the money, the army or the order at home. Because it is a *target*, it arrives at the speed a public revises an impression (`OPINION_DRIFT`), never as arithmetic billed the week the coverage turned.

**The cap is what keeps this from being a second opinion of the country.** Saturated in either direction — a corps as hostile or as friendly as the model allows, held there long enough for both judgments to drift the whole way — the edge was worth ±3.3 points of approval under the earlier compressed headline, at an opening polarization of 0.3, and has not been re-measured on the bloc headline. That is a term of hostile coverage costing what a term of hostile coverage costs: real enough to govern around, smaller than one live scandal (a quarter of `integrity` on its own), and far short of anything that ends a presidency by itself. The corps' own weekly drift now reads the headline in return (`press.ts`: `(approval − 0.5) × 0.4`, capped), which closes a loop through the judgments' slow revision and the 0.15 cap, not through a direct write.

**The plays are priced on the country, not on the state.** A backfired press conference used to cost six points of `standing` and two of `stability` — it moved the world's reading of the United States by more than a lost war does in a month, and shook order at home, for one bad hour at a podium. What is billed to the state is now a token (nothing above 0.027), and the weight moved to `PressPlay.moodBackfire`: a botched leak marks the country down on `integrity` and `competence`, a botched press conference on `competence`, and an interview is too small to reach the country at all. These are emitted as an ordinary `opinion` effect through `apply()`, so they are damped by polarization and drift back like every other judgment instead of standing as a permanent mark on the state.

### Treaties: the two-thirds the office cannot win by winning an election

Congress arrived with two arithmetics that are the same arithmetic: a simple majority confirms a Secretary and a simple majority appropriates the money. `treaties.ts` adds the one that is not. Sixty-seven senators is a bar an administration with a comfortable majority still fails, which makes it the only constraint on the board a *popular* president feels — and it is what decides what the top of the diplomatic ladder is actually worth, since a summit produces a document and the document binds nobody until the Senate says so.

It composes rather than duplicates: `countVote` against `supermajorityOf(100)`, the same call and the same bar the veto override already uses, and the compact term is read off `blocsInPlay` rather than a second membership model. `signTreaty` is deliberately not gated on the count — a president signing what the Hill will refuse is one of the most characteristic acts of the office, and refusing the signature would model a presidency that only ever promises what it can already deliver. Signing writes nothing into the relationship; ratification writes the instrument in both directions, and that separation is the entire point of the Senate's role in it.

**The surface splits the same way the constitution does.** All three verbs were exported and uncalled for a period, so a president could not make a treaty at all. Signing is now on the nation dossier (`NationCommand` via `PlayBoard` + `signTreaty`), beside the government it would be signed with. Ordinary play reads likelihood and confidence from `projectRatification` rather than the exact yeas; the engine still uses `countVote` internally. Ratifying and withdrawing sit with the treaty (DESIGN.md, `/world/treaty/:id`), because the Senate is the only party to a treaty that can say no. Sending it up is `issueRatification`: a three-day issued act (`kind: "treaty"`) freezes the forecast; `resolveDueTreaties` on the due date re-reads the live chamber so a Hill play between send and vote can still move the count, then writes ratified or rejected. `submitForRatification` remains the issue-then-resolve-at-due helper for tests. The plate never gates the signature on the count, for the reason above. A fourth kind, `executive-agreement`, skips the Senate altogether: `TreatyTerms.bindsOn` is `"signature"` rather than `"ratification"`, `signTreaty` writes the relationship movement immediately at a fraction of a ratified treaty's own effect, and it costs the President a smaller standing gain and a fall in `congress.disposition` for having gone around the chamber. `issueRatification` / `submitForRatification` refuse it outright — there is no vote to send it to — and `withdrawTreaty` now also accepts an in-force-by-signature record, unwinding what it did to the relationship, which is the one commitment on the board a president can end without anybody's permission; withdrawing a signature that is already before the Senate expires the outstanding issued act.

### Simulate: compacts, cohesion and coalitions

`blocs.ts` answers the last two things the dynamism audit said about the world's own politics: blocs were scenery, and nobody ever ganged up on anyone. A nation's `memberships` widened a band at founding and made an ally marginally likelier to join a war, and after that no compact could be strained and none could be shown to be hollow. Meanwhile a state could invade three neighbors in twenty turns while every relationship in the world moved toward or away from it and none of them moved toward *each other* — which is precisely the movement a coalition consists of.

A capital that stands with the feared government is not in the coalition against it: one aligned with it past the coalition ceiling (0.3), or sharing a compact with it that the feared government has not itself broken by pressing or fighting a fellow member. A coalition pulls its members' trust and alignment together only in the six weeks after it forms (`COALITION_PULL_WEEKS`, dated by the self-dated flag `coalition-since:<subject>:<turn>`); after that it stands without pulling, because closer ties are diplomacy priced elsewhere.

`compacts.ts` answers the half of that `blocs.ts` could not: what a compact obliges **the President**. Cohesion and coalition are both things that happen between other countries, so from the seat a mutual-defense guarantee and a summit club were still one fact — a line in a dossier and a slightly different opening board. The thing an alliance actually is, is a call: someone you are bound to is attacked, and you answer or you do not, and the ones watching draw a conclusion either way. `selectCompactCalls` derives that off the live board rather than storing it — a call exists while a member of a `kind: "defense"` compact the player also sits in is the defender in an active conflict — for the same reason removal pressure is derived: a stored copy would keep asking about a war that had ended. `CALL_GRACE_TURNS` is three, and each week of silence past it costs trust and alignment with **every** member of the compact rather than only the ally, which is the whole difference between a treaty and a favor. `answeredCall` reads the causal stamps `resolveAction` now writes: an act against the aggressor at rung two or above answers it, a rung-one statement does not, and an act against the wrong country is not an answer at all. A compact both belligerents sit in is a fracture rather than a call — `blocs.ts` already prices that, and asking the President to attack one member on behalf of another would be asking them to honor a treaty by breaking it. It reaches the desk as an attention item and content as a `compact-call:<org>` flag. `answeredCall(campaign, aggressor, since, by)` reads another member's answer the same way (an act at the answering rung its stamp names it taking, or fighting beside the ally), and `CompactCall.answeredBy` lists them. The call also runs the other way: in a war the United States fights on the defending side, `callCompact` calls a defense body it sits in, and each member answers in the war's week as above. `compactPhase` runs immediately after `blocPhase`, because both are read off the same two facts and the fracture/call split is only stable if one board decides both. **What an economic or political compact binds is still not modeled**, deliberately: a common external tariff and a shared position at a table are real obligations and are not this one, and writing them as the same mechanic would put a customs union and a defense guarantee back into a single number, which is what `Organization.kind` exists to prevent.

**Cohesion.** `fracturesIn` reads a compact whose own signatories are at war with each other or running an overt campaign past rung 2 against each other; a tariff dispute or a démarche between members is an ordinary quarrel and fractures nothing (world dynamics Wave 3: counted, it alone took 29 pairs below the hostile line on one idle seed). What that costs is deliberately *not* aimed at the offender — the ripple system already prices that, and charging it twice would double it. It is aimed at the bystanders' faith in each other: every remaining member-to-member bond in the bloc is pressed toward 0.12 below its anchor while the fracture runs (`FRACTURE_TRUST_DROP`, at `PRESSURE_BUILD_HALF_LIFE_DAYS`, never back past it; memory repairs it after), because the paper they are all holding has been demonstrated not to bind. A bloc needs three members before it has a bystander at all, and four before two bystanders have a bond between them to move.

**The betrayal is projection-visible.** A new `RippleRelation`, `bloc-partner`, is checked before every other relation and is the only one keyed to the *actor* rather than the target: a third party co-signed with both sides has not been alarmed, it has been informed. It carries the highest sensitivity in the table (0.12 against an ally's 0.08), so the player reads the cost of turning on their own alliance *before* committing rather than discovering it afterwards.

**Coalitions.** `alarmAt` folds a standing threat reading together with what the alarming state has actually been doing — but by **how far up the ladder it went**, not by how many files it has open. `severityOf` scores a pursuit's `rungReached` (rungs one and two are worth nothing, rung five is worth one) and an active war as one, because a démarche and a tariff schedule are how governments argue and a coalition does not form about an argument; a covert campaign is worth nothing at all, since one nobody can read is not something third parties can assemble over. A closed file fades over `RECORD_MEMORY_TURNS` (8) rather than vanishing the week it closes. The sum saturates rather than climbing linearly, because the fourth war does not frighten a capital twice as much as the second, it confirms what the second established; a nation with no record produces exactly zero alarm no matter how threatening it reads, which is what stops the map's starting threat values from assembling alliances on turn one. `alarmedBy` returns those over `ALARM_THRESHOLD` (0.45) **capped at `coalitionCap`** — `max(MIN_COALITION, 20% of the roster)`, twelve on the shipped sixty-one — keeping the capitals reading highest, which is the threshold rising with world-wide alarm: fifty-nine of sixty capitals is not a coalition forming, it is the rule saturating, and it was printed to the player as news four times in one presidency. Once `MIN_COALITION` capitals clear the bar for the same subject they move toward each other in alignment, **converging toward `COALITION_CEILING` (0.3)**. Shared alarm alone changes neither mutual trust nor trust toward the feared government: it is convergence of interest without a negotiated commitment or delivered cooperation. Actual cooperative acts keep their separate trust effects. No treaty is created by this phase. Uncapped it was the largest mover of the President's own diplomatic position across a presidency in which the President did nothing: +0.84 of alignment on idle edges, against 0.00 now. `coalition:<nation>` stands as a flag so the formation is reported once rather than every week it lasts, and is deliberately harder to form than to keep standing — it clears when **nobody** is alarmed any longer, not the week the set thins below the forming bar, because a coalition of seven that drops to two has lost members, and striking it off on that week is exactly what let its formation be re-announced a month later.

### Simulate: coercion propensity

`propensity.ts` gives the NPC agent the two questions a foreign ministry asks before it reaches for the economic ladder, neither of which it was asking: *do we have leverage over these people*, and *can we take the hit if they have leverage over us*. Without them a resource-poor importer running a trade deficit reached for an embargo exactly as readily as a surplus exporter its customers cannot replace.

The three propensities the plan called for — mercantilist, autarkic, sanction-prone — are **derived rather than authored**. Three new trait ranges across forty-nine nations would be three numbers a human had to invent per country, and the facts that decide this are already on the record: `mercantilist` is the trade surplus as a share of exports (a deficit reads zero, not negative — a country that buys more than it sells has nothing to withhold, not anti-leverage), `autarkic` is one minus energy import dependence and average trade dependence, and `sanctionProne` weights them 0.45/0.35 with 0.2 of opportunism, tilted toward what a state can *withhold* because leverage is what makes economic coercion work while self-sufficiency only makes it survivable.

`propensityFor` becomes an eighth `NpcScoreTerms` term (weight 0.9). Leverage **multiplies** rather than adds, so an appetite for sanctions against a country that would not notice scores zero; the actor's own exposure subtracts, scaled by how autarkic it is; and force gets the smaller negative half of the same fact, because a state does not lightly shoot the supplier it cannot do without. Diplomacy and covert work get nothing — neither is priced in goods. This gives `trade.balance` its first consumer, discharging a debt the registry had recorded as owed to "economic instruments", and adds `propensity` readers to `trade.exports`, `trade.tradeDependence` and `energy.importDependence`.

### Simulate: condition states

`conditions.ts` is what makes `geo.disasterExposure` mechanical: the six authored kinds had zero consumers, which the audit correctly read as depth the simulation did not have. A `NationCondition` opens from the nation's own exposure (a landlocked state never floods), or — for `unrest` — from its own politics, with an explicit war term, so a country at war is materially more likely to come apart behind the front. Scope (`city` / `region` / `nation`) scales every impact, so the same kind can be a story or a crisis. Each kind carries its own impact shape over regime stability, protest pressure, insurgency pressure, growth and reserves; the player's own metrics move when it is their country. Conditions escalate or fade on a seeded draw against a natural recovery floor, resolve when they burn out, and stand a `condition:<kind>:<nation>` flag while active — cleared on resolution — which is how authored content gates on them through the flag record that already exists. Onsets are drawn on two queues, not one. The world abroad is capped at two per week for the same reason `npcActionPhase` caps acting capitals — a week in which six foreign countries are struck at once reads as noise — and the country the player governs draws separately, from its own stream, against a budget of one. The split is the correction for a measured defect: on a single queue the home nation's candidate sat near the end of a ~112-entry list ordered by nation id, so the world's budget was already spent before the queue reached it in 22 percent of eligible weeks, and the home draw's position in the stream moved with how many other capitals happened to be eligible. That made unrest at home zero-sum against the health of fifty countries the player does not govern. When more candidates fire than a queue's budget allows, `claimOnsetSlots` (`condition-onset.ts`) opens the ones with the lowest roll over chance. Once a candidate has fired that ratio is an even draw whatever its chance, so the slots are shared evenly among everything that fired: a country prone to a condition opens more of it because it fires more often, and its place in the roster buys it nothing. Walking the list in nation order and stopping at the budget had given the world's two weekly slots to the start of the alphabet (debt crises opened in Andorra and Denmark and never in Lebanon or Venezuela, and the last quarter of the roster was struck by almost nothing). `economy-balance.test.ts` holds debt crises to the most prone governments and every quarter of the roster to at least 15% of foreign conditions, and `measure-npc-world.mjs` reports `conditionsByRosterQuarter`.

Both phases apply each packet before computing the next, because attrition and impacts are written as absolute stats: two wars sharing a belligerent, or two conditions in one country, must each read what the one before it left rather than a stale snapshot.

**Deploying against one: `response.ts`.** The thirteen kinds arrived, cost and passed, and nothing the President did with the bodies that exist to answer them changed their course. A `ResponseOperation` is one institution carrying one live condition at a `ResponsePosture`, and it is **derived, never stored** — the same choice `conditionFamily` made, and for the same reason: a derivation cannot drift from the records it reads. `RESPONSE_MEMBERS` names the deployable bodies per kind (FEMA, CDC/HHS, USDA, DOE, CISA, and the states); the five economic standing kinds have none, because no agency can be sent to a recession and giving them one would make a posture switch a substitute for the appropriation. Posture is not a dial the player sets — it is read off acts that already cost something: a declared emergency reaching the condition surges the response, a locale act answering that state sustains it, and otherwise the career staff are on it. `responseMitigation` returns a multiplier between `RESPONSE_FLOOR` (0.55) and 1 that scales a condition's weekly costs and the upward half of its severity draw; a best-run federal response still leaves 55% of the bill standing. **Law 8 holds through it by construction**: the multiplier only ever multiplies terms the `IMPACTS` table already writes, so it inherits that table's Law 8 proof rather than needing its own, and `standingHold` — the reading of the country that keeps a standing condition open — is deliberately not scaled. Effectiveness is `deriveImplementationQuality` on the lead body, unchanged, times posture effort, less exhaustion. Exhaustion is the body's total load against its own `staffScale`, which is where FEMA's authored 0.12 becomes a mechanic: two sustained nationwide disasters take its whole week, while HHS at 0.6 carries five. The states are a member of most responses and a subordinate of none — `statesConsent` requires either a governor whose disposition is at or above `GOVERNOR_CONSENT` or a declared emergency that reaches the place, and there is no third route. What accumulates is charged where the branch already accumulates: `responseLoads` converts open operations into the lead body's `backlog` and `morale`, emitted by `conditionPhase` (which runs before `institutionPhase`, so the branch's own week clears a backlog that already includes what the emergencies put on it). `deriveImplementationQuality` gains no term.

**The condition pipeline carries three families' worth of lifetime, not two.** A standing condition and an acute one that runs for weeks were the whole model; an **incident** is a third — one record, one store, one id scheme, distinguished only by `NationCondition.lifetime: "incident"` (`SCHEMA.md` §7b). An incident bills its whole cost on the week it arrives, resolves on the next tick charging nothing, and files exactly one feed item, tagged so `article-incidents.ts`'s `incidentArticle` writes it rather than the standing-condition prose (see the neutral-seams note above). Onset draws for the home country read the national record (`US_SIGNIFICANT_HAZARDS_PER_YEAR`, cut to `HOME_SIGNIFICANT_SHARE` 0.4 for the billion-dollar series) as a weekly hazard, scaling the player's own country only, and onset severity is `0.2 + 0.8 × u^(5/3)` off the same draw (mean 0.5, one in ten above 0.87), abroad too; `DISASTER_ONSET_RATE` (0.012) is unchanged for the other fifty. The home scope draw is city-weighted (city under 0.78, region under 0.95, against the world's 0.5 / 0.85), so raising the home count does not raise the bill by the same factor.

**A season is read off the week's own opening day, never the calendar as the phase happens to find it.** `weekOpenDate` (`feed-cap.ts`) answers what day a week opened on the same way whether the week ran across office days or closed in one tick, because the two layouts otherwise put the conditions phase in different months up to twelve times a year on the identical seed; `condition-onset.ts` imports it for that reason, though it belongs beside `dayOfWeekInOffice` in `clock.ts`. Seasons themselves are a table (`condition-seasons.ts`), normalized so twelve months of any seasonal kind average to 1 and flipped south of the equator (`monthInNorthernFrame`), so adding a season changes *when* a country is struck and never *how often*: hurricane June–November and tornado March–August (both read off the federal declaration record's own monthly peaks), winter storm November–March and heat wave June–September (inferred from the kind rather than measured, and the source comment says so).

**One source for hazard words, basin-aware for a tropical cyclone.** `condition-impacts.ts#hazardWords` is the only place a hazard's noun or headline label is chosen; `KIND_NOUN` and player-core's `CONDITION_NAMES` are gone, and `obligations.ts`'s `CLAIM_NOUN` derives from the same function. United States ground reads hurricane; the western Pacific reads typhoon; the Indian Ocean and the south Pacific read cyclone — the same storm under three names, because a service that called every basin's storm "cyclone" put a headline nobody in Miami or Manila would recognize over their own.

**The feed is bounded at two places, source and gate, because a busy world was crowding out the player's own week.** At the source, a condition abroad files a line when it opens and when it is over and not on the weeks it merely persists — a hundred foreign conditions filing a weekly bulletin was five hundred items in forty weeks and most of the feed. A condition at home still files every week, because the player governs there. At the gate, `applyFeed` (`feed-cap.ts`) is the one writer and it caps at `WEEKLY_FEED_CAP` (12 public items counted over the seven days from the week's open by `FeedItem.date`), digesting the surplus into `Campaign.feedDigest`. Never refused and still spending a slot: anything the player did, a condition *arriving* at home, and the structural kinds (`conflict`, `bloc`) — a war opening or a coalition forming is a single-digit event over two terms and must never be the item the cap drops. The world's background (`npc-action`, `operator`, `drift`) takes at most half the week; without that share the cap was a cap on whichever system ran first in the phase sequence, and none of nine coalition formations over two terms reached the player before this (`blocs.test.ts` caught it). A foreign condition's fold into one line per region per week happens first, so a fold never spends two slots. Measured on an idle 40-week run: 455 public items, 11.4 a week, against 739 before the cap and the source cut — a change to what the player is told, not to what the country is; `measure-npc-world.mjs` reads the feed and sees the same fold as a byte difference with no behavior change under it.

**A program pushing the wrong way earns exactly as little credit as no program at all.** `programAttention` (`conditions.ts`) now weights a chosen stance by the sign of its resting offset on the condition's own axis (`CONDITION_AXIS`, `condition-impacts.ts`, imported type-only from `policy.ts` to avoid closing a cycle): 1 if the stance pushes the condition's own cause the right way, 0.5 if it moves that cause not at all, 0 if it pushes the wrong way — never a negative number, so a government governing against its own crisis reads exactly as attentive as one that never chose a program. Measured on a recession: a growth-raising program recovers at 0.04 a week, a growth-lowering one at 0.02, an inherited program at 0.02 — a wrong-signed program halves the recovery rate it used to buy.

**An unrest onset at home now says why, without changing whether.** `condition-onset.ts`'s unrest path stamps `CausalProvenance.decision` with a `"unrest-onset"` reading naming the cohort this administration has hurt most relative to its axis (`weakestCohort`, read off displacement) and the heaviest harm it remembers (the `most-hurt-cohort` and `driving-act` terms) (`weigh("unrest-onset", terms, { threshold: 0, ... })`, threshold zero because the hazard has already fired — the terms explain the onset rather than deciding it). The onset **rate** itself is unchanged and still a hazard; the stamp explains an onset rather than deciding one.

### Simulate: the economy kept rather than derived — rates, books, credit and the debt limit

A week of an economy used to be read off its stats alone, so nothing about it had a history: no rate a debt was issued at, no rating an agency had moved, no ceiling a Congress had lifted. Three records now carry what has to be remembered (`SCHEMA.md` §7b: `campaign.macro`, `.books`, `.debtLimit`), each replaced whole by one weekly writer through its own effect kind, and opened at the oath and on loading an older save by `withEconomyRecords` (`economy-records.ts`).

**The rates (`macro.ts`, `macro-state.ts`, `macro-drivers.ts`, `external-pressure.ts`, `business-cycle.ts`).** `macroDriftPhase` is still the only writer of growth, inflation and unemployment (Law 8 below), and each still reverts toward one target, but the target is now the sum of named drivers composed once in `macro-drivers.ts`: the nation's own rest, the cycle, the standing program, the budget's impulse, the conditions it is living through, the world (wars, measures and partners abroad, which shift targets and never write levels), and the gap between output and its potential. The gap pulls growth back and reaches jobs through Okun's rule and prices through a Phillips term anchored on what the public expects inflation to be. Each nation keeps a `MacroState` (`{ potential, expectedInflation, policyRate }`), and its central bank moves its policy rate `POLICY_RATE_ADJUSTMENT` (0.06) of the way to its rule each week, floored at zero, the rule resting at the neutral real rate (1.24% for the United States, 1% elsewhere) plus expected inflation. `selectMacroDrivers(campaign, nation)` is the "what moved it" breakdown, so a screen reads the same sum the law used. **The cycle moves growth only.** Its offset has mean zero over the phases' expected spans (expansion +1, contraction −5, peak and trough 0, times an amplitude of 1 to 3.5 points), so over a whole cycle an economy grows at its own trend and the cycle only decides when; jobs and prices follow it through the gap it opens rather than through a push of their own. Each phase has a minimum dwell (104, 13, 26 and 13 weeks) and then a weekly exit hazard (0.008, 0.08, 0.05, 0.08), leaned on by the gap and the impulse (`CYCLE_HAZARD_LEVERAGE`), which gives expected spans of 229, 25, 46 and 25 weeks. Every nation draws its own place in the cycle the first week it runs, on the campaign's seed, and the played nation always draws an expansion part way through. **The three rate-kind conditions are labels.** `recession`, `inflation-shock` and `unemployment-shock` carry no `macroPressure` (and no condition carries an `approvalDrag` any longer: the field is deleted from `condition-impacts.ts`): they name what the rates are already doing, and what that costs the public reaches approval through the rates prosperity reads. What stays on them is what only the named state costs — order, the street and the reserves. `macro-balance.test.ts` holds the cycle's shape (H5, H6) and `measure-npc-world.mjs` reports it for the world. A signed United States tariff adds a temporary inflation-target shift (`projectTariffPrices`, `tariff-prices.ts`): its consumer-price step, household-weighted, spread across twenty-six weeks so the shifts sum to the step, then nothing. It does not write the inflation level. `closeWeek` appends the week's US `EconomySample` to `economyHistory`, capped at 104 (`ECONOMY_HISTORY_WINDOW`).

**Dated shocks (`external-shocks.ts`).** A development grant (a growth-target lift of the grant against the recipient's output, at most 2 points), a sabotage (−1 point of growth target) and a round of hiring or layoffs (an unemployment-target shift of the jobs against the labor force) are recorded as dated shocks held for 26 weeks and read by `externalMacroPressure` (growth) and `macroStep`'s unemployment drivers (`world`). None of them writes a rate: `macro.ts` stays the one writer of growth and unemployment, and a reading can say which shock moved a target and since when.

**The books (`national-books.ts`, `fiscal.ts`, `fiscal-impulse.ts`, `law-offsets.ts`, `fiscal-drivers.ts`, `discretionary-line.ts`, `pledge-outcomes.ts`).** `fiscalPhase` keeps a `NationBooks` for every government. The United States' statement collects revenue on the output the economy actually produces and writes outlays against the output it could produce, so a slump widens the deficit on its own (a point of gap moves receipts 1.3 points); both are nominal, pensions and health grow a tenth of a point a year faster than output, and the benefits a gap above the country's rest of unemployment forces are counted. A program moves the books through `FISCAL_PROGRAM_SHIFTS` and an enacted law through `LAW_OFFSETS`, each a share of output a year over its span, rather than through a level write to `economy.debt`. An enacted appropriation is a `LAW_OFFSETS` row on the discretionary line (the infrastructure act, 0.4% of output for five years), so it reaches the deficit, the impulse and the projection, never the President's authority; a row's `rest` is the law's supply-side effect, read by `lawRestingOffset` into `programRestingOffset`'s home branch for the law's span, so `macro.ts` stays the one writer of the rate. A statutory cap (the Fiscal Responsibility Act, the debt-limit deal's rider) and the crowding of the President's added stock squeeze the same line and are charged once, at the larger of the two, and the authority ceiling falls with the outlays. A funding lapse leaves 55% of the discretionary line unpaid each closed week (`LAPSE_DELAYED_SHARE`), keeps it as `books.lapseArrears` and pays it through `bookUsDebt` the first open week; the structural mix adds it back, so a lapse moves neither the projection nor the impulse. The tariff line is the FY2025 customs share of the take (3.72%) plus the live duties on the administration's own tariffs (`selectCustomsReceipts`), which add to revenue. The budget's push on demand (`impulse`) is the week's change in the structural budget weighted by kind (taxes 0.6, purchases 0.8, transfers 0.5) and fading over about half a year; a war's outlays and a crisis's forced ones never reach it. Public investment reaches demand as it is spent, not as it is authorized: `FISCAL_PROGRAM_SHIFTS` and `LAW_OFFSETS` mark the part of their outlays that builds (`investment`: the investment program's 1.2 points, the infrastructure act's 0.4), the books keep `investmentSpent` and move it `INVESTMENT_SPENDOUT` of the way to the authority each week (25% of a step in its first year, CBO's rate for the Federal-Aid Highway program; 44% by the second, 58% by the third), and `weekOfImpulse` counts only the spent part in the purchases the impulse reads. The outlays booked against the deficit, the debt and the projection are the authority's, unchanged. `projectFiscalImpulse` previews the first week's spending. `selectDeficitDrivers` splits the year's balance into inherited, cycle, program, laws, orders, world, interest and the limit, and the parts sum to the balance exactly (`orders` is the customs the President's tariffs collect; `limit` what the Treasury holds back at the limit or through a lapse). `projectFiscalCost` prices a draft by applying its effects to a copy of the campaign and reading the books before and after, so a program, a law's offset, a war's outlays and a direct write to the debt all price; `projectFiscalImpulse` reads the push a program would give demand. A fiscal price row states them before the President commits, in words ("Widens $X a year", "Narrows"), with the push on demand and its fade, and says the interest comes out of budget authority only where it does (a program, and a law the President signs; never a war's outlays or an order's own borrowing). A rung whose only charge is budget authority reads as paid from authority Congress already appropriated and adds nothing to the debt. It prices a policy stance, a bill (`enactmentEffects` prices the bill as law through `LAW_OFFSETS`, over the years the statute runs), joining a war, and a decision reply whose `impact.financial` borrows (debt and yearly interest before the choice; every other impact band stays hidden until the Record). The compare-and-authorize pattern in DESIGN.md is its planned reader. That borrowing is booked as `choiceBorrowing`, an amount added to the live stock when the reply is answered, so its interest crowds the discretionary line the way a program's does. Two decisions reach the books without a fiscal price: an order's authored `economy.debt` delta, and an instrument rung's charge to budget authority (it prices to nothing on the national books). A foreign government's books are kept in ratio space, opened on `FISCAL_ANCHORS` where the World Bank publishes a figure and on the engine's labeled rule for its letter and wealth where it does not. Each foreign book carries one rate, the published interest over its gross debt, as both its effective rate and its market anchor, so a government rated B or worse can borrow for less than the United States, and the stationary balance that is meant to hold a ratio still lets one drift under an inflation anchor far above that rate: `economy-balance.test.ts`'s H4 holds Argentina's ratio within a tenth of itself through the first year (200% inflation) and does not yet pass (it moves about a fifth).

**Credit (`sovereign.ts`).** Interest is priced, not multiplied: the stock carries an effective rate that rolls a slice at a time toward what the market asks today (for the United States in two tranches: bills turn over about four times a year at the policy rate, and notes and bonds, 7.4 years of remaining maturity, retire about one-fifteenth a year into the ten-year market rate), and the market moves from its opening yield with the projected debt ratio, the policy rate, expected inflation and the rating's spread per notch (`ratingSpread`), measured against where each stood when the books were opened. The United States' ten-year opens at the Treasury par curve's ten-year on the world's as-of date (5.11%, 2026-09-23): the world is pinned to that date and a President inherits the market's rate. The projected ratio is priced at the effective rate the stock will average over the decade as it rolls over (`usDecadeEffectiveRate`), so repricing the market already expects is not read, week by week, as news about the debt. The rating moves by notches, on a date, at a review (`REVIEW_WEEKS` 26), behind an outlook that turns first, and against fundamentals measured from where they stood when the books were opened: real interest as a share of revenue, the ratio ten years out on the structural balance, a notch for a brush with default at the limit, and a live crisis. A fiscal crisis is a failure of market access (a weak letter and a history of stress), not a level: Japan at 2.5 of output in its own currency is not more crisis-prone than Argentina at 0.85 in dollars. `selectSovereign` reads the letter, the notch, the outlook and the rate.

**The debt limit (`debt-limit.ts`).** It counts debt held by the public and what the government owes its own trust funds. When it binds, the Treasury runs its extraordinary measures; when they run out, the X-date, it pays the interest first and withholds whatever else the week's receipts cannot cover, and the withheld outlays are owed when the limit is lifted. Congress puts the lift on the floor a quarter before the room runs out (`headroomWeeks` at 13 or under), and a brush with default is counted against the rating for a while after. Once the limit binds, either debt-limit bill is counted as the stopgap is (`countFundingContinuityVote`, `legislation.ts#continuityVote`): members answer to default rather than to the President who sent it, so a President with no caucus of their own is lifted before the X-date like any other. The United States opens no `fiscal-crisis` while it holds AA or better and its market has not turned (`condition-onset.ts#usMarketStrained`): the onset needs a letter below AA, a stress history, the X-date, or a ten-year a point above the oath's. The United States' stock is debt held by the public, CBO's 101% of output (`SCHEMA.md` §4).

**The Record keeps the world's bookkeeping off the page.** Every weekly phase that writes accruals, apportionments or drift marks its own packet `background` (`budgetPhase`, `capacityPhase`, `opinionPhase`, `macroDriftPhase`, `fiscalPhase`'s accrual and `orderSettling`); `apply()` copies the mark onto the entry, and `foregroundLedger` (`player-core`) leaves those entries off the Record. A rating action, an X-date or a prioritization week from the same phase carries no mark and stays. The mark changes nothing the simulation computes and no causal id.

### Simulate: the country's order settles, and a department answers for its own

**`player.metrics.stability` has a restoring force.** Every writer of the President's reading of order pushed it one way or the other and none pulled it back, so a program's weekly cost, a season of continuing resolutions and a cabinet revolt summed without limit: careful government ended every year at zero and a program with a gift in it at one. `orderSettling` (`conditions.ts`, run in `conditionPhase` and filed in the background) now pulls the reading `STABILITY_REVERSION` (0.1) of the gap a week toward the order the country's institutions hold — its own authored regime stability on the founding's scale, which is where the regime itself reverts (`macro.ts`'s `nextPolitics`), not the regime as it stands this week, which conditions knock down faster than it recovers. A shock still lands where its writer puts it and fades as the country settles; a program's weekly stability becomes a held level, ten times its weekly figure above or below where the country would otherwise rest. The catalogue is authored for that: each stance's `perTurn.stability` is a tenth of the level it holds, so a program holds order about a point from its rest (emergency powers two), and what makes one cost more is its `enactment`, the disorder of standing it up, which fades as the country settles. Written as weekly rates before anything pulled order back, the same figures had held careful government's program some twenty points under the rest and a gift of investment ten over, which made borrowing the dominant program. No fiscal stance buys order or standing: what a budget does is its books (`FISCAL_PROGRAM_SHIFTS`, the impulse, and the interest that crowds the President's authority). `balance.test.ts`'s held-alone table (`HELD_ALONE=1`) holds every stance's order under 0.9 and every fiscal stance's within 0.05 of idle, and `economy-balance.test.ts`'s R1.1 holds investment within two points of careful play on approval at the end of a term, for every party. `stability-attribution.ts` books which stretch of a harness week moved stability and the treasury, falls and rises apart, so a phase that drains order shows in a week where the net reads flat. The nation's `politics.regimeStability` keeps its own reversion; the two are still not reconciled after founding.

**`player.metrics.standing` settles too.** Standing had the same defect: every writer pushed it and none pulled it back, so a term's slights and favors summed without limit and a reading could sit at a bound for years. `standingSettling` (`standing-settling.ts`, run beside `orderSettling` in `conditionPhase` and filed in the background) pulls it `STANDING_REVERSION` (0.02) of the gap a week toward the reach a power of the country's weight usually buys (`openingExpectations`, the same resting point the opening position measures against). Slower than order's 0.1 because a capital's view of a government is a record of dealings, and a good year of diplomacy should outlast a season.

**A department answers for its own business** (`department-ownership.ts`). One `DepartmentOwnership` per chair — the instruments it wields, the program domains it delivers, the scenario classes its secretary is asked about and the budget accounts it spends — written out for the United States rather than matched by hint, because a hint right for one constitution is wrong for another ("interior" is a home ministry in a parliamentary republic and a lands department in Washington), and because matching re-routed a department's business to whichever seat matched next the week its chair fell vacant. A charter nobody has written a table for keeps its hints. `ownershipOf(campaign, role)`, `policyDomainDepartment`, `scenarioClassDepartments` and `unclassedDepartment` are the readers; counsel (`counsel.ts`), the program's chair (`policy.ts`'s `resolveDomainChair`), the scorecard (`player-core`'s `departmentScorecard`) and the content audit's speaker rule all ask them.

### Simulate: the divergence probe

`divergence.ts` plus `divergence.test.ts` turn "no two campaigns feel the same" into a gate that fails the build. Two campaigns run from different seeds under an *identical* decision script — same charter, cradle, cabinet slots, same 12 turns — and `measureDivergence` scores four weighted facets: what actually happened (`events`, 0.4), `relationships` (0.25), `traits` (0.2), and the cast (`people`, 0.15).

Current figure: **0.96** (traits 1.0, people 1.0, relationships 0.97, events 0.92), against a bar of **0.6**. The bar sits far enough below today's figure to absorb ordinary content churn, and far above a world with a source removed. The probe runs in `pnpm run test`, so CI runs it, and it logs the figure rather than only passing — headroom is visible, not inferred.

The load-bearing test is the deliberate regression: strip the per-campaign trait roll so every nation wears its authored midpoint personality, and the traits facet drops to zero and the score falls. Without that check the figure would be decoration.

### Simulate: a world that moves on its own

Every engine seam a moving world needs is filled (below, "What fills the stubs"). The player-core readings for security, the world and the press abroad still answer neutrally.

**One temperament reader.** `temperament.ts` is where a foreign government's character is read. `liveTemperament(campaign, nation)` returns the runtime traits moved by the seated leader's `TemperamentOffset` (`Campaign.foreignPolitics.seats[nation].offset`, each axis clamped to 0..1); with no seat on file it returns the traits object itself, so a decision routed through it computes exactly what it computed reading the traits. `appetite(traits)` (0.6 × riskTolerance + 0.4 × aggression, clamped) and `inwardPull(traits)` (0.9 × isolationism) are the agent's own arithmetic, named once. `TEMPERAMENT_CENTER` is the median of the authored non-US governments (appetite 0.344, isolationism 0.37, legalism 0.50, reliability 0.51), derived at load and pinned by a test. `FIDELITY_WEIGHT` (full 1, standard 0.6, thin 0.25) moved here from `npc-turn.ts`, which keeps its old private name. `isWorldActor(campaign, nation)` is false for the President's own country, which enters a war only by an order.

**The decision vocabulary and the one "will" stream.** `willingness.ts`'s `Question` gains `join-war`, `answer-call`, `accept-treaty`, `accept-peace`, `org-vote`, `send-aid`, `world-event`, and three hazards stamped when they fire (`coup-hazard`, `operator-hazard`, `default-hazard`); `TermKey` gains the terms they weigh (obligation, reliability, bond, enmity, stakes, the defender's and aggressor's situation, regime danger, wars fought, appetite, isolationism, legalism, a call raised, trust in the United States, exhaustion, lost support, losing, vengefulness, need, reach, regime survival, rivals' ambition, destabilized, host pressure, debt burden, dyad kind, border, grievance), each with a phrase in `player-core/reasons/reason-lines.ts`. `willDraw(streams, question, ...subject)` is the only place the `"will"` stream is drawn; `governors.ts` and `minister-will.ts` draw through it with the same key, so no draw moved. `scripts/verify-willingness-route.mjs` (and its proof) holds five rules: every question has a builder that imports `weigh` and a test asserting its decision, or sits in `scripts/baselines/willingness-pending.json` with its owner; every `.stream("<key>"` call in an actor decision module is listed in `scripts/baselines/hazard-draws.json` with its kind and the terms that set its rate; only `willingness.ts` draws `"will"`; `"conflict-join"` appears nowhere (the join draw is retired); and no actor decision module reads riskTolerance, aggression, isolationism, legalism or reliability off the traits except a module on the pending list. `--closing` requires both pending lists empty and every hazard entry `live`.
