What the baseline moves is everything that reads a cohort's level: the crosstabs, the opinion record, the groups a workbench projection reports, the state-by-group table, and `turnout.ts`, whose bloc turnout is displaced by cohort harm through each cohort's composition (`blocTurnoutShift`, capped at `BLOC_TURNOUT_CAP` 0.04 per bloc), so the election's turnout driver is the ballot at actual turnout less the ballot at habitual turnout and can move where before every group held nearly one support.

**`demosPhase`.** Wired in `turn-phases.ts` after `opinionPhase` and before `removalPhase`. Cause-matched cancellation, then valence-split decay through `replaceStrains` (harms outlast favors: 0.06 vs 0.10; omissions 0.03), then `OpinionEffect.polarize` from cross-axis dispersion of centered strain under the joint `MAX_TURN_POLARIZE` ceiling (`COHORT_DISPERSION_POLARIZE`). Ordering pinned by test. A full recovery path (`CYNICISM` credit) isn't built.

**Routing.** `routeCohorts(campaign, signature, routed)` (`route-cohorts.ts`) runs beside `routeDomestic` off the same `DomesticSignature`, returns `CohortEffect`s (not packets). **Both domestic writers now call it.** `orderDomesticEffects` (`orders.ts`) and the legislative branch of `enactmentEffects` (`legislation.ts`) each routed to fifty states and to none of the sixteen cells the states are made of, so signing price controls moved a map and left every household in the country with no memory of it — the constituency model saw almost nothing a president did. It reads the state effects that actually landed rather than the signature alone, so the weighting follows who was moved. Affinity = exposure · folded axes + urban × placeTilt + federal × programReliance term + values · beliefs term; geography is the mass-weighted state share relative to the national share, weighted by `State.population` rather than by `|lean delta|` alone (uniform → ~1; concentrated industrial acts → >1 for goods-producing); weight below `COHORT_STRAIN_FLOOR` (0.004) emits nothing.

**Content schema.** `ScenarioImpact.human.cohorts` is additive-optional (`CohortId` from `@admin/world`, one to three). `cohortEffects` in `impact-model.ts` compiles one `CohortEffect` per named cohort beside unchanged `BORNE_BY_ROUTING` — cast splices them like `gazetteerEffects`. Cause is `domestic.signature` when present, else the scenario class; valence −1. The nineteenth condition kind `cohortStrain` gates on signed net strain. Audit rules in `scenario-audit-cohorts.ts`: `unnamed-group-toll`, `cohort-toll-implausible`, `dead-cohort-gate`.

**Content gates on the executive branch.** The twentieth condition kind, `institutionState`, and the `institutionStrain` `WeightBoost` source live in `packages/content/src/scenario-institutions.ts` — schema, reading, strain and the four audit rules in one module, on `scenario-topics.ts`'s precedent, because `scenario.ts` and `scenario-audit.ts` are both at their frozen size ceilings. It reads `campaign.institutions[institution]` (`delivery` = the derived `implementationQuality`, `politicization`, `backlog`, `vacantDays`) plus `selectOpenMatters`-style counting through the oversight store's exported `selectMattersFor` × `isMatterOpen`; nothing in `packages/engine` changed. The gate is evaluated in `scenario-cast.ts` beside every other condition kind, and the boost in `boostValue` beside `flagAge`. `openDocket` was specified and dropped: no per-institution docket store exists, and a gate that can read nothing is the dead content this whole slice is about. Rules: `institution-bounds-scale`, `unknown-institution`, `institution-vacancy-gate-unfounded`, `institution-politicization-unreachable`, plus `inert-boost` for a strain boost with no gate beside it. `underStrain` in `preview-board.ts` now hollows the branch so this content is reachable on the standard boards.

**Content gates on the oversight, funding and rulemaking machinery.** Four more condition kinds — `oversightMatter`, `shutdown`, `appropriation`, `pendingRule` — live in `packages/content/src/scenario-oversight.ts` with their readings and their audit rules, on `scenario-institutions.ts`'s precedent. They read `campaign.oversight` (via `selectOpenMatters`), `inShutdown` / `selectShutdown`, `campaign.congress.appropriation` and `campaign.pendingRules`, all through exported engine selectors; the only engine change is one line of `index.ts` re-exporting `rulemaking.js` so a preview board can run `rulemakingPhase`. The five executive-state kinds are assembled as `EXECUTIVE_STATE_CONDITIONS` and spread into `ScenarioCondition` on one line, evaluated through one `executiveStateMet` dispatcher in `scenario-cast.ts`, and audited through one `auditExecutiveStateGates` whose findings are named once as `ExecutiveFindingKind` — because `scenario.ts` and `scenario-audit.ts` were both at their frozen ceilings when it landed and every addition had to be paid for line for line (`scenario-audit.ts` has since shed its impact family to `scenario-audit-impact.ts` and sits at a ceiling of 1942). Deliberately absent, each because the state cannot be reached rather than because it was forgotten: matter `direction` (nothing opens a `directed` matter), stage `closed` (the census counts open matters), and `Appropriation.priorYear` (no first-year board produces a partly-carried book). New rules: `oversight-facets-contradict`, `oversight-count-unreachable`, `shutdown-gate-contradicts`, `pending-rule-count-unreachable`, plus `institution-open-matters-unreachable` — an `openMatters` gate above one, which the old plan had specified as a live cell and which the engine can never satisfy.

**Supplement preview boards (`preview-board-oversight.ts`).** `auditReachability` now measures a batch against the canonical spread *plus* whichever boards its own conditions demand, chosen by `supplementBoards(defs)`. The three are built by running the engine's own phases, never by writing state onto a campaign: the obstructed-audit week, the week the money stopped, and the rules in their window (details in `docs/SCHEMA.md`). This closes the failure mode the `openMatters` ruling described and did not prevent — a gate that parses clean, passes every bound, and is true on no board — by making the boards a function of the content rather than a thing a writer has to remember to pass in.

`STATE_CONSUMERS` records all twenty-nine `character.cohorts.<axis>.<cohort>` leaves as read by `route-cohorts.ts#cohortGeography`, `demos.ts#nationalCohortShares` and `state-cohort-opinion.ts#axisProblem`. `demography.growthRate` is read by the cohort age drift and `nationalCohortShares`, and is derived at assembly from the three vital rates beside it. `medianAge` and `literacy` are **owed**: they were once recorded as read by `macro.deathBaseline` and `macro.birthBaseline`, two functions that were never written, and nothing in the simulation reads either.

### 4e. The vitals lattice: the dataset half

The dataset carries the state every rate in the lattice is written against. The rate laws that move these numbers are in §4 above; birth and death rates are read by `vitalsPhase` but have no law of their own.

**The fields.** `NationStats.demography` carries `birthRate`, `deathRate` and `netMigration`, stored as fractions per person per year and rendered per 1,000; `growthRate` becomes their net, derived at assembly by `applyVitalRates`. `NationStats.economy` carries `costOfLiving`, an index at 1.000 for every nation at the snapshot, and `debt`, a nominal stock in USD millions. There is no stored `economy.debtToGdp`; it is the derived selector `debtToGdp(stats) = debt / max(1, gdp × costOfLiving)` in `packages/world/src/dataset/fiscal-identities.ts`. `State.character` carries a required `vitalAnchor` — three deviations from the national figure, population-weighted centered on zero. Four validator checks and a full set of `stat-consumers.ts` entries hold them; `docs/SCHEMA.md` §3z and §5i carry the field-level detail.

**The coupling graph.** Authored static facts (`wealthTier`, `literacy`, `medianAge`, `insurgencyPressure`, `corruptionIndex`, `borderControl`) feed `macroBaseline`, which also takes the business cycle's offset and each standing condition's `macroPressure`. `macroBaseline` rests each economy on its **authored record**, which the dataset holds to a published figure (`MACRO_ANCHORS`, vendored in `packages/world/src/dataset/seed/macro-anchors.tsv`: World Bank WDI 2020–2024 for 194 governments, the United States from BEA and BLS for 2024), shifted only by how far order has slipped since the founding (the wealth-and-order formula now read as a difference) and by the cycle's offset, so an idle economy stays where it was found instead of converging on a formula. The three economic rates take an additive weekly shock (`MACRO_SHOCK`, `macro.ts`); the political rates keep `MACRO_JITTER` and the same draw order. The rate conditions (recession, price shock, jobs shock, and housing's inflation reading) read the excess over the country's own rest, and hold from rest while opening past a line (`RATE_CONDITIONS` in `condition-onset.ts`, where onset moved out of `conditions.ts`). `crimeBaseline` reads joblessness against the higher of the country's own rate and five percent, and prices against `normalPriceLevel`, the path the country's own authored inflation would have taken. `macro.nextValue` is the only producer of a next rate value. Growth drives unemployment through an Okun term; inflation drives `costOfLiving`; unemployment and `costOfLiving` together drive `crimeIndex`; births and deaths drive the headcount. Beneath the rates sit the stocks, which are identities rather than rates: population compounds at `birthRate − deathRate + netMigration`, GDP at `growth`, `gdpPerCapita` is their quotient and never authored twice, and debt accrues the fiscal balance. The stocks then feed condition onset readings, the band derivation, and the vital edges that route cohort strain into turnout.

**Real versus nominal, stated once.** `economy.gdp` is real; receipts and outlays are nominal and multiply their GDP-share terms by `costOfLiving`; debt is a nominal stock. Inflation is therefore roughly fiscally neutral on the flow and erodes the real weight of the stock, so `debtToGdp` falls when prices rise. A president can inflate away the debt and pay for it in approval — a lever the game does not have today, and one that falls out of the identity rather than needing a term.

**The single-billing table.** Every cause, and the one channel it is billed through.

| Cause | Billed once at | Explicitly not billed at |
|---|---|---|
| A standing condition's macro cost | `macroPressure` → the target in `nextValue` | the level, in `conditionEffects` |
| A fiscal program's deficit | the take and outlay shifts → `balance` → `debt` | a direct `economy.debtToGdp` nation effect (the field is gone) |
| A routed domestic act | `routeDomestic` → `GrievanceMark` + `CohortStrain` | the state vital deviation it also causes (Law V3) |
| A national vital crossing a threshold | one `CohortStrain` on the edge | a strain per turn while the level holds |
| A tax holiday | `budgetPhase`'s forgone-receipts line | the granting reply's own treasury delta |
| Differential cohort participation | the turnout driver, zero under uniform turnout | the bloc ballot at habitual turnout, which carries preference and the turnout habit and nothing this administration caused |
| Inflation's fiscal effect | the `costOfLiving` deflator, on both revenue and outlay | a second erosion term anywhere |

**Why the stock and not the ratio.** A stored ratio invites a double bill: `fiscal-investment` lowers the take and raises discretionary outlays in `fiscal.ts`, which *is* its deficit, and a ratio delta beside it would state the same fact a second time with no ordering rule between them. Storing the stock removes the second statement. Engine readers (`governance.ts`, `operators.ts`, `fiscal.ts`, `sovereign-default.ts`, `condition-onset.ts` and the books) call the derived `debtToGdp` selector.

**`bands.wealthTier` reads the derived selector.** At `costOfLiving = 1.0`, `debt = debtToGdp × gdp`, and `debt-reproduces-ratio` pins that. How a band is derived is a Doc Sync surface in its own right, because a band is what all content targets.

### 4f. Party systems abroad

Every nation ships a `partySystem` (`docs/SCHEMA.md`) naming how power is
contested there and which tickets contest it. `packages/world/src/dataset/party-systems.ts`
authors the slates; `applyPartySystems` attaches them in the dataset pipeline.

Founding draws a generated politician's ticket from **their own country's**
slate (`engine/people.ts`), falling back to the charter only for the United
States, whose parties live on its charter because that is where the engine's
Congress reads the caucus anchor from. A country whose `contest` is `"none"`
generates politicians with no ticket, which is the correct outcome rather than
a gap.

Nothing on any surface may substitute one country's tickets for another's. A surface that prints a person's party looks it up on the person's own nation's slate first and falls back to that nation's charter only where the slate does not hold it (the United States' case). A foreign national with no ticket has no ticket, and the dossier prints no party rather than an American one.

### 4g. Where forces stand: zones, anchors and placement

Three tiers, all static data plus a derived reading; no position is stored.

1. **Command area** (player-core, live). `commandAreas(campaign)` (`packages/player-core/src/geo/force-placement.ts`) gives each geographic combatant command the nations `nationsInAor` assigns it today, `aorOverrides` included, plus the seas `seaCommand` gives it: the command holding most of a sea's `littoral`, the principal littoral breaking a tie, so a plan amendment that moves enough coast moves the sea. A functional command (strategic, special operations, transportation, cyber) has no area, and neither does space command, which nothing on the map is assigned to.
2. **Zone** (world). A nation's own territory under its nation id, or one of 13 `MARITIME_ZONES` (`packages/world/src/dataset/maritime-zones.ts`); `ZoneId` is the schema (`docs/SCHEMA.md` §5d).
3. **Anchor** (world). A sourced point per installation on `US_BASING`, or `zone-level` where a row is a country total, a rotation or a set of sites. Composite rows are split; `BASING_SPLIT_FROM` keeps the old labels readable.

**Placement.** `placeForces(campaign)` reads every active `basing` and `deployment` commitment. A basing commitment whose row is sourced draws at its anchor (`precision: "installation"`, labeled with the installation and its opened source). Everything else takes a seeded slot in its zone and is labeled with the zone (`precision: "zone"`): a zone-level row in its host; a deployment in its stored `location.theater`, or where it has none (a fixture, or a campaign never loaded through v56) in the zone `deploymentLocation` gives it: a nation's territory, or a war's defender's ground. The player and the engine place by one rule. Slots (`zoneSlots`, `geo/zone-slots.ts`) are up to 24 Poisson-disk points per zone, dart-thrown from a generator seeded by the zone id, on the nation's own land or, at sea, off every baked coast; a unit takes `hashIndex(campaign.seed + commitment.id)`, stepped past slots an earlier unit in the zone holds. A nation with no baked outline falls back to its recorded position. `personnel` is a sizing weight only: a band's midpoint, an even share of the host's country total for an installation "in the country total", or the order-of-battle count times the catalog's establishment. `clusterForces(campaign, placements, zoom)` groups by zoom: `world` one marker per command for the administration's forces and one per foreign government, `region` one per actor and zone, `close` one per placement.

**What reads it and what holds it.** The dataset validator's force-placement check (`validate/placement-checks.ts`) holds every sourced anchor inside its host's baked outline or within 10 km of it, and every sea to a simple, non-overlapping ring with open water. Outlines are read back from the same baked map geometry the board draws (`nationOutline`, `unprojectFromPlate`), so the check and the picture agree. `MARITIME_ZONE_CONSUMERS` and `INSTALLATION_ANCHOR_CONSUMERS` in `stat-consumers.ts` name force placement and the validator as the readers of every new field.

**The engine half.** `CommitmentLocation.theater` is a `ZoneId`. The board writes its two deployments into `SEA-persian-gulf` and `SEA-red-sea` and `selectBases` reports a deployment's theater by the zone's name. `openCommitment` and `joinerCommitment` write a location on every new deployment through `deploymentLocation` (`commitment-locations.ts`): the target government's zone, or for a war the defender's (the aggressor's for the defender's own forces). Save v56's `withTypedLocations` brings an older save onto the same terms: legacy theater strings through `THEATER_ZONES`, composite base labels through `BASING_SPLIT_FROM`, and a location on every placeable deployment, so `selectBases` reads an older save's installations as the current board does.

## 5. Content: the scenario system and delivery (bundled-only)

### Scenarios: the conditional crisis pool

A scenario is a decision in waiting: authored writing plus the conditions under which the live board makes it true. It is **data, never code** — a JSON document (`packages/content/src/scenario.ts`, zod) — because everything downstream depends on that: bulk generation, validation, storage, remote delivery, and a pool that can grow into five to six figures without a build.

| Piece | Where | What it owns |
|---|---|---|
| `ScenarioDef` / `ScenarioPack` | `content/src/scenario.ts` | The document shape: class (18-entry taxonomy from `tragedy` to `triumph`), counterpart cast, conditions, weight + urgency boosts, `once`, beats with role-hint speakers (`cabinet` / `player` / `narrator` — there is no counterpart speaker), an optional `transmission` carrying the counterpart's own words (with a `voice` of `state` or `nonstate` — a non-state voice is placed *in* the cast nation rather than credited to it, so an intercept from a militia is never captioned with the host government's name), replies with commitments, memory flags (`sets`), optional `press` headline, a required `locus` (a world-neutral place: `site` of `capital` / `city` / `coast` / `border` / `interior` / `chokepoint`, optional `ground` of `home` / `counterpart`, and an authored `descriptor` close-up — `ground` is best left unset, since `resolveLocus` already defaults a cast scenario onto the counterpart's geography and a `cast: "none"` scenario onto the player's own; `locus-ground-mismatch` refuses the authored override that contradicts its own descriptor), optional `particulars` (one to four `{ label, value }` wire-strip facts — a toll, a clock time, a crowd size — the one field family where figures are allowed: the audit runs every language rule on them except numeral-in-prose and the length judgments, whose bounds are the schema's `max(24)`/`max(36)`). |
| The audit | `content/src/scenario-audit.ts`, `content/src/audit-lexicon.ts`, `content/src/scenario-vocabulary.ts` | 48 rules across playability, stakes, prose quality, world neutrality, coherence, flag economy, commit-time framing, speakers and reachability; the impact family lives in `scenario-audit-impact.ts`, the time family in `scenario-audit-time.ts` (lexicon `audit-lexicon-time.ts`), the locus-and-place family in `scenario-audit-place.ts` and the speaker family in `scenario-audit-speakers.ts`, all re-exported unchanged. See "The audit rule family" below. |
| Casting + selection | `content/src/scenario-cast.ts` | Binds the counterpart from the board (`tensest` / `closest` / band match / declared set-piece nation / `warCounterpart`, the principal opposite the player in their hottest live war / `belligerent`, a principal of the hottest war the player is *not* in, optionally by `side` / `none` for trouble at home — the two war picks read the conflicts rather than the tension table, because a `tensest` cast beside a `warState` gate can pass on one war and bind the counterpart of another), evaluates conditions against live state, scales weight by urgency (tension, distrust, instability, removal pressure, condition severity, war intensity), draws seeded per `(seed, turn)`, renders `{{placeholders}}`, and resolves role hints against the actually-seated cabinet. |
| Locus resolution | `content/src/scenario-locus.ts` | Turns the authored `locus` into a real point on the cast nation's geography, pure in `(seed, scenario, turn, board)`: `capital` is the capital's own coordinates and name, `chokepoint` the nearest of the twelve real straits, `border` the seeded pick among the nation's real neighbors named as "A–B frontier", and `city` / `coast` / `interior` a non-capital dataset city of the site's kind drawn by population where the nation has one (56 of 195 foreign nations list any), otherwise a seeded offset named for the nation and carrying `precision: "area"` so the map draws an honest ring rather than false precision; never the capital. Sites degrade gracefully (a landlocked "coast" becomes a city, an island's "border" its coast). **A nation the dataset maps by state (the United States) places every `city` / `coast` / `interior` / `border` site, and a home `chokepoint` site as a `coast` one, inside one state instead:** the authored `locus.state` when there is one; else, for a later member of a running family or a scenario gated on an authored flag, the state the story's earlier scene happened in (`ScenarioFamilyRun.place`, then `Campaign.flagPlaces`; its city kept where it is of the site's kind); otherwise a state drawn on its own stream (`"locus-state"`, keyed on the seed and the scenario id, not the turn) over the states with a dataset city of the site's kind (`coast` a port; `interior` an interior or industrial city; `border` a border city; `city` any but the capital), weighted by fit (`content/src/scenario-locus-draw.ts`): √population × the state's mean share of the economy axes the scenario declares (`locus.sector` through `SECTOR_AXIS`; topics `energy`, `tech`, `war` → energy, tech, defense) × the number of placed disaster kinds whose `STATE_DISASTER_ELIGIBILITY` lists the state, for the `disaster` class or topic; a `conditionActive` gate on a placed disaster kind excludes every state that kind cannot strike, and is dropped if it would exclude them all. The square root is measured, not chosen: the candidate pools already drop most small states, and a draw in plain proportion to population put 16–22 of 104 unplaced scenarios in one state on each of six seeds, against a 9–12% population share; the root keeps the top state at 10–14. Nothing is read from prose; a fit the dataset cannot express (a frost, a fruit belt) is not inferred. The point is that state's largest city of the kind, `placeName` is the dataset name (`Houston, Texas`), `precision` stays `"area"`, `ResolvedLocus.city` names the city, and `ResolvedLocus.state` carries the state for the map, the reply's state-scoped effects and the decision's causal stamp (`ConversationScript.locales`) — gates and prose tokens read the authored `locus.state`, so a drawn state never decides whether a scenario is live or what it says. A family opener's place is pinned when its case is raised (`familyRaiseEffects`, an `unanswered` run), and its answer keeps it; a flag callback's place is written by `rememberReply` through the family effect (`callback`) at the answer, and only for a scene at home below the capital. A reply datelined in a home state also moves that state's governor by a capped `StateEffect.governorShift` (`scenario-governor.ts`). Washington stays Washington only where the scenario says so: a `capital` site (declared to be the seat of government itself — the White House, Congress, the courts, a department's headquarters), an authored `state: "DC"` or a DC city. An authored state with no dataset city (Delaware, Montana, Wyoming) stands at its capitol (`statePlace`). `scripts/verify-scenario-dc-fallback.mjs` (+ proof) resolves the bake and the packs on a sealed board and holds two per-scenario ratchet keys in `scripts/baselines/scenario-dc-fallback.json`: `dc-by-fallback:<id>` (a home scenario in or around DC without a Washington site; 83 before this resolver, 1 after it, 0 once a city-less state stood at its capitol) and `seeded-state:<id>` (a US place drawn by the seed because the scenario authors no city or state; 107, the content backfill's worklist). A scenario bound to a condition (`bind: "condition"`) is placed by `placeAt`, the dataset's one place resolver (see "Where an incident is" in §7a), at the condition's city or its state's largest city. `CastScenario.locus` carries the result, and the map-first camera frames a decision at its resolved place (DESIGN.md §1, Camera). Every border comes from real Natural Earth data, the player's own included. `WorldDataset.cities` is the gazetteer: ~200 US metros plus one capital per nation and a smaller honest extra set. US cities carry a `state`. `usaPlaces` is an offset view over US cities. When `locus.city` is set, `resolveLocus` pins that city's exact lat/lon/name (`precision: "exact"`), `ResolvedLocus.city` carries the CityId, and `placeName` is the dataset city (`Pittsburgh, Pennsylvania`). Without an id, `city` / `coast` / `interior` resolve as above: an authored, inherited or fit-weighted state at home; abroad a dataset city of the kind where the nation lists one, else a seeded area ring. `ScenarioLocus` may also carry optional `state` and `sector`. The audit `unplaced-domestic` is an **error**: domestic plant/factory/industrial-zone/tax-holiday copy without `locus.city`; strike/Midwest copy without city, state, or sector. `generic-place` refuses stand-in metros. Domestic economy with a bound city must write `{{city}}`. Foreign scenarios bind via cities, nations, sectors, and chokepoints — they must not grow US-state gates. Live overlays `campaign.states` and `campaign.sectors` mutate only through `StateEffect` / `SectorEffect` (and cities through `CityEffect`) via `apply()`. Save schema is 24 (v23 had cities; v24 adds states and sectors). The US constitution seats `kind: "judiciary"` ("the Federal Judiciary") and `role-attorney-general`; justice counsel routes to the Attorney General, not Interior. `Nation.sectors` is a closed 23 (no retail-wholesale); `trade.topExports` and `energy.mix` keys are `SectorId`s; mix sums to 1. The relief is Natural Earth `GRAY_50M_SR_W` (public domain, naturalearthdata.com), tonally matched to the near-black ground. It ships **reprojected into Natural Earth I** — 8192×4260, the projection's own 1000:520 plate — because the vectors drawn over it are Natural Earth I and the source is equirectangular: drawn flat, the terrain would sit up to 17 units of the 1000-unit plate north of its own coastline and 78 west of it near Alaska. `scripts/bake-map-terrain.mjs` (`pnpm bake:terrain`) does the warp — one source row per output row, since Natural Earth I's y depends only on latitude and its x is exactly linear in longitude at a fixed latitude — writing `assets/player/shaded-relief-ne1.jpg` from the unprojected `shaded-relief.jpg` kept beside it as the warp's input. `scripts/verify-map-registration.mjs` gates it on aspect ratio, which is a complete test: equirectangular is 2:1 and this plate is not. Whether the map-first renderer uses the plate is decided in the W1 map spike (DESIGN.md §1, Renderer). |
| Memory | `rememberReply()` | A committed answer stamps the fired flag (`once` gates on it), closes the desk for the rest of the turn (`desk:closed:<turn>` — one decision a month, which is also what spaces an arc out), the reply's own `sets` flags, and — where the reply carries `press` — a `press` feed item, so the papers react to the big answers. |
| The core pack | `content/src/packs/core.ts` | Pack version 3: 5 hand-written scenarios across 5 classes (war, unrest, hostage, economy, scandal), written deep rather than broad — every opening a full scene, every reply carrying real consequence, and one chain (paying the winter wages is what later puts the run on the banks). Version three is the first written against the presentation contract: every opening is pitched at the person being briefed rather than at a duty officer, and the two cast scenarios carry the counterpart's words as a `transmission` rather than as beats. They are the craft bar and the few-shot exemplars for generation, the fixtures that prove the loop, and the offline floor every install bundles — not a competing authoring model. Version two, the earlier 13-scenario version, and the 6-scenario pilot pack were retired with it. |

**What makes it dynamic.** Conditions read relationship state (the same nation brings a friend's quiet warning as a trusted partner and *the water they both claim* as a rival, within one campaign), counterpart personality, player metrics and accumulated alignment, flags earned by earlier answers (*the run on the banks* exists only because the winter wages were paid across a table), and the cabinet the player actually appointed: a `cabinetAttribute` condition (`role hint + attribute + bounds`, the same hint vocabulary a speaker names) reads whoever really holds that chair's `competence` / `loyalty` / `ambition` / `integrity` / `charisma`; a vacant or dead chair satisfies nothing. So what a player answers becomes who they are, who they *appointed* is part of what the board can even show, and who they are decides what arrives next.

### What a position reply reaches

A reply that commits an instrument goes down the coercive ladder and moves the world. A reply that takes a *position* moved the player's own metrics, pulled alignment, and left a line in the counterpart's history — and nothing else. So the peace scenarios shipped in the war corpus said the war was over while the `Conflict` object kept ticking attrition, and the aid scenarios said an exemption had been carved while the counterpart's reading of the player did not move a point. The prose was the only thing that changed.

The position commitment now carries two optional, schema-bounded fields, and nothing else in content can move the board:

- **`relationship`** — signed movement on the edge with the cast counterpart, limited to `trust`, `tension` and `militaryThreat`, each held to ±`POSITION_RELATIONSHIP_LIMIT` (0.15) by the zod schema *and* clamped again in the engine. The bound is the engine's own constant, imported by `content/src/scenario.ts` rather than restated, so content cannot drift past what a sentence is worth: the cheapest rung of the ladder moves a relationship about this far and costs a roll, a capability check and a public act to do it. `tradeDependence` and `alignment` are deliberately absent — neither is a thing a government can say into being. Both directed edges move, because the player's edge is what a `relationship` condition and every foreign screen read while the counterpart's edge is what decides whether it joins someone else's war.
- **`conflictMove`** — `offerTerms` / `acceptTerms` / `deescalate`, mirrored from the engine's `CONFLICT_MOVES` the same way `ScenarioInstrument` mirrors `InstrumentId`. `conversations.ts` resolves it against the live war between the player and the counterpart (`selectConflictBetween`) and translates it into an existing effect kind: a `flag` for an offer, a `conflict` for a settlement or a cooling. No new mutation path — `positionPacket` still returns one `EffectPacket` and `apply()` is still the only writer. The metric effect it carries is the authored `cost` realized on the live board by `realizePositionCost` (`position-assessment.ts`): the spent metrics — treasury, military readiness — pass through exact, and the assessed metrics — stability and standing — land as heavily as the administration's fragility makes a debit and as softly as the public's division makes a credit. The same packet carries the `PositionAssessment` the reply card printed (spent figures, assessed bands, confidence, basis), frozen so the Record shows what was assessed beside what was measured; `projectReply` quotes through the same function, so the card and the charge cannot come apart. With no live war the move is inert rather than fatal: the front ended between the desk opening and the answer being given.
- **`gazetteer`** — a closed recipe (`cityEmployment`, `taxBase`, `adminLean`, `taxHolidayYears`, `stateLean`, `sectorEmployment`, `sectorLaborPressure`), not the whole `Effect` union. `scenario-cast.ts` resolves it into one `CityEffect` per reply when `locus.city` is set (`taxHolidayTurns = years * TURNS_PER_YEAR`, including 0 to clear), plus `StateEffect` / `SectorEffect`, so a plant week can move employment, lean, holiday, and labor pressure through `apply()`.
- **`domestic`** — a zod mirror of the engine's `DomesticSignature` (`DomesticSignatureShape`), on both position and instrument commitments. Magnitude steps and route caps are imported from the engine. At cast, `domesticSignatureEffects` calls `routeDomestic` and splices the StateEffects into `resolved` beside any gazetteer effects (locus-specific vs systemic; a reply may carry both). Instrument commitments carry optional `resolved` so the same path reaches `commitReply` after the coercive ladder resolves.

Two things keep it honest. The audit's `ungrounded-conflict-move` (error) refuses a conflict move on a scenario that is not gated on the player being *in* a war — `warState` with role `any`, `aggressor` or `defender`; `none` gates on somebody else's front, and a domestic scenario has no counterpart to sign with — because a peace move in a week that cannot be wartime is a promise the board can never keep. And magnitudes are refused by the schema rather than the audit: a value outside the bound does not parse, so it is unrepresentable rather than merely discouraged. `scenario-cast.test.ts` proves the round trip on the reachability preview's own at-war board: the scenario is live, its accepting reply settles the war the board carries, and `conflictPhase` produces nothing for it on the next tick.

### The arc cell: generating chains instead of siblings

The flag machinery has been complete for months. `sets` stamps a deed, `flagSetFlag` turn-stamps it, a `flag` condition with `agedAtLeast` waits on it, the `flagAge` boost ripens it onto the desk, and the quoted-callback doctrine says what to do with it. Measured against the shipped corpus, almost none of it is spent: **826 distinct flags stamped, 43 ever read**, 65 scenarios of 275 gating on a flag at all.

The cause is the unit of work, not the doctrine. A generation cell has always meant "write N scenarios of this class at this severity" — a shape that produces N unrelated weeks however firmly rule 26 asks for a callback, because the writer has no earlier scenario in front of them to answer. It is also the shape that makes the strongest doctrine unusable: quoting the player back requires quoting a `remembered` line the writer has actually read, which means the reply that wrote it has to be in the same call.

An **arc cell** is one chain instead of `count` siblings: a setter, plus `count - 1` readers gated on the flags its replies stamp. `ScenarioBriefSpec.arc` marks it, `arcSection()` prints the shape, and the CLI takes it as a literal prefix — `arc:scandal:none:serious:3` — rather than a fourth optional positional segment, since the cell tail already ends in free-form steer text and no `ScenarioClass` is named `arc`. A count below two is refused at parse: a setter with nowhere to go is the shape the cell exists to replace.

Four rules in the section carry the weight, and two of them exist because of failures the shape invites rather than prevents. **A reader is a week, not an epilogue** — the scene where the player learns what their choice cost is the setter's rejoinder and has already happened, so a reader whose replies are all reactions to the setter is the wrong scenario. **Readers spread across different replies**, because an arc whose readers all hang off one branch rewards guessing which answer the writer preferred; `arc-branch-unread` reports the dead branch. The other two are opportunities: the quoted callback is trivially available here and nowhere else, and a reader may change desk — a scandal answered by covering for a chair comes back as an institution week when the courts want the file, which is how a government discovers its departments are one government.

That last rule contradicts the class contract as worded for an ordinary cell ("every scenario in this batch must actually be that"), so the contract is **restated for arcs** rather than left for a writer to reconcile: the setter must be the cell's class, and each reader declares whatever class it genuinely is. A brief that asserts both would be resolved by writing three scenarios of one class, which is the outcome the cell exists to avoid. Severity is deliberately *not* varied within an arc: the severity section is emphatic that a cell writes one tier, and a second contradiction would cost more than the range would buy.

### The rejoinder: what a position answers back

The section above gave a position *reach*. This one gives it an *aftermath*, and it is the larger of the two for how a week reads.

A reply that commits an instrument goes through `resolveAction` and earns a real ending: a success/partial/failure branch, named risk branches, the room's counsel recorded against the odds it was actually looking at, and a drawn `ConsequenceCard`. A reply that takes a position had none of that. `commitReply` returned the player's own line plus one narrator beat carrying `remembered` — the sentence they had just chosen, read back to them. Measured on the shipped corpus that is **749 of 827 replies**: nine answers in ten resolved into a number moving and an echo. That is the flatness a reader feels in the first ten minutes and cannot name, and it is not a prose problem — the prose clearing this gate is good.

**A generic aftermath pool cannot fill it, which is why this is authored.** `eligibleConsequences` keys on `instrument`, `rungBand` and `valence`. A position has no instrument, no rung, and no uncertain outcome to narrate, so there is nothing for a card to be selected by and nothing for it to report. A card general enough to fit every position would be vague by construction, which is worse than silence.

So the position commitment carries an optional `rejoinder`: `{ speaker, text }`, one beat spoken after the answer and before the week closes. Four properties keep it cheap and safe:

- **The speaker is `cabinet` or `narrator`, never `player`** (`ScenarioRejoinderSpeaker`, held to a subset of `ScenarioSpeaker` by a compile-time check). The player has just spoken; a rejoinder in their own mouth would be the game writing their reaction for them.
- **Prose only, no effects.** What the answer costs is priced on the commitment and measured by the stakes rules. A rejoinder that also moved the board would be a second price nothing audits.
- **It binds like any other beat.** `scenario-cast.ts`'s `renderReply` resolves the role hint against the seated cabinet and renders placeholders, so a rejoinder cannot reach the screen with braces in it or name a chair nobody holds. It is folded into `passages()`, so it clears every language, neutrality and length rule the opening does.
- **No renderer change.** `ReplyOutcome.beats` was always an array and `usePacedThread` is generic over its length, so the third beat paces into the thread exactly like the first two.

One audit rule, a warning. **`rejoinder-restates`** is the one that makes the field worth having: word-triple Jaccard between the rejoinder and both `remembered` and the reply text, on the same shingling `scenarioShingles` uses for corpus overlap (extracted to a shared `shinglesOf` rather than reimplemented). A rejoinder is meant to carry what the room did not know when it advised — the first cost arriving, the fact the decision shook loose — and one that re-says the choice is a beat the player learns to skip. Whether a position answers at all is no longer an audit question: the schema requires it, so a reply without one never reaches the audit.

`rejoinder` was optional for exactly as long as `dilemmaShape` was, and for the same reason: the surface has to exist before a corpus carries it, and 749 shipped positions cannot be backfilled by the edit that adds the field. **All 749 are now written, and the field is required.** Leaving it optional past that point is how a surface becomes one nothing has to fill in and so nothing does, which is this repo's recurring defect class. `rejoinder-coverage-thin` was removed in the same change rather than left standing at a coverage it can no longer measure — the parser refuses what the warning used to count, which is the stronger of the two guarantees and cheaper than both.

The echo ceiling (0.25) can now be measured rather than argued, and the measurement says it is a backstop rather than a filter: across the 749 backfilled rejoinders the highest echo is **0.200**, the 99th percentile is 0.080 and the median is 0.000, so the rule fires on none of them. That is one writer working to the rule, which is the weakest possible evidence for tightening it — a generated batch has every reason to echo harder than an author who has just read the `remembered` line. Retune it against the first *generated* batch, not against this one.

**Instrument and join replies now carry the same field**, closing the structural silence the content doctrine recorded (§ "A known structural gap"): every one of the corpus's 78 escalatory replies used to commit with no authored answer at all — the resolver's narration and a generic consequence card for an instrument, a narrator summary alone for a join. The gap was decided in favor of the commit-time beat rather than the staged-resolution seam, on a realism argument: a real room does not go quiet when the president gives the order — somebody acknowledges, names the first friction, puts a dissent on the record — and the *outcome* already has three voices (resolver narration, the consequence bank, staged resolution sequences). What was missing is the moment of commitment, so that is what the field carries. Two rules distinguish it from a position's rejoinder:

- **It is spoken blind to the roll.** `commitReply` speaks an instrument rejoinder *between* the player's line and the resolver's aftermath — it cannot come after a narration that already says how the attempt went — so the authored line must be true on every branch: acknowledgment, execution friction, dissent, never a result. `rejoinder-presumes-outcome` (warning, word-list, retuned like the echo ceiling) catches the obvious violations; the review pass owns the rest. A join's rejoinder follows the narrator's statement of entry, position-style, because entering a war has no dice at commit.
- **It is optional while the corpus backfills**, on exactly the path the position field took: the 78 shipped instrument/join replies cannot be backfilled by the edit that adds the surface. It becomes required the moment the backfill lands, for the same reason the position field did.

The echo rule audits it against the reply text only — an instrument has no `remembered` line to echo, the resolver narrates the decision itself.

### Standing with somebody: the `join` commitment

`joinConflict` is the engine's verb for entering somebody else's war, and a scenario reply needs a way to express it too. A writer wanting "our people go and stand with them" had exactly one tool — a military rung aimed at the cast counterpart — and on a `belligerent` cast with `side: "defender"` that counterpart *is the ally being defended*. The reply resolved as an expeditionary invasion of the country it meant to save, and opened a war against it. The scenario that shipped this way was caught at review; the cause was the missing verb, not the writer.

`ScenarioReply.commitment` therefore has a third kind: `{ kind: "join", side: "beside" | "against" }`. Three properties make it safe:

- **The side is relative to the cast, and resolved from the board.** `beside` enters on whichever side the counterpart is actually on, `against` opposite them — read at commit time by `joinEntry` in `conversations.ts`, never taken from the document. A scenario cannot enlist the player onto a side its own cast is not on, and if the board has moved since the scenario was written the reply is simply unavailable rather than quietly wrong.
- **It carries no cost of its own.** Entering is priced by the engine — standing rises for defending somebody under attack and falls for joining an attacker — and `joinCost(side)` is derived from the same constants `joinConflict` spends, so the projection cannot promise a price the commitment does not charge.
- **`ungrounded-join` (error) requires the `belligerent` cast.** It is the one cast that guarantees the counterpart is in a war at all; every other pick ranks relationships and can hand the reply a nation at peace, which is a door onto an empty field. A domestic scenario casts nobody to stand beside.

Nothing new mutates: `commitReply` returns `joinConflict`'s own packet, so a war entered from a conversation stores exactly as one entered from Ops or pulled in by the weekly ally draw.

### The presentation contract

Four things independently decided what a scenario looks like: the schema (what may be written), the audit (what is refused), the generation brief (what a writer is told), and the player's renderer (what is drawn). Each held its own partial copy of the same model, so a change to one had to be hand-carried to the other three and drifted silently when it was not.

The defect that surfaced it: a beat spoken by a foreign head of government rendered identically to one spoken by the player's own defense minister. The schema could express it, the audit had no rule against it, the brief never mentioned it, and the renderer had no way to tell them apart — so a foreign government appeared to be texting the president on their own secure channel, and `channelPresence` would even put a foreign flag at the top of it.

`packages/content/src/presentation.ts` is the single declaration, and the other four read from it.

- **A scenario has two channels.** `room` is the secure channel itself — the player's cabinet and a narrator, nobody else. `transmission` is everything that had to be *carried* into that room.
- **The counterpart speaker kind does not exist.** `ScenarioSpeaker` is built from the contract's `SCENARIO_SPEAKER_KINDS` (`cabinet`, `player`, `narrator`), and a compile-time assertion fails the build if the two lists ever disagree. A foreign government's words live in `ScenarioDef.transmission` — `{ relay, attribution, text }` — which makes the old framing *unwritable* rather than merely discouraged. An audit rule can be argued with; a shape that does not exist cannot.
- **How it arrived is authored, because it changes what the words are worth.** `relay` is one of `cable` (what they chose to send), `readout` (your own service's account), `broadcast` (aimed at their people as much as yours), `intercept` (what they did not intend you to have), or `communique`. `attribution` names an office and never a person, since the board casts the nation and rolls its officials per campaign.
- **Incoherence is refused by the schema, not the audit.** A `cast.pick: "none"` scenario carrying a transmission fails `ScenarioDef.superRefine` — there is no writer's judgment under which trouble entirely at home arrives as a foreign cable.
- **The renderer switches on the resolved speaker.** The decision screen (DESIGN.md, `/brief/decision/:id`; not built yet) draws each beat by its engine `ResolvedSpeaker` kind: a narrator line has no face, an official carries a portrait, a role title and a dossier link, the President carries name and title, a vacant chair says so, and a counterpart is labeled as their government and marked with the nation's flag. A transmission never enters the thread: it is drawn below the beats and the context notes as its own "What … sent" disclosure, credited to its source.
- **Register is a first-class rule.** `REGISTER_RULES` and `CHANNEL_RULES` are printed verbatim into every generation brief, so a writer is never told one thing while the gate checks another. `REGISTER_RULES` carries two halves and they fix different defects. The first fixes **altitude** — a briefing written as a watch officer's duty log rather than as something said to a head of government. The second fixes **substance**: prose at the correct altitude that still says nothing, because atmosphere is cheaper to write than a fact. A particular in every beat, a stated reason to care, wit only where a fact carries it, and less said rather than a figure invented. This is cross-cutting rather than a fifth director, because the four directors partition the class/severity cells between them — a register routed as a director would reach roughly a fifth of the corpus, and the register is not a taste, it is the house standard all four write to.

### The altitude rule

The first corpus was written at watch-officer altitude. *"The dockers did not clock on at six, every container quay in this country is stopped, and the vote is called for two this afternoon"* is accurate, concrete, and nobody has ever briefed a head of government that way. A president is told what is at stake, what is being decided, and by when; the operational facts are the evidence for that, not a substitute for it.

`auditBriefingRegister` (`duty-log-opening`) measures it. It reads only the **first sentence of the first beat**, and requires **two independent signals**: a clock reading (`CLOCK_TIME`) *and* the vocabulary of a working day (`ROUTINE_OPERATIONS_NOUNS` or `OPERATIONAL_COUNT`). Requiring both is what keeps it off good writing — "they shot down one of our planes at dawn" opens on an act of war at an hour and is exactly right.

It is a **warning, not an error**, and deliberately so. The underlying judgment is about what a sentence is *doing*, which no pattern decides; what the rule measures is a proxy that correlates with it. A warning routes the scenario to the review tier, where a person reads it — the honest place for a judgment of this kind. The hard version of the rule lives in the brief, where it shapes what gets written in the first place.

### The unreliable briefing, played fair

Until this shipped, every fact in a scenario's opening was true. That made the sourced hedges the writing already carried — *the second service disputes the timing*, *one defector says so* — decoration, and a player who learned to skip them was reading the game correctly. Doubt with no consequence is texture, not thought.

A scenario may now carry `intelConfidence` (see `SCHEMA.md` §7c), and when the claim it marks turns out to be false the aftermath pool has cards written to say so: the buildup was an exercise, the named man was a clerk, the figure came from an office defending its own budget.

**Unfair doubt is worse than no doubt.** Doubt a player cannot resolve by skill is noise, and noise teaches players to stop thinking — the opposite of what this is for. So the doctrine is three rules, and each one is a mechanism rather than a note in a brief. A gate a writer can talk their way past is not a gate.

1. **The tell is always present.** A marked briefing states its own thin sourcing in the prose, every time, so a careful reader can weigh the claim *before* answering. Enforced by `intel-tell-missing` (error) against `HEDGE_MARKERS` and `HEDGE_PATTERNS` (`hedgeIn`, `audit-lexicon.ts`). The patterns are what make the rule real: a bare "disputed" or "disagree" is not accepted, because in this corpus those words are overwhelmingly about a border or a policy, and accepting them let an unhedged marked briefing clear the gate — a lie shipping unmarked, which is the unfairness the marker exists to prevent. A doubt counts when it is *about the sourcing*: the second service disputes it, the estimate is contested by the desk that produced it, the two reports do not agree. Its companion `intel-doubt-unanswerable` (error) refuses a marked scenario no act can answer, because the aftermath layer only speaks for an act and a marker nothing can reveal is dead content that reads to its author like a working feature.
2. **The false rate is low and fixed by doctrine.** One in six low-confidence claims, in `FALSE_INTEL_RATE`, with no per-scenario dial and no way for a writer to tilt it — and never, under any seed, on a `confirmed` claim. Trusting your own services has to stay rational, or the mechanic collapses into blanket paranoia, which is exactly as unthinking as blanket trust.
3. **The reveal names the check that would have caught it.** "The attaché's report, which nobody asked to see, said the trucks were empty." Enforced by `reveal-without-the-check` (error) against `MISSED_CHECK_MARKERS`. This is the rule that makes the mechanic teach rather than gloat: the player leaves knowing a move they did not make, so the next hedged briefing is a decision instead of a coin toss.
4. **The reveal exists wherever the act can land.** A reveal is drawn from one cell of the instrument × band × valence matrix like every other card, so a cell holding no reveal is a false briefing that goes untold — the campaign privately holds that the yard was empty and the player is told only that the strike went well. `reveal-cell-uncovered` (error) holds all forty-five cells to carrying one, **including the success cells**: the resolver never learns the premise was hollow and will rule the act a success on its own terms, and an act that comes off perfectly against nothing is the sharpest form of this mechanic rather than an exception to it. `BRIEFING_CONSEQUENCES` meets the floor with one card per instrument per valence written to read at any rung, plus band-specific cards where a particular rung earns a sharper telling.

The counterweight ships with the reveals. `BRIEFING_CONSEQUENCES` also carries `intelFalse: false` cards, told when thin sourcing held, because a pool that only speaks when the services were wrong would train a player to distrust every hedge. Both sides carry the same raised weight (`BRIEFING_WEIGHT`): a briefing card is already rare, since it is only eligible in the week a marked claim's truth is settled, and at the floor's even weight it would then lose that week to the cards written for the act — a coin toss on top of a coin toss, with nothing for a player to learn from either. Weighting only the reveals would have taught that a hedge is usually a lie, which is not what the rate is.

The verdict reaches the aftermath layer through `ConsequenceContext.intelFalse` rather than a campaign flag, which is what puts the reveal in the same beat as the act it exonerates instead of in a later, unrelated week. The engine never decides it: which claim a scenario made is authored, so the draw belongs to the layer that owns the scenario, and the engine's only part is refusing to tell a reveal in a week the briefing was sound.

**Not yet proven.** A policy of checking single-sourced claims should beat both blind trust and blind distrust over a seeded campaign (DECISIONS.md §7.9). The mechanism is in place and the fairness rules are enforced, but that claim is unmeasured: no shipped scenario carries the marker yet, so there is nothing for a harness to run the three policies against. Until a marked batch exists, the false rate and the tells are set by doctrine rather than by evidence.

### Content targets shapes, not countries

Two mechanisms let one piece of writing be true in every campaign. Both are live in the engine and enforced by the audit, and they are proven by `scenario-cast.test.ts` and the audit's own rules rather than by authored content using them.

**A reply names the instrument it commits.** The opening has had an altitude rule since the first corpus review (`duty-log-opening`); the replies — the half the player actually says out loud — had none, and the core pack shipped "Send the team", which points at a force nobody in the room mentioned. Which instrument it is *is* the decision in a hostage crisis, so a reply that elides it has removed the choice and kept the consequence. `unnamed-capability` refuses a reply that sends, deploys, orders or launches "the team", "the unit", "the crew", "the men" or "the assets"; a qualifier in front of the noun ("the rescue team", "a special forces team") passes, because the defect is the missing qualifier rather than the word. The generation brief teaches the same rule, so a batch is written right rather than rejected after it is paid for.

**`fragile` does not mean weak.** It is the second rung of a four-way cut over a continuous score, and it holds states under real strain that still govern all of their territory. A scenario whose premise is that somebody other than the government holds ground — a hostage-taking, a terror cell, an insurgency — must cast on `post-conflict` / `client-state`, or on `volatile` / `collapsing`; the `implausible-host` audit rule refuses the rest. This is a rule rather than a note because the mis-cast is invisible in the document and only appears on the board: the hostage scenario in the core pack read perfectly and staged an armed group in the American interior.

**Band casting** finds the counterpart by shape — `{ pick: "band", powerTier: 5 }`, `stabilityBand: "collapsing"`, `archetype: "resource-exporter"` — and `bandMatches` resolves it against the live board, preferring the nation the player is most entangled with so the fit is the most charged one available, deterministically. A band-cast scenario names no country because it does not know which one it got. This is what makes nation 51 cost a data record rather than a content pass.

**Institution tokens** do the same for the player's own government. `Institution.kind` (`assembly` | `executive` | `judiciary`, `packages/world/src/schemas/org-cradle-charter.ts`) says what a body *is*, independent of what any charter calls it; content writes `{{assembly}}` and the founding charter supplies "National Assembly", "Officer Corps Council", or "Sanctuary Council". It is the same idiom as a scenario speaker naming a `roleHint` and the charter resolving the chair, applied to institutions.

`assembly` is the only kind every charter has, and the dataset validator enforces that (`SCHEMA.md` §7) — a government the player can be removed from always has some standing body doing the removing. `executive` and `judiciary` are genuinely absent under some charters: a junta has no independent court, a confederal council no single executive seat. So content that names them declares a `charterInstitution` condition (`{ institution, present }`), and the `ungated-institution-token` audit rule refuses to publish content that does not — which is what keeps a raw `{{judiciary}}` from ever reaching a player. The rule derives "which kinds are universal" from the dataset rather than a constant, so adding a charter without a court automatically demotes `judiciary` to gated.

The effect is that the same pack plays differently under a parliament, a presidency, and a clerical council: a scenario about a court defying the government simply never arrives where there is no court, and its mirror — about there being nowhere to appeal to — arrives only where that is true. `scenario-cast.test.ts` proves the token resolution against the US constitutional charter and the generic catalogue.

### Built for a five-figure corpus

The pool is planned in the tens of thousands, and three parts of the original design broke well before that. Each was measured, not assumed, on this repo's own code.

| Wall | Before | After |
|---|---|---|
| Per-turn selection | 239 ms at 10k, and every scenario's prose interpolated to choose one | 5.8 ms at 10k, one body rendered |
| Full audit | Quadratic — 9 s at 2k, over an hour projected at 50k | Linear — 1.18 ms/scenario, 59 s at 50k |
| Content in the app binary | ~29 MB at 10k, ~147 MB at 50k, parsed at boot | Bundled floor capped and tripwired; the rest sharded and lazy |

**Gating on what the board is carrying.** The war and disaster classes were keyed to `tension` and `stability` — proxies for a war and a famine rather than the war and the famine — so a scenario about the front could open in a country at peace and one about the floods in a country with no flood. Two `ScenarioCondition` kinds close that: `warState` (`any` / `aggressor` / `defender` / `none`, the *player's* part in it, where `none` means a world that has a war in it this government is staying out of, which is a different situation from peace) and `conditionActive` (an optional `ConditionKind`, with bounds reading the severity of the worst match at home, so one scenario can ask for an outbreak and another for an outbreak that has got out of hand). Two `WeightBoost` sources follow: `conditionSeverity` and `warIntensity` — the first sources that read the board's own multi-turn state rather than a metric standing in for it, which is what "dynamic urgency" was always supposed to mean.

**The pacing director (`content/src/scenario-director.ts`).** One weighted draw a turn with no memory produced two failures no volume of authored content fixes: the same class could arrive three weeks running (a player reads that as the game having about four things in it), and weight came from metrics, so the week a second front opened and a third province flooded the desk was as likely to raise a cultural dispute as either.

The director sits between the gates and the draw. `boardPressure` reads what the board is actually carrying per class (war intensity for the player's own wars, worst matching condition severity for disaster / public-health / unrest, an active pursuit aimed at the player for foreign-crisis) and returns **zero** for every class with no board state behind it — the floor that keeps a class competing on its authored weight rather than being silently re-weighted. `recencyFactor` is zero inside a six-turn `CLASS_COOLDOWN` and then tapers back to one across a fourteen-turn window, because a hard ban followed by full eligibility is a cliff the player can feel. Novelty is deliberately never *boosted*: a game that always shows you the least recent thing is as predictable as one that always shows you the same thing.

`deskCapacity` is what lifts the one-per-turn ceiling, and it lifts it for the right reason — a cascade week is the government being asked about more than one thing at once, not about one thing very loudly, so capacity counts *distinct* loaded subjects (capped at three) rather than scaling with severity. `stageScenarios` keeps a weighted draw rather than becoming best-candidate-wins, because a deterministic rule makes the campaign predictable from the board, which is repetition wearing a different hat; each pick removes its whole class from the remainder, so a cascade week is never two scenarios about the same thing.

Recency is read back out of the flag record as `staged:<class>:<turn>`, stamped by `rememberReply` alongside `scenario:<id>` and `desk:closed:<turn>`. It is a flag rather than a campaign field because a scenario's class is a **content** concept and the dependency-direction rule forbids the engine from knowing about content — a campaign carrying a list of content categories would be a save a build with a different content package could not load. It is stamped on reply rather than on staging, because a candidate that was ranked and not drawn has not been shown to anybody.

**Severity pacing.** Every scenario declares an authored `severity` (`routine` / `serious` / `critical`), and the director paces it the way it paces class. `rememberReply` stamps `staged-severity:<severity>:<turn>` when a scenario is answered; `severityFactor` damps a critical candidate inside a four-turn `CRITICAL_COOLDOWN` of the last critical to a floor of 0.15 — damped, never zeroed, and the damping scales away as that class's own `boardPressure` rises, so a war that is actually burning is never told to wait behind the pacing dial. `directorPicks` also drops the remaining criticals from the pool once one is picked: a cascade week may stack subjects, not severities — one building-shaking decision per week, maximum. `StagedCandidate` exposes the applied factor so the pacing probe reads it rather than inferring it.

**The announced fuse, and what a return leads with.** A delayed consequence the player was *told* was coming is the only return hook this game permits itself, and it is honest because it burns in turns and in nothing else — no wall clock touches it, no absence makes it worse, and a player who never leaves reaches the same week as one who does (DECISIONS.md §2.1). Mechanically a fuse is an authored construct that already existed: a reply stamps a deed flag, and some scenario in the pool waits on that flag with an `agedAtLeast` and ripens onto the desk with a `flagAge` boost. `announcedFuses(campaign, selectors)` requires **both** halves — the aged wait is what makes the answer late rather than scripted, the boost is what makes it arrive rather than merely be possible — and reports each live one as `{ flag, reader, turnsBurning, turnsRemaining, status }`, ordered by remaining turns then flag then reader so two builds of the same campaign lead with the same fuse. `turnsBurning` saturates at the wait's own length rather than climbing forever, which bounds the flag scan the way `turnsSinceStaged` is bounded. Status is `lit` (the turn the deed was stamped), `burning`, or `due` (the wait served). There is deliberately no `landed`: a fuse that landed is a scenario the player answered on the desk, and a fuse whose reader has fired is dropped. `digestLead(campaign, selectors, summary)` is the staging rule itself — the burning fuse's status first, the caller's own summary second — and it says nothing about which deed or which week, because the deed is a flag id and naming the week would spend the answer the player is waiting for. **Not yet wired to a surface:** `digestLead` and `announcedFuses` have no caller outside their tests; DESIGN.md's digest (`/brief/digest`) is the planned consumer.

**And the desk actually goes through it.** `ScenarioSelectorLike` gains `class`, so ranking happens over selectors and a five-figure pool is never rendered to be discarded; `stageFromResidentPool` is the pool-shaped entry the player shell calls, and `pendingConversations` returns the whole staged week with `pendingConversation` returning the one the desk opens on. Paired tests pin that the selector path, the whole-def path and the resident-pool path all stage the identical week — the repo's recurring defect is a system exported, tested and called by nobody, and a pacing layer the desk does not reach would be exactly that.

`scenario-director.test.ts` owns "nothing feels repetitive" as a **testable property** rather than a hope: a 52-turn probe over a synthetic pool spanning all eighteen classes asserts no class repeats inside its cooldown, that the desk is not starved (>80% of weeks stage something), that at least ten distinct classes appear, and that a campaign spent at war raises the war more than a peaceful one does *and still stays under half the weeks* — because a government at war is also asked about everything else. The pool is synthetic on purpose: the bundled pack authors five scenarios across five classes, so probing against it would measure how much content exists rather than how the director paces it.

**Selection reads selectors, never bodies.** A scenario splits into a `ScenarioSelector` (id, class, cast, conditions, weight, boosts, `once`, shard — `content/src/corpus/selector.ts`) and a body (all the prose). `liveSelectors` binds and gates candidates without interpolating anything; `drawSelector` draws over that; only the winner's body is fetched and rendered by `castBound`. Two costs fell out of this: rendering the pool to discard it, and — the larger one — re-deriving each cast's counterpart per scenario. "The tensest nation" is one answer the whole board shares, so binding is now memoized per distinct cast per turn, which is most of the 40× improvement.

**A pool is an index plus a way to fetch one body** (`content/src/corpus/pool.ts`). `memoryPool` holds bodies resident (the bundled floor, and every test); `lazyPool` fetches and caches by id (a shard file today, the content CDN later); `combinedPool` mounts packs as one. `drawFromResidentPool` is the synchronous path the app's desk uses inside a React tree; `drawFromPool` is the async one.

**Publishing is gated on who reviewed.** `studio scenarios review` asks whether the reviewing session also drafted the batch, and marks `shipped.json` provisional when it did. `studio corpus publish` refuses a provisional batch. Independence here is between *sessions*, not people: a reviewer that did not write the draft has no stake in the draft surviving, which is the whole of what a second pass buys, and it is available to a solo author. The corpus is the one place unreviewed prose does not merely ship — it becomes the standard every later batch is judged against for near-duplication. `--allow-provisional` overrides for the case where the corpus needs a floor before any review can run, and has to be typed.

**The corpus on disk** (`content/corpus/`, written by `studio corpus publish <jobId>`) is append-only JSONL shards of at most 500 bodies each, split by class, beside an `index.jsonl` of selectors and an `overlap.jsonl` of MinHash sketches. At 5,000 scenarios that is 20 MB of bodies against a 2 MB index and 3 MB of sketches, and the whole judging context loads in 74 ms — because neither the index nor the sketches require reading a body.

**Near-duplicate detection is bucketed, not pairwise** (`content/src/corpus/near-duplicate.ts`). MinHash signatures (64 rows in 16 bands) are LSH-banded so only plausible pairs are compared, and the comparison is exact Jaccard when both fingerprints are resident, or estimated from signature agreement when the other side came back from disk as a sketch. Banding proposes; it never decides. The index is additive, which fixes a second defect the old scan had regardless of speed: overlap and id collisions were only ever checked *within a batch*, so a scenario duplicating something shipped months earlier was invisible. `runJob` now passes the whole corpus as a `CorpusContext` to the gate.

**Review is tiered, because thousands of scenarios cannot honestly be read one by one.** `scaffoldReview` re-runs the audit and assigns each scenario a tier: **flagged** (the gate warned, or the corpus calls it a near-duplicate), **sampled** (a seeded share of the rest, deterministic per `(runId, scenarioId)` so it cannot be rerolled for a friendlier draw), or **clean**. Only flagged and sampled owe a human answer; clean ships on the gate unless someone rejects it. The sample rate is 1 up to 40 scenarios and floors at 2%, keeping the absolute number a person reads roughly constant as batches grow — and `shipped.json` records the coverage it actually got, so "shipped on a 2% sample" and "read end to end" stay distinguishable claims.

**Publishing also re-audits the corpus as a whole, not just the batch joining it.** The per-batch gate (`validateScenarioDocument`, run at review time) only ever sees one batch plus the flag vocabulary and duplicate index of what is already installed — it cannot see a rule like `flag-condition-never-set` fire across the *whole pool*, because a flag one batch writes and a much later batch reads looks satisfiable to each batch alone. `studio corpus publish` closes that gap: after the reviewer-independence guard passes and before anything is written, it runs `auditScenarios` over the union of every published body plus the incoming batch, and separately over the published corpus alone. An error the union produces that the published corpus alone did not is new — introduced by this batch — and refuses the publish, naming each finding. An error present in the published-corpus-alone run is not this batch's fault; it is printed as a labeled pre-existing defect and does not block, because holding a new, unrelated batch hostage to an old one's mistake fixes nothing. Warnings from the union never block, only count. `studio corpus stats` runs the published-corpus-alone half of the same check on every call, so a standing defect stays visible between publishes rather than only at the moment a new batch happens to surface it.

Two revision kinds joined the harness: `scenario-rejoin` (adds or rewrites the rejoinder on an instrument or join commitment, nothing else) and `scenario-arc` (moves `family`, each reply's `resolves` and `sets`). Every revision gate, the session driver's courtesy check and the finishing re-check now forgive, word for word, the errors the shipped original already carried in fields the kind may not touch (never reachability), and the arc kind leaves family pairing to the corpus-wide audit after write-back. `scripts/verify-deed-reachability.mjs` (+ proof, allowlist `scripts/deed-reachability-allowlist.json`) holds both directions of the deed flags: every deed content gates on has a live emitter for its family and outcome, every emittable deed is read or queued on the allowlist with an owner and a reason, and a reader behind a dead deed is reported down the chain.

**The flag economy is measured by a ledger, not by counting warnings** (`studio corpus arcs`, over `arcLedger` in `content/src/scenario-deeds.ts`). An arc is a scenario whose replies stamp deeds on two or more doors, and each door is *answered* when something in the pool gates on a flag it stamps. The three states are reported separately because the warning count alone reads backwards: `arc-branch-unread` fires only once at least one door of an arc has been answered, so **closing a promise raises it** — the arc moves out of the silent majority, where the rule cannot see it, into the lopsided set, where it can. A rising count there is the measurement improving, not the pool degrading, and the fix that would lower it (disconnecting readers) makes the content worse. The command and the warning read the same `arcLedger`, so the gate and the worklist can never disagree about the same pool; `--dir` measures a pending batch as part of the corpus it is joining, which is the only way to see a reader answering a setter that shipped months earlier. Measured over the shipped 289: **48 lopsided arcs carrying 95 dead branches, 241 fully silent, 0 whole** — 63 distinct flags read against 868 written.

Volume, coverage targets, and measured production cost for the full corpus: `docs/CONTENT_PLAN.md`.

### The audit rule family

`scenario-audit.ts` and its extracted families hold 48 rules (the impact family in `scenario-audit-impact.ts`, the two commit-time rules in `scenario-audit-time.ts`, the locus-and-place rules in `scenario-audit-place.ts`, and `speaker-department-mismatch` in `scenario-audit-speakers.ts`, a warning that holds each cabinet speaker to the department that owns the week's class under the United States' ownership table; a pack test counts it as debt beside the grounding family rather than asserting it to zero) (six of them read their vocabularies out of `scenario-vocabulary.ts`), and `scenario-reachability.ts` adds the one rule that needs a board rather than a document. A finding carries a **severity**: an `error` stops a publish, a `warning` informs and does not. `auditPasses(findings)` is the one place that distinction is decided, and `auditScenarios(defs, knownFlags, corpus)` takes the flag vocabulary of packs already installed so a batch is validated against the world it will land in rather than as if it were the only content that exists — and, since the promised-follow-up rule, the corpus context also carries `readFlags`, the flags the shipped corpus's own conditions gate on (the read side of the flag sidecar, `content/corpus/flags.jsonl`'s `reads` field, backfilled once from the bodies for corpora published before it existed).

| Group | Rules |
|---|---|
| Playability | `single-tone`, `costless-position`, `indistinct-replies` (two replies with the same cost/flag fingerprint are one choice wearing two hats), `duplicate-reply-id`, `duplicate-scenario-id` |
| Stakes | `shallow-stakes` (no reply reaches the `STAKES_FLOORS` heavy thresholds — a crisis with no expensive door is not a crisis), `token-position` (a position under every token threshold that also stamps no flag neither costs nor is remembered), `dominated-reply` (a position that is another position with extra downside and nothing gained, decidable only when flags, pulls, and press match) |
| Prose | `mechanics-language`, `british-spelling`, `unknown-placeholder`, `doubled-word`, `filler-language`, `stilted-register` (warning — the opposite register break from filler: "anteroom", "obligates", a curated `STILTED_WORDS` list of words a plain-spoken room replaces), `press-organization`, `numeral-in-prose`, `overlong-text` (per-field ceilings), `generic-subject` |
| World neutrality | `nation-name-in-generic`, `literal-capability-figure`, `charter-institution-literal`, `cabinet-title-literal`, `parliamentary-vocabulary` (error — player-facing text saying `minister` / `ministry` / `prime minister` / `premier` / `parliament` / `parliamentary` / `no-confidence` / `backbencher` about **the player's own** government, which is a presidency and the only playable seat there is. The finding names the replacement rather than only the offense: Secretary of …, the Department of …, Congress / the House / the Senate, impeachment / censure / a resolution, members of your own party. Exempt: a `transmission`, which is a foreign government's own document, and any sentence plainly about the counterpart — "their foreign minister", "the {{counterpart}} ministry". The bare `minister` family on a `cast: "none"` scenario is left to `cabinet-title-literal`, which already refuses it, so one defect yields one finding. The seat and budget shorthand — "the chair", "the line" — stays with `insider-shorthand` at warning, where the doctrine puts it), `hardcoded-individual`, `invented-proper-noun` (a capitalized word standing mid-sentence that is not a weekday, a month, or a token another rule owns — the bare-surname and named-place half of the invent-nobody rule, which `hardcoded-individual` only catches when a title is welded on: "Seat Marchen" and "Restwell Cemetery" both shipped clean before it), `ungated-institution-token` |
| Coherence | `domestic-incoherence`, `inert-boost` (a `tension` or `distrust` boost on a domestic scenario with no counterpart edge, or a `flagAge` boost on a scenario that waits on no flag), `ungrounded-conflict-move` (a reply settling, cooling or offering terms on a war the scenario is not gated on the player fighting), `ungrounded-join`, `ungrounded-state-grievance` (error — `stateGrievance` with `scope: "locus"` and no `locus.state`), `unrouted-domestic-act` (error — `cast: none` with `locus.state` whose replies declare neither `gazetteer` nor `domestic`), `unplaced-domestic-signature` (warning — a home scenario bound to a state or city whose position reply routes a national `domestic` signature without `gazetteer.stateLean`, so the place it happened moves no more than every other state;
