a locus at the capital is exempt as the seat of the decision; it fires on 14 shipped scenarios at 2026-09-22, the levee breach in St. Louis among them), `dead-grievance-gate` (warning — `stateGrievance` naming a signature slug no reply in the batch writes), `unnamed-group-toll` (error — `borneBy: "one-group"` without `human.cohorts`), `cohort-toll-implausible` (error — `thousands`/`mass` toll on cohorts whose combined national share is under 5%), `dead-cohort-gate` (warning — `cohortStrain` naming a cause no reply writes), `institution-bounds-scale` (error — an `institutionState` bound on the wrong scale for its field), `unknown-institution` (error), `institution-vacancy-gate-unfounded` (warning — a `vacantDays` gate at a body whose chair the founding fills, with no deed flag beside it), `institution-politicization-unreachable` (warning — politicization above 0.4 asked of a semi-autonomous or independent body), `undeclared-set-piece`, `pointless-set-piece`, `locus-asserts-landform`, `locus-ground-mismatch` (error — a cast scenario declaring `locus.ground: "home"` beside a descriptor standing on the counterpart's ground; the resolver is correct and the authored override is the defect, see "Where a scenario happens" below), `unplaced-domestic` (error — domestic plant/factory/industrial-zone/tax-holiday copy with no `locus.city`; strike/Midwest copy with no city, state, or sector), `generic-place` (error — stand-in metros such as "industrial zone"), `city-effect-unplaced`, `missing-city-token`, `unknown-city` (error — `locus.city` not in `WorldDataset.cities`, or the city's nation is not the locus ground; pick a dataset id), `financial-basis-without-band` (error — a stated cost basis with no band to say how it is authorized), `financial-basis-off-band` (warning — a stated basis an order of magnitude from its band's anchor at the week's severity, naming the nearer band; `scenario-audit-cost.ts`), `financial-scale-exceeds-severity` (error — `impact.financial.scale` louder than the week's `severity` allows: routine at most `supplemental`, serious at most `emergency`, critical anything, and `horizon: "permanent"` off routine entirely; the band is hidden until the Record, so nothing on the decision board contradicts a drift and a rendered routine week billed about eleven billion dollars), `instrument-reply-contradicts-rung` (error where an instrument reply's text names another rung's act and none of its own — "open talks on the fisheries line" committing diplomatic rung 3, which the engine calls *Recall Ambassador*; a warning where it names no rung's act at all. The vocabulary's spine is each rung's own name read out of `@admin/engine`'s `rungsForInstrument`, so a renamed rung updates the rule instead of drifting past it) |
| Flag economy | `flag-condition-never-set`, `callback-never-ripens` (warning — a flag-gated scenario carrying no `flagAge` weight boost, so the follow-up goes live and then competes at flat weight against the whole pool forever; gates on engine-stamped flags are exempt, since nothing stamps an age marker for them), `promised-follow-up-unread` (warning — a reply whose prose announces a continuing process, "open a formal review", "convene the inquiry", matched by `PROCESS_ANNOUNCEMENT`, while no flag it stamps is read by the batch or by the corpus's `readFlags`; measured against the shipped corpus a promise deferred to a later batch was kept 32 times out of 826, so the reader belongs in the same batch). `arc-branch-unread` (warning — a scenario where at least one reply's deed is read and at least one other reply's deed is read by nobody, batch or corpus; the finding names the dead branch). That last one is the defect the arc cell introduces and the only flag rule that looks at a setter's *replies* rather than at deeds in aggregate: a three-reply setter whose readers all hang off one reply passes `island-pack`, `flag-economy-imbalance` and `callback-concentration` alike, and still teaches the player that one answer was the real one. It stays quiet on a setter nothing reads yet, because packs compose and writing the setter first must not be an offense. The pool-scale counterpart is `deedsRemembered` (`scenario-deeds.ts`), a reported statistic rather than a rule: distinct flags set against distinct flags read, merged across a validate run and printed as one line. |
| Briefing fairness | `intel-tell-missing` (a scenario carrying a low `intelConfidence` whose prose hedges nowhere — the player is punished for believing something they had no way to weigh), `intel-doubt-unanswerable` (a marked scenario with no instrument reply, so no reveal card could ever be drawn — note that the domestic case is now refused earlier, at parse, because `cast.pick: "none"` can never carry an instrument reply and the two rules would otherwise contradict each other), and on the aftermath side `reveal-without-the-check` (an `intelFalse: true` card whose beats never name the check that would have caught it) and `reveal-cell-uncovered` (a matrix cell with no reveal in it, where an act on a false briefing lands and is never told so). All four are errors; see *The unreliable briefing, played fair* above. |
| Reachability | `unreachable-scenario` — cast against real sealed campaigns (`preview-board.ts`), the only rule that can see a scenario clearing every other one and still reaching no player |
| Warnings | `template-overlap`, `all-downside` (every reply pure loss — damage triage, not a decision), `island-pack` (a batch of five or more in which no scenario conditions on any authored flag: every deed stamped, none ever remembered), `rejoinder-restates` (a position's rejoinder repeating 25% or more of its own `remembered` line or reply text — the beat exists to carry what the room did not know, and one that re-says the choice is a beat the player learns to skip), `press-missing-on-heavy-reply` (a `critical` scenario with a heavy position answer and no `press` headline on any reply — a headline is the only aftermath a scenario can author besides the rejoinder, and a rendered campaign's three biggest decisions printed nothing. Critical only: measured against the approved reference set, two scenarios in ten carry a headline and four of its six serious-or-critical weeks carry none, so a rule reaching `serious` would be a rule that flags the bar) |
| Pool-scale diversity | Four warnings that judge a *batch* rather than a scenario, each catching a way a pool can be made of individually clean content and still read small. (A fifth, `rejoinder-coverage-thin`, retired when `rejoinder` became a schema requirement — the parser now refuses what it used to count.) `callback-concentration` (more than three scenarios gating on the same deed — a file the government answers for once, not every other week), `dilemma-shape-monoculture` (one shape holding more than half a batch, which is eight desks having one argument). The shape vocabulary itself was rebuilt after a reliability study: the original eight reproduced at Cohen's kappa 0.48 across three blind readers, the current eight at 0.83, and half the members changed — `dirty-hands` among those cut, having been selected zero times in 56 scenarios by any independent reader. See `SCHEMA.md` §7c, `stock-phrasing` (three or more scenarios sharing a five-word run — the batch leaning on a small stock of images), and `cabinet-gate-unused` (a batch of `CABINET_GATE_BATCH_FLOOR` (8) or more in which under `CABINET_GATE_SHARE_FLOOR` (10%) of scenarios carry a `cabinetAttribute` condition — one week in ten, which is what the brief asks for in as many words. The engine mechanism is built and tested and read by not one shipped scenario, so the promise that who the player appointed is legible in today's decision is currently kept by no content at all). They came out of reviewing a real 100-scenario batch that passed every per-scenario rule: its callbacks collapsed onto 12 core deeds while 487 sat unread in the corpus, and human review cut eight duplicates that scored 0.2–2.2% phrasing similarity against `template-overlap`'s 0.35 ceiling. |

**Reachability is the rule that needs a board.** Every other rule is a property of the document; this one seals real campaigns off the shipped dataset (`preview-board.ts`) and asks whether the scenario is ever true on one. It exists because two shapes of dead content shipped clean through everything else: a `metric: "treasury"` bound written on the nought-to-one scale every *other* metric uses, when treasury is absolute and a founding board holds tens of thousands of it; and a band cast plus a relationship bound, which is two independent filters rather than one narrower one — the band pick is resolved before the bound is read and is not chosen to satisfy it, so a scenario asking for a trading hub the player depends on gets whichever trading hub the band names and then fails the bound against it. The spread is eleven boards: three healthy foundings at turns one, four and eight, one under a charter that seats every institution kind, one under strain — low metrics, real removal pressure, and real insurgency and protest pressure on the country itself — one at war with the capitals gathered on both sides of it, one watching somebody else's war, one that has actually legislated a program, one founded on a landlocked compact member whose firmest friendship is a fellow signatory, and the two poles of a record long enough for the world to have read it. The last six exist because a board state content can gate on and no board carries is content that ships dead. Whatever flags the batch itself waits on are folded onto all three *late* boards — the healthy turn-eight founding and both war boards — rather than onto the healthy one alone. A callback whose earlier deed was itself wartime has nowhere else to be true, and stamping the flag only where there is no war makes the flag and the `warState` gate two filters that can never close together: the follow-up is then refused for a reason that is about the fixtures rather than about the content. The spread runs to turn ten, so what it measures is *early-campaign* reachability, deliberately the strict reading: a scenario a player cannot meet in their first year is very nearly one they never meet. Content genuinely written for a matured board is not wrong, it just hands `auditReachability` its own boards rather than being excused from the question.

**The stakes group is the depth floor.** The language gates keep a batch clean; the stakes gates keep it a game. A generator held only to prohibitions learns that the cheapest way through the validator is a set of near-zero, all-loss, interchangeable answers — every one valid, none a decision. `STAKES_FLOORS` (exported, quoted verbatim by the generation brief) defines both ends: *heavy* — instrument rung 3, a metric moved 0.04, treasury 60, or an alignment pull 0.06, one of which every scenario must offer — and *token*, beneath all of which a flagless position is refused as a reply that neither costs nor is remembered. The cheap, quiet option stays legal on purpose: restraint is a real answer, but it must stamp a flag so a later scenario can hold the player to it.

**World neutrality.** The question is whether a scenario survives being cast onto whichever nation the live board picks, under whichever charter applies (DECISIONS.md §7.6). A scenario that does not declare itself a set-piece may not name a nation, demonym, or capital; may not quote a capability figure that is true of exactly one country; and — set-piece or not, because the player's own charter is unknown either way — may not name a charter institution or hardcode a cabinet chair. The deny-lists are **generated from the dataset** (`audit-lexicon.ts`), not typed out: nation names, demonyms, and capitals come from the 196-government roster, institution names and chair titles from the charter catalogue. Adding a nation makes naming it start failing the same day, with no list to remember to update. Matching is case-sensitive, which is what separates Turkey the nation from turkey the bird.

**Player copy spells its numbers out.** `numeral-in-prose` refuses digits and currency marks anywhere a player can read, because a line is meant to land the way it would be said out loud in the room — "five million dollars", not "$5 million", which reads as a briefing document *about* a government rather than as the government. A currency mark is refused for a second reason: the world names no real country, so it has no dollar, pound, or euro to spend. Counting an ordinary detail out in words stays wanted; it is the figures that are refused, not the specificity.

That rule would have opened a hole in `literal-capability-figure`, which was digit-shaped: "40 thousand troops" was refused while "forty thousand troops" was not, and both are true of exactly one nation. So the capability rule now also matches the spelled form. Its window is **adjacency — a magnitude within three words of a capability noun — not the sentence**, because the sentence is too coarse: shipped content puts "four million routed offshore" and "the budget speech" in one sentence, and that is correct writing, since the sum is a bribe rather than the national budget.

The British-spelling gate matches **every form a stem inflects into**, not the stem alone. The list enumerated the `-ise` family's inflections by hand and had simply forgotten the `-our` family's, so the bare stem was refused while its `-able`, `-ing` and `-hood` forms shipped. The inflection set is explicit rather than a wildcard, since a wildcard reads the breed of dog as the color inflected, and a false positive here blocks a publish. The mechanics-word gate deliberately does **not** inflect: "rolling blackouts" and "statist" are prose, while a bare `roll` or `stat` is the engine leaking.

That gate covers *authored content* and nothing else, which meant the engine's own policy domain for the armed forces, the design system's color tokens and the geography's adjacency field were all still spelled the British way under a rule that only applied to half the repo. `scripts/verify-american-spelling.mjs` is the other half: the same stem-and-inflection idea applied to every identifier, comment and doc line in the repo. Its `-is` stems are matched only with the endings that make them verbs, because "nationalist" and "globalist" are American exactly as they stand and are the names of an alignment axis this game is built on. The handful of files that must contain a British spelling — the negative tests that prove the refusal works, and the generation brief that quotes examples of what will be refused — are named in the script rather than exempted by pattern.

Two rules were considered and deliberately **not** built. A "flag set but never read" rule fired 31 times on the bundled pack, all of them correct content — packs compose, and a reply stamps memory so a *later* pack can gate on it, so demanding the reader already exist would make writing the setter first an offense. A `once`-scenario flag-discipline rule is unnecessary by construction: `rememberReply()` stamps `scenarioFiredFlag(id)` on every commit, so the condition it would check can never fail.

**Generation is the production path.** `studio-cli/src/scenarios/brief.ts` builds the one repeatable prompt every batch runs through — contract, gates, craft rules, and two shipped exemplars embedded in full — and packs batches as resumable, spend-journaled job files for the existing runner. `studio-cli/src/scenarios/validate.ts` applies the gate: zod shape first, then the whole audit, batch-fails on one bad scenario.

**No driver can publish around the gate.** Each driver validates its own output, because each has something useful to do with the findings — the session driver writes them back beside the brief, the OpenRouter driver folds them into a retry decision. That is courtesy, not enforcement. The enforcement is `studio-cli/src/jobs/gate.ts`: `runJob` passes **every** result through `gateResult(job.kind, item, result)` on its way to the progress journal, so "done" is recorded there or not at all. A driver that skipped validation entirely, or a new driver written later by someone who never read those files, still cannot journal a clean item. A job kind with no registered gate **fails closed** — an unvalidated batch is never worth more than no batch. Spend is still ledgered for a gate-rejected item: the money left regardless of whether the output was worth keeping. `scripts/verify-publish-gate.mjs` proves all of this against the *built* output rather than the sources — it runs the shipped CLI against a known-bad fixture (`studio-cli/__fixtures__/rejected-scenarios.json`) and asserts the refusal names the band-consistency and charter-leakage rules by name, re-validates the bundled core pack to prove the gate is a gate and not a wall, validates `content/packs` if it exists, and statically scans the validator's whole dependency closure for network or cloud-SDK reachability.

**Any agentic session is a driver.** The session driver (`jobs/drivers/session-driver.ts`) turns generation into a file handshake, so Claude Code, Codex, OpenCode, or a human with an editor can work a batch with no API key and no SDK: `studio scenarios run <id>` materializes each item's brief plus a self-contained README into `content/jobs/work/<id>/`; the session writes `<item>.out.json` beside each brief; the next run validates every output through the same gate the OpenRouter driver faces and journals it done — or writes `<item>.findings.json` naming each defect for the session to fix. Done items are never re-checked or re-spent. The CLI surface is three commands (`studio scenarios job | run | validate`, `studio-cli/src/cli.ts`), and the repo skill `skills/scenario-generate` (tool-neutral; `.claude/skills/` holds a discovery pointer) gives any session the full procedure. A drafter without the repository works from the authoring kit, committed at `content/kit/` and rewritten by every `corpus build` from the bake: `studio scenarios kit` writes plain files (instructions, a brief composed from the same section builders as the job brief, the cell catalog, the writer's doctrine, exemplars, a JSON Schema generated from `ScenarioDef`, the corpus listing and deeds, and a NEEDS file measured from the bake) stamped with a version that hashes the rules, held current by `scripts/verify-kit-current.mjs`, and `studio scenarios ingest <file>` refuses a stale version, sorts the file's scenarios into a session-driver job by cell and runs the same gate (`studio-cli/src/scenarios/kit.ts`, `kit-brief.ts`, `ingest.ts`, `kit-commands.ts`; procedure in `skills/scenario-ingest`).

**The unattended path runs the same gate.** `OpenRouterDriver` (`jobs/drivers/openrouter-driver.ts`) calls the chat completions API directly — no session in the loop — and holds every output to the identical `validateScenarioDocument` gate `session-driver.ts` already applies, so which driver wrote a scenario is invisible once it's published. Three things make it genuinely unattended rather than a seam-prover:

- **Sensible retry.** A transient failure (a thrown network error, an HTTP 5xx) retries with backoff up to `maxAttempts`; a content failure (unparseable JSON, a validator rejection) does not — the same prompt to the same model will not fix itself, so it reports `failed` once and the next `studio scenarios run` retries it fresh through the same resumable contract every driver shares. A permanently-failing item is never silently dropped: `ItemResult.error` now threads through `runner.ts`'s `ProgressEntry` and `RunSummary.failedReasons` (previously discarded after the driver returned it), so `cmdRun` prints *why* an item is still open, not just its id.
- **Per-stage model selection.** `JobItem.stage` (optional; today's single-stage scenario batches leave it unset) selects a model from `OpenRouterModels.perStage`, falling back to `.default` — ready for the drafter/architect/repair/audit/coherence pipeline (§3's keep list) once it lands on the job model, without the driver changing shape again.
- **Spend stays owner-gated, with two doors rather than one.** `cli.ts`'s `cmdRun` requires `OPENROUTER_API_KEY`, and no billed driver — OpenRouter, image, or Replicate — is constructed until `clearSpendGate` (`studio-cli/src/jobs/spend-authorization.ts`) says it may be. An **attended** run takes the door it always did: the operator types the job id back at a prompt, the same "type the resource name to confirm" pattern destructive operations use elsewhere, and a mismatch aborts with nothing run and nothing spent. An **unattended** run — cron, launchd, a scheduled agent — takes `--authorize-spend <jobId> --as <name>`, which refuses if the id does not name the job actually loaded and otherwise writes a `spend-authorization` line (`authorizedBy`, `at`, `jobId`, `estimatedSpendUsd`) into the job's progress journal *before the first request*. Later invocations of the same resumable job proceed on the authorization already journaled, which is what makes a cron entry safe to leave running. A run with neither a terminal nor a recorded authorization refuses and names the flag: a prompt nobody can answer is not a gate, it is a hang. The name is never prompted for — it comes from `--as` or `git config user.name`, on the same reasoning `--owner-review` uses, because a prompt at this terminal is answered by whatever is driving it, which is usually an agent. The journal therefore carries two line kinds (`ProgressLine` in `jobs/job-file.ts`); every reader goes through `progressEntryOf`, so an authorization line is invisible to the resume and budget readers rather than a parse error. Spend itself is estimated from the response's token usage and ledgered per item exactly like every other driver; OpenRouter's own billing remains the reconciliation source of truth.

### Delivery (bundled-only)

The client ships the whole published corpus inside the binary and plays it with no network at all. There is no remote content-delivery path (DECISIONS.md §7.3).

**Content is not stored alongside code, and it is not database rows either.** Scenarios are published artifacts, content-addressed and integrity-checked, baked into the app at build time rather than served live. Three reasons that is the right shape: a first launch with no network must be playable, so a bundled starter pack is mandatory and audited (`auditStarterPack`); the draw runs synchronously (`drawFromResidentPool`), which cannot await a round trip; and a shard is immutable and provable, which a mutable table is not. `content/corpus/` is the *output* of `studio corpus publish` and is git-ignored for the same reason a build directory is — tracking it was what made published content read as source.

`services/api/src/content/manifest.ts` still holds the manifest index used by the build pipeline: **`ContentManifest`** (`packages/contracts/src/content.ts`) is the index of one published content set — a `contentSetId`, a `schemaVersion`, a `manifestRevision`, and one `ContentEntry` per pool shard carrying its path, byte size, sha-256, item count, a `bundled` flag, and the pool keys its items `requires`. `checkManifestCompatibility` and `auditStarterPack` are the two functions that matter: the first names a version mismatch as either "update the app" or "content is stale" depending on direction; the second fails a content set whose bundled shards reach a pool that is not itself bundled, or one that bundles nothing at all — the check that keeps a first launch with no network from hanging.

No generation at runtime (DECISIONS.md §7.1) is enforced mechanically rather than by review: `services/api/src/content/no-runtime-generation.test.ts` fails if any non-test source file in the service mentions a model provider, or if the service's `package.json` declares one as a dependency.

**The client** (`packages/player-core/src/content/`) mounts only the baked corpus. `bundled-corpus.generated.ts`, written by `studio corpus build`, carries every published shard byte for byte with the digest the manifest gave it — the player draws all 322 scenarios offline (manifest revision 21). `report.ts` surfaces any parse problem the bake hit. `mountDeliveredScenarios` (`packages/player-core/src/conversation/scripts.ts`) still exists as the mounting seam the pool draws from, layering the authored core pack over the bake; it is exercised directly by tests, not by any remote-fetch caller.

**The producer** (`studio corpus build`, `studio-cli/src/corpus/content-manifest.ts`) indexes the sharded corpus into `content/dist/<contentSetId>/` and into the app-bundled corpus (`writePlayerBundle`). The build refuses outright if the starter-pack audit fails, because that is the last moment a first launch hanging on a plane is a build error rather than a player's problem. There is no `studio corpus deploy`; a built `content/dist/` set has no consumer today. The studio console's What-is-live page (`apps/studio/prod-status.mjs`) reads the same committed bake, scenario by scenario, and calls a published scenario the bake does not carry staged, and a bake it cannot read is reported as unverified with the reason, never as an implied zero.



## 6. Services

`services/api` is a thin backend, and thin is load-bearing: no game logic lives here, and every module in it is a pure class over an injected store or transport, so nothing in the service needs a running server to test.

**Accounts.** A session is bound to the account, not to a device or surface. Access tokens are short-lived and HMAC-signed; refresh tokens are opaque, single-use, and rotate on redemption. Deletion scrubs the email and revokes every outstanding session.

**There is no cloud save.** The local save is the only copy and is canonical for play (DECISIONS.md §9.5).

**Entitlements (the server verifier port; no storefront verifier yet).** God mode is a one-time lifetime unlock bound to an account. The security property is structural rather than defensive: **there is no field on `EntitlementClaim` that means "I already own this."** A claim carries a platform, a receipt, and a product id, and the only route to `granted` runs through a verifier returning `valid`, so a patched client has nothing to forge — a test posts a wire payload carrying `entitled: true`, `godMode: true`, and a fabricated entitlements array and gets `refused`, with `resolve()` still false.

`ReceiptVerification` has three cases, and the third is the point: `valid`, `invalid`, and `unavailable` — an unreachable or unconfigured store never looks like a refused purchase, never grants, and never revokes what an account already holds, so an outage does not lock out a legitimate purchaser. `verifiersFromEnv` builds no verifier today, so every `redeem` answers `verification-unavailable` and nothing grants. A Steam verifier plugs in there without touching the service: built only when its credentials are present, and failing at startup naming a missing variable, because a half-configured store is a misconfiguration rather than an opt-out. Secrets are required, never defaulted (`requireEnv` / `MissingConfigError`).

`StorePlatform` is `"steam"`, the one storefront the game sells through. What exists is the port a Steam ownership verifier implements: `ReceiptVerifier`, the injected `HttpTransport`, and `attempt` / `parseJson`, which turn a network fault or a malformed response into `unavailable` rather than `invalid`. The forgery tests pin both routes to a false grant: a verifier's `invalid` answer beats every self-asserting field a patched client can send, and a claim with no verifier configured answers `verification-unavailable`.

Outstanding: the Steam ownership verifier, the persistence implementations behind `AccountStore` / `EntitlementStore` (in-memory reference implementations ship today), and the HTTP routing layer.

## 6b. God mode

God mode is the paid sandbox tier: omniscience, direct world editing, invulnerability, and — designed but not implemented — time control. Its whole implementation is one builder module, `packages/engine/src/god.ts`, and the reason it is that small is the reason it is safe to sell.

**Every god action is an ordinary `EffectPacket`.** There is no second mutator and nowhere to put one: each function in `god.ts` returns a packet with `source: "godmode"` and `actor: "developer"` whose effects are the same kinds ordinary play uses, and the caller hands it to `apply()`. A god edit is therefore clamped by the same relationship ranges and the same metric bounds as a decision taken in the fiction — a test writes `trust: 9` and reads back `1`, and the result still parses against the world schema. God mode cannot express a campaign the engine could not have reached on its own; it can only reach one faster. Settling a war by decree goes through `conflicts.ts`'s `settleByAgreement` rather than writing a status directly, for the same reason: a conflict ended outside the terminal transition would carry a status no consequence rule knows how to read. `editCity` is the same contract for a metro: it returns a `CityEffect` packet, and `apply()` clamps employment, taxBase, demShare, lean, and tax-holiday remaining.

**Every god action is attributable.** `actor: "developer"` is copied onto the `ActionRecord` by `apply()` like any other packet's, so an amendment is visible in the ledger beside the week it changed, and `selectPlayerLedger` still returns only what the administration itself decided. Nothing about god mode is silent.

**The flag is permanent, and permanence is enforced at the gate.** `campaign.godMode` (§7b of `SCHEMA.md`) has no `false` state and no effect that clears it. `apply()` re-attaches it to whatever state a packet produced — so an effect that rebuilt the campaign without the field cannot drop it — and counts every `source: "godmode"` packet into `actions`, including the enabling one. Exclusion from achievements and leaderboards is asked as `isRankable(campaign)`, one rule in one place rather than a convention each future scoreboard has to remember.

**Omniscience is a read, never a write.** It lives in the read path only: `hasIntelOnRelationship` (`state.ts`) short-circuits the intel comparison, and `feedVisibility` (`npc-turn.ts`) returns `"intelligence"` for covert activity nobody caught. Both are computations over live state, so revealing something changes nothing — asserted by a test that snapshots the campaign, reads through both gates, and compares. The ordinary intelligence limits are proven still intact by the same suite. Covert activity surfaces as *intelligence* rather than as news on purpose: a god campaign should still be able to tell what the world knows from what only it knows.

**Invulnerability stops the arc one beat short.** `advanceRemovalArc` still computes pressure every turn and still fires every earlier beat, so a sandbox campaign can be seen to be failing on the same reading an ordinary one is; only the terminal beat never lands. Suppressing the reading instead would hide the one figure that says whether an edit made things better or worse. The paired test plays the identical collapse script twice: an ordinary administration is removed, a god one is not, and its pressure is still above the first threshold.

**The entitlement seam.** `services/api` verifies the receipt (§6 Entitlements) and is the only thing that can grant. On the client, `packages/player-core/src/entitlement/` holds the local cache and the shape a surface reads through: `createEntitlementStore` resolves `{ godMode, source }`, defaulting to locked, preferring a verified server answer over the local development grant, and treating an unreadable cache as a reason to grant nothing. The development grant reports `source: "dev-override"`, because a grant that is invisible is a grant nobody audits. No player surface reads the cache yet. **Purchase and restore flows are not built**; the seam is where they land (DECISIONS.md §10.9 records the open choice between server and client verification).

**Player surface.** God Mode is one settings row, shown only to players who hold the entitlement (DECISIONS.md §10.1). Opening the record is irreversible and the row says so before it is confirmed; there is no reversal control because there is no reversal. An amended administration is marked wherever the campaign is shown, so the fact travels with the campaign. Not built yet.

**Time control is designed, not implemented.** Rewind and branching would be deterministic replay from the seed plus the decision log rather than a snapshot stack, which is what a pure, seeded engine already buys. The only state it lands today is `godMode.branchPoints` and the `godMode` effect that writes it.

## 7. The player

The player is being rebuilt map-first. [DESIGN.md](DESIGN.md) is the design brief and [the map-first program](audits/2026-10-10-map-first/PROGRAM.md) is the build order. This section says what exists in source and what is planned. The design system and the shell (W3), and the Brief with the decision loop and a first Record (W4), are built, and of the Nation screens the economy and the budget (W5), and of the Washington screens Congress and the press office (W6); the rest of Washington, the World screens and the rest of Nation are not.

The player is being rebuilt map-first. [DESIGN.md](DESIGN.md) is the design brief and [the map-first program](audits/2026-10-10-map-first/PROGRAM.md) is the build order. This section says what exists in source and what is planned. The design system and the shell (W3), the Brief with the decision loop and a first Record (W4), and the Nation home with the state dossier (W5a) are built; the Washington and World screens and the rest of Nation are not.

The player is being rebuilt map-first. [DESIGN.md](DESIGN.md) is the design brief and [the map-first program](audits/2026-10-10-map-first/PROGRAM.md) is the build order. This section says what exists in source and what is planned. The design system and the shell (W3), the Brief with the decision loop and a first Record (W4), and Washington's executive tree, office and person screens (W6) are built; the rest of Washington, and the Nation and World screens, are not.

The player is being rebuilt map-first. [DESIGN.md](DESIGN.md) is the design brief and [the map-first program](audits/2026-10-10-map-first/PROGRAM.md) is the build order. This section says what exists in source and what is planned. The design system and the shell (W3), and the Brief with the decision loop and a first Record (W4), are built, and of the Nation screens the economy and the budget (W5), and of the Washington screens the courts and oversight (W6); the rest of Washington, the World screens and the rest of Nation are not.

**The universal player (`apps/player`, `packages/ui`; DECISIONS §8.1, owner direction 2026-10-16).** One Expo app built with Expo Router, React Native and Tamagui ships to iOS, Android and the web, and its web export is what Electron will host. The program and wave ledger are in `audits/2026-10-16-universal-player/PROGRAM.md`. Pieces:

- **Theme.** `packages/ui/src/theme/from-design.ts` projects `packages/design` into Tamagui tokens and themes. Every theme carries the same roles (`ThemeRoles`): light, dark and their high-contrast forms, each with `chrome`, `paper` and `situation` sub-themes. Media widths are `phone`, `tablet`, `desktop` and `wide`.
- **Shell.** `AppShell` puts the navy navigation in a bottom tab bar on phones, a compact rail from 768px and labeled navigation from 1440px. The situation bar sits above every working page.
- **Data.** Screens read player-core view models; `packages/ui` never reads the engine.
- **Device state.** `apps/player/src/device` mirrors the legacy `app/` folder: one `StoragePort` per device (`src/host/storage.ts` uses expo-sqlite's key-value store on iOS and Android, `storage.web.ts` uses IndexedDB or the desktop bridge), then the preferences, the entitlement, one campaign store restored once, and the draft store over it (`DraftsContext`).
- **New player-core modules** (renderer-free): `authority/` (65 records of the law, the Handbook's "The law" chapter), `calendar/` (`buildCalendar`, derived on read), `notifications/` (`buildInbox`, `reminderPlan`), `compare/` (benchmarks, entities, the President's own record, with honest refusals), `seals/` (an emblem spec for every institution, organization and outlet) and `map/` (lens rows). The Preferences gained `inboxSeen` (per campaign seed) and `deviceReminders`.
- **Platform differences** live only in `.native`/`.web` pairs behind one interface: the animation driver, the vault and the map.

**What exists.** `apps/web` is a Vite + React DOM + TanStack Router application: the shell and screens below, and the map engine (`src/map/`) described after them. `apps/desktop` (Electron Forge) loads the same built bundle from `administration://game/`; it is not a second frontend. `packages/player-core` holds the presentation-independent logic every screen reads: the campaign store, saves, founding, preferences, the entitlement cache, the attention list and cases (`desk/`), summons clocks, dossiers, act previews, the glossary (`glossary/`) and the route tree's typed destinations (`navigation/places.ts`).

- **Tokens** (`packages/design`). One TypeScript source per family (`color`, `typography`, `spacing` with radius and layout, `motion`, `portrait`) and its web projection `tokens.css`, held together value for value by `tokens/parity.test.ts`; `tokens/color.test.ts` holds the contrast rules DESIGN.md §3 states. `layers.css` declares the cascade order every sheet uses (`reset, tokens, components, rooms, states`). `color.party` stays exported for `player-core/src/format/party-color.ts`.
- **Primitives** (`packages/ui-web`): `AppFrame`, `TopBar` and `Wordmark`, `ModeNav`, `WorkSurface` (phone detents), `CommandPalette`, `Button`, `Field`, `Choice`, `Toggle`, `Stat`, `Gauge`, `Series`, `PartyBar`, `Hemicycle`, `Portrait` (with `faceSource`, which draws a genome through `@admin/ui-web/portrait`), `Flag`, `Icon` (bound to `assets/icons`), `CompareAuthorize` (refuses a comparison without exactly one baseline; draws each option as a card on rows shared through subgrid, wrapping rather than scrolling sideways, with an optional emblem, accent and verdict strip per option and a `lead` row kind for the option's own words), `Movement` (a signed move from one zero line, scaled with `movementScale` against the largest move it is compared with), `Receipt` and `EmptyState`. Their sheets are `src/styles/*.css`, imported once through `@admin/ui-web/ui.css`, all in the `components` layer.
- **Shell** (`apps/web/src/shell`). `Frame` mounts the screen-wide `<MapLayer/>` (inside `<MapDirectiveProvider>` and `<MapDataProvider campaign>`) only on the title screen, for its turning globe; every other screen draws its map in the page with `MapView` (`apps/web/src/map/MapView.tsx`: the same GL runtime, lens registry and palette, framed with its key, a list view from `lensRows`, Alaska and Hawaii insets on the US camera, a flat projection, and each region opening its screen through `map-routes.ts` unless the screen passes `onChoose`). `Frame` also mounts, behind the top bar (mark, date, `ContinueControl`, `SaveStatus`, search, settings), the mode navigation (shown once a campaign is in play, outside the title and the founding) and search (⌘K or Ctrl+K; places whose route exists, then glossary terms). It passes the map its `settings` (`display.ts` `mapSettingsOf`: the effective camera, density, high contrast, and any color-vision palette as the map's `color-vision` variant), its `insets` (`chrome-insets.ts`: the bar, the mode rail or tab bar, the docked panel and the phone sheet, read from their boxes on resize, when the navigation comes or goes, when a sheet settles at a detent and when its height transition ends, never during a drag) and `onSelect` (`map-routes.ts`: `state:` to `/nation/state/:id`, `district:` to its state with `?district=`, `nation:` to `/world/nation/:id`, each only when that route exists in the router; otherwise it returns false and the map detaches the choice itself). Before the campaign store has loaded, only the title screen renders; every other screen waits for it. `Screen` is every screen's wrapper: it declares the map directive from the screen's class (a Workspace screen sends `paused: true`), sets the document title, moves focus to the title on arrival, and lays the work in a `WorkSurface`. `InTerm` sends a mode screen to the title when no campaign is in play and to `/recovery` when the vault failed to open.
- **Device state** (`apps/web/src/app`). `DeviceProviders` opens one `StoragePort` (`device-storage.ts`: the desktop bridge's files, else IndexedDB with an in-memory fallback that says so) and reads the preferences and the entitlement over it at once (`device-settings.ts`). The campaign store (`DeviceStores.tsx`) is a dynamic import started when the map's `map:first-paint` mark is observed, or after 1.5s if it never is; it restores the vault once and hands the store up. Until then `useCampaignState` reads `loading` and `useStoresIfReady` null. A chunk that fails to load is thrown from render to the router's error screen. A development entitlement grant is honored only in a development build (`honoredEntitlement`).
- **Screens.** `/` (title), `/founding` (the eight stages, the oath preview, `sealFounding` and the day-one `signOrder` calls), `/administrations` (five slots: open through `load` then `restore`, `discard`, import through `begin`), `/settings` (camera, map density, high contrast, color-vision palette, attention policy, and the God Mode row when the entitlement is honored), `/recovery` (`retrySave` and the saves that cannot be opened), `/settings` also turns the first-run walkthrough back on, and the Brief and the Record described under "The Brief and the decision loop" below. `/washington` and `/world` are their modes' homes with their camera and an honest "not built yet"; `/nation` is described under "The Nation and its states" below.

- **Screens.** `/` (title), `/founding` (the eight stages, the oath preview, `sealFounding` and the day-one `signOrder` calls), `/administrations` (five slots: open through `load` then `restore`, `discard`, import through `begin`), `/settings` (camera, map density, high contrast, color-vision palette, attention policy, and the God Mode row when the entitlement is honored), `/recovery` (`retrySave` and the saves that cannot be opened), `/settings` also turns the first-run walkthrough back on, and the Brief and the Record described under "The Brief and the decision loop" below, and the executive half of Washington described under "Washington: the executive branch" below. `/nation` and `/world` are their modes' homes with their camera and an honest "not built yet".

**The Brief and the decision loop (W4).** Every screen here reads player-core selectors and commits through the store against the campaign it was shown (`basis`), so a press on a board that has moved is refused, never charged at a price nobody quoted.

- `/brief` (`routes/brief.index.tsx`, `brief/`): the run's own sentence and its stops while the digest is unread (`stopWords`, `stopLines`, `matterDoor`); the waiting decision story first (`TopMatter`: place, flag, response window); then `briefMatters` in the composer's order, each row with its kind, whether it holds the week, a countdown only where a real window runs (`ClockMeter` over `MatterClock`) or its age, and Put off (`deferMatterPacket`); the running clocks due or closing (`runningClocks`, the calendar folded); the Now strip (`NowStrip`: approval's dial with the six judgments from `approvalBreakdown`, then four readings from `nowReadings` on meters, each with its week's move and a door to its metric screen); removal pressure against the three proceedings' lines (`removalStanding`, folded while calm); and `TimeControls`, one office day (`stepClockDay`) and the Chief of Staff's hand-off (`handOffAndContinue`), held by the same open work Continue is. The map frames the top decision's place (`decisionCamera`) under the approval lens, a row the pointer rests on previews its own place, and after a run it plays the delta lens over the country.
- `/brief/decision/:id` (`decision/`): a Workspace screen. `Situation` gives the place, the particulars, who is involved, the cost of waiting against the response window and what nobody knows, with the voices folded. `CompareAuthorize` sets every reply from `openConversation` (through `decisionComparison`, which adds a "How the cost is priced" row from the reply's `costBasis` when any reply sends forces) beside `withWaitBaseline`'s first column, leaving it unanswered, whose cells are `silencePrice`: `inactionPacket` applied to a copy of the campaign, so the readings and judgments shown are the entry `apply()` writes. The answer picked shows the cabinet's counsel (`counselOnReply`, `counselSentence`). Below it, the dispositions the case declares besides a reply (`scenarioDispositions`: put off, decline, leave unanswered); decline and leave-unanswered charge the same silence once (`leaveUnansweredPackets`, shared with the Chief of Staff's hand-off) through `commitAll`. Authorizing runs `commitReply` then `store.commit(rememberReply(...))` and lands on `DecisionReceipt`: what it moved (`readingMoves`, drawn as meters with a tick where each stood before), the judgments it moved, the room's aftermath, and the door to its Record entry; the map plays the delta lens at the decision's place. The first decision opens `FirstRun`, a four-step walkthrough with glossary terms; it is put away in `dismissedBriefs`, and "Turn off guidance" writes `hideGuidance`, which settings can undo.
- `/brief/digest`: the last run's stops, what moved (`metricMoves` over the `StopBrief`), stories that ended, the wire and the security items; Done reading calls `acknowledgeBrief`.
- `/brief/clock/:id`: a family case (`caseDetail`: its clock, what lapses if it runs out, the door to its own act where that screen exists, and the tray of `CASE_TRAY_DISPOSITIONS` the case accepts, committed through `commitCaseDisposition`), or a statutory clock (`summonsReading`).
- `/brief/scandal/:id`: heat and stage, who it names and who keeps it alive, counsel's read of the file (`scandalCounselRead`), and the three answers (`SCANDAL_RESPONSE_VERBS`) against staying silent through `respondToScandal`; how it landed shows only once answered.
- `/brief/metric/:id`: retired; `place.metric(id)` (`packages/player-core/src/navigation/places.ts`) names the screen that owns each reading and the route redirects there. Approval, its six judgments and `approvalBreakdown` are read on `/nation`; the shared `MetricReading` panel (`apps/web/src/shell/MetricReading.tsx`) draws a reading's history and the acts that moved it on Budget and Emergency; Security (`world/security/Readiness.tsx`) and World (`world/overview/StandingPanel.tsx`) draw readiness and standing in their own panels.
- `/record` and `/record/event/:id`: the timeline newest first (`recordRows`, filterable by who acted) with impact assessments (`impactReports`, filed estimates only); an entry with its readings before and after (`recordEntry`, walked back off the ledger's recorded costs), its judgments, what it answered to and what followed, and the assessments it caused, the map framing its state or government.

**The economy and the budget (W5).** `/nation` is an index route (`routes/nation.index.tsx`), so the Nation screens below it render as its siblings. Both screens commit through `nation/economy/commit.ts` (`useNationCommit`: the packet is built at press time on the campaign the screen showed, committed with `basis`, recorded for the delta lens) and show `ActReceipt` with the Record door; the map plays the delta lens while the receipt stands.

- `/nation/economy` (`nation/economy/`, Hybrid): the cycle as a four-phase loop (`cycleReading`); unemployment, inflation and growth with their published history, the stretch since the oath and the rate each economy rests on (`economyReading`, `economyHistory`); the Fed's rate with each decision as a step (`fedReading`), the stock index against its crash line (`equityReading`), the release calendar (`releaseTimeline`) and every commodity with its line and stocks (`commodityBoard`); the household bills with their lag (`householdReadings`); the goods trade balance (`tradeBalance`). The levers are one `CompareAuthorize` over `leverComparison` (`player-core/src/economy/lever-comparison.ts`): waiting first, then each `marketLevers` entry priced, or refused in the law's words, with crude and the gallon six weeks out read by applying the very packet `marketLeverAct` commits and stepping the week's own market phase (`campaignPhaseSequence`'s `commodityPhase`) on both sides.
- `/nation/budget` (`nation/budget/`, Workspace): funding left against the fiscal year (`appropriationStatus`), the allocation editor (a slider per account between zero and `accountCap`, ticks at the line in force and the line that holds the account), the live draft projection and the request against keeping the book, all read from `budgetDraftReading` (`player-core/src/budget/appropriation-draft.ts`), which returns the `projectBudgetDraft` candidate the request sends to `requestAppropriation`, the floor count per changed line (`projectAppropriation`) and the authority flow in force and drafted (`authorityFlow`); then the federal books (`fiscalBooks`: debt against output and its ten-year path, the year's balance, interest against revenue, the rating and its outlook, the debt limit's use and headroom, revenue and outlays by line).
- **Economy lenses** (`map/lenses/economy-lenses.ts`): `jobs` and `prices` against the country (diverging, the hard end warm), `output` (sequential with magnitude radius) and `federal-dependence`, read from `stateEconomyBoard` (`player-core/src/economy/state-economy.ts`): the country's live rate through each state's authored `vitalAnchor`, and each state's authored share of output applied to this week's national product, so the states still sum to the nation.

**The domestic program and emergency powers (W5).** The same commit discipline: each act is built from the campaign on screen and committed with it as `basis` (`nation/policy/use-act.ts`), recorded for the delta lens, and receipted (`ActDone`: what it set, what it moved, the Record entry). Every comparison is a player-core `ChoiceTable` (`policy/choice-table.ts`: columns, one of them the do-nothing baseline, and rows whose cells carry their tone and, where a cell is a set of signed movements, those movements as numbers (`ChoiceMove`: `metricCell`, `alignmentCell`, the resting-economy and added-debt cells), so the screen draws them as bars on one scale per row; a row may be the `lead`) drawn through `CompareAuthorize` by `ChoiceCompare`, which also counts each column's movements into its verdict.

- `/nation/policy` (`?domain=`, Hybrid): `policyPortfolio` (the six domains with the stance in force, chair and delivery, authority, stand-up weeks, whether a position needs a statute, and the open pledges riding on the domain from `plankDeliverable`); `stanceComparison` sets every stance in the domain from `projectPolicyStance` against keeping the one in force (paid once, every week, added debt from `fiscalPreview`, where the economy settles, alignment shift, who pushes back, stand-up, promises kept or broken, who carries it), and locks a column `enactPolicy` itself would refuse, with its reason (a revenue program waits on its statute). Authorizing commits `enactPolicy`. The map lens is `share-<domain>`: each state's share of its economy in the axes that domain lands on (`POLICY_DOMAIN_AXES`, `stateIndustryShares`), ramped to the state that lives most off them. Below it, `orderPipeline`: every signature by stage (rule being written, before the courts, in force, undone; read off `campaign.orders` and `pendingRules`) and the catalogue as ready to sign or shut with `orderAvailability`'s reason; opening an order drafts it at its desk.
- `/nation/policy/order/:id` (Workspace, framing the states its domestic signature lands on): `orderDesk` reads `projectOrder` (challenge odds, ground and reasoning, the Attorney General's review, how long the rule takes), the authority it runs on, its effects path by path, its added debt and the pledges it keeps, and compares signing against leaving it unsigned. Authorizing commits `signOrder` against the campaign's own streams; the receipt says whether it was challenged.
- `/nation/emergency` (Hybrid, lens `conditions`: each state's worst running condition, gold where a declaration reaches it): `emergencyBoard` lists declarations in force (`weeksToLapse`, the continuation window, `weeklyStandingCost`), the conditions board with the scopes a declaration could honestly take (`liveCauses`, not already under one), incidents, and the states a Guard federalization could be asked of (`guardRequestCandidates`). Each act opens a comparison against doing nothing (`declarationChoices`, `renewChoices`, `endChoices`, `guardChoices`); what a declaration opens or ending gives back is read by applying the act's own packet to a copy and diffing `availableOrders`. Authorizing commits `declareEmergency` (state or nation scope), `renewEmergency`, `endEmergency` or `requestGuardFederalization`.

**The Nation and its states (W5a).** Both screens are Spatial and keep their lens in the address (`?lens=`, approval when absent). The map's `onSelect` reaches them through `map-routes.ts` now that the routes exist, so a state or district chosen on the map or in its list opens here.

- `/nation` (`routes/nation.index.tsx`, `nation/state/NationHome.tsx`): the US camera under approval, the electoral call, partisan lean or population by state (`state-population`). `nationOverview` gives approval and this week's electors as dials, the states' spread across five approval bands in states and electoral votes, the conditions and declared emergencies at home, and every state ranked by the lens (`stateRows`, `rankStates`), each a door with its reading on a meter.
- `/nation/state/:id` (`routes/nation.state.$id.tsx`, `nation/state/`): the state detached with its district lines, capitol and largest metros pinned. `stateDossier` reads the sections: Overview (flag, seat, the governor's party and disposition on a meter with `GOVERNOR_ASSENT_FLOOR` marked, approval with its `stateApprovalHistory` trend, the call and the divergence line from `stateApprovalReading`, lean, people, output), Politics (Senate seats, the House delegation from `districtHolder`, every district as a door), Economy (`stateVitals`, the six economic axes, federal dependence, metros), Conditions (live conditions, open requests with their clocks, declared emergencies, the marks the state holds) and Acts. `?district=` detaches a district a second level (`assembleDistrictDossier`) and frames it.
- `?act=<condition kind>` opens the locale-act desk, a Workspace: `localeMatters` lists what a locale act can answer (a governor's open request, a live condition in the state), and `localeActOptions` prices every `LOCALE_ACTS` entry off the packet `takeLocaleAct` builds against the campaign shown, with the lock the engine names when `localeAvailability` refuses. `CompareAuthorize` sets them beside waiting (`localeWaitBaseline`: when the request runs out). Authorizing commits that same packet with `basis`, so the price shown is the price charged, and `LocaleReceipt` says what was charged, what became of the request and what moved, under the delta lens, with the door to the Record entry. The dossier's foot carries the verb for its first matter, and the desk opens on a `docked` `CompareAuthorize`, whose act bar holds to the bottom of the surface, so on a phone both sit in the lowest 112px. Docking is opt-in: a comparison further down a screen would pin the bar over rows not yet reached.

**Force and collection at home.** Federal power pointed at Americans is a set of locale acts, each opened by its statute's predicate and not by a declaration or a protest (DECISIONS §6.22; the last section of this file, "Domestic force: missions, orders and court orders"). The coercive acts are `property-surge`, `immigration-operation`, `task-force`, `federalize`, `proclaim`, `troops`, `priority` and `surveil-under-order`; `surveil` stays in the catalogue as a refused act so the desk can say which law closes it. Each declares a `coercion` risk, and `takeLocaleAct` adds `coercionOutcome` (`engine/src/domestic-coercion.ts`) to its packet: a `coerced:<kind>:<state>` deed flag with its turn marker, `surveilling-domestic:<state>` for collection (which `committeeInquiryPhase` reads as an intelligence cause, opened only by a hostile panel), a run cost once the act is the second inside `COERCION_TEMPO_WEEKS` (competence and unity), and an incident drawn on its own `coercion-incident` stream keyed by state, week and the run so far. The run raises the odds (`TEMPO_ODDS_STEP`, capped at `MAX_INCIDENT_ODDS`) and the weight (`TEMPO_SEVERITY_STEP`, capped at `MAX_SEVERITY`). An incident moves opinion (integrity, unity, polarization), stability and the nation's `protestPressure`, which is what opens unrest; a grave one (a killing, surveillance reaching reporters) sets a stamped `scandal:` flag, which the scandal phase opens at full gravity and the inquiry phase can follow, and removal pressure reads the approval it costs. The desk shows only the odds (`LocaleActOption.risk`); the receipt reads the incident back off the pre-act campaign (`localeIncident`), the same draw the packet committed. A coercive act whose occasion is not `any` can also be sent with no unrest to answer: `takeHomeAct` (gated by `homeActAvailability`, which supplies no cause, so an act that answers disorder is refused there and routine enforcement is not) charges `UNPROVOKED_STANDING` times the standing, and `coercionOutcome(..., provoked = false)` adds an integrity cost and polarization up front and raises the odds and weight by `UNPROVOKED_ODDS` and `UNPROVOKED_SEVERITY`. An immigration operation is always provoked (`occasion: "enforcement"`: routine federal law runs in every state) and carries no protest easing from the home path. The player reaches it from the state's Response tab and the state screen's foot (`?power=1`, `PowerActDesk` over `homeActOptions`).

**Congress and the press office (W6).** `/washington` is an index route (`routes/washington.index.tsx`), so its screens render as siblings. Every act here is one `CompareAuthorize` over a player-core `ActComparison` (`congress/act-comparison.ts`: each option read as its change from today with the engine's refusal in its own words, every cell built from the same engine call its packet comes from, and a `waiting` line where not acting runs a clock; there is no do-nothing column, DECISIONS §8.13), committed through `washington/congress/HillAct.tsx` (`useNationCommit` underneath, with the receipt returned to the block that authorized it) and received with `ActReceipt` while the map plays the delta lens.

- `/washington/congress` (`washington/congress/`, Hybrid, camera `us`): each chamber as a `Hemicycle` with its majority spoke (`chamberReadings`, `chamberParties`), the room's and each floor's disposition (`dispositionReading`, `chamberStandings` over `chamberDisposition`), the leaders and chairs (`hillPosts`), the committees with the ones investigating first (`committeeReadings` over `committeePanel` and `selectInquiriesFor`), the floor schedule by stage with each bill's count (`floorSchedule`, `settledBills`: `projectStage` in the chambers, `projectOverride` on the desk), transmitting the administration's own bill (`transmitComparison`), war powers and the appropriation (`warPowersReading`, `appropriationReading`) with the thirty-day extension where a sixty-day clock runs (`extensionNotices`, `certifyExtension`), and one state's delegation (`delegationReading`). Lenses: `senate`, `house`, `house-hex`.
- `/washington/congress/bill/:id`: the stage pipeline (`billPipeline`), the week's odds (`billStageReading`), the whip count per chamber (`whipCount` in `congress/whip-count.ts`: the engine's caucus tallies placed across state delegations in proportion to each delegation's disposition, summing to the engine's count to the vote, with the presiding officer's tie-break kept apart), what it does and the override it would face, signing or vetoing against leaving it on the desk (`deskComparison`, `signBill`, `vetoBill`; a veto waits on its message), and lobbying a chosen member. Lens `whip:<chamber>:<bill id>`.
- `/washington/congress/post/:id`: the holder framed on their home state (`stateOfBirthplace`), their disposition and following, what has been tried with them, and the three approaches against leaving them be (`lobbyComparison`, `issueLobbying`).
- `/washington/office/press` (`washington/press/`, Hybrid, lens `press-reach`): the podium (`podiumReading`: the secretary, what the room will ask, the silence, the corps's standing), the three plays against saying nothing with the desk they are aimed at (`pressActComparison`, `issuePressAction`), framing a recent order (`framingOffers`, `framingComparison`, `frameDecision`), the secretary taking the hit (`takeTheHitComparison`, `secretaryTakesTheHit`), the coverage trend and outings (`pressCoverageTrend`, `pressHistory`) and the desks (`deskProfiles`).

**Washington: the executive branch (W6).** Every act here is built by its engine entry point against the campaign the screen showed and committed through `washington/executive/acts.ts` (`useChairActs`) with that campaign as `basis`, then lands on `ActReceipt` (what it set, what it moved, the Record entry) with the delta lens. Each act is drawn by `CompareAuthorize` against a do-nothing column, its rows priced by the act's own projection through player-core `pricedRows` (`government/act-price.ts`).

- `/washington` (`routes/washington.index.tsx`, `washington/executive/ExecutiveTree.tsx`): the tree from `executiveTree` (`government/executive-tree.ts`): the White House staff chairs and the press office, the Executive Office, the fifteen departments in order of precedence with their agencies folded under each, the intelligence community and the independent agencies. Each chair shows its emblem (`officeEmblem`), who holds it and how (`officeHolding`: confirmed, acting or vacant, read from the cabinet seat, the White House staff record or the body's own leader), their competence and loyalty as four-step bands (`judgeAttribute`, never a figure), and what waits from it (`officeAlerts`: live requests and offers to resign, open inquiries). A switch cuts it to `attentionTree`. The map frames `dc` and draws the office pins (`marks: "offices"`, `marks/sources.ts` `officeMarks` over `executivePins`): gold confirmed, hollow gold acting, red vacant, a red ring where something waits; a cluster of offices frames as `pin:` keys a few blocks across, and a single pin opens its office through `Frame`'s `onActivateMark`. Doors to Congress, the courts and oversight are words until those screens exist.
- `/washington/office/:id` (`washington/executive/OfficeScreen.tsx`): `officeFile` (`government/office-file.ts`) for the office id. The camera is `office:<institution>`, which rings the office's own pin. It shows the headquarters line (the federal site's name and state, or the recorded reason there is none), the three staffing chips, the holder, the body's delivery, capacity, staffing and morale on meters with its backlog and vacant days, the holder's five readings, the agencies under it, the combatant commands under Defense, and the Record entries naming the body or its holder. The acts: a Secretary's offer to resign (`answerResignationOffer`, accept or decline, priced by `projectResignationAnswer`), their requests (`takeMinisterProposal`, `projectMinisterProposal`), a bloc formed out of the chair (`reconcileFaction`, `projectFactionReconciliation`), dismissal of a confirmed Secretary (`dismissMinister`, `projectDismissal`), and filling the chair, which opens a Workspace screen: for any seat the Senate confirms at the top of its body, the appointments desk narrowed to that one office (the shared seat row, below), or the acting shortlist (`actingShortlist`) committed by `appointActing` where the law allows an acting. A nomination already before the Senate for the seat is said instead.
- `/washington/person/:id` (`washington/person/`): `personFile` (`dossier/person-file.ts`): the role line from `personDossier`'s standing, the camera on their office, state or capital, the five readings from `assessmentProfile` as bands with how sure the read is, what each moves (`attributeConsequence`) and loyalty's direction since they were seated, their circle (`connectionsForPerson`), a career timeline (their posts before office, then the Record entries naming them) and the counsel they gave. The acts against keeping things as they are: dismissal (`dismissMinister`), removal of an officer below the cabinet (`removeOfficer`, with the cause in writing for a for-cause office), and withdrawal of a pending nomination (`withdrawNomination`), each priced by `personLever` or `withdrawalLever`.

**The courts and oversight (W6).** `/washington` is an index route (`routes/washington.index.tsx`), so the Washington screens render as its siblings. Both screens commit through `useNationCommit`, whose commission may return a run of packets (committed through `commitAll` and saved once), and show `ActReceipt` without the delta-lens line (`mapNote={null}`), because these acts move institutions rather than state approval.

- `/washington/courts` (`washington/courts/`, Workspace, camera `keep`): the bench from `benchReading` (`player-core/src/judiciary/courts.ts`: the nine seats, each justice placed left to right on the economy, open seats and any name before the Senate for them); `?judge=` opens `justiceFile` (the three lean scales, the readings the bench reads for a justice in words, and every vote the engine stored on `judiciary.cases`); the docket from `courtDocket` (procedural stages and recorded holdings; only pending Supreme merits receive `pendingStrikeChance`, computed from the eligible bench and the same case-specific merits callback used by adjudication); nominations: the appointments desk narrowed to the courts (the shared seat rows, below; a Supreme Court seat is never preselected) and `judicialNominations` (`judicial-nominations.ts`: a pending name's withdrawal priced off `withdrawNomination` against keeping it).
- `/washington/courts/case/:id` (Workspace, camera `keep`): `caseFile` (`case-file.ts`): what is challenged and on what ground, what a strike-down undoes (`reversedEffects`, or the note that a rule not yet in force is only canceled) and what it cannot (`unreversedCost`), the timeline, the ruling with every vote and the opinion, and while pending the paths the bench reads on its day (a catalog act that authorizes the order, an open seat). No engine act withdraws or defends a challenged order, and the screen says so.
- `/washington/oversight` (`washington/oversight/`, Hybrid): the inquiry pipeline by stage with its weight on the Hill (`inquiryPipeline`, `player-core/src/oversight/oversight-board.ts`), and a gold office pin at the headquarters of every agency an inspector general is auditing (`inquiryPins`, passed as the directive's `pins`; the camera frames their `office:` set, or the metro with none); the directive composer (`directive-composer.ts`: `directiveTargets`, and `directivePreview`, which reads the statutory limit, the status, the politicization and oversight rise and whether an inquiry opens off the packet `issueDirective` would commit); the inspectors general with the removal notice priced off `fileInspectorRemovalNotice` (`inspectorNoticeTerms`: when the office empties, the audits it obstructs, Congress's goodwill before and after); officers below the cabinet, each way of emptying the chair priced off its own `removeOfficer` packet (`removalTerms`, one column per statutory cause for a for-cause office); the appointments desk (`?view=appointments`; `&seat=<office>` narrows it to one seat, the door the Brief's vacancy rows use): `appointmentDesk` (`player-core/src/appointments/desk.ts`) lists every open Senate-confirmed seat, cabinet then agencies and boards then courts, each a `DeskSeat` whose `DeskCandidate`s carry the pool's reasons in words (`reason-words.ts`), a fit band against the list, competence with loyalty or integrity as bands, the Senate's count as a staff projection, vetting findings, who objects and the cost off the `openNomination` packet; `deskPicks` keeps the President's choices and gives every other seat but a Supreme Court one the best name nobody else is picked for; `deskPlan` sends them through `fillVacantOffices` and names any refusal; the web's `SeatRow` (`washington/appointments/`) opens a seat in place to its candidates as a `ChoiceList` against leaving it open, and one `CompareAuthorize` sends every chosen name through one review; and covert findings whose notice is still owed, sent through `notifyCommittees`.
- **Screen pins.** `MapDirective.pins` (and `Screen`'s `pins` and `marks`) carries pins a screen supplies itself; the map draws them as `office` marks (or `force` marks where the pin says `kind: "force"`) beside the campaign's incidents and forces, compared by id so a re-render sends no new directive.
- **Arcs.** `MapDirective.arcs` (and `Screen`'s `arcs`) carries lines between governments (`from`, `to`, `kind` trade, ally or rival, a 0..1 weight drawn as one of three strokes); `MapArcs.tsx` projects each endpoint from its region centroid, bows it upward by a share of its length capped in pixels, and hides it while the camera moves.

**The World and its act workspace (W7a).** `/world`, `/world/nation/:id` and `/world/nation/:id/act` are index and sibling routes (`routes/world.index.tsx`, `world.nation.$id.index.tsx`, `world.nation.$id.act.tsx`; there is no `world.tsx` layout). Everything on them is a player-core reader composed into one view model; the screens in `apps/web/src/world/nation/` carry no rules.

- `/world` (Spatial, camera `world`, `?lens=` standing | forces | trade, standing when absent): `worldRows` (`world/world-board.ts`) ranks every other government for the lens, which is the accessible twin of the map's own colors (`relations`, `forces`, and the new `trade-share` lens in `map/lenses/trade-share-lens.ts`), with `searchWorldRows` over name, capital and adjective. `worldWatchlist` gives the governments that moved (the relationship ledger with the United States first, then wars, acts and blocs on the wire, a disaster at home last), what is in flight (instrument operations, requests to Congress, treaties before the Senate, tariffs awaiting an answer) and every war with its intensity. Doors to organizations, wars and treaties go through `Place`, which draws words until the route exists (`useBuilt`).
- `/world/nation/:id` (Spatial, camera and selection `nation:<id>`, lens `relations`): `nationFile` (`world/nation-file.ts`) assembles the readers that each own one fact (`standingToward`, `nationVitals`, `bilateralTradePartners`, `nationForces`, `nationPosture`, `nationProgram`, `recentMoves`, `foreignCoverageReading`) into the five sections Overview, Relationship, Economy, Forces and Moves. `bilateralTradePartners` (`world/trade-partners.ts`) is the E6 selector: the United States' flow with the country, the measures in force on it (each US measure links to `?manage=` to be lifted) and the country's own largest partners by share of its trade. US forces inside the host come from `usForcesIn` (E2 placement) and are drawn as `force` pins; `nationFile.arcs` (trade partners, close partners, rivals) are drawn as `MapDirective.arcs` by `map/MapArcs.tsx`, an SVG layer projected from region centroids that follows the camera at rest like the pins. Arcs are decoration to a screen reader; their twins are the partner and tie lists in the panel.
- `/world/nation/:id/act` (Workspace): `actWorkspace` (`act/workspace.ts`) reads the address (`parseActConfig`: `goal`, `option`, `count`, `sector`, `rate`, `support`, `partners`, `strikeObjective`, `civilianProtection`, `manage`) and returns the objectives with their open acts (`catalogue.ts`), every act of the chosen objective priced beside the others (`previewOptions`), the terms the chosen act takes, and its assessment against doing nothing (`act/assessment.ts`, `assessment-ties.ts`: rows of baseline and act cells in words). `buildAct` (`act/build.ts`) builds the packet from the same campaign by `issueAction`, `engagementPacket`, `signTreaty`, `imposeTariff` or `issueForceAuthorization`, and `chargedBy` is tested equal to the money the comparison quoted for all twenty-five instrument rungs. `buildStandDown` (`withdrawInstrumentAct`) and `buildLift` (`liftMeasure`) end what is under way through the same compare-and-authorize shape. The web commit (`use-act-commit.ts`) commits with the campaign as `basis`, so a press on a board that has moved is refused. The receipt shows the money charged, the engine's answer (a declined meeting is receipted as refused), where the relationship stood before and after, and what moved at home.

**The screens of a set: wars, bodies and instruments (W7b).** `/world` is an index route (`routes/world.index.tsx`) so these render as its siblings. Each frames its members as a set (`set:nation:A|nation:B`, `unionBounds` in `map/camera-keys.ts`, which also keeps a member that straddles the prime meridian) and names a lens whose id carries its subject, as the whip count does (`map/lenses/set-lenses.ts`: `sides:<war id>` in side colors with principals in the body tone and joiners lit, `body:<org id>` by how each member stands toward the United States, `parties:<treaty id>`). Every reading is a player-core selector with a test: `warScreenReading` (`world/war-screen.ts`: sides, joiners and why, the front, support and exhaustion only where the services read the side, forces placed through `placeForces` and drawn as screen pins, the War Powers paper, the peace table, callable compacts, authorizations), `orgScreenReading` (`world/org-screen.ts`) and `treatyScreenReading` (`world/treaty-screen.ts`; an id is a signed record, a standing `compact:<org id>`, or a `draft:<kind>:<nation id>` that reads as the record once it is signed). Every act is a `CompareAuthorize` over an `ActComparison` built from the same engine call its packet comes from (`world/war-acts.ts`: join, authorization, cool, put terms, settle, leave, file the report, terminate force, call a compact; `treaty-screen.ts`: sign, send to the Senate, withdraw), committed through `world/set/SetAct.tsx` (`useNationCommit` underneath) with the receipt drawn at the head of the panel, because leaving a war removes the block that offered it.

Continue (`shell/ContinueControl.tsx`) runs `continueUntilAttention`, records the run for the delta lens (`shell/advance.ts`) and lands on the Brief unless the player has moved on while the save is written. `app/last-change.ts` keeps the sitting's last change (an act or a run: the campaign before and after, where it happened, the events it wrote), session-only; `Frame` hands the map its `before` while the `after` is still the campaign in play. A door to a screen a later wave adds is drawn as words (`shell/built-routes.ts`).

**Display settings reach the map through the root element.** `app/display.ts` writes `data-camera` (`fly | quick | cut`; Cut under reduced motion; Fly before the first administration and Quick after it unless chosen), `data-map-density` (`fine | standard | bold`), `data-contrast="high"` and `data-vision` (`protan | deutan | tritan`) on `<html>`, and `Screen` writes `data-sheet` (`peek | half | full`) when the phone sheet settles. `tokens.css` swaps inks and palettes on the last two; the map takes the same settings as props from `Frame`, and re-reads its palette when the root's `data-vision` or `data-contrast` changes (protan to tritan is one map variant over different tokens). It imports nothing from the shell.

**First paint and chunking.** The title screen is the first paint: the entry chunk holds React, the router, the shell, the map engine and the dataset tables the map names regions and frames keys with (`worldNations`, `stateById`, the federal sites). The engine, the rest of the dataset and the corpus load with the campaign store after the map's first frame; the map's campaign lenses (`lenses/registry.ts`) load the first time a directive names a non-neutral lens, and its pins (`marks/sources.ts`) with the first campaign (`lazy-module.ts`); the in-term chrome (`TermChrome.tsx`) and search with its glossary load when first shown. `@admin/world`, `@admin/engine`, `@admin/content` and `@admin/player-core` are `"sideEffects": false`, so taking one function does not pull a package; none has a side-effect-only import, and one added later would be dropped from the web build. `vite.config.ts` puts the baked corpus in a `corpus` chunk of its own. `scripts/measure-map-spike.mjs --first-paint` measures the title's first paint on a production build (`audits/2026-10-10-map-first/spike-w1.md`, "W3 integration").

**The planned player.** One persistent dotted map sits behind every screen, drawn on a Canvas 2D layer at the device pixel ratio with d3-geo runtime projection (a Natural Earth world view blending into azimuthal equal-area at country zoom and deeper). Marks (pins, capitals, offices, force clusters, incident pulses) are real DOM buttons projected from lon/lat. Every route declares a `Focus` (`world | region | nation | state | district | point | set | zone`) and a lens; a player-core resolver turns the focus into camera bounds, and a lens recolors the dots at nation, state or district level. Work happens in an opaque surface laid over the map: a docked panel on desktop, a bottom sheet on phone. Five modes (Brief, Washington, Nation, World, Record) carry the screens listed in DESIGN.md §2. Whether the runtime-projected canvas meets its performance budget is decided in the W1 spike; the fallback is a pre-baked plate with an affine camera.

**The map engine (`apps/web/src/map/`, built in W2).** The runtime-projected canvas passed the W1 spike (`audits/2026-10-10-map-first/spike-w1.md`) and is now a product layer. Since 2026-10-12 (DECISIONS §8.8) the engine is drawn in two places only: `MapView`, set inside a page on any screen where geography answers the question, and the screen-wide `MapLayer` the shell keeps for the title's turning globe. Both compose the same hooks (`gl/use-gl-runtime.ts`, `gl/use-gl-sync.ts`, `use-map-layer.ts` `useLenses`) and the same lens registry; a new reading is a new lens, never a new renderer. The page map uses the flat projection (the camera's world floor is projection-aware in `gl/use-gl-sync.ts`).

- **Contract.** A screen calls `useMapDirective({ camera, lens?, selection?, paused?, marks?, turning? })` (`directive.tsx`). `MapDataProvider` takes the store's `Campaign` or null, so the map draws with no administration (the title screen's turning world), and the campaign before the sitting's last change as `baseline`, which only the delta lens reads. `MapLayer` takes `insets` (what the rail, panel, sheet at its settled detent and tab bar cover), `settings` (`camera: fly | quick | cut`, `density`, `highContrast`, `palette: standard | color-vision`), and `onSelect(ref)`, `onActivateMark(mark)` and `onEmpty()` for the shell to route. `onSelect` returns whether the shell routed the choice; absent, or false, the map frames and detaches it itself until the next directive, and a click on empty sea drops that local choice before calling `onEmpty`.
