- **Camera** (`camera-keys.ts`, `target.ts`, `motion.ts`). `resolveCameraKey` turns `world`, `us`, `dc`, `office:<institution id>` (its `federalSitePoint`: inside the Washington metro it frames the metro focused on the site, outside it frames the metro's span around the site, and an institution with no recorded site or an unknown id frames the metro), `state:`, `district:`, `nation:`, `zone:` (a government or a `SEA-` zone), `point:<lon>,<lat>` and `set:<key>|<key>` into bounds; `keep` and unknown keys hold the current framing. Framing fits inside the visible area (`visibleArea`), and the US home view leaves a band for the Alaska and Hawaii insets (`insets.ts`, `UsInsets.tsx`). Moves follow the motion rules: Cut and reduced motion jump, a move inside the framing and within 1.5 zoom levels takes 250ms or less, Fly flies a long one (van Wijk, 600–1100ms) and Quick holds it to 350ms. Camera moves are sequenced, so a later directive overtakes an earlier one still loading.
- **Lenses** (`lenses/`). `MapLens` is the DESIGN.md contract: `level`, `scale`, `encoding`, `legend`, and `read(region, effective)` returning the value in words, a rank and a fill (solid with an optional magnitude radius, or a mix drawn as a proportional hatch); null is land outside the lens's subject (neutral), a no-data reading is dim. `resolveLens` builds `neutral`, `approval` (engine `stateApprovals`), `lean`, `call` (`projectedStateCalls`, split states hatched), `relations` (player-core `standingToward`), `forces` (player-core `placeForces`, radius by presence), `house` (delegation hatch at country zoom, district shapes at state zoom), `house-hex` (equal-area hex cartogram), `population`, `conditions` and `share-<domain>` (both built in `nation/`, above), and `delta` (each state's approval move from `baseline` to the campaign now, both ends in its reading; no data until something has changed); an unknown id is neutral. The layer names its resolved lens as `data-lens` on its root. The effective level is `planLevels`: never finer than the zoom supports, and never coarser than the selection needs. Colors come only from `--map-lens-*` properties in `map.css`, which point at design tokens and swap for the color-vision-safe and high-contrast palettes.

- **Lenses** (`lenses/`). `MapLens` is the DESIGN.md contract: `level`, `scale`, `encoding`, `legend`, and `read(region, effective)` returning the value in words, a rank and a fill (solid with an optional magnitude radius, or a mix drawn as a proportional hatch); null is land outside the lens's subject (neutral), a no-data reading is dim. `resolveLens` builds `neutral`, `approval` (engine `stateApprovals`), `lean`, `call` (`projectedStateCalls`, split states hatched), `relations` (player-core `standingToward`), `forces` (player-core `placeForces`, radius by presence), `house` (the campaign's apportioned delegations hatched at country zoom, each district's holder from `districtHolder` at state zoom; no seats before an administration is in office), `house-hex` (equal-area hex cartogram), `senate` (each state's two seats from its delegation), `whip:<chamber>:<bill id>` (a bill's whip count by delegation), `press-reach` (each state's regional paper by its stance), `population`, `governors` (each state's governor in four bands, Allied to Opposed, by disposition toward the President; player-core `lenses/governor-lens.ts`, also drawn on the universal player's `/nation` stage) and `delta` (each state's approval move from `baseline` to the campaign now, both ends in its reading; no data until something has changed); an unknown id is neutral. The layer names its resolved lens as `data-lens` on its root. The effective level is `planLevels`: never finer than the zoom supports, and never coarser than the selection needs. Colors come only from `--map-lens-*` properties in `map.css`, which point at design tokens and swap for the color-vision-safe and high-contrast palettes.
- **Detachment** (`detach.ts`, `frame-draw.ts`). Others fade to 22% (35% in high contrast); the selection lifts 6px at 1.03 scale as a block over 280ms: a cast shadow, an edge wall stacked to 5px in the seam's dark, its face in the full lens fill and hatch with a light tint, and a 1.5px glowing outline. No dot is drawn on a filled ground; the dot lattice draws only the title screen's turning globe. A state lifts with its districts.
- **Marks** (`marks/`, `MapMarks.tsx`). Incidents come from player-core `pinnableItems` and `placeOf`, forces from `clusterForces` at the zoom's force tier. They are real buttons, clustered in screen space, capped at 25 with "N more", in one `role="group"` with roving focus; a pan moves them by style without a React render, and they hide during a flight.

- **Lenses** (`lenses/`). `MapLens` is the DESIGN.md contract: `level`, `scale`, `encoding`, `legend`, and `read(region, effective)` returning the value in words, a rank and a fill (solid with an optional magnitude radius, or a mix drawn as a proportional hatch); null is land outside the lens's subject (neutral), a no-data reading is dim. `resolveLens` builds `neutral`, `approval` (engine `stateApprovals`), `lean`, `call` (`projectedStateCalls`, split states hatched), `relations` (player-core `standingToward`), `forces` (player-core `placeForces`, radius by presence), `house` (delegation hatch at country zoom, district shapes at state zoom), `house-hex` (equal-area hex cartogram), `population`, `state-population` and `delta` (each state's approval move from `baseline` to the campaign now, both ends in its reading; no data until something has changed); an unknown id is neutral. The layer names its resolved lens as `data-lens` on its root. The effective level is `planLevels`: never finer than the zoom supports, and never coarser than the selection needs. Where districts are drawn under a state lens, `readRegion` gives each district its own reading when the lens has one (lean) and its state's otherwise, said as statewide; the list still ranks states. Colors come only from `--map-lens-*` properties in `map.css`, which point at design tokens and swap for the color-vision-safe and high-contrast palettes.
- **Detachment** (`detach.ts`, `frame-draw.ts`). Others fade to 22% (35% in high contrast); the selection lifts 6px at 1.03 scale as a block over 280ms: a cast shadow, an edge wall stacked to 5px in the seam's dark, its face in the full lens fill and hatch with a light tint, and a 1.5px glowing outline. No dot is drawn on a filled ground; the dot lattice draws only the title screen's turning globe. A state lifts with its districts and asks for the district level, so its inner lines draw at state zoom. A district selected with its own state (`state:CO|district:CO-03`) detaches a second level (`focusedRegions`): the rest of the state steps back to 45% and the district draws at full strength with a 2.5px outline.
- **Marks** (`marks/`, `MapMarks.tsx`). Incidents come from player-core `pinnableItems` and `placeOf`, forces from `clusterForces` at the zoom's force tier, and a selection that detaches one state pins its capitol (`STATE_CAPITOLS`) and largest metros (`stateMetros`), named on the map and weighted under any incident. They are real buttons, clustered in screen space, capped at 25 with "N more", in one `role="group"` with roving focus; a pan moves them by style without a React render, and they hide during a flight.
- **Ground and borders** (`paint.ts`, `hatch.ts`, `frame-draw.ts`). `paint` on a filled ground returns `fills` (each drawn region's lens color, a mix's majority color, or quiet land) and `hatches` (each mix's colors and weights) and no dot buckets. `hatchPattern` draws a mix as 45° bands in proportion (`hatchBands`) on a seamless tile held to a 7px screen pitch and anchored to the plane, cached by colors, weights and device ratio. Borders draw twice: a 1.4px dark seam (`--map-lens-border`), then a light line (`--map-lens-line`, the `mapBoard.line` token) weighted by layer through `regionLayers`: world 1px, states 0.8px, districts 0.55px.
- **Labels** (`labels/`, `MapLabels.tsx`). `labelCandidates` projects every drawn region's label anchor (`Region.label`, the pole of inaccessibility `scripts/map-label-anchors.mjs` bakes into `regions.json` as `l`, with its area as `a`) and the on-screen extent of its bounds; `labelTexts` gives each region's forms longest first (a state's name and postal code, a seat's "PA-7 · Allentown" from `districtPlaceName` and its code, a government's name and an authored short form only where readers know one). `placeLabels` (pure, tested) places them greedily by priority (the selection, then area), taking the longest form the shape has room for or, for a state or a microstate at region zoom, a callout on a leader line, and drops any name that would touch another, a pin's box (`pinBoxes`) or the edge of the uncovered area; at most 16 under 768px and 48 above. Behind a selection the other districts are not named and other states and governments are named dim. The layer is `aria-hidden`, updates imperatively on the view bus without a React render, and hides during a flight.

- **Camera** (`camera-keys.ts`, `target.ts`, `motion.ts`). `resolveCameraKey` turns `world`, `us`, `dc`, `office:<institution id>` (its `federalSitePoint`: inside the Washington metro it frames the metro focused on the site, outside it frames the metro's span around the site, and an institution with no recorded site or an unknown id frames the metro), `state:`, `district:`, `nation:`, `zone:` (a government or a `SEA-` zone), `point:<lon>,<lat>`, `pin:<lon>,<lat>` (a few blocks across, for clustered office pins) and `set:<key>|<key>` into bounds; `keep` and unknown keys hold the current framing. Framing fits inside the visible area (`visibleArea`), and the US home view leaves a band for the Alaska and Hawaii insets (`insets.ts`, `UsInsets.tsx`). Moves follow the motion rules: Cut and reduced motion jump, a move inside the framing and within 1.5 zoom levels takes 250ms or less, Fly flies a long one (van Wijk, 600–1100ms) and Quick holds it to 350ms. Camera moves are sequenced, so a later directive overtakes an earlier one still loading.
- **Lenses** (`lenses/`). `MapLens` is the DESIGN.md contract: `level`, `scale`, `encoding`, `legend`, and `read(region, effective)` returning the value in words, a rank and a fill (solid with an optional magnitude radius, or a mix drawn as a proportional hatch); null is land outside the lens's subject (neutral), a no-data reading is dim. `resolveLens` builds `neutral`, `approval` (engine `stateApprovals`), `lean`, `call` (`projectedStateCalls`, split states hatched), `relations` (player-core `standingToward`), `forces` (player-core `placeForces`, radius by presence), `house` (delegation hatch at country zoom, district shapes at state zoom), `house-hex` (equal-area hex cartogram), `population` and `delta` (each state's approval move from `baseline` to the campaign now, both ends in its reading; no data until something has changed); an unknown id is neutral. The layer names its resolved lens as `data-lens` on its root. The effective level is `planLevels`: never finer than the zoom supports, and never coarser than the selection needs. Colors come only from `--map-lens-*` properties in `map.css`, which point at design tokens and swap for the color-vision-safe and high-contrast palettes.
- **Detachment** (`detach.ts`, `frame-draw.ts`). Others fade to 22% (35% in high contrast); the selection lifts 6px at 1.03 scale as a block over 280ms: a cast shadow, an edge wall stacked to 5px in the seam's dark, its face in the full lens fill and hatch with a light tint, and a 1.5px glowing outline. No dot is drawn on a filled ground; the dot lattice draws only the title screen's turning globe. A state lifts with its districts.
- **Marks** (`marks/`, `MapMarks.tsx`). Incidents come from player-core `pinnableItems` and `placeOf`, forces from `clusterForces` at the zoom's force tier, and offices (`marks: "offices"`) from `executivePins`, each carrying its staffing, whether something waits on the President, and the screen it opens; the mark whose id is the directive's camera key is ringed. They are real buttons, clustered in screen space, capped at 25 with "N more", in one `role="group"` with roving focus; a pan moves them by style without a React render, and they hide during a flight.
- **Accessible list** (`MapDataList.tsx`). A toggle that is visible on focus opens the lens as a ranked, captioned table (`lensRows`) with the pins listed as their marks' twins; arrow keys step through the rows and preview each on the map. Under forced colors the list replaces the canvas.
- **Renderer additions.** The pitch is capped at the DESIGN ceiling continuously (`octaveFor`), crowded centroid rings thin on fine lattices (`ringFor`), a touch pan never starts within 24px of the left or bottom edge and two pointers pinch (`renderer-input.ts`), a paused map stops its loop and draws no motion, but a framing, selection or color change while paused lands at once as one new still frame (so a founding that names a home state lights it behind the workspace, and a deep link to a workspace screen still paints), and the title screen turns the world by panning and re-projecting every four seconds under a crossfade.
- **Harness.** `/dev/map` mounts the real `MapLayer` under every directive, setting and campaign (`harness/`); it exists in a dev server, and in a production build only with `VITE_MAP_HARNESS=1` for `scripts/measure-map-spike.mjs`.

**Engine work the map needs** (PROGRAM.md, "Engine and data changes"): derived state approval (`regional-opinion.ts`, save v55), force placement by zone (built: §4g, save v56), sourced federal sites and state capitols, one `placeOf(item)` for incidents, scenario places that resolve to a state rather than an offset from DC, and the player-core selectors each wave needs. State approval and force placement are built; the rest is tracked in `docs/BACKLOG.md`.

**Boundaries.** One route tree per player that adapts on width, never on a platform flag (DECISIONS.md §8.5). Electron and Steam stay behind a typed `administrationDesktop` bridge. `scripts/verify-renderer-boundary.mjs` (with its proof) keeps engine, world, content, contracts, player-core and design free of React, React Native, Expo and Tamagui. It keeps `packages/ui` free of routers, hosts and the simulation, keeps Electron and Steamworks out of `apps/player`, and keeps React Native, Expo and Tamagui out of the legacy `apps/web` and `packages/ui-web`.

| Module | Responsibility |
|---|---|
| `packages/player-core/src/campaign/store.ts` | The only state holder. Every transition delegates to the engine and republishes an immutable `Campaign`. |
| `packages/player-core/src/campaign/founding-options.ts` | Derives every founding choice from `worldDataset`. |
| `packages/player-core/src/persistence/save-store.ts` | Local save against a `StoragePort`. One manifest plus one payload per administration, up to `MAX_ACTIVE_CAMPAIGNS` (5). |
| `packages/player-core/src/drafts/` | The President's unsigned changes (`createDraftStore` over the campaign store). A draft is keyed by subject (`budget`, `tariff:<nation>`) and holds its spec, the authority that acts on it (`on-signing`, `congress`, `senate`), a `CampaignStamp` basis (seed, turn, date, ledger length and a hash of the newest ledger record, because a reload replaces the campaign object the store's own guard compares) and its priced preview with the law it cites. `review` reprices on the campaign in play and freezes the packets; `sign` commits exactly those through `commit`/`commitAll` with that campaign as `basis`, so stale terms are refused as for every act and a draft revised after its review is refused too. A signing mark is written beside the draft before the commit and kept with the device's reason when the save fails: a reload that finds the campaign further along drops the draft, one that finds it at the signing basis makes it pending again, so nothing is signed twice. Drafts persist beside the save under `campaign:drafts:<id>` (`SaveStore.readDrafts`/`writeDrafts`, bound to a named slot and deleted with it), never inside the engine's save. Each draft is signed on its own: separate powers never commit as one. |
| `packages/player-core/src/persistence/payload-codec.ts` | How a payload is kept: the save JSON gzipped through the platform `CompressionStream` and stored as `gz1:` plus base64, so the string port, IndexedDB and the desktop's atomic files are unchanged. A payload without the marker is read as plain JSON and rewritten compressed on its next save. At 196 governments a 208-week save is 12.3–12.9 MB of JSON and 1.8–1.9 MB stored. |
| `packages/player-core/src/navigation/places.ts` | The route tree's typed destinations. |
| `packages/player-core/src/preferences/preferences-store.ts` | The device's display and attention settings, outside the save: camera, map density, high contrast, color-vision palette, attention per kind. Unknown values read as absent. |
| `apps/web/src/shell/ContinueControl.tsx` | The one control that spends time. Held while a decision or a pausing matter waits (`verify-no-silent-skip` holds the guard); runs `continueUntilAttention` against the campaign it was shown and lands on the Brief, which says how far time moved and what stopped it. |
| `packages/player-core/src/desk/brief-matters.ts` | What needs the President: decisions, every owed row in the composer's order, and running stories still unanswered; which hold the week; a countdown only where a real window runs; each row's door and put-off. |
| `packages/player-core/src/conversation/scenario-dispositions.ts`, `decision-silence.ts` | The price of leaving a decision unanswered (`silencePrice`), the dispositions its case declares besides a reply and their packets, and `silenceLine`, the clock line said beside the answers (no do-nothing column, DECISIONS §8.13). |
| `packages/player-core/src/record/record-entry.ts`, `impact-reports.ts` | The Record's rows and one entry read whole (readings before and after, judgments, causes and consequences, place), a run's or an act's moves, and impact assessments from filed reports only. |
| `packages/player-core/src/desk/now-readings.ts`, `approval-breakdown.ts`, `campaign/stop-words.ts` | The Brief's five readings with their week's move, removal pressure against the proceedings' lines, approval's six judgments with the week's move, and why a run stopped in words. |
| `packages/player-core/src/world/nation-overview.ts`, `state-dossier.ts`, `locale-act-options.ts` | The country by state (approval spread, electors, ranked rows), one state's dossier, and the locale acts priced off the engine's own packets beside waiting. |
| `apps/web/src/founding/seal.ts` | The oath: the preview seal on the oath stage, the seal itself (reusing the preview when the draft has not moved), then the day-one orders, each signed against the campaign the last one left. |

**Persistence.** IndexedDB in the browser and atomic files under `userData/saves/` in Electron. Machine settings stay out of `saves/` so Steam Cloud can sync campaigns without them (DECISIONS.md §9.1).

**Build.** `pnpm --filter @admin/web run build` emits static assets. Hosted deploys need an SPA rewrite to `index.html` (`apps/web/vercel.json`, `apps/web/public/_redirects`). `pnpm --filter @admin/desktop start` loads that bundle. Vite `base` is `/` so nested deep links keep resolving `/assets` from the origin.

**The security desk and the combatant commands (W7c).** `/world` is an index route (`routes/world.index.tsx`), so `/world/security` and `/world/security/command/:id` render as its siblings. Both screens are drawn over the forces lens (`lens="forces"`, `marks="forces"`): `forceMarks` clusters `placeForces` by zoom, one marker per command from afar, one per zone at a region's scale, one per installation up close, and nothing a save stores. Every act is a player-core `ChoiceTable` against a do-nothing column, drawn through `ChoiceCompare` with a docked verb, committed with `useNationCommit` against the campaign the table was priced on, and received with `ActReceipt`. A price on a table is read off the packet the act commits (`packetCost`, or the campaign `apply()` makes of it), so the price shown is the price charged.

- `/world/security` (`world/security/`, Hybrid, camera `world`; wide while an act is open): `securityBoard` (`player-core/src/security/security-board.ts`) composes today's items by urgency (`dailyItems` over `pdbReading`), the eleven commands (`commandCards`: posture ordered and held, commander, the chain's execution, forces abroad against the largest command, never a head count), readiness by service (`serviceReadiness` over `nationForces`), the War Powers paper still open (`warPowersRows`), force authorizations before Congress and in force (`authorizationRows`), covert findings (`covertReading`), standing collection (`collectionReading`), assessments (`assessmentRows`) and commitments (`commitmentsReading` with `commitmentWeeklyCost`). Pointing at or focusing a geographic command sets the map's selection to its countries (`commandSelection`), which lights the area through the ordinary selection set (`nation:A|nation:B`). The acts: sign a finding in each of its three notice tiers or close it (`covert-reading.ts`: `signChoices`, `closeFindingChoices`; a finding counsel will not paper is still committed, as the engine records the attempt, and is receipted as refused); task a collection or take an element off a question (`collection-reading.ts`: `taskChoices` says what the community does, including a declined tasking and its oversight and inspector-general price; a second tasking of one element on one day is locked because the engine would replace the first); commission one of the three assessment questions or press a judgment either way (`assessment-reading.ts`: commissioning shows who writes it and how sure it will be, and withholds what it will say; pressing prices the building off the packet's institution change); and resume, suspend or wind down a commitment (`commitment-reading.ts`, trust and tension read off the packet's relationship effect).
- `/world/security/command/:id` (`CommandScreen`, Spatial): `commandFile` reads one command: its area (`commandAreas`, amendments included) as the camera (`commandCamera`, a `set:` key of `nation:` and `zone:` members) and as the selection that set-detaches it, forces by zone and base from `placeForces` (a sourced anchor is named, anything else is labeled zone-level and never given a site), the assignment shares by service, the chain's breaks, and the amendments to its map. The four acts are in `command-acts.ts`: `postureChoices` (the weekly readiness bill is `POSTURE_READINESS_COST`, the relief in a war `POSTURE_PRESSURE_RELIEF`, and an engaged theater stays heightened), `reliefChoices` (the chain's execution before and after, read by applying the relief), `reassignChoices` and `amendChoices`.
- **Fixture and gates.** `scripts/fixtures/security-desk.mjs` builds on `war-joined` with a finding, a tasking and an assessment; `tests/world-security.spec.ts` runs it at all six widths. The ledger rows `setCommandPosture`, `relieveCommander`, `reassignForces`, `amendUnifiedCommandPlan`, `setCommitmentStatus`, `signFinding`, `closeFinding`, `taskCollection`, `closeTasking`, `commissionAssessment`, `pressAssessment` and `commitmentWeeklyCost` are reached from these screens.

## 7a. Player logic beside the engine

These parts of the simulation and of player-core are what the map-first screens read.

### Where an incident is

`packages/world/src/dataset/places.ts` is the one place resolver: `placeAt({ nation, state?, city? })` returns a `Place` (`nation`, `level`, `lat`, `lon`, `name`, optional `state` and `city`) at the named city (`level: "city"`), else the state's largest dataset city (`level: "state"`, so one state never yields two points), or the state's capitol from `STATE_CAPITOLS` where the gazetteer has no city in it (Delaware, Montana and Wyoming: `level: "state"`, no `city`, named "Helena, Montana"), else the nation's own point (`level: "nation"`, keeping the `state` it was asked about). It answers nothing only for a nation the dataset does not know. `cityPlace`, `statePlace` and `largestCityIn(state, fits?)` are its parts. It sits in the dataset because the content resolver and player-core both read it and neither may import the other.

`packages/player-core/src/geo/place-of.ts` is the player's side: `placeOf(item)` over the `PinnableItem` union — a `condition` (`NationCondition`, by its own city or state), an `emergency` (`DeclaredEmergency`, the state it reaches or the nation), and a `mark` or `claim` (`GrievanceMark` / `PendingClaim`, by the state that holds it) — and `pinnableItems(campaign)`, every live one in a declared order (active conditions and standing emergencies by id, then each state's marks and claims in `STATE_IDS` order). A scenario bound to a condition is placed by the same `placeAt`, so the wildfire, the emergency declared over it and the week about it land on one point. Delaware, Montana and Wyoming have no dataset city, so their items pin at their capitols, and `verify-incident-places` (+ proof) fails an item there that lands anywhere else.

### Where the government sits

`packages/world/src/dataset/federal-sites.ts` gives every engine institution its headquarters, keyed by institution id so the dataset never reads the engine: `federalSitePoint(id)` is the point the map-first Washington camera frames for an `office:<id>` key (DESIGN.md §2, Washington), or undefined where the row records `location: "none"` with its reason (13 of 76: the judiciary as a whole, the states, the career service, and ten bodies no opened record gives an address or coordinate for). A location is never inherited from a parent: the military departments stand at the Pentagon because their own records say so, and INR stands nowhere because no record of its own names a building. `dataset/state-capitols.ts` gives the 50 states and DC their capitols, which `placeAt` falls back to. The dataset validator's `checkSites` (`validate/site-checks.ts`) holds every point inside the state it names on the baked Census outline (`maps/state-outlines.ts`, which projects a point through the same `geoAlbersUsa` the outlines were baked with) and every cabinet department to a point; the engine's `institution-sites.test.ts` holds the table to `INSTITUTION_CATALOG` both ways. `FEDERAL_SITE_CONSUMERS` and `STATE_CAPITOL_CONSUMERS` in `stat-consumers.ts` record the readers; the site's name is the office file's headquarters line and the office pins' place. Sources: `packages/world/sources/founding-data/federal-sites-sources.md`.

`scripts/verify-incident-places.mjs` (+ proof) holds it from the engine's own types: every record the `Campaign` keeps that names a place (a field typed `StateId`, `CityId` or `EmergencyScope`) and every list a `StateRuntime` keeps is either a kind `placeOf` answers for or is named in the gate as not a pin, with the reason (a person's governor overlay, a construction project's host state, and a `ScenarioPlace`, which points the next scene of a story at where the last one happened and is not an incident of its own). It then resolves every condition kind at nation scope, in every state and in every dataset city, an emergency for the nation and every state, and a mark and a claim in every state, and fails any item with no finite point, a city item off its city, a state item outside its state where the state has a city, or a state item off its capitol where it has none.

### Conversations: the decision model

A conversation is how a decision reaches the player. `packages/engine/src/conversations.ts` owns the model (the `ConversationScript` type sits beside it in `conversation-script.ts` and is re-exported from it); DESIGN.md's decision screen (`/brief/decision/:id`) renders it.

| Concept | Shape | Note |
|---|---|---|
| `ConversationScript` | id, counterpart nation, subject, opening beats, replies, `briefingDoubted?`, `locales?` (where the matter happens, stamped onto the answer's provenance) | Authored content. Nothing in the module composes prose. `briefingDoubted` names a thread whose opening is drawn from an unconfirmed brief, so the doubt reaches the player on every course before they choose rather than only in the verdict afterward. |
| `SpeakerRef` | `cabinet(role)` \| `player` \| `counterpart` \| `narrator` | A beat names an **office**, never a person. |
| `ReplyCommitment` | `instrument(instrument, rung)` \| `position(cost, pull, remembered)` | An instrument reply goes down the ladder; a position is words that still cost standing at home and are remembered abroad. |
| `ReplyProjection` | availability + missing capability, cost, success chance, risks, ripples, ripple visibility, `contest?`, `deterrence?`, `conflict?`, `consultation?`, `capacity?`, `exposure?`, `exhausted?` | One shape whether or not there is anything to roll, so a surface cannot quietly omit a trade-off. `cost` is no longer the authored magnitude alone — it is clamped to what the current board can actually pay, so it reads as what `apply()` will really charge; `exhausted` names the readings that hit their floor on the way there. `exposure` carries a position's `PositionExposure` (above) where an instrument carries `risks`. |
| `ConversationBeat` | resolved speaker + text | What the surface renders, including the consequence beats after a reply. |

`commitReply(campaign, script, option, streams, extras?)` threads the same `ResolutionExtras` the resolver takes, and supplies the reply's own `tone` as authoring context — the register the player answered in is a fact only the conversation knows. When a card is drawn its beats are spoken as separate narrator beats in the thread rather than as one joined summary; with no pool, the thread reads exactly as before.

**Speaker truth.** `resolveSpeaker` reads the campaign's own cabinet, so the office is filled by whoever actually holds it and a vacant or dead minister's chair renders as an empty chair rather than having words put in their mouth.

**The trade-off gate.** `assertPlayable` — run by `openConversation`, so no surface can render past it — rejects a thread with fewer than two replies, replies in a single tone, or a position reply whose cost is empty or explicitly zero. It is the surface-side half of the content rule (DECISIONS.md §7.4): a costless decision fails loudly, the way `apply()` rejects a packet with no narration.

**In player-core.** `packages/player-core/src/conversation/scripts.ts` draws the pending decision from the scenario pool (§5) when the desk is open; the player authors no story of its own. `counsel-lines.ts` composes each minister's structured opinion into the sentence beside the price, `labels.ts` names stances and reasons in words, `filing.ts` dates the decision to the day the President first saw it (the earliest put-off on the ledger, else today), and `thread-dedupe.ts` drops an opening beat that restates a figure the particulars grid already shows. The decision screen heads the thread like a briefing file: dated, classified by severity, with the scenario's whole `ScenarioContext` (standing, precedent, pressures, the price of inaction, what nobody knows). The open thread is **pinned**: committing changes the campaign, which can change the next draw, and the thread being read must never swap out from under the reader. Committing goes through `store.commit(rememberReply(...))` → `apply()` → save, so the consequence, the memory flags, the closed desk, and any press headline land on the record before the thread shows them.

**Feed kinds** include `press` (`FeedItem.kind`): the papers' reaction to a big answer, written per reply.

### Foreign visibility and the fog

**NPC-to-NPC legibility and intelligence-gated visibility.** A nation dossier's relations reading lists a nation's relationships with every other nation it holds a standing link to, via `selectRelationshipsFrom`, ranked by `|trust| + tension` so the sharpest alignments and rivalries surface first. What actually renders is gated by `hasIntelOnRelationship(campaign, relationship)` (`packages/engine/src/state.ts`): the player's own relationship with any nation is always legible — it is lived diplomatic experience, not something to spy on — but an NPC-to-NPC relationship is legible only once the player's `intelCapability` outreads the pair's average `counterIntel`, the same "outread" comparison `npc-turn.ts`'s `feedVisibility` already applies per-action (§3), applied here to standing state instead of a single event. `selectDiscoverableRelationshipsFrom(campaign, from)` is the gated selector the dossier renders through; the ungated `selectRelationshipsFrom` stays exported for callers that need the true graph rather than the player's view of it (`npc-agent.ts` scoring NPC decisions, `scenario-cast.ts` casting scenarios). Like feed visibility, this is a live computation over current stats, not stored per-relationship knowledge — it can change turn to turn as `intelCapability` or `counterIntel` move (`operators.ts` can degrade a host's `counterIntel`). The normal reading shows only discoverable relationships and states that unknown ties may exist. It never prints a count of hidden edges, because that count itself would disclose unobserved information.

The same outread comparison, run against a single government rather than a pair, is `hasIntelOnNation(campaign, nation)` — what the player's services can read of what a foreign government holds *internally*, its own assessments rather than its dealings with third parties. It gates the dossier's "their file on you" reading (§3), so reading another state's read of you costs the same capability that reading its dealings does. Both selectors reduce to `outreads()`, one god-mode (`information.omniscient`) short-circuit and one "an unknown service is assumed covered" default. They spend different margins out of the player's `intelCapability`: 0.20 for a pair's average cover, 0.42 for one government's own counsel, and shared organization membership grants no private-government access. The capability gate remains a country-level abstraction; explicit liaison and per-fact access are pending. Historical coverage counts from the former membership exception do not describe this gate; `verify-foreign-truth` measures the current roster.

The one exception is the temperament band (`player-core reasons/temperament-words.ts`): for a read government, each of the eight traits is placed among the world's authored values at quintile cuts and said as a comparison ("quicker to use force than most governments"), never a figure; a thin or standard record's bands carry the file's provenance sentence. The player's own nation is always legible to itself. The reading keeps the two silences apart exactly as the relations card does: intelligence that has not reached inside that government, versus a file that is genuinely close to empty because too little has been asked of this administration on the record.

**Foreign behavioral traits are assessment-gated, not readable state.** The eight `NationTraits` are rolled per campaign in `founding.ts` and are the literal coefficients `npc-agent.ts` multiplies. No surface may read one. `assessments.ts`'s behavior question turns them into a `Likelihood` word plus a `Confidence` plus named gaps, and the country file draws that only where `hasIntelOnNation` is true; where it is false it draws none rather than eight hedged guesses. The player's own government is directly observed at full confidence — self-knowledge is not collection.

**What the fog withholds is the unobservable half.** Under `visibility === "fogged"` no reading of a foreign scalar renders as a percentage anywhere in `nation-forces.ts` or `nation-behavior.ts` — composites band, counted quantities are withheld, the matchup drops its paired scores and its odds. Two figures are exempt and named in the gate: our own side of a contest layer, and the dial weight saying how much of the call a layer is.

**One epistemic contract.** `packages/engine/src/reading.ts` carries `Reading`: band, source, confidence, optional range and gaps. No player part draws an engine `Reading` yet; people read as a band plus a confidence through player-core's `judgeAttribute` (four pips, the band word and low, moderate or high confidence), never as a percentage. The confidence ladder is `Confidence` in `packages/engine/src/assessments.ts`; the design system declares none. `state-legislature.ts` keeps a literal copy of the ladder, and no gate refuses another.

**Acting on a foreign government is one workspace** (DESIGN.md, `/world/nation/:id/act`). The options come from player-core `act/catalogue.ts`, which sorts the engine's own tables into five goals: every `INSTRUMENT_RUNGS` rung by a rule on its instrument (assistance → build ties, diplomatic → pressure, economic rungs one and two → trade and three up → pressure, covert → covert, military → force), every `ENGAGEMENT_KINDS` kind and `TREATY_TERMS` kind under build ties, the sector tariff under trade and the request for an authorization of force under force. `act/preview.ts` prices each card off the projection its act would be committed on (`projectAction` at the chosen force package, `engagementReading` and `engagementHold`, `signTreaty` with `projectRatification`, `tariffIncidence`, `projectForceAuthorization`), and `act/plan.ts` reads the relationship moves out of the same packets, so the workspace prices nothing itself. A military card's `basis` states the projection's `costBasis` in words (`act/force-cost-words.ts`): what is sent, the price of each, any cost-index or distance adjustment, the sum charged now and the weekly bill after. `engagementHold` (engine `engagement.ts`) says which gate keeps a meeting off the table, so a held card can say why instead of disappearing.

**Options are compared on what they move, and a locked one names its floor.** `act/option-effects.ts` reads what each option of the goal moves off the packet the engine would write: a meeting off `projectEngagement` (or `ENGAGEMENT_TERMS` scaled by `warmthScale` while it is held), a treaty off `TREATY_TERMS.effect` on both edges plus the signature packet's metric effects, an instrument rung off engine `resolution.ts#projectSuccessEffects` (the success half of `resolveAction`'s resolve with no draw and no gate, so a locked rung can say what it would do). Each `EffectMark` carries the engine's delta for drawing only, a band word for printing (`metricImpact`'s slight/moderate/major), and a `share` of `EFFECT_SCALE`, the largest single relationship step any `TREATY_TERMS` entry takes, so every option draws on one scale. `act/prerequisite.ts` reads the gate that closed a held option: the engagement holds carry their reading and floor (`host-distrusts` their trust against `VISIT_HOST_TRUST`, `too-little-trust` both sides against `DECLARATION_TRUST`, `too-calm` tension against `SUMMIT_TENSION`, which is not buildable), a treaty's floor is `TREATY_TERMS.minTrust` on our edge and past it `treaty-counterpart.ts#counterpartTrustFloor` on theirs (where their trust would have to stand for `counterpartWillingness` to clear, other terms held), and a rung's comes back on its refused gate as `ProjectionResult.shortfall` (`instrument-gates.ts#GateShortfall`, set by `requireAtLeast`'s optional reading; only `trustAtLeast` passes one, since reserves and readiness are not built by an act toward this government). Their side reads as unknown without intelligence on them. The builders are the goal's open options whose marks raise the unmet reading, largest step first. `ActWorkspace.compared` holds the options the address's `compare` list names, each with its assessment against doing nothing, and `then` the locked option a building act was opened for; the receipt re-reads that option on the board after the act and leads back to it.

**The universal act workspace reads every goal at once.** `actWorkspace` reads every option in `ACT_OPTIONS` for its effects and prerequisites, not only the filtered goal, so an act from any goal can build a locked one and a compared option outside the filter keeps its readings (measured at 7 to 23 ms a call on the opening campaign). `ActConfig.goal` is now a filter: `sections` lists the goals it shows (all of them when it is unset), each with its families, and `chosen` is the option the address names whatever the filter. `OptionPreview.money` (`ActMoney`: `now`, `weekly`, `perYearIn`, USD millions) carries the money as figures, read off the projection's `cost.treasury` and `costBasis.weekly` for an instrument rung and off `tariffIncidence().revenue` for a tariff, so a list can draw it on one scale and a recurring act never reads cheaper than a one-time one. `apps/player/src/world/act/ActWorkspace.tsx` composes three Sections (where things stand, options, plan) and a bar pinned under the page's scroll that carries the one verb; `OptionList.tsx` draws options with `packages/ui/src/review/Results.tsx` (`ResultsList`, `ResultRow`, `OddsSteps`, `MoneyBar`, `ChangeBar`), each reading a change from today, with no do-nothing row (DECISIONS §8.13). The assessment's baseline cells stay in player-core for the surfaces that still deal cards. `SectionNav.web.tsx` calls `Element.prototype.scrollTo` because React Native Web replaces `scrollTo` on its scroll views with its own signature. Every open option also carries its full `assessment` (about 10 ms for the whole list), so a row can draw its risks and reactions before it is picked, and `OptionEffects.notes` says what an act starts that no reading shows (a War Powers report, read off the `warPowers` effect's `open` obligation). `marksOf` reads military pressure from either edge of the pair, since the engine writes an act's pressure on the actor's edge. A tariff order may cover several goods from one country: `ActConfig.goods` (`goods=steel:20,cars:15` in the address) lists them, and `act/tariff-package.ts` reads and builds the order as a fold (`readTariffPackage`, `buildTariffPackage`), each good priced and built by `buildAct` on the campaign the goods before it leave after `apply()`, so measure and retaliation ids stay distinct. The packets commit as one run through `authorizeAct` (`Prepared` takes an array) and one review. One law covers the whole order; the general plan model, a package header in the Record and booked revenue wait on BL-172, BL-174 and BL-178.

### Taking office: founding

Founding has eight stages in `FOUNDING_STAGE_IDS` order: person, record, politics, ticket, platform, staff, cabinet, oath. The nation, the Constitution and the seat of government are settled facts the player inherits, so what is left to author is who the President is, who runs with them, what they promise, and who they seat. The logic is in `packages/player-core/src/campaign/` (`founding-stages.ts`, `founding-options.ts`, `founding-preview.ts`); the map-first screens are rebuilt in W3 (DESIGN.md, `/founding`).

**One draft, sealed early.** The draft holds only what the player chose and re-derives every default the way the seal does, so an untouched field and a field set to its default seal the same campaign. `stageStanding` and `blockedStages` give each stage its status. `previewFounding` validates and then seals, so what the preview shows equals what the oath produces by construction; `openingFor` adds the opening position with the career hook lines exactly as the seal does. Every refusal is a `FoundingRejection` sentence from `validateFounding`, platform refusals included, routed to the stage that owns it. Shortlists are ranked by `rankBench` for the President being composed.

**The President is a biography the player writes, not a candidate they pick.** The person, record and politics stages ask name, face, home state, presentation and pronouns; the career (`LeaderBackground`, eight US backgrounds) and the route to office (`LeaderRoute`, three); and the three ideology axes and the party. The five readings are shown read-only, because they are the *consequence* of those answers: `composeLeader` runs on every change and again at the seal. There are no free stat sliders: a slider lets a player buy a leader who is masterful, devoted, incorruptible and beloved at once, which is a person no career produces and no rule was written to score. See SCHEMA.md §"The player is composed, not drawn". The face is built, not picked, and stored as a `face:` reference that ages with the President across the term (§4b). An uploaded image held as the device-local `portraitUri` wins wherever the President's face is drawn.

**The ticket, the platform and the staff are recorded; only the platform reaches the simulation.** The running mate comes from `runningMateSlate` (co-partisans, balanced against the President), the platform from `platformPlanks` with up to three planks promoted to first-hundred-days pledges (`plankFitsFirstHundredDays`), and the Chief of Staff, the National Security Adviser and the Press Secretary from `staffSlate`. A post left open is filled with the best co-partisan fit at the seal, and `founding.staff` records who was seated. The mandate is a choice too, and sets the Congress the President inherits. No system reads the Vice President or the three staff after the seal: `ticketBalance` only orders the running-mate shortlist. The Senate read on a candidate is `projectConfirmation`, which weighs the nominee's competence and integrity and the chamber's disposition, not party; opening approval is the same on every founding. `differenceFromSeated` names the one graded reading where a candidate parts from the person seated in the post.

**The opening position** is what makes the cabinet a decision. `openingPosition` returns four figures with what each is worth against the country's own reading, then every contribution ranked by size, each with the in-fiction sentence the engine returned with it. Nothing is computed in the player, so what the player is shown at founding and what the campaign starts from cannot drift apart.

**There is no flag step.** The player's flag is the real one the `Flag` primitive resolves from the nation id, so there is nothing to author and nothing to store.

### Administrations and saves

**A device holds five administrations** (DECISIONS.md §9.2). `MAX_ACTIVE_CAMPAIGNS` in `packages/player-core/src/persistence/save-store.ts` is the limit, enforced in the store rather than on a button: founding, duplicating from the archive, the dev seed and a recovery write all end in `create`, so a rule guarding one door holds for all of them. The cap gates founding and never reading: a device already holding more keeps every campaign listed, loadable and playable, and cannot found another until it is back under five. A damaged, retired-world or retired-calendar save counts against the five, because it occupies a slot and a payload on the device.

**Every write is bound to the campaign it came from.** The store captures the id in play before its first await and serializes every manifest mutation through one queue, so a save of one administration can't land on another's payload key, and the five-slot limit can't be raced.

### Policy: the standing program

`packages/engine/src/policy.ts`.

Conversations are reactive: the world raises something and the player answers. The instrument ladder projects power at another country. Appointments decide who runs a ministry but not what the ministry is *for*. Policy is what this administration is actually trying to do at home.

Every domain the charter allows always holds exactly one stance, so the program is the government's whole posture rather than a list of things to buy. Each domain names the chair carrying it and what that chair's competence is worth (`×1.34` on a 92% treasury minister), because a program is only as good as the hand on it. The approval the program is weighed against is the projection's own `approvalNow`, not a second copy of the weights.

A charter that seats no minister for a domain names which chairs it does not create.

Wired into the turn loop as `policyPhase` beside the NPC, operator and cabinet phases (registered in `packages/engine/src/turn-phases.ts`). It files **one** packet a turn for the whole program rather than one per domain: six ledger entries a week saying "policy happened" is noise the record has to be read around. The packet carries `actor: "player"` — a standing program working another week is the player's own decision still being paid for, not something the world did to them.

**A stance carries a price at the point of choosing.** `projectPolicyStance(campaign, charter, domain, stanceId)` → `PolicyStanceProjection`: `available` and (where it is not) `missing`, `enactment` (paid once, the week it is signed) and `perTurn` (what holding it costs every week after) kept separate because they are different questions, the `chair` answerable for it with the seated `minister` and their `quality`, the stance's `alignment` pull, `approvalNow`, and one `reasoning` sentence. It runs through `projectPolicyDraft` rather than beside it, so the price shown and the price charged cannot drift, and it is pure and seed-independent like every other projection. `projectPolicyDraft` takes an optional `charter` for the same reason this one requires it: its refusals name a seat, and a seat is named by the charter's own `roleTitles` ("Secretary of the Treasury") rather than by splitting the neutral role id apart ("treasury minister") — the ids are a cross-charter contract and the words in them are not English anybody governs in.

**Fiscal policy is gated on statute, not on the direct path every other domain keeps.** A president does not set the tax and entitlement program by picking a position — revenue bills originate in the House, the President proposes rather than enacts, and tax law is permanent statute. `revenueStatuteRequired(campaign, domain)` refuses `enactPolicyDraft` on the `fiscal` domain until `act-tax-relief-and-growth` has passed the Hill, naming the bill and telling the President to transmit it and take the count; every other domain still changes on the President's own word. The refusal is a route rather than a wall: the program waits on the count, and the balance population plays it that way, retrying what it has not got each week and transmitting the named bill whenever the Hill will take one up. The enactment lump asks `obligationAvailability` — like every other unilateral obligation — only where it spends anything: `enactPolicyDraft` skips the guard entirely when the lump is zero, because a program that obligates no money is a change of posture, and a lapse in appropriations does not stop a President from changing their mind about a stance that costs nothing to hold. Where it does spend, a program the authority cannot cover is refused with the shortfall named rather than booked against the floor. Trade is the audit's other named revenue family and is deliberately not gated the same way: tariff schedules are the one revenue power Congress has genuinely delegated to the President, so `imposeTariff` carries the statutory authority it is already taken under rather than a bill nobody wrote.

See SCHEMA.md §7b for the stored shape, the gating rules, and how competence scales delivery.

### The estimates: what the state buys itself

`packages/engine/src/budget.ts`.

Every rung of the instrument ladder is gated on a capability figure — `softPower.diplomaticReach`, `economy.reserves`, `security.intelCapability`, `military.branchStrength.*` and the lift capacities. Without a budget route, a country would have exactly the reach it was authored with, permanently: a policy stance nudges only four of the sixteen paths it owns, and `military.branchStrength`, `airliftCapacity` and `sealiftCapacity` have no other writer outside authoring.

The appropriation is that route. Four statecraft accounts, **one per instrument**, so money buys reach on a ladder the player already reads — and a fifth line that is not an instrument at all (below). Each holds a standing annual **share of GDP** rather than a sum: reach, tradecraft and readiness are qualities of an institution, and what buys them is the fraction of national income a state commits — so a 26-million-person country that spends like an intelligence state gets an intelligence service, and the giant next door has to spend the same share to climb at the same rate. Size still prices everything the ladder gates on absolutely (an embargo needs trade to withhold), which is the part that should depend on size; what stops depending on it is ambition.

Three properties make it a decision rather than a shop:

- **A sustainment line.** Every account costs `sustainAtFull × level` a year just to hold, where `level` is the weighted state of the account's own targets. An appropriation below it is a drawdown — the services shrink, the corps loses posts, the fleet rusts — so spending nothing is a decision with consequences. A founding opens at exactly sustainment (`sustainingBudget`), which is why "hold what I inherited" is a position the player can take rather than the one position they cannot.
- **Only the change reaches the treasury.** The establishment a country already keeps is funded out of revenue this game does not model, so `turnCost` is the *net* of appropriation against sustainment: building costs money weekly, cutting returns it weekly, holding is free. Charging the whole establishment would bill the player twice for the country they were handed.
- **Arrears are real.** A vote larger than the treasury can carry builds only the fraction it could pay for and settles the rest in stability, with a press item saying so. A drawdown is never rationed: a government that cuts is not short of the money it is no longer spending.

`appropriationQuality` reads the chair through `budgetChair` (`stewardship.ts`), and the split domestic line reads each department's runner; both resolve a chair through `selectChairRunner`, so an acting Secretary prices the account as a confirmed one would and only a chair nobody runs is caretaker, which is the existing instrument→role matching for the four statecraft accounts and a domestic-secretary match for the fifth, so the clerical charter that folds intelligence into the interior portfolio is handled by the same rule rather than a special case. Competence scales what is *built* and never what a drawdown costs — the same asymmetry `movementToDeltas` applies.

`CAPABILITY_MILESTONES` (`budget-capability.ts`, with `turnsToReach` and `nextMilestone`; split from `budget.ts` at the Wave 2 integration to keep both under the file-size limit) declares, in machine-readable form, the capability figure each rung is gated on, so the screen can say what the money is for ("Proxy Support opens in 26 weeks"). The gates themselves stay closures over live state in `instruments.ts`; `budget.test.ts` proves the declaration has not drifted by building a campaign one point below each threshold and asserting the rung it names still refuses. Only rungs gated on something money can move are listed — a blockade needs a coastal target, and promising that on a budget screen would be a promise the ladder does not keep.

Wired into the turn as `budgetPhase`, immediately after `policyPhase`: both draw on the same treasury, so the order is fixed — the country is governed first, and the estimates are drawn on what governing it left behind. 

What the state is *capable of* (these four readings) is a reading the player needs beside how the administration is faring, because it decides whether half the ladder is even offered. DESIGN.md places it under `/nation/budget`.

### Capacity: the ceiling the ladder never had

`packages/engine/src/capacity.ts`.

Authored figures like `military.branchStrength.land` are static: the United States holds 0.7 however often it invades. A gate on a static figure means nothing an administration does reduces what it can do next week. Capacity is the figure operations spend.

A **capacity** is an institutional reading that operations spend and time restores toward what the establishment supports. There are two, in one table and one phase, so the two ladders give the same answer to the same question:

| Capacity | Anchor | Spent by | Recovery |
|---|---|---|---|
| `military.readiness` | the authored figure, raised (never lowered) in proportion to how far appropriations have grown the establishment (`readinessAnchor`: doctrine, the four branch strengths, air- and sealift), less `commitmentDrag` | military rungs 3/4/5, assistance rungs 4/5 | 0.02 a week |
| `security.intelCapability` | the authored figure | covert rungs 4/5 | 0.015 a week |

Branch strength still says what the services *are*; readiness says what is available now. The military rungs are therefore gated **twice**, and `CAPABILITY_MILESTONES` carries both halves so the budget screen can say that an appropriation buys tempo rather than a permanent license. At these rates an expeditionary invasion is affordable roughly every eight weeks. Readiness is spent when an act lands, so `forceAvailable` (`instrument-gates.ts`) subtracts what the actor's issued rung 3 to 5 military acts still waiting to execute will spend, at their unjittered `MILITARY_READINESS_COST` scaled by the ordered package: a run of timed strikes is refused at issue once the orders already given use up the gate, rather than shown as lawful and refused when the first one lands.

Three further constraints ship with it. The invasion, blockade and strike rungs, plus carrier presence, want **named force packages** composed from uncommitted formations of a typed unit (`ForcePackage`: branch, unitType, count, and an optional `preferred`) rather than a literal 200 airframes or 8 brigades. `composeForcePackages(campaign, actor, target?)` is the staff table, and with a target it sizes each package against what that target fields: `specAgainst` reads the target's `branchStrength` for the arm the package faces (`opposingStrength`: air for a strike, naval for a blockade, land for a corps) and proposes `floor + (fixed size − floor) × strength^1.5`, floors 24 airframes / 4 hulls / 2 brigades, never above the package's old fixed size, with the minimum at half the proposal (never above the old minimum). Carrier presence is not sized. `instruments.ts` accepts an optional `force` on the resolution request and scales effects against the package's `preferred` (the old fixed size when absent, as on every earlier save), so the staff's proposal for any target resolves at scale 1 and a peer target behaves exactly as before. Money is not scaled that way: it is the units sent times their price (`force-costs.ts`), so a smaller package sized against a weaker target costs less. `issueAction` recomputes `preferred` from the target rather than trusting the request, prices the projection on that normalized package so the escrow equals the bill, and stores it on the act; at execution the capability gate takes its floor from the stored `preferred`, so a target growing stronger during preparation cannot expire a legal order. It also opens a standing commitment for those units. The bench a package is composed from is the order of battle as the budget has grown it (`establishmentScale`: live branch strength over the strength the campaign opened with, `BRANCH_STRENGTH_OF` mapping each service to its reading) at the share national readiness can field this week (`readyShare`: readiness over its anchor), so a force spent in one crisis is visibly smaller in the next, and `selectForceBench` reads establishment, fielded, committed and available for one holding. An absent decomposition still reads as *unknown*, never as an empty bench, because most nations author none and reading the absence as zero would refuse them rungs they have always been able to take. A scale above what is free is refused with the engine sentence, not a toast. The weekly `tickConflict` then reads committed strength (`theaterScale`) rather than a static `branchStrength`. The three rungs that stand a *state* rather than perform an act (full embargo, severed relations, security guarantee) refuse while that state stands: breaking off relations twice is not twice as severe, it is the second one meaning nothing. And an embargo costs the actor its own export sector, and a severance its own diplomatic reach, because both are two-way facts.

`capacityPhase` runs immediately after `cityPhase` (itself after wars and conditions), because refit is what the week after an action is. It runs for NPCs on the same arithmetic as for the player: a rule that only depleted the player's capacity would hand the world a tempo the player cannot match.

### Commitments: standing allocations the world remembers

`packages/engine/src/commitments.ts` (DECISIONS.md §6.6).

Aid packages, arms transfers, deployments, basing, war sustainment and reconstruction are durable facts, not one-shot effects. A `Commitment` is that fact: kind, target, a per-turn resource carrying its own `UnitSpec`, the budget line or order-of-battle path it draws down, the turn it started, whether it is standing or ends, and a status.

Three things read it and nothing else has to. `fiscal.ts` counts an active money commitment as an outlay line, so the deficit feels it. `capacity.ts` counts an active force commitment as units that are **not at home**, so a second crisis finds a thinner bench and the readiness ceiling itself comes down by `commitmentDrag` — forces abroad are forces not resting, not training and not in the depot. And `commitmentPhase` emits the week's total through the ordinary `EffectPacket` path: there is no side-channel, `apply()` is still the only mutator, and a commitment's week is a packet like any other. It runs after `budgetPhase`, because a promise abroad is drawn on what the appropriation left rather than on what the vote said.

Four rungs leave one behind — an embargo is enforced, an invasion is sustained, a guarantee is garrisoned, a transfer is a schedule of deliveries — each priced as a share of national income at the real order of magnitude for the thing (about a hundred billion a year for one sustained expeditionary war, a couple of billion for sanctions enforcement). `openCommitment` **refuses** a deployment the order of battle cannot supply rather than promising it anyway: a promise discovered as empty at the moment it is called on is worse than being told now. A wound-down commitment is kept rather than deleted, same as a settled war.

**Missions at home.** A commitment's target is a nation, a conflict, an organization or, since the domestic force acts, one of the President's own states (`CommitmentTarget.kind: "state"`). A state-targeted commitment is not a promise abroad: it writes no `deed-toward` flag, it has no map location (`commitment-locations.ts` returns nothing for it), and it is excluded from the kept-promises standing credit. `Commitment.authorizedBy` names the order record the force was sent under. `expired` is true whenever that order's `prospectiveOrderAuthority` is not `operative`, so a court's interim injunction, a rescission or a strike-down releases the brigade or the Guard the next week and stops the bill. `setCommitmentStatus` refuses a hand wind-down of such a commitment ("Rescind the order that sent them"). Because troops are mobilized before the first week on the street, a rescission inside `MISSION_MINIMUM_WEEKS` (2) charges the remaining weeks of that minimum at the weekly amount (`minimumOwed`); a court's pause or strike-down charges none. `CommitmentDraft.excepted` lets federal force sent to keep order go on through a lapse in appropriations (31 U.S.C. §1342: emergencies involving the safety of human life or the protection of property); `moneyShortfall` tests a full year of the weekly amount, not the term.

### The fiscal statement

`packages/engine/src/fiscal.ts`.

Four capability accounts and a treasury number are a statecraft budget, not a national one. `selectFiscalStatement` says what the government spends on pensions, where the money comes from, and whether the country runs a deficit: **revenue** by source (income, corporate, tariff, other) read off the standing fiscal and trade programs, **outlay** by function (defense, entitlements, discretionary, foreign affairs, debt service) with defense taken from the `military.budget` the dataset already states rather than a second figure, the balance, and `commitmentCost` reported separately because it is the part a decision can end. `selectDebtTrajectory` makes the debt a **series**: two governments at 1.2 of output with opposite balances are in completely different situations, and one reading shows them as identical.

`selectSolvency(campaign, allocation?)` is the same file's answer to a narrower and more immediate question: can this administration pay for what it has just voted itself? It returns `appropriationCost` (the capability accounts' weekly draw above the sustainment line), `commitmentCost` (what the standing commitments draw weekly), their sum as `weeklyDraw`, `turnsFunded` (`Infinity` when nothing net is drawn — a government holding the line can hold it forever), an `underfunded` flag and a `summary`. The threshold is `SOLVENCY_HORIZON_TURNS`, **a year**, which is what the appropriation is *for*: the honest statement is "this draft does not reach the next appropriation", not "this draft runs out eventually". It counts commitments (the sustainment of a war, a basing arrangement, a standing program) as well as the capability appropriation, because those are the spending a president is least able to see and most likely to accumulate. Three of the four accounts at their cap plus one war's sustainment does not reach the next appropriation.

Everything is derived. Nothing is stored, so a screen recomputes it live while the player is still deciding and two screens cannot disagree about the deficit. The tariff line is the duties in force at the oath plus the duties on the administration's own tariffs, read live from the trade register: a trade program's name moves nothing, and a tariff collects every week it stands and nothing after it is lifted.

**Two books for one government, and why they are not reconciled.** `selectFiscalStatement` is the *country's* year — every dollar of federal revenue against every dollar of federal outlay, pensions and interest included. `player.metrics.treasury` is not a slice of it and never was, and it is not cash either. It is **budget authority**: the part of the enacted discretionary appropriation this administration is currently holding, founded at a quarter of the year's line (`openingBudgetAuthority`, `funding.ts` — 357,485 for the United States) and released a week at a time by `apportionmentPhase`. That is why an executive order charges it, an appropriation above the sustainment line draws it, and holding the establishment the country already funds costs it nothing. It is floored at zero and capped at the carry share: a government cannot be overdrawn on its own appropriation, and an unobligated balance is not banked. The founding opens on the carry share at most: `openingPosition` lets a seat's treasury push take authority away but never add past the share, and `appropriationBound` states the difference as one contribution line, so the founded stock equals `openingBudgetAuthority` and `authorityCeiling`. It keeps the key `treasury` and only the key, because that key is the scenario schema's commitment-cost field across two dozen content files. A stance's `perTurn` treasury figure (`treasuryOfGdp`) is an annual rate prorated to the week, and its stability and standing are weekly movements applied whole, which the country's order settling back (`orderSettling`, below) turns into a held level rather than a running total; while the appropriation has lapsed the money line stops for the weeks of the shutdown. Its `enactment` is a lump booked whole in the week it is signed, so enactment figures are sized against the authority's stock rather than against a year of national output — the heaviest on the catalogue obligates about an eighth of what a founding holds.

Folding the weekly national balance into the authority is rejected on the arithmetic. The federal balance is −1,964,384 a year against output of 27,498,847: **7.1% of output**, not "roughly a tenth". One week of it is 37,777, which is **10.6% of the 357,485 an administration opens holding** and a runway of **nine and a half weeks** — so a campaign really would be out of authority inside a quarter having decided nothing, and every price in the game would have to be rescaled against a number moving for reasons no player caused. That is not one book; it is the wrong book, because it charges an administration for the pension system it inherited. So the seam is documented rather than closed, here and in `SCHEMA.md`, and the comment block above `selectFiscalOutlook` in `fiscal.ts` carries the same statement where an engineer will actually meet it.

What the budget gets instead is the reading the seam would hide. `selectFiscalOutlook(campaign, allocation?)` composes `selectSolvency` and `selectFiscalStatement` into a forward view: `annualDraw` (a year of the standing draft), the `discretionary` outlay line it is written inside, `shareOfDiscretionary`, `balanceWithDraw`, and `survivesTheYear` — which is `selectSolvency`'s own `underfunded` inverted rather than a second opinion about the same question. No player surface reads `selectFiscalOutlook` yet.

**It has no player reader yet.** DESIGN.md places it at `/nation/budget`: one deficit/surplus lead with outlay and treasury scans, outlay as a share bar over DataRows, revenue as a share bar only, the commitment cost stated separately as the endable part, and the debt trajectory drawn as the series it is rather than the single ratio. The lead computes no money — every figure comes off `selectFiscalStatement`, which is what keeps the screen and the deficit in step. It sits **above** the four accounts on purpose: they are a share of this outlay, and a president moving defense without knowing what defense already costs against pensions and interest is moving a slider rather than a budget.

### Public opinion (`opinion.ts`)

The country's own reading of the administration, and the headline itself.

`standing` is what *other nations* make of the President; stability, readiness and the treasury say how strong the state is. None of them is what the governed think, which is the question a head of government governs against, and without it a relief operation that saved a province and a cover-up that spared the government embarrassment would resolve into the same numbers.

Six judgments are stored (`player.opinion`, see SCHEMA.md); approval is derived from them under **salient weights**, which is what makes the model political rather than arithmetic. A public does not weigh six things evenly and permanently — it weighs whatever is on the front page — so a live war multiplies security, a live emergency (a disaster, the epidemic or unrest: `reliefConditions`; the economic standing kinds are billed through the economy and not here) multiplies services and competence, economic pain multiplies prosperity, and a standing scandal multiplies integrity. Economic pain is measured against the term-start economy, not an absolute line: prices or joblessness 3 points of inflation or 2 of unemployment past where the term began (`ECONOMIC_PAIN`). Prosperity reads the live economy against the term start, plus a price-level term (`PRICE_LEVEL_WEIGHT`, prices above where the term-start inflation would have put them), so cooling inflation does not buy back prices already paid. The multipliers apply to the resting weights (`OPINION_WEIGHTS`) and are renormalized, so salience shifts the ordering instead of overwriting it and approval stays a 0..1 reading whatever is happening. `OPINION_WEIGHTS` is the primary balance lever for the whole domestic half of the game.

Salience fires on **change**, not on level. A story makes its judgment louder when it breaks or gets materially worse (`SALIENCE_ESCALATION`: a quarter worse and at least 0.05 worse) and for `SALIENCE_HOLD_WEEKS` (8) after, then the country has priced it in: the level still bills its judgment through the targets, but it no longer reweights the week. `Campaign.salienceMemory` holds when each live story last broke and at what level, and `opinionPhase` writes it through `OpinionEffect.salience`. While a judgment is salient it revises at `SALIENT_OPINION_DRIFT` (0.20) a week instead of `OPINION_DRIFT` (0.06); steep moves come from targets plus that fast revision, not from direct writes.

A live war the country is a party to, its own or one it joined (`atWarAsParty`), multiplies security, and `opinionPhase`'s weekly revision is filed in the background, off the Record. `opinionPhase` runs late in the turn — after the world has acted, the wars and conditions have taken their week, the cabinet has answered and the press has hardened around it, and before removal — and drifts each judgment toward what the country can currently see (`opinionTargets`, read off live metrics, conditions, conflicts and the live scandal board — see `press-scandal.ts` above) at `OPINION_DRIFT` per turn.

**`opinionTargets` lives in `opinion-causes.ts`, and it is the sum of its named terms.** A second function that explains the first would go stale against it the week either changed, so the decomposition holds the targets: `selectOpinionCauses()` returns each contributing term named, signed and sized under an `OpinionCauseKey`, and the target is their sum, which means the explanation cannot disagree with the figure. Clamps that bite report a `bounded` line rather than being swallowed. `heaviestCause` names the heaviest material drag (at least `MATERIAL_DRAG`, 2.5 points of the judgment), or what holds the judgment up most when nothing drags it, and never the resting base or the bound. Where a judgment has fallen over the four-week window, player-core's `approvalDrag` and `approvalDrivers` (`desk/opinion-causes.ts`) name the term that moved instead: prosperity's terms are re-read by the engine's own `opinionCausesFor` against the economy as `economyHistory` kept it when the window opened, so an inflation shock is blamed on prices and not on a heavier joblessness term that held still. The other judgments' inputs are not kept week by week, so they name their heaviest standing term. `selectSalience()` names what made a judgment loud this week, and `OPINION_HALF_LIFE_TURNS` is derived from `OPINION_DRIFT` rather than restated. Importers reach it from `opinion-causes.ts` directly, since a re-export inside the engine's import cycle loads in the wrong order. `opinionPhase` also files the week's scandal headline, if any: breaking, fading or resolving is reported off the same derived stage rather than a stored transition. Slow on purpose: a public carries an impression and revises it rather than re-rating a government weekly, which makes a sustained condition worth more than a dramatic week.

**One bill per cause.** A condition reaches approval through its target term and its salience and nowhere else. The conditions' weekly `approvalDrag` writer, its field and `APPROVAL_DRIVER` are deleted (`conditions.ts`, `condition-impacts.ts`): a drag written beside a target billed one fact twice and appeared in no cause list. Security starts at `SECURITY_BASE` (0.6) plus `SECURITY_ORDER_WEIGHT` (0.4) × (stability − the order the country's institutions usually hold, from `openingExpectations`), so order bills its departure from rest once, in security; competence's stability term is deleted and `COMPETENCE_BASE` is 0.35. Vacancies cost competence as a share of the cabinet's seats (`VACANCY_WEIGHT` 0.75 × the empty share), because a count charged an empty seat the same on a five-seat cabinet as on a fifteen-seat one. Unity is `UNITY_BASE` (0.6) − `UNITY_DIVISION_WEIGHT` (1.6) × the stock's drift above the era's anchor (`polarizationDrift`), so it reads what this administration did to division and not the era's level, which the five blocs already carry. `fragility.ts`'s `fiscalSpace` reads `treasuryHealth`, the scale every other reader of the treasury uses. The recurring writers' direct moves on a judgment are budgeted at `WEEKLY_DIRECT_MOOD_BUDGET` (0.05 per judgment per week, `capTurnMood` in `turn-loop.ts`): anything recurring that should move a judgment further is a named target term, where the cause is visible. `position-assessment.ts` softens a credit by `creditDamping`, full at the era's division and half at the stock's ceiling, reading only the division this administration added.

**A new administration opens above its record, and the President's own side is a floor under it.** Both live inside the bloc formula (`electorate.ts`), not beside it. The honeymoon is `HONEYMOON_VALENCE` (0.3) added to the valence every bloc reads, on a straight line to zero by `HONEYMOON_WEEKS` (26) (`honeymoonValence(turn)`), so it reaches each bloc by that bloc's slope: identifiers move least and pure independents most, and the headline carries about two points of it at the oath at the era's polarization (`DESIGN.md` §4.6, a probe). The polls, the ballot, the states and the removal arc all read it because there is one valence; the earlier `HONEYMOON_BONUS` addend on the headline, which kept it from the coalitions and cohorts, and `honeymoonBonus` are deleted. The partisan floor is the bloc intercept, `BLOC_INTERCEPT` × polarization × partisanship, with the President's own blocs' intercept scaled by (1 − erosion): erosion = integrity gravity (integrity under `EROSION_INTEGRITY_LINE` 0.4) × live-scandal exposure × `erosionPassThrough` (full in a depolarized country, half at the era's stock). It is a product, so neither a private doubt nor a story about a government the country still believes honest moves it. That is why ordinary bad governing leaves the President's identifiers approving (about 78% at a 30% headline at the era's stock, a design probe) and a scandal that collapses integrity does not. `partisanBase`, `PARTISAN_BASE_FLOOR` and `POLARIZATION_ANCHOR` are deleted.

`opinion-causes.ts`'s decomposition also carries the promise a President made: a settled pledge writes the term directly (`PROMISE_INTEGRITY`: a broken first-hundred-days promise −0.12, a broken term promise −0.05, a kept one +0.04 or +0.015) and fades on a straight line over `PROMISE_MEMORY_WEEKS` (52), so a broken promise is carried by the weekly revision rather than erased the week after it breaks.

`coalitionBreakPhase`, the `coalition-break:<coalition>` flag and the `coalition-break` willingness question are deleted with the coalitions (`coalition-break.ts`, `coalitions.ts`); nothing durable is stored for a break. What leaves the President's caucus is read live off the President's own identifiers (`caucusBreakShare`), and a party giving up on its own President entirely is the inability watch (`RemovalArc.inability`, "Removal is two votes, not a reading").

**Approval is the headline.** It is the poll `opinion.ts` derives from the six judgments a public makes (`OPINION_WEIGHTS`: prosperity 0.28, security 0.22, services 0.16, integrity 0.14, competence 0.12, unity 0.08, reweighted by `SALIENCE_MULTIPLIERS` for whatever the week is about, then renormalized).
